# **Husserl's *Thing and Space* (1907)**

# INTRODUCTION

### **§1. The world of natural experience and the world of scientific theory**

This is the opening section of the Thing and Space lectures proper — the first section after the five introductory lectures (published separately as The Idea of Phenomenology) in which the phenomenological reduction was established. §1 sets the scene for the entire constitutive investigation by characterizing the world as it stands before us in the natural attitude and then locating the lectures' specific task within the larger phenomenological project.

**Situating the lectures.** Husserl opens by noting that the "general introduction" (i.e., the Five Lectures) is complete. The concepts of phenomenological reduction, pure phenomenon, and the constitution of the object of cognition within cognition have been clarified, at least provisionally. What follows will deal with "the foundational parts of a future phenomenology of experience" — a clarification of the essence of experiential givenness in its "lower forms and levels," beginning from the "immediate and first beginnings" and carried out as deeply and broadly as possible.

Husserl pauses to clarify terminology. He resists calling this a "theory of experience," both because the word "theory" carries connotations of mathematical-natural-scientific explanation that are inappropriate to phenomenology, and because the neo-Kantian Marburg School (Cohen) has already appropriated the term to cover all the problems of pure theoretical reason. The lectures' ambitions are more modest: to cultivate the first field of experience, its phenomena and givens. The higher problems — the constitution of natural-scientific reality through deduction, induction, and logically mediated cognition — will have to wait. "We will be doing well if we cultivate this field so effectively that our successors can then attempt to raise the higher forms of the problems."

**The natural attitude.** The core of the section is a rich, carefully layered description of the world as given in the natural attitude — before any phenomenological reflection. Husserl builds this up in concentric rings:

*The spatial-temporal world.* In the natural attitude, an existing world stands before our eyes, extending infinitely in space, enduring through time. It consists of an inexhaustible abundance of things that endure, change, combine, separate, exercise effects on one another and undergo them.

*The ego as center of reference.* We find ourselves in the midst of this world, but with a "pre-eminent position": we are centers of reference for the rest of the world, which is our *environment* (*Umwelt*). Environing objects have their properties and relations "for themselves," but they also have a position relative to us — initially spatio-temporal, but also "spiritual."

*Perception and its horizons.* We immediately perceive a first environment around us, contemporaneous with us, standing in the relation of being seen, touched, heard. Actual perceptions are connected to possibilities of perception, to presentifying intuitions. The nexuses of immediate perception contain "conductors" (*Leitfäden*) leading from perception to perception, from a first environment to ever new ones, and thereby the perceptual gaze attains things in the order of spatiality. This is already an embryonic statement of the horizon-structure of perception — the very structure the rest of the lectures will analyze constitutively.

*Temporal environment.* We also have a temporal environment: memory functions as a conductor leading us back in time step by step, and expectation opens the future. Past and future reality enter into relation to us through these acts.

*Higher acts.* Upon these lower acts are erected thinking, concluding, theorizing, as well as emotional acts (evaluating, willing, acting). These constitute new relations to the world belonging to different spheres.

*Other egos.* In this same world we find other egos, who have their own environments, their own perceptual perspectives, their own conclusions and feelings. They occupy different positions and therefore possess different immediate environments, but if positions were exchanged, so would environments and perceptions be exchanged. The world breaks down into physical and spiritual things — merely physical things and those that are spiritual as well (beings with lived experiences, perceiving, remembering, etc.). Spiritual beings are also physical: they possess color, shape, spatial position, temporal duration. But they additionally possess lived experiences and "spiritual properties" connected to physical states through functional nexuses of psycho-physical causality.

**The relation of science to experience.** Husserl then addresses the relationship between scientific and prescientific experience. The sciences of physical nature concern things with respect to their physical qualities; psychology and psychophysics concern lived experiences. Both relate to the reality we see, touch, grasp through our senses.

Scientific conceptions may deviate enormously from prescientific experience — science may teach that sense qualities have no immediate objective reference. Yet the natural scientist always determines *that thing there* — the one seen, held, laid on the scale. All reality-judgments of natural science lead back to straightforward perceptions and memories. All mediate grounding rests on immediate givenness. The lived experiences in which reality is given immediately are perception, memory, and (in a certain immediacy) expectation.

Husserl acknowledges hallucination, illusion, deceptive memory, and delusional expectation. But it would be obvious nonsense to explain *everything* given immediately from these sources as illusory — doing so would destroy not only commonsense reality but the reality of science itself.

**The justification for beginning "from below."** The section closes with a methodological reflection that grounds the entire lecture course. The foregoing consideration, which "still moves completely on natural soil," shows that we can in good conscience begin from below — from "lower and common experience" — without fear that this starting point is irrelevant to the highest problems of the constitution of scientific reality. The phenomenology of straightforward perceptual experience is not a detour or a preliminary exercise; it is the foundation without which the higher constitutive problems cannot even be posed.

**Why this section matters.** §1 accomplishes several essential tasks for the lectures as a whole.

First, it establishes the *scope and limits* of the investigation. The lectures will address only the lowest stratum of experiential givenness — perceptual constitution prior to all deduction, induction, and higher theoretical cognition. This is not a limitation born of modesty alone; it reflects Husserl's conviction that the higher constitutive strata presuppose and build upon the lower, so that clarity about perception is a necessary condition for any future phenomenology of scientific cognition.

Second, the description of the natural attitude provides the *material* that the constitutive analyses will work on. Every feature mentioned here — the spatial extension of the world, the temporal horizons, the ego as center of reference, the perceptual conductors leading from environment to environment, the relationship between what is properly seen and what is co-apprehended — will reappear in the constitutive analyses as a problem to be phenomenologically accounted for. The "conductors" from perception to perception are precisely what the kinaesthetic analyses of §§58–69 will explicate. The ego as "center of reference" is the Ego-point whose constitutive role §§64 and 69 will analyze. The infinite extension of the world in space is the horizon that §§59 and 61 will show to be constituted through the progressive amplification of the oculomotor field.

Third, the argument that science rests on immediate experience and that it would be nonsensical to dismiss all immediate givenness as illusory secures the *legitimacy* of beginning with perception. This is not epistemological foundationalism in the classical sense — Husserl is not claiming that perceptual beliefs are infallible — but rather the claim that the *sense* of reality, including scientific reality, is constituted in and through the structures of immediate experience. The constitutive investigation to follow will show *how* this happens, step by step.

Finally, the section exemplifies Husserl's characteristic methodological movement: begin in the natural attitude, describe what is given there with maximal fidelity, and then — in the sections that follow — subject that givenness to phenomenological analysis. The natural attitude is not abandoned or refuted but *clarified* through constitutive investigation. The world that §1 describes in its full richness and self-evidence is the same world whose constitution the lectures will spend eighty-three sections analyzing.

# SECTION I: THE FOUNDATIONS OF A PHENOMENOLOGICAL THEORY OF PERCEPTION

## Chapter 1: Fundamental Determinations of Outer Perception

### **§2. Restriction of the field of research. The preliminary concept of outer perception**

Section 2 is the first substantive section of the lectures proper (§1 having served as a general orientation to the field of investigation). It performs three tasks: it delimits the **subject matter** of the lectures, articulates the **methodological stance** that will govern the investigation, and provides a preliminary **phenomenological description** of outer perception through examples, identifying the structural features that the subsequent analyses will develop.

**The subject matter: experiential objectivity at the lowest level.** Husserl opens by stating the goal: to study the self-constitution — the self-manifestation — of experiential objectivity at its lowest level. This means the lived experiences of straightforward intuition or intuitive grasping, upon which the higher acts of the specifically logical sphere are first built. The logical acts "process" the underlying "sensuous material" and thereby bring about the constitution of scientific objectivity. But it is the lower, pre-logical level — the level of perception and its immediate extensions — that the lectures will investigate.

The primary focus is **perception**, studied first for itself and then in connection with all Objectivating phenomena close to it and on the same level. The correlation between perception and perceived thinghood is the guiding thread. Under "perceived thinghood" Husserl includes: the thing in the stricter sense of physical thing; the spiritual thing (the ensouled being); the distinction between one's own Ego and the alien Ego; and not merely the isolated thing but the thing together with its thingly environment, insofar as perception claims to function as the constituting phenomenon for this environment.

**Methodological openness.** Husserl immediately emphasizes the provisional character of the starting point. We do not yet know what perception *is* — not in the strict sense. We have the word and a vague meaning attached to it. The task is to go back to the phenomena themselves under the guidance of this vague meaning, to study them intuitively, and to create fixed concepts that would purely express the phenomenological givens. The goals themselves are not perfectly clear and will determine themselves only as the investigation proceeds. This is a characteristic expression of Husserl's methodological principle: phenomenology does not begin with fixed definitions but allows concepts to form themselves through the analysis.

**Independence from psychology and philosophy.** Husserl insists that the investigation cannot tie itself to the conceptual determinations of psychologists and philosophers. Those determinations are carried out from interests and points of view foreign to phenomenology. The goal of purely phenomenological analysis and the principle of the phenomenological reduction are alien to them; misunderstandings, confusions, and crude errors hold sway in their determinations from the outset. The investigation will proceed not indirectly, on the basis of what others say, but by approaching the matters themselves and allowing itself to be instructed by them.

**Proceeding by examples.** Husserl then turns to specific examples of **outer perception** — perceptions of physical things. Seeing, hearing, touching, smelling, tasting bring before our eyes examples of thing-perceptions. These words are taken from ordinary speech and used in their ordinary sense. "I see" always means: I see something — a thing, a property, a process. I see a house, the flying up of a bird, the falling of leaves. I see the color of the house, the structure and size of the leaf, the form of its motion. I hear the tone of a violin, the shouting of children, the droning of a bee. I also see and hear myself and other people — I see my hands, hear my own voice.

**The exclusion of the psychic.** Husserl notes that we also say "I see" in relation to the psychic domain — I see the anger in someone's appearance, the scorn in his look, the insincerity. But even a superficial consideration distinguishes this seeing from the seeing of a color or movement. The face and facial expression are *seen*; they are apprehended as the *expression* of something psychic which is not itself seen. The lectures will at first **exclude** this "seeing of what is psychic." This exclusion is methodologically crucial: it restricts the investigation to the constitution of *physical* thinghood, setting aside the constitution of psychic and intersubjective objectivity.

**The double relation of perception.** The consideration of examples reveals a unity in the way we speak of perception, and Husserl notices a **double relation**. Perception is perception *of* some object (some thing), and perception is the perception *of* a perceiving Ego. I perceive — specifically, this or that. The relation to the Ego belongs to perception as a lived experience, and it is found in every kind of lived experience (I phantasize, I judge, I feel — in each case, there is an Ego that phantasizes, judges, feels).

But in the case of perception, alongside this general Ego-relation, there is also a **perceptual relation of the Object to the Ego-Body** (*Ichleib*), and further, a certain constitution in the character of **total perception**: I have my standpoint, and pertaining to this standpoint, a perceived environment to which the specifically perceived thing belongs. This is the first appearance in the lectures of the Ego-Body and the perceptual standpoint — themes that will become central in the kinaesthetic analyses of Sections IV–V.

**The double abstraction.** Husserl announces two provisional abstractions that will govern the initial analyses: first, the investigation will abstract as much as possible from the Ego-relations (the relation to the Ego-Body and the standpoint). Second, it will not immediately focus on the differences between "total perception" (the entire perceptual field with its background) and the "separate perception" of the Object that is especially called perceived. These differences are noted and used to designate a restriction to separate perceptions.

This double abstraction defines the scope of Sections II–III (§§14–43): the analysis of the internal structure of thing-perception (presentational contents, apprehension, proper and improper appearance, the kinetic synthesis, adequacy) proceeds under the simplification that the Ego-Body and the perceptual standpoint are not yet thematized. It is only when these abstractions are lifted — in §§44ff — that the kinaesthetic body and its constitutive role come into view.

**Significance.** §2 establishes the entire framework within which the lectures will operate. Its most important contributions are: (1) the identification of the subject matter as the constitution of physical thinghood at the lowest experiential level, prior to logical and scientific processing; (2) the insistence on proceeding from examples rather than from inherited conceptual determinations; (3) the preliminary identification of the double relation of perception (to the object and to the Ego-Body) and of the structure of total versus separate perception; and (4) the provisional abstractions that will be progressively lifted as the analysis deepens. Every subsequent section of the lectures can be located within the program announced here — from the static analysis of §§14–25 (the internal structure of separate perception, abstracting from the Ego), through the kinetic analysis of §§26–43 (the manifold of changing perception, still largely abstracting from the Ego-Body), to the kinaesthetic analysis of §§44–57 and beyond (where the Ego-Body and its movement are finally thematized as constitutive conditions of spatial givenness).

### **§3. Essential cognition of perception on the basis of phantasized perceptions**

Section 3 is a compact but methodologically crucial section. It establishes the precise **epistemological mode** in which the lectures will proceed: eidetic analysis carried out under the phenomenological reduction, with the striking thesis that **phantasized** perceptions serve just as well as actual perceptions for the grasping of essential structures. The section also makes the first concrete move toward analysis by specifying the example that will guide the investigation (the perception of a house) and clarifying the layered, progressive character of phenomenological inquiry.

**The phenomenological reduction in operation.** Husserl begins by confirming that the preliminary consideration of §2 already employs the phenomenological reduction. Physical existence does not have validity as existence; such questions are completely left out of play. When we have examples of perception before our eyes and propose to study the case of separate perception, we do not tear this perception out of its phenomenological context. We are at liberty to look into the phenomenon and its Objectivating accomplishment and to study its essential peculiarities.

This "looking" involves an **absolute givenness** — but this does not mean that the background and the Ego whose phenomenon it is are nothing, simply because within the framework of this looking they do not present themselves as given. The separate perception is seen as absolute givenness and is the foundation of the assertions destined to express what is purely presented in it. About anything else precisely nothing is said. Everything remains open until we find the occasion to draw upon relevant new givens.

**The example: the perception of a house.** Husserl then announces the guiding example: the perception of a house. We will give an account of what we find phenomenologically — with everything that does not concern us counting as nothing: Ego, house, and perception of the house as a psychological lived experience. The question at issue is the **essence** of this perception, just as it is given in the consciousness that is intuiting and holding fast to the essence as identical. The unique fact, the phenomenological singularity of the "this here," is not the goal; what matters is what can be grasped as identically the same across variations.

**The layered character of phenomenological analysis.** Husserl acknowledges that what is first acquired may not have ultimate validity — it may need to be greatly deepened and may harbor unsuspected problems. But it is the general nature of phenomenology to press on **layer by layer from the surface into the depths**. Products of a first analysis require a new purifying distillation; the new products do likewise, until the final, completely pure and clear result is attained. This programmatic remark accurately describes the actual progression of the lectures: the initial static analysis (§§14–18) will be deepened by the kinetic analysis (§§26–39), which will in turn be deepened by the kinaesthetic analysis (§§44–57), each layer uncovering structures that the previous layer presupposed but did not thematize.

**The central methodological thesis: phantasized perceptions suffice.** The most distinctive and philosophically important claim of §3 is that the examples serving the analysis do not need to be *actual* perceptions. It is not a condition of phenomenological analysis that the grasping of essence must be carried out on the basis of individual instances of actually existing lived experiences. We might begin with actual perceptions — perceiving this bench, this surface, and reflecting on that perception (so-called inner perception). But the positing of existence involved in reflection — the positing of the perception as *cogitatio*, as actual, as currently existing — remains out of play. It has nothing to say.

**Phantasized presentifications** of perceptions serve just as well, insofar as they place before our eyes perceptions, and we then in fact see — and can grasp as given with evidence — what we want to grasp: the essence of perception, the meaning of something like "perception." We have no interest in the dignity of givenness as actual lived experience versus mere presentification. Nor are we interested in the constitution of the conscious formations making up the evidence we are carrying out step by step. We investigate the essential peculiarities of perception, not the evidence in which we investigate them.

This thesis has profound implications. It means that phenomenological analysis is not introspective psychology — it does not depend on having actual perceptual experiences and inspecting them from within. It can work just as well with imagined, phantasized, or merely possible perceptions. What matters is that the essential structures are grasped with evidence, and this can happen on the basis of any exemplar, actual or imagined, that brings the relevant essence to intuitive givenness. This is what later phenomenology calls the method of **free variation in phantasy** (*freie Variation in der Phantasie*) — the technique of varying examples imaginatively in order to identify what remains invariant as the essence.

**Singular essences and essential generalization.** Husserl closes with a clarification of the ontological status of the givens in play. If the existence of the exemplar perceptions is set out of play — even their existence as *cogitationes* — and if mere phantasized perceptions fully suffice, then what is given in the absolute sense is not something *existent* and yet is a *being*: namely, a **singular essence** in each case. This singular perception here, whether existing or not, is a singular essence. It is to these singular givens that the evident essential generalizations of a higher level relate: from them we draw out the universal essence "perception in general," which is singularized in them as such and such.

**Significance.** §3 establishes the epistemological and methodological foundation on which every subsequent analysis in the lectures rests. Three points are essential:

First, the analysis operates under the phenomenological reduction: no existence-claims about things, Ego, or perceptions-as-psychological-events are in play. What is investigated is the *essence* of perception, not facts about particular perceptions.

Second, phantasized perceptions are on equal footing with actual ones for the purposes of eidetic analysis. This liberates the investigation from dependence on contingent perceptual situations and makes it possible to pursue the essential structures of perception through imaginative variation — a method that the lectures will employ constantly (e.g., in imagining an infinitely complete intellect with a three-dimensional field of view in §33, or in varying the dimensions and topology of space in §35).

Third, the layered, progressive character of phenomenological inquiry is explicitly acknowledged. The initial analyses are provisional — they will be deepened, corrected, and enriched as the investigation penetrates further. This programmatic honesty is borne out by the actual course of the lectures, where the initial static analysis is progressively complicated and enriched by the kinetic, the adequacy-theoretic, and the kinaesthetic analyses, each layer revealing structures that could not have been seen from the previous level.

### **§4. Intentionality as the essential determination of perception**

Section 4 introduces the first essential characterization of perception: its **intentionality**, its essential relatedness to an object. More importantly, it identifies what is *distinctive* about perceptual intentionality as opposed to the intentionality of other types of consciousness (phantasy, image-consciousness, thought), and thereby establishes the concept of **presence in the flesh** (*leibhaftige Gegenwart*) as the defining character of perception. This concept will function as the phenomenological starting point from which the entire subsequent analysis unfolds.

**Intentionality as an essential character.** Husserl begins with the basic observation: to speak about perception is to refer to the thing perceived. Within the sphere of pure evidence, the relation to the object constitutes an essential character of perception. In perceiving this bench or that house — or in presentifying such a perceiving to ourselves — we find that saying "this perception is a perception of a bench" expresses something that belongs *indissociably* to the essence of the perception in question. This is not an empirical observation about particular perceptions but an essential, eidetic finding about what perception *is*.

**The generality of intentionality beyond perception.** But this object-relatedness is not unique to perception. Other *cogitationes*, other pure phenomena, share it: phantasy of a house, image-presentation of a house, thinking of a house. In all these cases, the relation to an object — expressed by the word "of" — pertains essentially. Without entering into an investigation of the essences of these other types, we recognize with evidence that the object-relation is essential to them too.

**The distinctive character of perceptual intentionality.** What distinguishes perception from phantasy, image-consciousness, and thought is not intentionality as such but the **mode** of intentionality — the *how* of the object's givenness. Husserl formulates this in a passage that is foundational for the entire lectures:

In perception, the object stands there as **there in the flesh** (*leibhaftig da*). More precisely: as **actually present**, as **self-given there in the current now**. This is the specific character of perceptual givenness.

In phantasy, by contrast, the object does not stand there as in the flesh, as actual, as currently present. It stands before our eyes, but not as something currently given now. It may be thought of as now, or as simultaneous with the current now, but this "now" is a *thought* now, not the now that pertains to presence in the flesh, to perceptual presence. The phantasized is merely "represented" (*vorgestellt*) — it places something before us, presents it, but it does not give itself as itself, actual and now.

In the image, the imaged (*Bildsujet*) does not stand there in the flesh but only *as if* it were in the flesh. Something that is given in the flesh — the image-object (the painted canvas, the mental picture) — presents something that is not given in the flesh, and does so in the manner proper to an image.

Husserl acknowledges that this is a "first and still quite rough characterization" and that more precise research into the relations among these various forms of givenness would require comprehensive and difficult investigations. The lectures will not undertake those investigations; the distinction is established here only to isolate what is specific to perception so that its analysis can proceed.

**Presence in the flesh does not entail existence.** Husserl immediately adds a crucial clarification. The foregoing is *not* to be understood as implying that the existence of the perceived Object pertains to the essence of every perception. That would make talk of a perception whose object did not exist countersensical; illusory perceptions would be unthinkable. Rather, it is the **essential character** of perception to be "consciousness" of the Object's presence in the flesh — to be the *phenomenon* of it. To perceive a house means to have the consciousness, to have the phenomenon, of a house standing there in the flesh. How matters stand with the "so-called existence" of the house, with the "true Being" of the house, and what this existence means — about all that nothing is said.

This distinction — between the perceptual character of presence-in-the-flesh and the metaphysical question of the existence of the perceived — is one of the most fundamental in the lectures. It is the phenomenological reduction applied specifically to perception: we study the *essence* of perceptual givenness (the character of bodily presence) without presupposing or denying the existence of what is so given. Illusion and veridical perception share the same essential perceptual character; what distinguishes them is not something internal to the phenomenological structure of the perception itself but something that belongs to the nexus of experience within which the perception is integrated (as §43 on belief-positing will later indicate).

**Significance.** §4 provides the foundational concept of the entire lectures: **presence in the flesh** (*Leibhaftigkeit*). This is the phenomenological characterization of what distinguishes perception from all other modes of consciousness. It is not a theory about perception's reliability or its causal relation to objects; it is a description of *what it is like* to perceive — the specific manner in which the object is given. Everything that follows in the lectures is an analysis of how this character of bodily presence is constituted: through presentational contents and apprehension (§§14–15), through proper and improper appearance (§16), through the kinetic synthesis and its degrees of fullness (§§26–39), and through the kinaesthetic motivation of appearance-manifolds (§§44–57). The concept of presence in the flesh is the *explanandum* of the entire investigation; the constitutive analysis is the *explanans*.

The section also establishes the fundamental phenomenological principle that the mode of givenness (in the flesh, in phantasy, in image) is an *essential* character of the consciousness in question, not something added to an otherwise neutral "representation." Perception is not representation plus something extra (a judgment of existence, a causal relation to the object); it is a sui generis mode of consciousness in which the object is given in a way that is irreducible to any combination of non-perceptual elements. This anti-reductive principle is what makes the detailed constitutive analysis of the lectures necessary and possible: if perception were merely representation-plus-judgment, there would be nothing specifically perceptual to analyze. Because perception has its own essential character — presence in the flesh — the analysis of that character and its constitutive conditions is a genuine and self-standing phenomenological task.

### **§5. Presence in the flesh and belief. Perception \[Perzeption\] and position-taking**

Section 5 draws a fundamental distinction within the concept of perception identified in §4. It separates two moments that ordinary usage conflates: the **presence in the flesh** of the object (*Leibhaftigkeit*) and the **belief** (*Glaube*) in the object's existence. This distinction produces a refined terminological apparatus — *Perzeption* versus *Wahrnehmung* — and establishes the analytical strategy that will govern the rest of the lectures: the systematic bracketing of the doxic (belief-related) characters in order to study the appearance-stratum in its own right.

**The fusion of presence and belief in ordinary perception.** Husserl begins by noting that in the fundamental examples of perception taken in its usual sense, the moment of belief is *fused* with the moment of presence in the flesh. The perception — the phenomenon — of the house as standing there in the flesh is *at once* the belief that it is standing there. In normal, unproblematic perception, these two characters are given together so intimately that they seem to be one and the same. We perceive the house, and in perceiving it we believe it is there.

**The separability of presence and belief.** But they are not one and the same. Husserl shows this through a series of contrasting examples:

In an **unmasked hallucination** — one that I recognize as such — I find in place of belief *disbelief*. The object may still stand there with the full character of presence in the flesh (I still "see" the phantom), but I do not believe in its existence. This shows that presence in the flesh can persist even when belief is replaced by its opposite.

In cases of **perceptual doubt** — where I am uncertain whether what I am experiencing is perception or hallucination — both belief and disbelief are lacking. Instead there is doubt, and perhaps the suspension of every position-taking. Yet even here the phenomenon of the object's standing there in the flesh persists or can persist. I see *something* that presents itself bodily, even though I withhold judgment about its reality.

**The phenomenological distinction.** If we carry out the obvious phenomenological reductions on these cases, a distinction appears in the essence of perception in the ordinary sense: presence in the flesh is **fundamental and essential** to perception as such, while belief **can either supervene or be lacking**. The two are related but separable: presence in the flesh is a necessary condition for something to count as perception (even hallucination and doubted perception involve presence in the flesh), while belief is a further character that may or may not accompany it.

How these two characteristics are related, and how this relates to the questions of the sense of existence or non-existence and of justified versus unjustified belief — these are "substrates for new investigations" that the lectures will not pursue in detail. §43 will briefly address the role of belief-positing in the kinetic synthesis, but the full analysis of doxic modalities lies beyond the scope of these lectures.

**The terminological distinction: *Perzeption* versus *Wahrnehmung*.** Husserl introduces a terminological distinction to hold fast the conceptual separation. The German word *Wahrnehmung* ("perception") is ordinarily taken in a sense that includes the taking-for-true (*Für-wahr-Nehmen*), the character of belief. Husserl proposes to use the Latinized term **Perzeption** for the concept that is "more restricted in content" — perception understood as presence in the flesh *without* the moment of belief — and then to speak of **perceptual belief** (*perzeptiver Glaube*), which would be *Wahrnehmung* in the normal sense, and of perceptual disbelief, doubt, etc. as further doxic modifications built on the *Perzeption*.

Nevertheless, where the differences in position-taking are irrelevant — where there is no cause to separate them — Husserl will continue to speak of *Wahrnehmung*, leaving it open whether it is a matter of mere *Perzeption* or *Perzeption* with belief or with other phenomenal characters. Basically the lectures will be analyzing *Perzeptionen* — the appearance-stratum characterized by presence in the flesh — though using the more familiar German word for convenience.

**Significance.** §5 establishes one of the most consequential methodological decisions of the lectures: the decision to analyze the **appearance-stratum** (*Perzeption*, presence in the flesh) in abstraction from the **doxic stratum** (belief, doubt, disbelief, suspension). This is not merely a terminological convenience; it defines the level at which the entire constitutive analysis will operate.

The justification for this abstraction is that presence in the flesh is the *constant* element across all the doxic variations — it is what is common to veridical perception, illusion, hallucination, doubt, and suspended judgment, insofar as all of these involve the object's standing there bodily. Belief and its modifications are *variable* characters that supervene on this constant stratum. Since the lectures aim to analyze the essential structure of perceptual givenness as such — how the thing is constituted as present in the flesh through presentational contents, apprehension, proper and improper appearance, the kinetic synthesis, and kinaesthetic motivation — the doxic characters can and should be set aside. They belong to a "higher stratum" (as §40 will confirm in the recapitulation) that is built on the appearance-stratum but does not determine its internal structure.

This decision has a precise parallel in the structure of *Ideas I*, where Husserl distinguishes the noematic "sense" (the manner in which the object is given) from the doxic "thetic character" (the mode of belief in which it is posited). The *Thing and Space* lectures show this distinction already fully at work in 1907, predating *Ideas I* by six years. The entire architectonic of the lectures — from §§14–43's analysis of appearance, through §§44–57's kinaesthetic analysis, to the incipient analysis of spatial constitution in Sections V–VI — is an analysis of the appearance-stratum in the sense of *Perzeption*. The doxic dimension surfaces only briefly, in §43, where Husserl acknowledges that the full constitution of the thing as an object of *experience* (not merely of appearance) requires the unifying function of belief running through the appearance-manifold. But this acknowledgment is precisely the point at which the lectures mark the boundary of their own investigation and gesture toward a further level of analysis that they do not undertake.

### **§6. Assertions about perceptions and assertions about perceived objects. Real \[reell\] and intentional components of perception**

Section 6 introduces what will become one of the most fundamental structural distinctions in the lectures: the distinction between the **real** (*reell*) content of perception and its **intentional** content. This distinction — between what genuinely belongs to the perception as a phenomenological datum and what belongs to the object as it is presented in the perception — is the conceptual axis around which the entire subsequent analysis of thing-constitution will turn.

**Perception and object are not the same.** Husserl begins with an observation that follows immediately from the intentionality thesis of §4: the evidence that perception is perception *of* this or that object already tells us that perception and object are not one and the same. Two series of evident assertions are possible at any time: assertions about the perception, and assertions about the object in the sense it has in perception. These are not interchangeable. Perception is not a thing; the perception of a surface is not a surface. The surface is quadrangular; the perception is not. Without making prejudgments about existence or non-existence, evident assertions can be formulated about the perceived object (as presenting itself in the flesh) — expressing that it is perceived and how it is perceived (as black, quadrangular, etc.). And on the other hand, evident assertions are possible concerning the perception as a phenomenon.

**The insufficiency of the act-content / object distinction.** Husserl notes that it is fashionable to distinguish between the "act-content" and the "object" for phenomena whose essence involves relating to an object. But this distinction is, he says, "by no means adequately clear, nor is it sufficient." The lectures will need something more differentiated.

**The fourfold distinction.** Husserl provisionally introduces a more articulated framework. He distinguishes:

First, the **appearance** and the **appearing object** — the perception as a lived experience versus the thing that presents itself through it.

Second, within each of these, the **content**: the *content of the appearance* (the real \[*reell*\] components of the appearance — its parts and inner moments as a phenomenon) and the *content of the object* (the parts and features with which the perception lets the object appear in the flesh).

The real (*reell*) content of the perception consists of what can be established phenomenologically with evidence as genuinely belonging to the perception as a phenomenon — its parts, moments, and determinations. The intentional content — the content of the appearing object — is what the perception presents its object as having: this shape, this color, this spatial extension.

The crucial point: the parts and features of the perception that presents the object are **not** the parts and features of the object that is presented. The real content and the intentional content are rigorously distinct. The color-datum in the perception is not the color of the object; the spatial extension of the pre-empirical visual field is not the spatial extension of the thing. This is the distinction that will be developed in detail from §14 onward, where presentational contents (real components of the appearance) will be carefully distinguished from the objective determinations they present.

**The problem of transcendence.** Husserl then raises a difficulty that will haunt the lectures: the **asymmetry** between the two kinds of givenness. The perception, subjected to phenomenological reduction, is an **absolute givenness** — I possess it, as it were, for itself, with all that essentially makes it up. It is "immanent." The intentional object, however, is precisely "transcendent." It appears in the flesh — it is essential to perception to present it in the flesh — but do I actually *possess* it itself, given along with the moments that really constitute it? Do I actually possess the table in its three-dimensional extension, which pertains to its essence? Do I really possess its essence?

And yet, Husserl notes, I have the evidence that the table *is* three-dimensional — in the sense of this presentation in the flesh. It appears as three-dimensional and as characterized in this or that way. The givenness proper to the perception (the phenomenon) is other than the givenness pertaining to the "perceived as such." The two instances of evidence are of different characters. At the same time, the second evidence belongs in a certain sense within the framework of the first, insofar as the essence of perception itself involves the presentation of an object in the flesh, presented as qualified in this or that way.

Husserl candidly acknowledges: "Further research is therefore needed. We are not now advanced far enough to resolve this difficulty."

**Significance.** §6 is foundational in several ways:

First, the **real/intentional distinction** is the conceptual instrument that makes possible the entire analysis of §§14–18. The distinction between presentational contents (real components of the appearance) and objective determinations (intentional content) is precisely the distinction between the real and the intentional content of perception. The analysis of how the one "presents" the other — through apprehension, through the proper/improper structure, through adumbration — is the analysis of how the intentional content is constituted on the basis of the real content.

Second, the **problem of transcendence** that Husserl raises here — the asymmetry between the absolute givenness of the perception and the perspectival, inadequate givenness of the perceived object — is the problem that the entire course of the lectures works to resolve. The analyses of §§32–39 (the essential inadequacy of thing-perception), §§44–57 (the kinaesthetic constitution), and §§58ff (the transition to three-dimensional space) are all, in a sense, responses to this problem. The object is transcendent — it is not really immanent in the perception — and yet it is given, given in the flesh. How? Through the constitution of the intentional content on the basis of the real content, mediated by the structures of apprehension, adumbration, and kinaesthetic motivation.

Third, §6 already indicates that the relation between real and intentional content is not one of simple correspondence or mirroring. The perception has its own parts and moments; the object has different parts and moments. The task is not to find a point-by-point isomorphism but to understand how the one constitutes the other — how the real content of sensation and apprehension gives rise to the consciousness of an object that transcends it. This is, in essence, the constitutive problem that phenomenology exists to address, and §6 formulates it with exemplary clarity at the very beginning of the investigation.

### **§7. Preliminary indication of the method of the further investigation**

Section 7 is the final section of Chapter 1 and of the introductory portion of the lectures. It lays out the **methodological path** the investigation will follow, identifies the **problem of transcendence** as the central difficulty that motivates deeper analysis, and then makes a pragmatic decision about how to proceed within the constraints of a lecture course. The section is short but architectonically important: it maps the entire trajectory of the lectures in advance and explains why the investigation will take the form it does.

**The strict method: layer by layer.** Husserl begins by describing what the method of progression from layer to layer would look like if carried out strictly. There would be two stages:

*First stage.* We carry out the phenomenological reduction and express, in series, the evidences we encounter with respect to perceptions. We analyze everything pertaining to the "essence" of perception, everything immanent in it. We find the relation to the object — the circumstance that perception is perception of this or that object. We find evidences concerning the object intended in perception as to its content, character, parts, and properties. We then place in relation the **real** (*reell*) content of the perception with its **intentional** content — the content belonging to its object. Through this contrast, real moments of the perception come clearly and evidently to the fore: sensations versus properties of the object (lived color versus the object's color, lived tone-content versus the object's tone, sensation of roughness versus the thing's roughness). There comes to be set in relief what is real in the perception: sensation, apprehension-character, belief-character, etc.

This first stage is essentially the program of §§14–18: the analysis of presentational contents and apprehension, proper and improper appearance, the distinction between sensational data and objective determinations.

*Second stage.* All this becomes problematic insofar as we make these evident assertions and yet do not understand *how they are possible*. The fundamental difficulty of the **constitution of the object in the phenomenon** comes to the fore: how are evident assertions possible about an objectivity that is not actually given in the phenomenon? How are comparisons possible between the intentional object and the immanent moments of the phenomenon?

Husserl then unfolds this difficulty in a series of questions that effectively preview the entire course of the lectures: How will we understand perceptual beliefs, which relate to the actual Being of the perceived object, and which are "confirmed" and "conflict," which get determined more precisely or determined in completely novel ways through new perceptions that bring the object to "ever more complete" givenness and show in ever new directions "what the object is in actuality"? How is all this to be understood, since in the entire process of cognition merely ever new sequences of lived experiences flow on, and despite all the pertinent evidence, no place in lived experience can be indicated where the object is *real* (*reell*)?

This is a compressed statement of the problematic that §§26–39 (the kinetic synthesis, harmony and conflict, more precise determination and re-determination, the question of adequacy) and §§44–57 (the kinaesthetic constitution) will work to resolve.

**The higher stratum: studying transcendence within immanence.** Husserl formulates the goal with maximum precision: we must study the **possibility of transcendent intention and validity** in the sphere of the most pure immanence — the sphere in which every constatation brings to intuition a kind of givenness containing absolutely nothing unclear. Objectivity is constituted in lived experience; we must bring to pure givenness the evidence-consciousness itself, follow its alterations, and subject it to an analysis establishing purely intuitively what actually lies there, what lies in the essence of such nexuses of lived experiences.

This is the higher stratum of investigation, which can itself separate into further strata. Instead of merely *living in* the evidences (taking them at face value), we must *consider* them — comport ourselves to them by analyzing purely immanently what is given absolutely and indubitably.

**The pragmatic decision.** Husserl then makes a practical decision about how to proceed. The strict method — first gathering all the first-level evidences, then taking up the problems of transcendence at a second level — would entail a great deal of labor, since everything presented at the first level would need to be presented again at the second. Given the limited time of the lecture course, Husserl decides to proceed differently: he will enter step by step directly into the difficulties and their solution, to the extent that the solution can be carried through at each point. The first-level evidences and the higher-level problems of transcendence will be addressed together as they arise, rather than being strictly separated into sequential stages.

This pragmatic decision explains the actual form of the lectures, which interleave descriptive analysis with constitutive-problematic reflection. The analyses of §§14–18 do not merely describe the structures of perception; they already raise the constitutive questions (how does apprehension animate sensation? what is the mode of givenness of improperly appearing determinations?). The kinetic analyses of §§26–39 do not merely describe changing appearances; they confront the problem of how identity is constituted through multiplicity. The kinaesthetic analyses of §§44–57 do not merely describe the K-i correlation; they address how the correlation constitutes the transcendent spatial thing. At every stage, description and constitutive questioning proceed together.

**Significance.** §7 is the section where the methodological self-consciousness of the lectures is most explicit. Its importance is threefold:

First, it identifies the **problem of transcendence** — how an object that is not really immanent in perception can nonetheless be given with evidence in perception — as the master-problem of the entire investigation. This is the problem that §6 raised in the form of the asymmetry between real and intentional content, and that every subsequent section of the lectures will work to resolve at progressively deeper levels. The analyses of adumbration, of the kinetic synthesis, of the essential inadequacy of thing-perception, and of the kinaesthetic constitution are all, at bottom, contributions to understanding how transcendence is constituted in immanence.

Second, the two-stage method that Husserl describes — first-level description followed by higher-level constitutive analysis — corresponds to the distinction between what *Ideas I* will call **noematic description** (describing the object as it is intended) and **noetic-constitutive analysis** (clarifying how the noetic acts constitute the noematic sense). The *Thing and Space* lectures show this methodological duality already operative in 1907, even though the terminology of noesis and noema is not yet in place.

Third, the pragmatic decision to interleave description and constitutive questioning — rather than strictly separating them — gives the lectures their distinctive character as a progressive, spiraling investigation that returns to the same phenomena at ever deeper levels of analysis. This is the "layer by layer" progression that §3 described as the general nature of phenomenology, now given its concrete methodological form. The result is a text that, while often difficult to follow in its forward movement, has a remarkable internal coherence: every section presupposes and builds upon the earlier ones, and every constitutive difficulty encountered at one level motivates the deepening of the analysis at the next.

## 

## Chapter 2: The Methodological Possibility of the Analysis of Perception

### **§8. The absolute givenness of perception in phenomenological reflection. Broadening of the concept of perception**

Section 8 opens Chapter 2 ("The Methodological Possibility of the Analysis of Perception") and Section II of the lectures. It addresses a problem left open by §§6–7: if the transcendent object is not really (*reell*) immanent in perception, how can phenomenological analysis proceed with absolute certainty? The answer leads Husserl to introduce a fundamental distinction between two kinds of perception — one that merely *presents* and one that *seizes* — and to broaden the concept of perception beyond the perception of things to encompass the reflective grasp of lived experiences and essences.

**The starting point: the distinction is itself absolutely given.** Husserl begins by noting that a component of the solution to the problem of transcendence already resides in a simple observation: the distinction between real (*reell*) givenness and merely appearing (but not real) givenness is itself absolutely indubitable and absolutely given. This is not a theoretical inference but something that shows itself in the phenomenological consideration of lived experiences.

**The absolute givenness of the reduced lived experience.** Consider an actual lived experience — a feeling just lived through, a perception just carried out, a phantasy-representation — taken just as it is "in itself," with every judgment leading beyond into transcendence excluded. In this attitude, the lived experience is not a psychological datum but an absolute phenomenological datum upon which we gaze and which is given in this gazing. How is it given? It stands there **in the flesh** — not phantasized, not thought in a simile, not conceived symbolically or conceptually, but given before our eyes as itself, actual, and now.

**The broadened concept of perception.** Husserl observes that this "gaze" at a lived experience — carried out under the phenomenological reduction — has, most generally, the same basic character as the perception of a thing. This basic character can determine a **further concept of perception**, one not bound to the givenness of things. The perception of a thing thereby becomes the object of another "perception" — the reflective gaze, the "reflection" (as has been said since Locke). Any lived experience, insofar as the regard is directed to it under the phenomenological reduction, is indubitably given — given absolutely and really.

**The crucial difference: absolute givenness precludes doubt.** But what does "absolutely" mean here? In the case of outer perception (as §5 established), belief was separable from presence in the flesh: there could be a presentation in the flesh connected to disbelief or doubt. In the case of the reduced lived experience, **this is not so**. It is evident that the essence of the reduced lived experience of perception (in the reflective mode) is incompatible with disbelief and doubt.

The reason is structural: this lived experience is not only a consciousness whose essential character is to be consciousness of the actual presence of the object; it is also, as an **absolutely giving consciousness**, characterized as one that *possesses* the object as actual and in the flesh in such a way that disbelief and doubt are precluded. In a certain sense, it even precludes belief — for belief in the ordinary sense is a mere *aiming at* Being. In aiming, the goal is not yet given; it must still be attained. In absolutely giving perception, by contrast, the perceptual grasp is a *seizing* of what is self-given.

**Two kinds of perception.** This leads to a fundamental distinction between two modes of perception:

First, the perception which is the **mere presentation** of an object in the flesh — outer perception, where the thing stands there bodily but is not possessed with absolute certainty, where belief can be separated from presence, where doubt and disbelief remain possible. The object "appears" as actually present, but this appearing is a mode of *presentation*, not of absolute self-givenness.

Second, the perception whose essence it is not only to present but also to **seize** the object itself in the flesh — reflective phenomenological perception, where the lived experience is possessed absolutely, where no gap remains between the consciousness and its object, where doubt is in principle precluded.

Both share the character of consciousness of presence in the flesh — this is what makes both deserve the name "perception." But in normal outer perception, the presence in the flesh has the character of *mere appearance*, a presentation that does not attain absolute self-givenness. In reflective perception of the reduced lived experience, the presence in the flesh has the character of *self-possession*, absolute givenness that precludes every possible doubt.

**Extension to essence-intuition.** Husserl extends the same distinction to the grasping of essences and universal intuitions. Abstraction and generalization, intuitively grounded on individual intuitions of houses, clarify the essence of a house and present the essence as given — as there, "so to say, in the flesh." But it does merely *present* itself. On the other hand, an essence can be given absolutely and indubitably: the essence of a type of lived experience, grasped in the phenomenological reduction and in intuitive abstraction, does not merely present itself but is an **absolute givenness** in rigorous "immanent" abstraction.

**Significance.** §8 is methodologically foundational for two reasons:

First, it secures the **epistemic ground** on which the entire investigation stands. The problem of §§6–7 was: how can we make evident assertions about an object that is not really immanent in perception? The answer of §8 is: we can, because the lived experiences in which the transcendent object is constituted are themselves absolutely given in phenomenological reflection. The analysis of perception does not depend on somehow reaching through to the transcendent thing; it works entirely within the sphere of absolute givenness, examining the lived experiences of perception as they are given in reflective phenomenological intuition. The transcendent thing is studied not in itself but as the intentional correlate of these absolutely given lived experiences — as the "perceived as such," the object in the sense it has in the perception.

Second, the distinction between **presenting** and **seizing** — between outer perception and reflective-immanent perception — establishes a two-level structure that governs the entire methodology. The *object* of the investigation (thing-perception) belongs to the level of mere presentation, where the object transcends the perception and is given inadequately. The *method* of the investigation (phenomenological reflection on the lived experiences of perception) belongs to the level of absolute seizing, where the lived experiences are possessed with certainty. The investigation studies transcendent, inadequately given objectivity by reflecting on the absolutely given lived experiences in which that objectivity constitutes itself. This two-level structure — transcendent object studied through immanent reflection — is the fundamental methodological architecture of phenomenology, and §8 is where Husserl establishes it for the purposes of these lectures.

The distinction also prepares the ground for the separation, developed in §§9–13, between **self-posing** perception (immanent perception that seizes its object absolutely) and **presentational** perception (outer perception that merely presents its transcendent object). This separation will be the framework within which the entire analysis of the real/intentional distinction, of presentational contents and apprehension, and of proper and improper appearance proceeds.

### **§9. Self-posing perceptions and presentational perceptions. Inseparability of perception and belief within self-posing perception**

Section 9 takes the distinction sketched in §8 between two kinds of perception and elevates it into a terminologically precise, architectonically fundamental distinction between **self-posing** (*selbststellend*) perceptions and **presentational** (*darstellend*) perceptions. This distinction defines the senses of immanence and transcendence that will operate throughout the lectures, and it clarifies why thing-perception is constitutively different from the phenomenological reflection that analyzes it.

**The terminological distinction.** Self-posing perceptions are those in which the object is *possessed* — given absolutely, seized in the flesh, with no gap between consciousness and object. The paradigm case (from §8) is the phenomenological reflection on a reduced lived experience: the lived experience is given as it is, fully and without remainder. Presentational perceptions are those in which the object *presents itself* — stands there in the flesh — but is not possessed absolutely. The paradigm case is outer perception of a thing: the house stands there bodily, but it transcends the perception, is given perspectivally, and admits of doubt.

**The definition of immanence and transcendence.** Through this distinction, Husserl defines the fundamental phenomenological concepts of immanence and transcendence. The **self-posed** is called *immanent*; the **presented** (whether in the mode of presence in the flesh or as self-presented) is called *transcendent*. Parts or moments of the self-posed are said to be **really** (*reell*) **immanent** to it, insofar as they can come to an evident self-posing — that is, insofar as the essence of the total self-posing founds the possibility of a new self-posing whose objects are in an evident way partially identical with the object of the total self-posing.

Husserl then adds a crucial complication: what is self-posed can itself be a perception, a representation, etc. — an immanent object which for its part self-poses or presents an object. If the self-posed perception is itself a *presentational* perception, then the object it presents is not really immanent to the original self-posing but is **transcendent** — a "merely intentional Object." But if the self-posed perception is itself a *self-posing*, then its Object is immanent to the first perception. This establishes a recursive structure: self-posing can be applied to self-posing, and the immanence/transcendence status of the object depends on the character of the perception that gives it.

Husserl notes that the same distinctions will be carried over to phantasy: phantasy self-posings versus phantasy presentations, phantasy-immanence versus phantasy-transcendence. But the time for that has not yet come.

**The inseparability of having and positing in self-posing.** Husserl returns to the asymmetry between the two kinds of perception with respect to the belief-character. In the sphere of absolutely giving (self-posing) perceptions, we cannot speak of a perception separable in itself that is now bound with belief, now with disbelief, now with doubt. The self-possession of evident perception consists in a **having and positing** that analysis cannot separate. The having is a real (*reell*) having — the object is genuinely possessed in the perception. The positing is akin to belief but is not belief in the ordinary sense (which is a mere *aiming at* Being). It is what excludes disbelief, what makes up the consciousness of Being, and what constitutes **absolute Being in givenness**. This character is common to all self-posing perceptions.

What differentiates self-posing perceptions from one another resides in what is given in them — their content. What they share as "giving" is the consciousness of the presence of something in the flesh, and this they share with all perceptions whatsoever (including presentational ones). The difference lies in the *mode* of this presence: in self-posing, presence is absolute possession; in presentational perception, presence is mere appearance.

**The warning against factorizing.** Husserl adds an important warning against a tempting error: one must not simply multiply factors — presence in the flesh, self-posing, content — as if any arbitrary content could be combined with the character of self-posing. The fact that thing-perception involves presence in the flesh does not mean thinghood can be self-posed. On the contrary, there is a **necessary transcendence of thinghood**: although the thing presents itself in the flesh in every individual perception, self-posing is excluded for it. This is the very thesis that the lectures will demonstrate through the analyses of §§32–39 (the essential inadequacy of thing-perception): the thing can never be given in the mode of absolute self-possession because its givenness is constitutively perspectival, one-sided, and interminable.

On the other hand, the perceptual *nexus* — the continuous manifold of perceptions through which the thing is progressively given — exhibits "degrees of increase toward the 'perfection' of the presentation, in which something akin to self-posing is carried out." This remark anticipates the analysis of increasing saturation and optimal givenness in §§32–36: within the course of perceptual exploration, the thing comes to ever more complete givenness, and at the maximum points of saturation, something resembling (but never reaching) absolute self-givenness is achieved.

**The eidetic character of the analysis.** Husserl closes by reaffirming the eidetic orientation: objects or contents are not first something in themselves and then multiplied by arbitrary cognitive characters. Rather, the analysis considers only givennesses, sets in relief their various sides, grasps their separability or inseparability, and does all this in the domain of essences. The distinctions drawn are not theoretical constructions imposed on the phenomena but essential structures exhibited in the phenomena themselves.

**Significance.** §9 is the section where the conceptual infrastructure of the lectures is most precisely established. Its contributions are threefold:

First, the self-posing / presentational distinction provides the rigorous phenomenological grounding for the concepts of **immanence and transcendence** that will be operative throughout. The thing is transcendent not because it is "outside" consciousness (a metaphysical claim the lectures eschew) but because its mode of givenness is presentational rather than self-posing — it appears in the flesh but is not possessed with absolute certainty. The lived experiences of perception, by contrast, are immanent because they are self-posed — given absolutely in phenomenological reflection. This distinction is what makes the entire constitutive analysis possible: we study transcendent thinghood by reflecting on the immanent lived experiences in which it constitutes itself.

Second, the thesis of the **necessary transcendence of thinghood** is stated here for the first time, though its full demonstration will come only in §§32–39. The thing cannot be self-posed; its givenness is constitutively presentational. This is not a contingent limitation of human perception but an essential, eidetic feature of what it is to be a thing. The analyses of perspectival one-sidedness, the kinetic synthesis, the braid of increase and decrease, the finitude of the field against the infinity of space — all are developments of this thesis.

Third, the remark about "degrees of increase toward perfection" in the perceptual nexus plants the seed for the entire investigation of adequacy and optimality in §§32–39. Even though self-posing is excluded for things, the perceptual nexus exhibits a progressive movement *toward* something like self-posing — a movement that, as the lectures will show, is constitutively interminable and can never reach its ideal limit. This progressive, asymptotic character of thing-perception — always approaching but never achieving absolute self-givenness — is the deepest structural insight of the lectures, and §9 is where it first comes into view.

### **§10. Identity-consciousness and difference-consciousness in presentational perception**

Section 10 introduces and analyzes the fundamental phenomenological structures through which the **sameness** and **difference** of objects across multiple perceptions are constituted. The section establishes that identity and difference are not external judgments imposed on perceptions from without but are grounded in the **essences** of the perceptions themselves, and it works through the epistemological difficulties that arise from the fact that identity-consciousness, like the perceptions it connects, is presentational rather than self-posing. This section lays the conceptual groundwork for the entire kinetic analysis of §§26–39 and the kinaesthetic analysis of §§44–57, where the identity of the thing through a manifold of changing appearances is the central problem.

**The fundamental asymmetry between self-posing and presentational perception.** Husserl begins by contrasting self-posing and presentational perceptions with respect to identity. In self-posing perceptions, the identity of the object and the identity of the perception are one and the same: different perceptions have different objects. There is no gap between the perception and what it gives, so the distinctness of perceptions guarantees the distinctness of their objects.

In presentational perceptions, this is not so. Two perceptions can be essentially non-identical — very different in their real (*reell*) content — and yet relate to the **same object**. A house seen from the front and a house seen from the back are, according to their real content, very different perceptions, and yet they are perceptions of the same house. This is the basic phenomenological fact — objective identity through appearance-multiplicity — that will govern the entire subsequent analysis.

**Identity-consciousness as a founded phenomenon.** How does the sameness of the object across different perceptions come about? Husserl's answer: we find in the essences of the perceptions something that joins them or that allows and requires a certain conjunction. This conjunction is a conjunction of **identity**, expressed in the statement that the different perceptions intend or present the same thing. An **identity-consciousness** — a peculiar phenomenon, itself given in a self-posing — binds perception together with perception. This consciousness is not a perception in the manner of the examples at hand (it is not itself a thing-perception), but it is in a certain sense a giving consciousness: it is related to an object — the identity of what is perceived here and there — and it possesses something of the character of intuition or perception in a broader sense.

**The precision: what is identified.** Husserl carefully distinguishes what the identity-consciousness does from what it does not do. It unites the two perceptions and thereby brings about the consciousness of their *object* as one and the same — but it does *not* identify the two *perceptions* and present them as the same perception. If A and B are identified, this means phenomenologically that an identity-consciousness connects the respective representations of A and B. An identification of the two perceptions themselves would require representations *of* the perceptions plus a unity-consciousness connecting those representations — a different and higher-level act.

**The epistemological difficulty.** Husserl immediately confronts the objection: if the object is not properly given in either perception — if we possess only its presentations — how can the identity be given in the proper sense? And can there not be deception? I perceive a house from the front, walk around, perceive the back — I say it is the same house, but perhaps it was a different house that I did not notice while walking. This was "semblant evidence" that could be false.

Husserl accepts this: neither the individual perceptions nor the identity-consciousness give the object or its identity in absolute indubitability. Every external perception is compatible, by its essence, with doubt about its object. And the identity-consciousness, although it constitutes the consciousness of identity, remains in a certain sense a merely representational identity-consciousness — a mere intending of identity. It is compatible with disbelief and doubt as to the actuality of the identity.

**The resolution: identity grounded in essence.** What then *is* evident on the strength of the identity-consciousness actually carried out? Husserl's answer is precise: the perceptions stand in the synthesis of identification; the unity of the identity-consciousness encompasses them. And this is not an accidental event. The identity-consciousness is not "strapping tape" with which any two arbitrary phenomena can be joined. Whether the connection is possible depends on the **essence** of the phenomena involved. A perception of an elephant and a perception of a stone cannot be fit, by their essence, into an identification; their essence excludes this. Perceptions that we call perceptions of the same object manifest themselves as such in the unity of the identity-consciousness that they **found** — that they found by their essence.

To intend the same object with evidence in several perceptions means nothing other than that the perceptions, by essence, fit themselves into the unity of an identity-consciousness — that in their essence the possibility of such unification is grounded a priori. Two perceptual essences ground, in pure intuition, an essence that encompasses them: the identity-consciousness, which is one with them in pure evidence.

Husserl adds the crucial qualification: this happens **only in the synthesis of continuity**. Two discrete, unconnected perceptions cannot demonstrate objective identity with evidence; identity-consciousness requires the continuous transition that §44 will later identify as the essential condition for the self-demonstration of objective identity.

**Difference-consciousness.** Husserl then provides the parallel analysis for difference. Two perceptions of different objects are connected through a **difference-consciousness** — the consciousness "not the same." This arises characteristically when an identity-consciousness is disrupted: if we had an identity-consciousness connecting P₁ and P₂, and then substituted for P₂ a P₁' belonging to another object, the non-identity would "leap out." An intention toward identity grasps P₁ and P₁' as one, but this intention "conflicts" with P₁ and P₁' as given in the intuition that takes them together.

Identity-consciousness and difference-consciousness are mutually exclusive for identically taken members: they found a relation of conflict. It pertains to the essence of P₁ and P₂ (same object) that they ground the possibility of identity-consciousness and exclude difference-consciousness. Conversely, P₁ and P₁' (different objects) ground difference-consciousness and exclude identity-consciousness.

**The expressions as phenomenological descriptions.** Husserl summarizes: "fitting together into an identity-consciousness" and "fitting together into a non-identity-consciousness" are significant expressions for the states of affairs we designate as "two perceptions represent the same object" and "they represent different objects." All these cognitions are carried out in the sphere of pure self-posing and eidetic intuition.

**Significance.** §10 is foundational for the entire subsequent course of the lectures in several ways:

First, the concept of **identity-consciousness** grounded in the essences of perceptions is the conceptual instrument that makes possible the analysis of §§27–30: the identity of the unchanged thing through the manifold of changing appearances. The "synthesis of total identification" (§27), the "harmony of sense" (§29), and the "continuous synthesis of manifold perceptions" (§30) are all specific forms of the identity-consciousness introduced here.

Second, the concept of **difference-consciousness** and its relation to conflict is what will ground the analysis of re-determination and disappointment (§29): when the back side of the thing is "otherwise" than anticipated, the new appearance conflicts with the earlier apprehension, and a difference-consciousness with respect to the improperly intended features arises within the overall identity-consciousness with respect to the thing.

Third, the thesis that identity-consciousness requires the **synthesis of continuity** — that discrete perceptions cannot by themselves demonstrate identity — is the thesis that §44 will develop into the principle that the identity of the spatial thing is given only in the continuous kinetic series of perceptions, and that this series must be kinaesthetically motivated.

Fourth, the acknowledgment that identity-consciousness in presentational perception is always fallible — always compatible with doubt and possible deception — connects directly to the thesis of the essential inadequacy of thing-perception (§§32–39) and to the recognition that the thing's identity is constituted progressively through an interminable process of confirmation, re-determination, and fulfillment that never achieves absolute certainty.

### **§11. Resolution of a difficulty: even intentional components of perception given in the mode of self-posing**

Section 11 takes up the problem that §§6–7 left open and that §§8–10 have been preparing to resolve: how can we make evident assertions about a transcendent object that is not really (*reell*) immanent in perception? The section provides Husserl's answer, which turns on reconceiving the "relation to an object" in terms of the identity-consciousness and difference-consciousness analyzed in §10. It thereby resolves the apparent mystery of transcendence and opens the path to the concrete analyses of the real/intentional distinction that will occupy §§12–18.

**The problem restated.** Every presentational perception of the outer type presents a "so-called object" that does not reside in it as a real part and is not given in the mode of a self-posed object. And yet evident assertions should be possible about moments or parts of the perception in comparison — and in other relations — with the parts and properties of the object. The evidences exist. How are they possible? How do we know anything immediately about the presented, transcendent object?
