**The resolution: intentional relation as fittingness for identity-consciousness.** The key move is to reconceive the intentional relation — the "relation to an object" — in purely phenomenological terms. From a phenomenological point of view, this relation is nothing other than the **fittingness** (*Geeignetheit*), based in the essence of the Objectivating lived experience, to found an identity-consciousness. More precisely: it is the peculiar essence of perception that makes it fit to found identity-consciousness (and thereby exclude difference-consciousness), or conversely to found difference-consciousness (and thereby exclude identity-consciousness).

In the essence of the respective perception are grounded ideal possibilities for identity-connections with other perceptions of such and such a character, actual or possible. If we had before our eyes all essences of singular perception, and compared them with a given perception A, the essences would break down into two classes: those that found with A an identity-consciousness (perceptions of the "same" object) and those that found a consciousness of non-identity (perceptions of a "different" object). In the case of a perception of a house, the first class would include the ideal total content of "possible perceptions" of the same house — and this extends to phantasy representations, memory representations, image representations, and all other kinds of representation of this house.

Husserl emphasizes: there is still no question of actuality or non-actuality, existence or non-existence. At issue is only the mere "intentional relation" to an object, attributed to the representation whether or not an existential judgment pronounces the object existing.

**Intending as an immanent determination of consciousness.** What has been clarified is the "intending or representing precisely this or that object" as something pertaining to the respective representation *itself* — as an **immanent determination** of consciousness. To say what sort of consciousness is in play everywhere comes down to saying what sort of representation and what sort of object are in question. The same holds for intending identity, non-identity, being-qualified-in-such-and-such-a-way, etc. The evidence that a given intention intends identity, quality, etc., is possible even though these are not and cannot be given as really (*reell*) immanent — and this evidence is elucidated according to the same type already outlined (through the founding of identity-consciousness in essence).

This is a crucial result: the intentional content — what the perception is "about" — is not something external to the perception that we must somehow reach beyond to grasp. It is an **immanent determination** of the consciousness itself, accessible through the self-posing analysis of the perception's essence and its fittingness for identity- and difference-connections with other perceptions.

**The real/intentional distinction reconceived.** Husserl then applies this framework to the evidence that the object of outer perception is not really contained in the perception. The self-posing of the perception of a house is, by its very essence, **not compatible** with the perception of the house itself in the unity of an identity-consciousness. It is also not compatible in such a unity with a perception that brings to perception any part of the house. This is the phenomenological expression of the real/intentional distinction: the perception and its object, or any part of its object, fall in different classes with respect to identity-consciousness. They found not identity but difference — or more precisely, the self-posing of the perception and the perception of the house are compatible in a **difference-consciousness**, not in an identity-consciousness.

This means the transcendence of the object is not a metaphysical thesis about two different realms of being; it is an essential structural fact about the relations of identity and difference that hold between the perception (as self-posed) and its object (as presented). The object transcends the perception in the precise sense that the essence of the one excludes identity-consciousness with the essence of the other.

**The transitivity of identification.** Husserl briefly notes that the axiom of transitivity of identity (if A \= B and B \= C, then A \= C) receives its phenomenological elucidation here: the synoptic connection of identity-consciousness AB with identity-consciousness BC encloses in its essence the possibility of identity-consciousness AC and the incompatibility of this connection with difference-consciousness AC.

**From total to partial identification.** In the closing passage, Husserl signals the transition to §12 by noting that total identification is not the only kind. The incompatibility of the self-posing of the perception with the perception of a *part* of the house already involves **partial identification or partial differentiation**. These partial identifications and differentiations are at issue when we consider the evidences arising from the "comparison" of the real content of the perception with the content of the object — that is, the parts and moments of the perception versus the parts and moments of the object.

**Significance.** §11 is the section that resolves the methodological impasse posed by §§6–7 and enables the entire subsequent analysis to proceed. Its central achievement is the reconception of intentional transcendence in terms of the identity-consciousness/difference-consciousness framework. Three consequences are paramount:

First, the "relation to an object" is demystified. It is not a mysterious reaching-beyond of consciousness to something entirely external; it is an immanent essential character of the perception — its fittingness for determinate patterns of identification and differentiation with other perceptions. This character is accessible to self-posing eidetic analysis and harbors no mystery.

Second, the real/intentional distinction is grounded phenomenologically. The object is "not really immanent" in the precise sense that the self-posing of the perception and the perception of the object (or any part of it) found a difference-consciousness, not an identity-consciousness. This is not a negative result (we cannot reach the object\!) but a positive characterization of the specific mode in which the object is given — through presentation rather than self-posing, through appearance rather than absolute possession.

Third, the introduction of **partial** identification and differentiation opens the path to the detailed analyses of §§12–18. The comparison of the real content of perception with its intentional content — sensations versus objective properties, presentational contents versus objective determinations — proceeds through partial identifications and differentiations that set in relief the various moments of the perception and their respective functions. The apparatus of total and partial identification, developed in §§10–11, provides the methodological tool for the concrete phenomenological analysis that will now begin.

### **§12. The relation of part and whole in presentational perception. Partial and total identification**

Section 12 extends the analysis of identity-consciousness and difference-consciousness from §§10–11 to the more differentiated structures of **part-whole relations**, thereby establishing the phenomenological framework for understanding the internal articulation of both perceptions and their objects. The section develops a phenomenological account of what it means for something to be a *part* of something, a *property* of something, or a *whole* made up of parts — and it shows how these categorial relations are grounded in specific modes of the identifying and differentiating consciousness.

**Parts given through partial identification.** Husserl begins with the observation that given parts are given in **partial identification**. In immanent analysis, a self-posing yields the whole as absolute Being; another self-posing sets a part in relief. But the part first *becomes* a part of the whole in the partial identification that brings the one and the other to **partial coincidence**. Total identification consists in the uniform consciousness "the same." Partial identification is different: the conjoined representations are *not* fully compatible in the way required for total identification.

**Total and partial identification as basic forms.** Total identification is a basic form of consciousness in which the sense of "one and the same object" is demonstrated originally. Partial identification is likewise a basic form — a consciousness of coincidence, but one in which a **"surplus" of non-coincidence** stands out. In this basic structure of Objectivating consciousness, the sense of the talk about part and whole, containing and being-contained, possessing and being-possessed arises. These are not external logical categories imposed on experience but phenomenological structures grounded in the essential character of the identifying consciousness.

**The differentiation of partial identification: pieces and properties.** Partial identification is not uniform; it encompasses various cases corresponding to basic differences in the type of part-relations. Husserl distinguishes:

**Pieces** (*Stücke*) — parts in the stricter sense, which supplement themselves with coordinate parts to form the whole. The whole is "put together" out of the pieces; they are members of a whole in the pregnant sense.

**Properties** (*Eigenschaften*) — inner features that the whole has as subject, as bearer, in the mode of determination, in the mode of predicate. Properties do not "put the object together" the way pieces compose a whole.

Beyond properties in this narrow sense, there are also **qualities** in a broader sense — outer features that fall to the subject not purely but in relation to something else, thus only in a unity-consciousness encompassing still another Object. These are relational determinations.

Husserl notes that obviously different modes of identification must be distinguished here — modes that are closely akin (hence all grouped under "partial identification") yet count as proper, distinct modes. The thorough kinship of essence resides in the "is" — in the unity-consciousness that operates everywhere: unity as absolute identity, unity as whole-and-piece, unity as subject-and-property, unity as subject-and-relational-determination.

**Further structures: compatibility, incompatibility, apophantic forms.** The idea of the consciousness of unity includes further structural possibilities: the combination of parts into a whole, the compatibility and incompatibility of elements that are supposed to go together, the compatibility or incompatibility of properties and relations attributed to a subject. Furthermore, it includes the **apophantic forms** — the logical-grammatical forms standing in essential relation to "is" and "is not": "and," "or," the plural, the singular, etc. Husserl does not pursue these here but signals their relevance.

**The methodological upshot.** The main point, Husserl says, is that for the study of things as objects, we are led back to the study of the **unity of giving acts** — to identification, distinction, and their various differentiations and appurtenant formations, which express themselves in the a priori forms of possible assertions, in their purely grammatical categories. If the question of validity enters, these become purely logical laws. The elucidation of the logical and the making intelligible of the possibility of objective validity are one and the same. But since the lectures concern only the constitution of things within perception, a few further provisional steps in the analysis will suffice.

**The levels of identification.** In a passage that connects back to the analyses of §§8–9, Husserl distinguishes various modes and levels of the identity-consciousness itself. It can be an empty or full intention — one can intend identity in merely symbolic thinking without "properly" having it before one's eyes. Proper identification can be carried out on the basis of empty intentions of the members of the conjunction, or on the basis of full intuitions. The synthetic act itself then acquires the character of intuition. To this act, as to other intuitions, belongs the distinction between adequate and inadequate intuition: perceiving consciousness that posits identity as existing versus absolutely self-giving consciousness in which the self-givenness of identity comes to light.

Husserl formulates the proposition: where two absolutely self-giving acts are joined through a proper identity-consciousness, the latter is necessarily a self-giving consciousness. In the phenomenological analysis, when we synthesize an immanent whole and its real parts in a self-posing, the intuition of the part-relation is also absolutely giving. The evidence that the coincidence exists, that the one is in the other, is evidence just as absolute as that of the conjoined members.

**Significance.** §12 provides the categorial apparatus — part, whole, piece, property, relation — that the subsequent analyses will employ throughout. Its contributions are threefold:

First, the phenomenological grounding of part-whole relations in partial identification provides the framework for the analysis of §§14–18. When Husserl distinguishes presentational contents from objective determinations, or the proper appearance from the improper, or the front side from the back side, he is employing specific modes of partial identification and differentiation. The front side is a *part* of the thing in the sense of a non-autonomous moment (not a piece); the color-datum is a *part* of the perception in the sense of a real component; the objective color is a *determination* of the thing in the sense of a property. Each of these part-relations is a specific mode of partial identification, phenomenologically grounded.

Second, the distinction between pieces and properties is directly relevant to the analysis of §16 and §23, where Husserl will distinguish *pieces* of a thing (which could in principle exist autonomously) from *sides* (which are non-autonomous moments inseparable from the thing). The piece/moment distinction of the Third *Logical Investigation* is here given its specifically perceptual-constitutive application.

Third, the analysis of the levels of identification — from empty to full, from inadequate to adequate — directly anticipates the kinetic analysis of §§27–30, where the progressive fulfillment of intentions through the course of perception is described as a movement from emptier to fuller identification, and the question of whether absolute identification (adequate perception) is achievable is raised and ultimately answered negatively.

The section thus serves as the bridge between the methodological-foundational work of §§8–11 and the concrete phenomenological analysis of perception that begins with §13 and unfolds through §§14–18. Every distinction drawn in the later sections — between sensation and quality, between presentation and apprehension, between proper and improper appearance — is a specific application of the general framework of identification, differentiation, and part-whole relations established here.

### **§13. Rejection of a misunderstanding: articulation of the giving consciousness not an articulation of the object**

Section 13 is the final section of Chapter 2 and the last of the methodological-foundational sections before the concrete phenomenological analysis begins in §14. It takes up a specific skeptical objection to the method of phenomenological analysis — that analysis *falsifies* its object by articulating what was originally unarticulated — refutes it decisively, and in doing so provides a statement of the most fundamental principles of phenomenology that is among the most explicit in the entire lectures.

**The objection.** The skeptical argument runs as follows: when we find, in partial identification, a part in the whole, and analyze the whole into its parts, we must not confuse the articulation of the *giving consciousness* with an articulation of the *object*. But does not the very process of analysis introduce something new? Prior to the analysis, the whole is simply there. In the act of setting a part into relief — attending to it separately, making it an object for itself — the phenomenon changes essentially. Something "quite different" is present when the partial identification takes place than was present before. How then can we justifiably say that what the analysis provides as something for itself was already "contained" in the whole prior to the analysis? The analysis modifies; it introduces a subjective, falsifying moment. We do not have a whole phenomenon that *contains* the part-phenomenon; we have first one phenomenon (the unarticulated whole), and then something quite different (the articulated complex), and we have no right to identify the part set into relief in the second with something contained in the first.

**The refutation.** Husserl's response is sharp: this is "fundamentally false idle talk" amounting to an absurd, self-annulling skepticism that presupposes what it denies. If the skeptic's claims could be true, their truth would have to demonstrate itself in self-posing consideration and analysis of the phenomena — but the skeptic's own argument employs precisely the distinctions (total perception, part, setting into relief, identification) whose legitimacy it denies. The skeptic distinguishes parts in the total consciousness of givenness while asserting that all such distinguishing involves falsification.

Considered directly: in the self-posing identification, the perception of the whole and the perception of the part are brought into synthesis. This synthesis is **absolute evidence** — the absolutely giving consciousness that the whole has the part. If we ask what distinguishes the part prior to the analysis from the part after, the answer is: **nothing**. Much occurs, to be sure, but this is from the side of the *constituting consciousness*, to whose essence it pertains to elapse in such and such a way, to articulate itself in this or that manner. As a whole of such a character, it is evidence that the whole has the part. The assertion says purely and simply: the whole has the part. It does not say the whole has the part only after the analysis or has it differently before than after. All that is nonsense.

**The fundamental principle: consciousness and object are not to be conflated.** The flux and articulation of the absolutely giving consciousness is **not** the flux and articulation of the given object. The skeptical argument jumbles consciousness and object. The "phenomenon" alters — the consciousness of the givenness of the whole changes as the analysis proceeds. But the *object* given in that consciousness — the part-whole relation — does not thereby change. It is precisely the nature of the existence in the flesh of objectivity that it comes to givenness through a consciousness that has its own elapsing, articulation, and modification, without these subjective processes being transferred to the object.

If the self-posing intuition makes the consciousness of givenness its own object and distinguishes alterations, parts, and moments in it, then there belongs to this distinguishing its own differently elapsing series of separations, settings into relief, and partial identifications. And it is again evident that the essence of such an elapsing constitutes the givenness and absolute givenness of the objectivity and its parts.

**The deeper error: the natural attitude's hidden presupposition.** Husserl diagnoses the root of the skeptical confusion: the conception acts as if objectivity and Being of every sort were something in itself without relation to consciousness, as if consciousness accidentally approached the object and operated on it — carrying out alterations in the mode of a natural operation. In the background lies the presumed obviousness that things are in themselves prior to all thought, and the Ego-subject is a new thing that works on and produces something with the thing, imposes formations on it, and the thing is given to the subject only in these imposed formations.

This entire picture collapses once the phenomenological reduction is carried out and the natural attitude with its "obviousnesses" is suspended. All talk of object and objective Being is a phenomenon and has its meaning; it refers to further nexuses of consciousness which, in self-posing, come to absolute givenness. Ultimately it refers to the absolute phenomenon of the respective givenness, which has its essential constitution: to be a consciousness of something in the flesh and a consciousness of givenness. In these nexuses, all sense of the talk of the object and objective existence demonstrates itself.

**The fundamental position: consciousness as absolute Being.** Husserl closes with a compressed statement of phenomenological first philosophy. There is an opposition between consciousness and object in the stricter sense: all transcendent objectivity has its primal ground and bearer in consciousness. The thing is constituted in consciousness; intentionality gives sense to it and to its "true Being." This intentionality comes to light in nexuses of consciousness of a determinate kind and is essentially inseparable from such nexuses. Consciousness itself, however, is **absolute Being** — not thingly Being. Self-posing brings it to givenness as absolute. It is identifiable and therefore an object, but it is not first constituted in nexuses of consciousness the way things are. It is simple and intuited. "The world is borne, as it were, by consciousness, but consciousness itself needs no bearer."

Is the thing then "only the nexus of my psychic acts"? Husserl immediately blocks this misreading: the phenomenological reduction is **not** the solipsistic reduction. The Ego is itself something thingly, constituted only in intentional nexuses. The justification for relating formations of consciousness to an Ego or a person is grounded only in Objectivating thinking and its logic. The thinking spoken of in phenomenological analysis "belongs to no one." We do not merely abstract from the Ego (as if it still stood there but was not referred to); we completely exclude the positing of a transcendent Ego and adhere to the absolute: consciousness in the pure sense.

**Significance.** §13 is the philosophical culmination of the introductory movement (§§1–13) and the immediate gateway to the concrete analyses of §§14ff. Its significance is fourfold:

First, the refutation of the analytical-skeptical objection secures the legitimacy of the entire method employed in the lectures. Every subsequent analysis — of sensations and objective qualities, of proper and improper appearance, of the kinetic synthesis, of the kinaesthetic constitution — involves the articulation of parts and moments within the unity of perceptual consciousness. The skeptic's objection, if valid, would undermine all of this. Its refutation clears the way.

Second, the principle that the articulation of giving consciousness is not the articulation of the given object is the key to understanding the entire real/intentional distinction of §§14–18. The perception has its own real parts (sensations, apprehension-characters); the object has its own parts and properties. The analysis distinguishes them precisely because the articulation of consciousness and the articulation of the object are distinct. The skeptic's error — conflating the two — is precisely the error that the entire analysis exists to overcome.

Third, the statement that consciousness is absolute Being while things are constituted in consciousness provides the deepest ontological grounding for the lectures' constitutive project. The question "how is the thing constituted in consciousness?" is not a question about how a pre-existing thing gets represented in a pre-existing mind; it is the question of how the sense and being of thinghood first arises in the essential nexuses of intentional consciousness. This is the question the lectures answer through the progressive analysis of sensation, apprehension, the kinetic synthesis, and the kinaesthetic motivation.

Fourth, the explicit exclusion of solipsism and the insistence that the phenomenological reduction is not the solipsistic reduction — that the Ego is itself constituted, and that the thinking of phenomenological analysis "belongs to no one" — places the entire investigation within the framework of transcendental phenomenology rather than any form of subjective idealism. The analyses that follow study the constitution of things not in "my" consciousness but in consciousness as such, in its essential structures.

# 

# SECTION II: ANALYSIS OF UNCHANGED OUTER PERCEPTION

## Chapter 3: The Elements of Perceptual Correlation

### **§14. Contents of sensation and the qualities of things**

This is one of the foundational sections of the entire lecture course. It establishes, with careful phenomenological evidence, the fundamental distinction between the *contents of sensation* immanent to the perceptual act and the *qualities of the perceived object* — and it uses this distinction to reach a series of results that will govern every subsequent analysis in the lectures. Despite its relatively short length, it introduces the concepts of adumbration (*Abschattung*), perspectival givenness, and the non-identity of sensation and perceived quality, while simultaneously refuting the image-theory of perception.

**The initial distinction.** Husserl begins with a concrete example: the perception of a house, specifically a perception containing no change whatsoever — an unchanged object held fast in its identical essence. Comparing the content of the perception with the content of its object, a series of parallel but distinct pairs emerges: sensed color versus the color of the perceived house, sensed roughness versus the object's roughness, sensed extension and form versus perceived spatial expanse, size, and structure. The latter — the object's qualities — are "filled up and covered in such and such a way" by the sensuous qualities.

**The real-immanent vs. the transcendent.** The sensed red is a *real* (*reell*) moment of the perception itself — the perception literally *contains* the moment red. But the perception is not itself red; redness is not a property of the perception but of the perceived thing. Perception cannot, as a matter of evidence, be designated a thing. Likewise, perception contains a moment of extension, but it would be "a fundamental perversion" to claim that the perception is extended — extension "by its very sense applies only to things: it designates a certain spatial modification." Space is a necessary form of things and is not a form of lived experience.

Here Husserl takes explicit aim at Kant: "Form of intuition" is a "fundamentally false expression" that implies "a fatally erroneous position." Space does not belong to the structure of intuiting consciousness; it belongs to what is intuited — the thing. This is a sharp anti-Kantian thesis stated with remarkable directness, and it orients the entire constitutive project: the lectures will show how space is constituted as the form of things *through* the structures of perceptual consciousness, but space itself remains on the object-side of the correlation, not the subject-side.

**The close correspondence — and its limits.** Husserl observes that to every sensed content there *corresponds* a moment of the perceived object, and the correspondence is so intimate that we use the same terms for both sides: sensed color — the object's coloration, sensed sound — the object's sound. But this terminological parallelism is deceptive. It is *not* something one and the same that is named twice, as though an identical complex of contents could be, when grasped transcendently, a thing, and when sensed immanently, a complex of sensations. What is immanent to perception is not the same as what is posited transcendently as a thing. If they were the same, we would have two things — one immanent and one transcendent — and there would be no reason to deny the name "thing" to the immanent one. This, Husserl says, leads to countersense: the thing is not a givenness that can be given immanently in straightforward perception.

**Perspectival givenness and the many-one relation.** Husserl then introduces a finding that will be pivotal for everything that follows. It is at least *possible* — and the later analyses will show it is *essentially necessary* — that many perceptions, essentially distinct in their complex of sensations, are perceptions of one and the same object, with that object taken as determined in completely the same way and as unchanged. Each of the various perceptions displays the object only from one "side," and each from a "different side." One perception brings to proper presentation certain groups of features while denying proper givenness to others; the next perception reverses this.

The relation between the immanent sensation-complex and the properly presented features of the object is a relation of correspondence: when I see the house from the front, the coloration of the front corresponds, in all its partial moments, to sensed color, and conversely. But even within this restricted domain of proper presentation, sameness between sensation and quality *cannot arise*.

**The adumbration argument.** Husserl demonstrates this with a brilliant example drawn from Locke: the uniformly yellow sphere. On the object-side, the coloration of the properly appearing front side is *uniform*. But if we attend to the immanent content of the perception, we find "a perpetual adumbration of the yellow" — a continuous shading and variation across the sensory field. And this is not accidental: "a necessary connection exists here: only if such an adumbration is sensed does a uniformly colored sphere present itself." The uniform quality *requires* a non-uniform sensation for its presentation.

Husserl draws the general consequence: the identity of an objective feature is compatible with — and in many cases *necessarily requires* — continuous alteration of the corresponding sensations. Approaching or receding from the sphere yields constantly new perceptions. The extensional moment of the sensation "demands a continuous alteration, if the manifold perceptions are to acquire unity in the consciousness: it is the same sphere, unchanged in its extension and form."

**The refutation of the image-theory.** The section concludes with a decisive negative result: "Identity of the Objective feature in no way signifies identity of the corresponding sensation. The sensation does not offer a reduplication of the feature." Perception does not contain an image of the object in the ordinary sense — a second thing representing an original by way of similarity. Perception contains neither a repetition of a whole thing nor of individual features. "The image-theory is, looked at from any side, countersensical. Here we have one of these sides."

**Why this section matters.** §14 establishes, in a compact space, virtually all the phenomenological principles that the rest of the lectures will develop constitutively:

The **sensation/quality distinction** — the difference between the real (*reell*) contents of the perceptual act and the transcendent properties of the perceived object — is the foundation of the content-apprehension schema that §15 will develop and that drives the entire constitutive analysis. It is also the 1907 predecessor of the *hyle/noema* distinction of *Ideas I*.

**Adumbration** (*Abschattung*) — the continuous perspectival variation of sensory content through which an identical objective quality presents itself — is here introduced for perhaps the first time as a structural, *essential* feature of thing-perception, not merely an empirical observation. It is this principle that will be worked out in exhaustive detail in the analyses of the visual field (§§20ff.), the oculomotor field (§§49ff.), and the expansion-modification (§§64–69).

The **many-one relation** between perceptions and the perceived object — the fact that essentially different sensation-complexes can present the same unchanged thing — is what generates the constitutive problem that the lectures exist to solve. How can an infinite manifold of differing sensations constitute the givenness of one identical, self-same thing? The entire kinaesthetic analysis (§§44ff.) is the answer.

The **refutation of the image-theory** removes the most tempting but most superficial answer to this constitutive question. If sensations were copies of objective features, constitution would be trivially intelligible — the sensation would simply mirror the object. By showing that sensations are essentially *unlike* the qualities they present (the non-uniform adumbration presents the uniform color), Husserl forces the constitutive problem into its genuine depth: the relation between sensation and object must be one of *presentation* (*Darstellung*), not reproduction, and understanding this presentational function is exactly the task of the phenomenological analysis of spatial thing-constitution.

The **anti-Kantian thesis** that space is a form of things, not of intuition, is equally consequential. It means that the constitutive analysis must show how space emerges on the object-side as a form of the constituted thing, through but not *in* the structures of consciousness. This is precisely what the analyses of §§58–69 accomplish: space is constituted as the ordered context of things through the motivated streaming of image-modifications, but it is constituted *as* the form of things themselves — not as a subjective framework imposed upon formless data.

### **§15. Presentational contents and apprehension (perception)**

This section completes the analysis begun in §14 by identifying and characterizing the crucial *surplus* in perception beyond the contents of sensation — what Husserl calls the **apprehension** (*Auffassung*). Together with §14, it establishes the full content-apprehension schema that will serve as the constitutive framework for the rest of the lectures. The section is philosophically dense, touching on questions about the nature of consciousness, the relationship between sensation and perception, and the ontological status of the presentational function — questions Husserl candidly acknowledges he cannot yet fully resolve.

**The surplus beyond sensation.** Husserl begins by recapitulating the provisional concept of sensation from §14: certain contents reside really (*reell*) in perception, standing in a determinate relation to the corresponding qualities of the perceived object. But — as §14 showed — their complex does not exhaust the content of perception. Two converging lines of evidence point to a surplus:

The first comes from §14's result that essentially different sensation-complexes can be perceptions of the same object. The second is a new argument, introduced through the *mannequin example*: the same complex of sensation-contents can ground perceptions of *different* objects. Standing before a mannequin, one and the same sensory fundament supports two conflicting perceptions — the perception of a mannequin-thing and the perception of a presented human being. Since the sensation-complex is identical but the perceived object differs, there must be something in perception beyond the sensation that accounts for the difference.

This turns our gaze, Husserl says, toward "the surplus which, beyond the complex of sensation, is to be found really \[*reell*\] in the perception and which, in the most intimate fusion with what is sensed, first constitutes perception."

**The apprehension-character.** The contents of sensation in themselves still contain nothing of the character of perception — nothing of perception's directedness toward one perceived object, nothing that makes an object "stand there as a thing in the flesh." Husserl calls this excess the **apprehension-character** (*Auffassungscharakter*), and says that the contents of sensation "undergo apprehension." In what is perhaps the section's most famous sentence, he writes that these contents "would in themselves be, as it were, dead matter, but through the apprehension they acquire animating significance in such a way that they are able to present an object."

This is the metaphor of *animation* or *beseelung* — the apprehension *animates* or breathes life into the otherwise inert sensory material, transforming it from a mere real (*reell*) ingredient of consciousness into something that *presents* a transcendent object. The contents, once animated, become **presentational contents** (*darstellende Inhalte*), and what is presented in them are the determinations of the object.

**Apprehension distinguished from belief.** Husserl immediately clarifies that apprehension is not the same as belief or position-taking (*Stellungnahme*). The concept of apprehension is narrower: it concerns the presentational function as such, abstracting from whether we believe, doubt, or withhold judgment about the existence of what is presented. It is apprehension that distinguishes mere self-posing consciousness (where we attend to the content itself) from presentational perception (where the content functions as presenting something it itself is not). This distinction will later develop into the *Ideas I* distinction between noetic sense-bestowing and doxic modalities.

**Two ways of defining sensation.** Husserl identifies two possible approaches to defining sensation. One is *functional*: sensed contents are those that function as presentational in outer perception. The other is *intrinsic*: all contents capable of this function share an intrinsic generic kinship — they belong to a highest genus, an "essentially unitary genus," encompassing color, sound, tactile quality, and so on. Husserl considers calling this genus "sensible content" but notes the term's disadvantages. He then invokes Brentano's concept of "physical phenomena," acknowledging that Brentano had these contents in view, and proposes calling them **"physical data"** (*physische Data*) — contents within the domain of absolute givennesses that are capable of functioning as presentational contents of physical things, and whose capability rests on their intrinsic generic type.

**The radical difference between data and apprehension.** The genus of physical data differs *toto coelo* — entirely, fundamentally — from what falls under the title of "consciousness" (apprehension, belief, doubt, etc.). Their functions are not interchangeable: a physical datum cannot function as apprehension, and apprehension cannot function as content of sensation (at least in outer perception). This is a strong and deliberate thesis: there are two fundamentally distinct classes of real (*reell*) ingredients in the perceptual act, and they cannot substitute for one another. It is the 1907 anticipation of the *hyle/noesis* distinction of *Ideas I*, though stated without the Greek terminology.

Husserl then opens a question he will not settle here: could what falls under the title of "consciousness" itself undergo an apprehension and constitute "transcendences of an essentially new group, psychic transcendences"? If so, one would need to speak of physical and psychic sensations, corresponding roughly to the traditional (but unclarified) distinction between outer and inner sense. This is a forward-looking remark that gestures toward the analyses of inner consciousness and time-constitution.

**The question of the relation between datum and apprehension.** The section's most philosophically subtle passage concerns the *mode of connection* between the physical datum and its apprehension. Husserl raises a question he explicitly declines to settle: is the interpretive apprehension "immediately one with" the physical content, or does the content first have its own immanent consciousness, upon which the apprehending is then built as a further stratum?

He considers and criticizes the traditional distinction between "mere perception" (*Perzeption*) and "apperception" (*Apperzeption*), which is often understood as if mere perception or sensation were a "pure and simple possession" — an immanently perceptive consciousness of a content — and apperception were a further act built upon it. Husserl objects: reflection does not show that a self-posing of the physical content is contained within the normal perception of a thing. The physical content is "lived" (*erlebt*) by the whole perception that contains both it and its apprehension immanently. But "lived" does not mean that the content is the object of an immanent perception or self-posing; "lived does not without further ado mean 'conscious' in the sense of just any consciousness."

This is a remarkably delicate phenomenological observation. Husserl is refusing to accept that normal thing-perception contains, as a real (*reell*) part, a separate act of "mere sensing" upon which a separate act of "interpreting" is layered. The two — content and apprehension — are found "in the most intimate fusion." Whether they can be separated is an open question, and the lectures will proceed without presupposing an answer. Husserl resolves the terminological issue by dropping "apperception" entirely (following Stumpf's recommendation) and retaining only "apprehension."

**Perception, representation, and appearance.** The section closes by drawing terminological consequences. The contents of apprehension taken in their apprehension constitute what can be called "perceptual representation" — a concept opposed to perception pure and simple. Mere representation is not yet judgment; perception implies belief or positing. The concept of *appearance* (*Erscheinung*) is thereby clarified: "Talk of the 'appearance of the object' does not prejudge whether what appears exists or not; even in hallucination we have an appearance. Thus appearance is obviously nothing other than perception."

**Why this section matters.** §15 is philosophically foundational for the lectures in several ways.

It establishes the **content-apprehension schema** that will govern every subsequent constitutive analysis. Every analysis of the visual field, the oculomotor field, the kinaesthetic systems, and the constitution of space will work within this framework: sensory contents undergo kinaesthetically motivated modifications, and apprehension animates these modifications into the presentation of identical, transcendent spatial things.

The **mannequin argument** demonstrates that the presentational function of sensory contents is not intrinsic to them but depends on the apprehension — the same contents can present different objects. This is essential for the constitutive project because it shows that the identity of the perceived thing is not "in" the sensory material but is constituted *through* the apprehensional achievement that unifies the material.

The distinction between **physical data and consciousness** as two fundamentally heterogeneous classes of real (*reell*) perceptual ingredients anticipates the *hyle/noesis* distinction of *Ideas I*. But the 1907 formulation is in certain respects more cautious: Husserl explicitly leaves open the question of how datum and apprehension are related, whether the datum has its own consciousness prior to apprehension, and whether consciousness-contents can themselves undergo apprehension to constitute psychic transcendences. These open questions will occupy Husserl for years to come — in the time-consciousness lectures, in *Ideas I* and *II*, and in the later genetic phenomenology.

The refusal to accept the traditional **Perzeption/Apperzeption** layering is also consequential. By insisting that reflection does not find a "mere sensing" within normal thing-perception, Husserl blocks the empiricist picture of perception as sensation-plus-interpretation. The content and its apprehension are given in "most intimate fusion" — a formulation that already strains the schema of "dead matter" plus "animation" and points toward the difficulties that will eventually lead Husserl to complicate and partially abandon the content-apprehension schema in his later work.

### **§16. The object of apprehension as appearance. Appearance in the proper sense**

This section introduces and elaborates what becomes the central structural distinction governing the entire analysis of thing-perception: the distinction between **proper appearance** (*eigentliche Erscheinung*) and **improper appearance** (*uneigentliche Erscheinung*).

Husserl begins by tying the concept of "appearance" back to the results of §15. There, he established that perception involves both sensational contents and an apprehension-character that animates them. Now he points out that "appearance" in the broad sense is simply equivalent to perception itself — it doesn't presuppose the existence of what appears (even hallucinations count as appearances). But a **narrower, more technical** concept of appearance immediately presents itself once we attend to the difference between what is *properly* perceived and what is perceived only *improperly*.

The key phenomenological observation is this: when I see a house, I properly see only the front side. The sensational contents (colors, shapes, textures currently given) have a presentational function, but that function is exhausted in presenting only a *limited complex of objective determinations* — precisely those belonging to the appearing side. This complex is what Husserl calls **the appearing side** (*die erscheinende Seite*) of the thing. And yet, the total apprehension of the perception intends far more than this side. The house is given as a *whole* object, with a back, an interior, and so on. The apprehension thus divides into two strata:

1. **Proper appearance** — correlated with the side that actually comes to sensuous presentation through the presentational contents. This is what I genuinely *intuit*, what is given "in the flesh" in the strict sense.

2. **Improper appearance** — the apprehensional surplus that constitutes the rest of the object (the back, the interior, the hidden sides). This component has no presentational contents of its own; the sensations are entirely used up in the proper appearance. Yet it is not *nothing* — it is co-apprehended, and it is what makes the properly appearing side *be* a side at all, rather than a free-floating fragment.

This leads Husserl to a crucial claim about the **non-autonomy** (*Unselbständigkeit*) of the side versus the **relative autonomy** of the piece. A *piece* of a thing (like the visible portion of a partially hidden tree) could in principle exist on its own — the thing would still be a thing if reduced to that piece. But a *side* is essentially dependent: it can only be what it is *as* a side of a full object. You cannot think a front side without co-intending some supplementary determinations that complete the thing. This is why proper appearance inherently demands improper appearance as its complement — they are inseparable structural moments of any outer perception.

From this Husserl draws the thesis of the **essential inadequacy of outer perception**: a fully proper, three-dimensional intuition that would present every determination of a spatial thing all at once is impossible. There is, in his words, only an *improper* intuition of space. Every individual outer perception is constitutively one-sided, and this one-sidedness is not a contingent deficiency but belongs to the very essence of thing-perception as such.

He also makes a subtle but important distinction between the one-sidedness of *perspectival* presentation (the front/back structure) and the incompleteness of *piece-wise* perception (parts being hidden behind other objects). The latter is contingent — the hidden pieces of the tree *could* come into view. The former is essential and ineliminable. Even if you saw the whole tree with nothing occluded, you'd still see it only from *one side*.

Finally, Husserl addresses a potential confusion: seeing only the front side does not mean seeing only a *piece* of the thing (the front half, as it were). The front side is rather the total unity of determinations falling within the scope of proper appearance. How the thing's perceptually given articulations (trunk, branches, leaves) distribute across the proper and improper appearance is determined by the particular perception in question, not by an arbitrary partition imposed after the fact.

This section lays the groundwork for everything that follows in the lectures — the kinetic synthesis of perception (§§26ff), the question of adequate perception (§§32ff), and ultimately the kinaesthetic constitution of spatial objectivity. The proper/improper distinction is the structural engine driving the whole account of how a three-dimensional thing constitutes itself through necessarily perspectival, one-sided givenness.

### **§17. Essential appurtenance of determinate genuses of sensible data to objective determinations**

This section is quite brief — essentially a transitional passage that draws out two consequences from the proper/improper distinction established in §16, one concerning proper appearance and one concerning improper appearance.

**On the side of proper appearance**, Husserl addresses the nature of the presentational relation between sensational content and objective determination. At the end of §16, he noted that the proper appearance involves something like presentation "through resemblance" — color presents color, sensed roughness presents the object's roughness. But he immediately flags that "resemblance" here cannot be taken in its ordinary sense. The presented objective quality is not a second, merely transcendent sensational content; it is something *objective* that by its very nature can never *become* a content. There is a radical distinction between presentational content and what it presents.

What the so-called resemblance relation really amounts to, Husserl argues, is an **essential, genus-level binding** between types of sensible data and types of objective determinations. A sound-datum can only present an objective feature of the sound-type; a color-datum can only present a color-quality of the object; a color-datum *cannot* present warmth, a sound-datum *cannot* present color. The pairing is not arbitrary or associative — it is grounded in the essential generic character of the sensible data themselves. This is why the same names ("color," "sound," "roughness") apply to both the sensational content and the objective determination: not because they are identical, but because they belong together essentially in the presentational function. Husserl is careful to note that this is not mere terminological pedantry — the later analyses of spatial constitution will show why this eidetic correlation matters.

**On the side of improper appearance**, Husserl states the complementary point more starkly: the improper appearance contains *nothing* of presentation. The objective determinations that are improperly co-given (the back of the house, the hidden interior) are co-apprehended but not "sensibilized" — no sensational material corresponds to them. They must be co-apprehended, because without them there would be no object at all before us, not even a side (since a side can only be a side *of* something). But the mode of their givenness is fundamentally different from that of properly appearing determinations.

Husserl then offers a significant self-correction. He reports that in earlier lectures he had described the improperly appearing determinations as represented "indirectly," "by contiguity," and "symbolically" — in contrast to the direct, resemblance-based, intuitive presentation of proper appearance. He now expresses serious reservations about this formulation. The problem is that it still attributes a kind of presentational function to the sensational contents with respect to the improperly given determinations, as if the sensations mediated their givenness indirectly. But that is not what happens: the sensations have no presentational relation to the improperly appearing features at all. Whatever constitutive role the improper appearance plays, it operates at the level of the total apprehension and the synthetic nexus of perception — not at the level of individual sensational contents serving as signs or symbols.

This self-correction is noteworthy in the broader development of Husserl's thought. The earlier "direct/indirect" and "resemblance/contiguity" language echoes the framework of the *Logical Investigations* (particularly the Sixth Investigation's account of intuitive and signitive fulfillment). By 1907, Husserl is pulling away from that framework for the specific case of thing-perception, recognizing that the improper component of perceptual apprehension doesn't fit neatly into the signitiv/intuitiv dichotomy. This prepares the ground for the more sophisticated account of horizonal intentionality and the kinaesthetic motivation of appearance-manifolds that the later sections of *Thing and Space* develop.

### **§18. The mode of givenness of improperly appearing determinations**

Section 18 picks up the problem left open at the end of §17: if the improperly appearing determinations of a thing are co-apprehended but not presented through sensational contents, then how exactly are they given? Husserl proceeds in three main moves: a negative argument against a tempting but inadequate explanation, a positive characterization using the language of "full" and "empty" intentions, and then a further distinction between determinateness and indeterminateness that cuts across the full/empty divide.

**The rejection of the phantasy-supplementation thesis.** Husserl begins by ruling out the most natural candidate explanation: that what doesn't properly appear in perception is instead given through concomitant phantasy-representations. We do sometimes have accompanying phantasy images of, say, the back side of a perceived house. But the suggestion that phantasy *generally* supplies the improperly appearing moments — that the front side is perceived while the back side is imagined — collapses under a simple regress argument. Phantasy-appearance has exactly the same structure as perceptual appearance: it too presents its object one-sidedly, with its own proper and improper components. If I phantasize the back of the house, that phantasy itself has a front side that is properly presented and further sides that are not. So the phantasy would need yet another phantasy to fill in *its* improper moments, and so on. The structural problem of improper givenness simply replicates itself within the phantasy rather than being resolved by it.

Husserl adds a further consideration about unity. Even if phantasy did present the back side, we'd need to explain what binds the perceptual presentation of the front and the phantasy-presentation of the back into a single consciousness of one and the same object. Analysis reveals that each — perception and phantasy alike — already carries its own apprehensional components that refer beyond what it properly presents. Perception of the front side already includes components that point toward a back side; a phantasy of the back side already includes components pointing toward a front. Each is therefore a complete, self-standing representation in its own right. When phantasy does accompany perception (which genuinely happens), what occurs is a *synthesis of identification* between two independently complete representations — a fulfillment relation in which the empty apprehensional components on each side find their counterpart in the full presentations of the other. But this synthesis is contingent, not constitutive. The perception is already a full representation of the whole object *before* any phantasy supplements it.

**Full and empty intentions.** This leads to Husserl's positive characterization: perception is a complex of **full intentions** (*volle Intentionen*) and **empty intentions** (*leere Intentionen*), or equivalently, full and empty rays of apprehension. The full intentions are the properly presentational ones — backed by sensational material, they bring their objective correlates to genuine intuitive givenness. The empty intentions lack any presentational material; they bring nothing to presentation but nonetheless have their *direction* toward the relevant moments of the object. They are what make the perception be a perception of a *whole* thing rather than merely of a detached surface-fragment.

Husserl is careful to distinguish two senses of "empty" here. The empty apprehensional components within a perception are not the same as *obscure* representations (such as dim or intermittent phantasies). Obscure phantasies still present something, however faintly — they have sensational material, just degraded. The empty components of perceptual apprehension have no presentational material *at all*. They are a structurally distinct mode of intentional directedness.

This full/empty terminology clearly carries forward the framework from the Sixth *Logical Investigation*, but now applied specifically to the internal structure of a *single* perceptual act rather than to the relation between separate acts of different types (signitive vs. intuitive). The innovation is that one and the same perception harbors both modes within itself as inseparable structural moments.

**Determinateness and indeterminateness.** Husserl then introduces a second distinction that *crosses* the first: the difference between **determinate** and **indeterminate** apprehension. This operates in a different dimension entirely from the full/empty distinction. A full (properly presenting) intention can be more or less determinate — seeing a house's color in clear sunlight versus in fog. And empty intentions can likewise be determinate or indeterminate — I may know exactly what the back of a familiar object looks like, or I may have only the vaguest apprehension that it has *some* color, *some* form.

Husserl insists that indeterminateness is never *absolute*. Complete indeterminateness would be nonsensical. Even the most indeterminate apprehension of the unseen back side is delimited within general essential frameworks: the thing must have *some* spatial figure, *some* coloration, *some* surface quality. The indeterminateness is always a *bounded* indeterminateness, circumscribed by the generic essential structure of thinghood. When I perceive a box, the apprehension gives it a back side with "a" color, "a" form — not *this* determinate color or form, but necessarily *some* color, *some* form within those general domains.

The determinability inherent in this indeterminateness is not a matter of conceptual classification or predication — it's not that I *could* apply the general term "coloration" to it. Rather, it is a **perceptual** determinability: a capacity to be filled in through further perceptual appearances that replace indeterminate intentions with determinate ones. I turn the box over; the formerly indeterminate back side now stands before me with a specific color. The determinate apprehension *coincides* with and *determines* the indeterminate one in a synthesis of identification.

This final move is what connects §18 to the next major phase of the lectures — Section III on the kinetic synthesis of perception. The processes of "more precise determination" (*nähere Bestimmung*), fulfillment of empty intentions, and continued confirmation of full ones all belong to the domain of synthetic perceptual nexuses. Husserl is signaling that the static analysis of a single perception (which has occupied §§14–18) must now give way to the dynamic analysis of how perceptions connect, unfold, and progressively determine the thing through temporal courses of experience.

## 

## Chapter 4: The Constitution of the Temporal and Spatial Extension of the Appearance

### **§19. The temporal extension of the appearance. Pre-empirical (pre-phenomenal) temporality**

Section 19 marks the transition from the static analysis of perceptual structure (§§14–18) to the new thematic domain of Section III: the constitution of the thing's spatiotemporal extension within appearance. It's the opening move of a deeper level of analysis, and it introduces one of the most consequential distinctions in the lectures: the difference between **pre-phenomenal (pre-empirical, transcendental) temporality** and **phenomenal (Objective) temporality**.

**The transition and the new theme.** Husserl begins by noting that the preceding analyses dealt with the most general structures of identification and differentiation. Now the task shifts to the *specific* synthetic formations that belong to the perceptual domain — formations not exhausted by the abstract categories of identity, whole/part, subject/determination. Two kinds of unity are at issue: discrete syntheses that bind separate appearances together at a higher level, and **continuous unities** that already belong to the immanent essence of a single concrete perception. These continuous unities are what Husserl calls the "extensions of appearance" (*Extensionen der Erscheinung*), and they are the phenomenological ground in which the spatial and temporal expanse of things is constituted. Every spatiotemporal predicate we attribute to things has its origin here.

The earlier analyses (§§14–18) tacitly operated with a simplifying abstraction: they considered unchanged perceptions of unchanged objects and ignored the moment of temporal extension. Husserl now lifts that abstraction.

**Pre-phenomenal temporality.** Even the simplest perception — say, looking at a house for one minute — has temporal duration. This duration is divisible: each temporal portion of the perception is itself a fully concrete perception containing all the structural moments already identified (presentational contents, apprehension, proper and improper appearance). Perception is essentially a **continuous unity** of perceptual phases.

Now comes the crucial distinction. When we perform the phenomenological reduction, Objective time — clock time, the determination "one minute," "thirty seconds" — falls away. But the extension and divisibility of perception do not fall away. They belong immanently to what is absolutely given, to perception as a phenomenological datum. This immanent extension is what Husserl calls **pre-phenomenal** or **transcendental temporality**, and he sharply distinguishes it from **phenomenal temporality**, which is the time attributed to objects — the time of things as constituted through the apprehension.

The distinction is architectonically fundamental. There are two temporal "forms" at play in every perception: one that is *reell* (really immanent) — the pre-phenomenal temporal extension of the appearance itself — and one that is *intentional* and Objective — the temporal duration of the perceived thing. The first constitutes the second, but they are not identical. Husserl warns against confusing them: the "form of intuition" (the immanent temporal form of the perceptual appearance) is not the same as the "form of the intuited" (the temporal form of the appearing thing).

**The constitution of objective time in pre-phenomenal extension.** In each temporal phase of appearance, the thing appears with its full thingly content — the same content, if the thing is unchanging. But the thing doesn't merely appear as a bundle of qualities; it appears as *temporally extended*, as enduring, as filling a span of time with its being. The pre-phenomenal temporal form of the perception is what constitutes this phenomenal temporal form of the thing. Every cross-section of the pre-phenomenal extension contains the full complement of sensation and apprehension (both proper and improper), and in this cross-section the thingly content is constituted. But the *temporality* of the thing — its having a duration, its filling successive temporal points — is constituted in the *continuity* of these phases, in the entire pre-phenomenal extension.

**Continuous unity and synthesis.** Husserl emphasizes that the temporal unity of perception is *primary*. It is not that discrete perceptual phases first exist independently and are then joined by a subsequent synthesis. Rather, perception is always already a continuous, unbroken unity. Phases and pieces are distinguishable within it — they can be made prominent through abstraction and differentiation — but they exist only as dependent moments of the whole. When we *do* abstract two phases and compare them, they enter into a synthesis of identification: both present the same thing, the same thingly content, but as filling different points of appearing time.

**The problem of change and the deeper abysses of time-consciousness.** Having sketched the relatively tractable case of *rest* (unchanged perception of an unchanged thing), Husserl briefly indicates the far greater difficulties that arise with *change*. When the thing changes, successive perceptual phases no longer offer the same sensational material and apprehension. The thing appears differently determined from moment to moment, yet as *the same* thing. How is unity constituted within perpetual otherness? And even in the case of rest, merely saying that there is a pre-phenomenal extension that constitutes time doesn't yet *clarify* the constitution. What gives each phase its position as *this* temporal point? What accounts for the distinction between "now" and "just past"? What explains the perpetual self-generation of the Now and its perpetual sinking back into the past — the "eternal drama" as Husserl puts it — in which every past itself becomes a *further* past?

Husserl acknowledges these as "immense difficulties" that have defied the greatest thinkers and that his own analysis has barely begun to penetrate. He signals that he will return to them, but for now the lectures will proceed to the sibling theme of *spatial* extension (§20), laying bare the main structural lines before attempting deeper clarification.

This section is significant in the broader context of Husserl's development because the 1907 lectures on thing and space are contemporaneous with the internal time-consciousness lectures (whose materials Heidegger later edited). The pre-phenomenal/phenomenal distinction introduced here is essentially the same problematic explored in those time-consciousness analyses — the question of how the absolute flow of time-constituting consciousness relates to the constituted time of immanent and transcendent objects. Husserl is self-consciously marking the point where the analysis of thing-perception opens onto the deepest stratum of phenomenological constitution, even as he defers its full treatment in favor of the spatial problematic that is the primary concern of these lectures.

### **§20. Spatial extension of the appearance: materia prima and materia secunda**

Section 20 is the spatial counterpart to §19's treatment of temporal extension, but it goes considerably further by introducing a constitutive stratification within the thing's material determinations that has major consequences for the rest of the lectures.

**The parallel with temporal extension.** Husserl opens by declaring spatiality and temporality siblings. Just as the thing endures and fills a time, it fills a space — its space, which may vary at different temporal points. If we abstract from time and extract a single point of the thing's duration, what remains is the thing's spatial expanse: a **spatial form** and a **spatial filling**. What fills space is "matter" — Husserl stresses that this term is to be taken in a naïve, purely phenomenological sense, meaning simply whatever stands in perception as filling space.

**The materia prima / materia secunda distinction.** This is the central contribution of §20 and one of the more original moments in the lectures. Husserl notices that "spatial form" and "matter" as spatial filling do not exhaustively capture everything we attribute to the perceived thing. A significant distinction emerges depending on how strictly we take the concept of *filling space*.

Some sensible determinations genuinely fill or cover the thing's space: colorations that extend over surfaces, tactile qualities like smoothness and roughness, thermal determinations. In visual perception these are *seen* as covering the thing; in tactile perception they are *felt* as filling it. These are the **materializing determinations** — they constitute the **materia prima**. They fill up the spatial form, and since spatial form in itself is nothing (it cannot exist as an empty abstraction), the materia prima is what first creates the thing as a concrete spatial unity. Without it there is no thing at all.

But other sensible determinations — acoustic qualities, smell, weight, and various empirical "activities" and causal properties — relate to the thing differently. The sound of a violin is apprehended *as* the violin's sound, but it does not fill the violin's space the way its color and surface texture do. There must *already be* a spatially constituted object for the sound to be attributed to it. These determinations are **appended** to an already-constituted thing; they make up the **materia secunda**.

Husserl explicitly flags that this is the genuine and phenomenologically significant sense of the old distinction between "primary" and "secondary" qualities — but he refuses to use that historically burdened terminology, since the Lockean and post-Lockean tradition thoroughly confused the relevant issues. His distinction is not between qualities that "really" belong to things versus subjective additions. It is a distinction in the *order of constitution*: materializing determinations constitute the spatial thing in the first place; appended determinations presuppose an already-constituted spatial thing as their bearer.

**The case of sound and the critique of analogical substruction.** Husserl devotes careful attention to the case of sound to sharpen the distinction. We do ordinarily speak of sound "filling" a space — the sound of an orchestra fills a concert hall. But this, Husserl argues, is an analogical substruction, not a genuine perceptual givenness. When I hear sound in a hall, I do not *perceive* space as filled by a sonic quality the way I perceive surfaces as covered by color. The space of the hall appears visually as determined by its corporeal limits, its walls covered with visual qualities. Nowhere does a "sonic covering" or "sonic filling" actually appear. The talk of sound filling or diffusing through space is guided by an analogy — the image of a fluid spreading through a container — which is itself a visual or tactile representation of genuine space-filling. The propagation of sonic effects gets analogized through a model drawn from the register of authentic spatial materialization, but it is not itself an instance of it.

This is a subtle and phenomenologically rigorous point. Husserl is insisting on a strict fidelity to what is *given* in perceptual appearance rather than to what we habitually *say* about things on the basis of theoretical or analogical constructions. Sound is heard as *from* the violin, as *in* the hall, but this "in" and "from" have a different constitutive character than the "on" and "over" of color on a surface.

**Spatial extension of the appearance itself.** Having distinguished the two strata of thingly determinations, Husserl returns to the structural point that parallels §19: the spatial extension of the perceived thing is constituted in a spatial **extension of the appearance** — a pre-phenomenal spatial extension analogous to the pre-phenomenal temporal extension analyzed previously. This extension belongs to every temporal point of the total appearance and is essentially different from the temporal extension. Like the temporal extension, it is continuous and unitary, admitting differentiation into pieces and phases. Fragmenting the spatial extension of appearance fragments the phenomenal thing with respect to its objective spatiality and its filling of space.

But — and Husserl notes this as a complication that will matter greatly — the relations here are more involved than in the temporal case, because spatiality is constituted *partly in proper and partly in improper appearance*. The temporal extension runs along a single line, as it were, but spatiality is three-dimensional and so offers far more complicated possibilities of fragmentation. The proper appearance gives certain spatial determinations presentationally; the improper appearance co-intends the rest without sensational backing. This entanglement of proper and improper appearance in the constitution of spatiality is precisely what the later sections of the lectures — especially the analyses of kinaesthetic systems and the transition from the oculomotor field to objective space — will work to unravel.

The section thus serves a dual function: it establishes the parallel between spatial and temporal extension at the level of appearance, and it introduces the materia prima / materia secunda stratification that determines which sensible determinations are constitutively primary for the spatial thing and which presuppose an already-constituted spatial bearer. This stratification effectively delimits the proper subject matter for the remainder of the lectures: it is the materializing determinations — visual and tactile qualities as genuine space-fillers — whose constitutive analysis will occupy the investigation of kinaesthetic motivation and the progressive build-up of three-dimensional spatial corporeality.

### **§21. The spatial diffusion of the thing's qualities and of the presentational contents**

Section 21 develops the internal phenomenological structure of the spatial extension introduced in §20. Where §20 established the materia prima / materia secunda distinction and identified the spatial extension of appearance as constitutive of the thing's spatiality, §21 now examines in detail how the space-filling qualities and their corresponding presentational contents are organized — specifically, the forms of **continuity** and **discontinuity** that govern both the pre-phenomenal extension of sensation and the Objective extension of the thing.

**Extension in the sensational contents themselves.** Husserl begins by narrowing the focus to the materializing determinations — the genuinely space-filling qualities (color, tactile determinations) — and setting aside the merely appended ones (sound, smell, etc.) that §20 relegated to the materia secunda. The presentational contents that correspond to these materializing qualities are themselves *extended*. The color-sensations that present the thing's coloration are not scattered atomic points; they have a rigorous unity and a rigorous form — namely, the form of **pre-phenomenal spatiality**. This pre-phenomenal spatial extension of the sensational data mirrors the Objective spatial extension of the thing: fragmenting the pre-phenomenal expanse fragments the thing's Objective space and its filling.

The apprehension, too, is extended — it is not just the sensational material but the *entire* appearance that has spatial extension. When we fragment the spatially extended appearance, we thereby fragment the phenomenal thing with respect to both its Objective spatiality and its space-filling content.

**The complication of proper and improper appearance.** Husserl notes immediately, however, that spatial extension is more complicated than temporal extension because spatiality is constituted *partly in proper and partly in improper appearance*. Temporal extension runs, as it were, along a single line — but spatiality is three-dimensional and the fragmentation of the spatial appearance can take many different forms. One can fragment within the proper appearance alone (for instance, making prominent a part of the visible front side and letting a spatial part of the thing correspond to it), or the fragmentation can reside entirely in the domain of improper appearance. The manifold possibilities of fragmentation here vastly exceed those in the temporal case, though Husserl cautions against simply projecting geometrical representations onto the phenomenological situation.

**Continuous and discrete filling of space.** The heart of §21 is an analysis of how the materializing determinations fill the thing's space. The filling can be **continuous** (the quality varies without leaps across the expanse) or **discrete** at isolated spatial limits — points, lines, surfaces — where the filling determination makes a "leap." Husserl illustrates with the example of a sphere. A uniformly yellow sphere has a color that adumbrates itself continuously and without discontinuity across the pre-phenomenal expanse of the appearance. But if the sphere is partitioned into differently colored fields, then boundary lines appear — lines of discontinuity where one color leaps over into another. The case of uniform covering is the limit-case: color passes over into itself, so to speak, without alteration.

**Two kinds of continuity.** Husserl then draws a distinction that is subtle but essential for understanding the structure of spatial filling:

First, there is the **continuity of spatial extension as such** — the continuity that belongs to the expanse itself, the continuous passage from point to point, from line to line. This is the formal continuity of space as a manifold, and it comes to consciousness most vividly when we allow a qualitative discontinuity (an "edge" between differently colored regions) to migrate continuously across an otherwise uniform expanse.

Second, there is the **continuity of the filling determinations themselves** — the qualitative continuity, for example the flowing transition from red through purple to violet. This is continuity within the genus of the quality, and it has multiple aspects: quality in the narrow sense (hue), intensity, saturation, brilliance, and so on.

The crucial asymmetry between these two kinds of continuity is that spatial extension is *thoroughly* continuous — it admits no gaps or hiatuses — whereas qualitative filling *can* be broken by discontinuities (leaps in color tone, brilliance, etc.) at isolated places.

**Two eidetic laws.** Husserl closes by formulating two laws governing the relation between extension and filling:

The first law: continuity is extension, and qualitative continuity is qualitative extension. This entails fragmentability and the ideal possibility of abstracting into phases, where each phase corresponds to a species of quality. All such phases must belong under one and the same essential genus. Continuous transitions are possible only *within* a genus — between color nuance and color nuance, between sound and sound — and are excluded by essential law *across* genera (between color and sound, between color nuance and brilliance, between sound and smell). This holds at both the phenomenal and pre-phenomenal levels.

The second law: if a spatial extension is filled by qualities of any genus, then the qualitative filling is *necessarily in general continuous* and can display leaps only at **isolated places**.
