Constant covering (uniform quality throughout) is the limit-case. Wherever there is a leap, continuum collides against continuum of the same genus, and the leap constitutes an "edge." Husserl attributes this principle to Brentano, who had formulated it decades earlier: when a continuum undergoes filling through determinations of a given genus, only isolated discontinuities are admissible, and they are always interspersed between stretches of continuous covering.

This second law is especially significant because it is a material a priori principle — not a logical or mathematical stipulation but a law grounded in the essence of spatial filling as such. It governs both the phenomenal thing (the Objective coloration of a surface must be generally continuous, with edges only at isolated boundaries) and the pre-phenomenal sensational expanse (the adumbrational color-data must display the same structure of general continuity punctuated by isolated discontinuities). Husserl acknowledges that the exact relationship between the pre-phenomenal and phenomenal instantiations of this law would require further investigation, but the structural parallelism is clear.

The section thus provides the fine-grained eidetic topology of spatial filling that the later analyses of adumbration (*Abschattung*) will presuppose. When Husserl turns in subsequent sections to the question of how the thing's spatial determinations are given through continuous manifolds of appearance — and how the kinaesthetic systems motivate the transitions within those manifolds — the laws established here about continuity, discontinuity, genus-restriction, and the isolation of qualitative leaps will serve as structural constraints on what counts as a possible course of spatial appearance.

### **§22. The significance of the various spatial fillings for proper and improper givenness. Visual and tactile components of appearance**

Section 22 takes the materia prima / materia secunda distinction from §20 and the analysis of spatial extension from §21, and works out their consequences for the *internal stratification* of perceptual appearance. The central discovery is that the materia prima is itself layered into distinct sensory strata — principally visual and tactile — and that the interrelation of these strata generates a new and more complicated articulation of the proper/improper distinction than anything considered so far.

**The unity of the thing's space and the plurality of its fillings.** Husserl begins by recapitulating: the materializing determinations constitute the thing as a static, space-filled concretum, grounding the possibility that further (appended) determinations can supervene. It belongs to the essence of materialization that the thing has a *unitary* space, and all the various kinds of filling belong to this one space. Each kind — coloration, tactile quality — forms its own continuous filling of that space, and these fillings coalesce into the unity of the total materia prima. At the Objective level, the extension of the coloration *is the same* as the extension of roughness or smoothness; they cover the same body, the same space.

**The pre-empirical dissociation.** But — and this is the pivot of the section — when we pass from the Objective level to the pre-empirical realm, this identity of extension *does not hold*. The pre-empirical color-extension and the pre-empirical tactile-extension are not the same moment. They cannot be said without further ado to coincide. This is a genuinely surprising result: at the level of the thing, visual and tactile determinations fill one and the same space; but at the level of pre-phenomenal sensation, the visual and tactile extensions are fundamentally heterogeneous and do not combine into a single pre-empirical expanse.

**The example of the sheet of paper.** Husserl develops this through a concrete phenomenological description. If I merely see a sheet of paper, the perception is purely visual: there is a visual extension of sensation, filled with visual matter, presenting the paper's visual determinations as space-filling. No tactile sensational material is present. If I simultaneously see the paper and lay my hand on it, the perception becomes *mixed* — but in a peculiar way. The seen parts of the paper are not simultaneously felt, and the felt parts (under the hand) are not simultaneously seen. The appearing surface, which Objectively is one and continuous, is covered *in part* visually and *in part* tactually. The two kinds of filling, fundamentally different in genus, coalesce into a single materia prima only insofar as they join together as *spatially continuous* — they cover the whole appearing side taken together.

**The new complexity of the proper/improper distinction.** This stratification forces a more refined articulation of proper and improper appearance. The distinction between proper and improper now *crosses paths* with the distinction between sensory strata of the materia prima. Consider the visual stratum and the tactile stratum separately. In merely visual perception, the properly appearing front side presents the visual space-filling determinations; the tactile filling of that same side belongs entirely to improper appearance. So the improper appearance now breaks down into (at least) two structurally different components: first, the objective determinations that do not come to proper appearance *at all* (the back side, which is neither seen nor touched); and second, the determinations that *do* come to proper appearance in one stratum (visually) but *not* in another (tactually). The front side is seen but not touched; its tactile determinations are improperly co-apprehended even though its visual determinations are properly given.

**Degrees of clarity and the question of phantasy.** This stratification produces a phenomenologically observable difference in "clarity" among improperly given determinations. The roughness of a file that I *see* but do not touch is given with far greater clarity than the determinations of the back side, which I neither see nor touch. The seen roughness is, as Husserl puts it, "almost as if seen and yet not seen" — it is *detected* in the visual appearance, present in the proper appearance in a peculiar way, and yet not properly given as a tactile determination. The back side, by contrast, is co-intended only through genuinely empty intentions.

Husserl considers whether this greater clarity might be explained by coincident phantasy — a phantasy-image of tactile qualities overlaid on the visual appearance — and admits this interpretation "has much to recommend it." But he resists endorsing it fully. The presentification in phantasy might not be indispensable even here; the greater clarity of the co-perceived tactile stratum on the front side might have other sources. He offers a suggestive metaphorical formulation: the parallel stratum "draws from the seen stratum a power, a much greater power than the back side draws from the front." Spatial *interpenetration* — the fact that visual and tactile strata occupy the same Objective space — confers more "power" on the co-apprehension than mere spatial *contiguity* (the relation of the front side to the back). Husserl candidly acknowledges this is "mythical" language, but insists something genuinely phenomenological lies behind it.

**The pre-empirical non-identity of visual and tactile extensions.** The most technically demanding passage in §22 concerns the precise pre-empirical description of mixed perception. The visual sensations have their own pre-empirical extension; the tactile sensations have theirs. These two extensions have, at the level of sensation, *nothing to do with one another*. They are never perceptually given together as corresponding to the *same* Objective piece of surface — they always pertain to *disjunct* pieces. The seen piece and the adjacent touched piece combine in the *thing* to form one continuous surface; in the thing they limit one another. But the corresponding sensational extensions do not combine at the level of sensation.

Husserl's description of what happens where the hand covers the paper is particularly precise. The visual extension of sensation does not break where the hand is; it runs on continuously. A coherent piece of visual extension *is* present in between, but it belongs to the apprehension of the *touching hand*, not of the paper. The apprehension of the paper lets it pass "under" the hand and be "hidden" by it, so the appearing front side of the paper has no visual *gaps* — only gaps in the properly appearing visual presentation of the paper, which are filled by improper appearance.

**One perception or two?** Husserl asks whether the mixed perception of the paper constitutes a "double perception." His answer is dialectical: in the strict sense, there is *one* perception, one unbroken unity of sensation and apprehension constituting one thing standing there bodily. But within this unity, two self-coherent strata are distinguishable — a visual stratum constituting the full visual thing and a tactile stratum constituting the full tactile thing. These are not two things but one thing with manifold filling: the *same* space filled in multiple ways, with the fillings not layered on top of each other but thoroughly *coinciding*. Every part, surface, line, and point has the same manifold filling; every geometrical division divides each filling alike.

**The role of apprehension in the interpenetration.** The coincidence of the strata is not a function of sensation but of *apprehension*. The pre-empirical extensions of visual and tactile sensation do not in themselves produce any ordering of one with respect to the other. It is the apprehension that bestows on the extensional moment of visual sensation the value of appearing spatiality, and likewise on the tactile extensional moment, so that the visual appears "placed between" the tactile and vice versa. The double apprehension coincides in such a way that the total expanse — reaching beyond what is properly given in either stratum — constitutes one identical body, merely covered or filled in a manifold way.

Husserl closes by marking this as a "great problem" — the precise manner of this constitution — whose solution the lectures will pursue later. The problem is effectively the question of how the heterogeneous sensory fields, each with its own pre-empirical extension, get unified into a single spatial thing through apprehensional synthesis. This is the problem that the later analyses of kinaesthetic systems (Section IV) and the transition from the oculomotor field to objective space (Section V) will take up, since it is ultimately through the kinaesthetic coordination of visual and tactile manifolds that the stratified materia prima gets constituted as one body in one space.

### **§23. Proper appearance (side) and appended determinations**

Section 23 is a short but carefully discriminating section that uses the materia prima / materia secunda distinction to define more precisely the concept of the **appearing side** (*Seite*) of a thing, and then asks whether and how the appended (materia secunda) determinations participate in the structure of sides.

**The pregnant concept of "side."** Husserl's first move is to show that the materia prima / materia secunda distinction lets us give a more exact, "pregnant" sense to the notion of a side. Within the complex of determinations that come to proper appearance, we can now distinguish between those relating to the **body** (*Leib*) of the thing — what Husserl here calls the **phantom** — and those pertaining to its **filling matter**. These form either a single self-coherent unity or several such unities, and they are further articulated into appearing *visual* side and appearing *tactile* side, following the stratification analysis of §22.

Husserl gives quick examples to illustrate the various configurations. If I see a thing from the front and touch its back, I have a visual side and a tactile side that are *separated* in proper appearance — they pertain to different spatial regions of the thing. If my touching hand splits the visual image of the thing's face into two disjunct parts, then the visual side consists of two separated pieces that are intentionally bound together through the intervening tactile side; together they form a single, self-enclosed, unitary side.

The essential point is that **the side is a filled expanse** — specifically, an expanse of *surface* that delimits the three-dimensionally filled body. The body comes to appearance *only* through filled surfaces. Husserl adds that "points" and "lines" that may appear as filled are to be included under the title of surfaces for these purposes — mathematical objects in the strict sense are not at issue here.

**Do appended determinations have sides?** The second and more exploratory part of §23 asks whether the concept of a side applies to the materia secunda as well. Husserl had characterized appended determinations as non-constitutive in the primary sense — they presuppose an already-constituted thing to which they are then appended, and they are not "filling" in the proper sense. But now he pauses and introduces a qualification: the essential feature of materia prima might not consist in *filling in general* but specifically in **constitutive filling**. Some appended determinations may actually fill space in a secondary, derivative way.

The key example is **thermal determinations** — warmth and coldness. Husserl confesses he had characterized these as materializing in the previous lecture, "though not in good conscience." On reflection, they are appended determinations: the body must *first* be constituted through tactile and visual materializing determinations before it can appear as warm or cold. If I hold a metal sphere, the temperature may change while the tactile spatial determination (resistance, shape, smoothness) remains unaltered and serves as the foundation of localization. The warmth fills space, but it does so by *overflowing* or *covering over* the already-constituted tactile space of the body. It has no proper space of its own; it diffuses *in* the tactile space and is bound to it.

Husserl extends this observation to the pre-empirical level: pre-empirical extension properly belongs to the tactile moment and only belongs to the moment of warmth or coldness by a kind of transfer — as if the tactile extension lends its "scope of action" to the thermal quality. The same applies to pain-sensation, which is localized in the edge or point of a knife but which in itself bears no moment of location and no extension.

If we grant, then, that appended determinations can fill space in this secondary sense, the concept of a side can be applied to them — but only **mediately**, since the foundation for forming the concept of a side resides in the materia prima insofar as it comes to appearance. The thermal "side" of the thing is parasitic on the tactile side.

Still further removed are determinations that do not even follow the original extension but stand only in a loose relation of localization — Husserl's example is the sound of a kettledrum, which is related to the seen skin of the drum and "comes to appearance with it" but does not diffuse over its surface in the way warmth diffuses over the tactile body. The concept of a side can be applied here only in an extremely attenuated sense, if at all.

**What is at stake.** Husserl closes by noting that the interest here is not terminological — not about whether we call this or that a "side" — but about the phenomenological and ontological structures these analyses bring to light. The section effectively establishes a graduated hierarchy within the thing's determinations as regards their relation to spatial givenness:

At the top are the materializing determinations (visual and tactile), which constitute the body and its sides in the primary sense — they *are* the filled surfaces through which the thing comes to appearance. Next are appended determinations like warmth that genuinely fill space but do so only derivatively, by borrowing the extension of the already-constituted tactile body. At the bottom are appended determinations like sound that relate to the thing only through loose localization and do not fill its space even in a secondary sense.

This hierarchy is important for the subsequent analyses because it determines what falls within the scope of the constitutive problem of spatial corporeality. The lectures will primarily concern themselves with the visual and tactile strata that constitute the phantom — the spatial body as such — and will treat thermal, acoustic, and other appended determinations as presupposing this more fundamental level of constitution. The concept of the "phantom" (the body as constituted purely through its materializing filling, prior to the appending of causal and dispositional properties) will become an explicit and important term in *Ideas II*, and §23 shows the concept already at work in the 1907 lectures, even if the terminology is not yet fully stabilized.

### **§24. Environing things and perceptual nexus**

Section 24 marks a significant broadening of the investigation. The analyses from §14 through §23 all operated under a tacit abstraction: they treated the perceived thing as if it stood alone in the perceptual world. Husserl now lifts that abstraction and confronts the fact that a perceived thing is never given in isolation but always in the midst of an **environment** (*Umgebung*) of co-perceived things. This immediately raises a cluster of new constitutive problems that the preceding analyses are insufficient to resolve.

**The perceptual environment.** The basic phenomenological observation is straightforward: the lamp rests on the table amid books, papers, and other things. These environing things are equally "perceived." What unifies the specially attended-to thing with the co-perceived things is a **spatial nexus** — the individually perceived thing has its own space (its body), but this space fits within a more encompassing total space embracing all physical things. The co-perceived things always include the **Ego-Body** (*Ichleib*), which, as a body among bodies, is likewise in the total space. But the Ego-Body occupies a constitutively **pre-eminent** position: it stands as the ever-abiding point of reference to which all spatial relations are attached. It determines apparent right and left, front and back, above and below. Everything that appears is its environment.

This is one of the first moments in the lectures where Husserl explicitly thematizes the constitutive role of the lived body as an orientational center — a theme that will become central in *Ideas II* and that anticipates the full treatment of kinaesthetic systems in Sections IV and V of the present lectures. The Ego-Body is not just another thing among things; it is the zero-point of spatial orientation from which the entire perceptual world is organized.

**Special perception, total perception, and attention.** Husserl then distinguishes between the thing perceived "in a special sense" (what we attend to — this house, that tree) and the **total perception** of the environing world out of which that thing is perceptually extracted. Are these phenomenologically the same kind of consciousness? Clearly not: what is specially perceived is what we *heed*, what we direct attention toward. The background things stand there but without preferential attention. Husserl signals that the role of **attention** in perception will need investigation — a theme he does not fully develop here but which becomes significant in later works.

Yet attention alone does not resolve the constitutive question. Even when attention encompasses two things at once, they do not appear as *one* thing with two pieces. An individual thing has its own unity — its own motility, changeability, independent of the environing things — and a manifold of things, though encompassed by one total space, is not a total thing filling that space the way an individual thing fills its own body-space. Husserl notes that scientific thinking might give us grounds to regard the world as a sort of total thing (insofar as individual things are encompassed by the unity of lawfulness), but phenomenologically what matters is what the world is *as it stands there straightforwardly in perception*.

**The insufficiency of the previous distinctions.** This leads Husserl to a frank acknowledgment: the distinctions established so far (presentational content / apprehension, proper / improper appearance, materia prima / materia secunda, etc.) are **insufficient** to account for the constitution of actual individual things. Those distinctions apply equally well to the total perception, which brings to givenness not just one thing but the manifold of things, the totality of space in which bodies are filled with separate matter and "crystallize" into separate things for themselves. What constitutively *individuates* one thing as against another within the total perceptual field — what makes the lamp be *this* thing, the table *that* thing, rather than both being undifferentiated zones of a single perceptual whole — remains an open problem.

**The continuum of appearance.** Husserl draws an important structural conclusion: the appearance of any individual thing is never isolated and self-standing but is **embedded in a continuum of appearance**. This continuum itself admits the distinction between apprehension and contents of apprehension and fuses into the unity of a **total appearance**. The sensational contents functioning as presentational for one thing have their *connection* with other contents functioning as presentational for other things. The apprehension pertaining to one thing "continues on" into the further apprehensions relating to new physical data, and a **total apprehension** arises, in which a total space presents itself — one space embracing the many individual things.

The total space and the perceived world come to proper appearance, in turn, only partially and from one side — exactly the same structure of proper/improper appearance that holds for individual things applies to the world as a whole. The appearing side of the world includes the appearing sides of all things given in the same perceptual glance, plus the empty space between them. The unity here is continuous and in accord with everything described previously; the entire structure of proper and improper appearance, of presentational contents and apprehension, scales up from the individual thing to the total perceptual situation.

**The constitutive problems.** Husserl closes by enumerating the open constitutive problems that emerge from this broadening:

First, what makes up the inner characteristic of an apprehension of a *thing* — an appearance that is embedded in the total appearance and yet self-enclosed? That is, what constitutively individuates a thing-apprehension within the continuum?

Second, how is the unity of **total space** constituted for perception — the space that encompasses all bodies but is not itself a body?

Third, how is the "wonderful separate position" of the **Ego** constituted phenomenologically — the Ego as correlate and referential center of the thing and the whole surrounding world?

Fourth — and Husserl flags this as a parallel and complementary problem — how is the **temporal environment** constituted? Just as the thing appears in a spatial environment, it appears in a temporal one. Every thing has its own duration, but there is only *one* time into which all durations are integrated. Different things or processes appear as simultaneous — and this simultaneity is not like the coincidence of visual and tactile fillings in the same space. Separate, non-coincident things endure in numerically *one* time. A footnote by the editor indicates that this passage on temporal environment was also published in connection with the internal time-consciousness lectures (Husserliana X), showing how tightly the 1907 thing-and-space analyses are intertwined with Husserl's concurrent work on time-constitution.

The section thus serves as a hinge in the overall architecture of the lectures. It closes the relatively self-contained analyses of the internal structure of thing-perception (§§14–23) by showing that those analyses presuppose but do not yet account for the broader perceptual context — the environing world, the total space, the Ego-Body as orientational center, the unity of time. These problems set the agenda for what follows: §25 will introduce the visual and tactile fields as the pre-empirical substrates of the total appearance, and the later sections on kinaesthetic systems will take up the Ego-Body's constitutive role in earnest.

### **§25. Visual field and tactile field**

Section 25 closes out Chapter 4 (on the constitution of the temporal and spatial extension of appearance) by introducing the concepts of the **visual field** and the **tactile field** as the pre-empirical substrates of total perception, and by formulating the constitutive problems that the entire preceding analysis has brought to light but left unresolved.

**The visual field.** Husserl begins with a concise definition: the presentational contents of the total visual appearance form a **continuous nexus**, and this nexus is the visual field. The field is a pre-empirical expanse with determinate visual fillings. All the visual pre-empirical expanses that provide the presentational foundation for the various things given in the same total perception fit within this field as *pieces*. Conversely, every piece of the visual field is, through the perceptual apprehension, presentational for some thing or other: these physical data pertain to the presentation of this house, those to that tree, others to the vault of heaven.

The visual field is therefore not a thing and not an objective space — it is the total pre-empirical visual expanse that underlies and makes possible the total visual appearance of the world. It is the sensational substrate out of which, through apprehension, the manifold of visually appearing things is constituted. It corresponds, at the level of sensation, to what the total space is at the level of the Objective world.

**The tactile field.** The same holds for the parallel field that is primarily constitutive for things — the tactile field. But Husserl immediately adds a qualification: not just any arbitrary fragmentation of these fields produces, with the appropriate apprehension, an appearance of a thing. The fields partition themselves in a **determinate manner**. Only where we already have appearances of things can we say that further fragmentation within those appearances constitutes things (that is, pieces of things). The articulation of the field into thing-presentations is not arbitrary but follows the structure of the apprehension.

**Improper "fields."** Besides the two proper fields (visual and tactile), one could speak — but only in an *improper* sense — of an auditory field, an olfactory field, a thermal field. These would grasp the physical data of those other classes insofar as they are apprehended as pertaining to things. But Husserl denies these the status of genuine fields, precisely because the data in question lack **original pre-empirical extension**. They cannot constitute space and things in the primary sense. They do not, properly speaking, form fields, even if other modes of fusion may be proper to them. This is a direct consequence of the materia prima / materia secunda distinction from §20: only the materializing determinations (visual and tactile) possess the kind of pre-empirical spatial extension that can ground a genuine sensory field; the appended determinations parasitically borrow their localization from the already-constituted spatial thing.

The restriction to two proper fields — visual and tactile — is architectonically important. It means that the constitutive analysis of spatial thinghood needs to account for the structure and interrelation of just these two fields and the apprehensions extended over them. This is exactly what the later sections on kinaesthetic systems will do: the oculomotor kinaestheses govern the visual field (Sections IV–V), while the tactile field and its kinaesthetic correlates provide the complementary stratum.

**Apprehensional articulation.** Extended over the proper fields, "as it were," are the apprehensions. They form an apprehensional unity that is internally articulated in a peculiar way: to every articulated member — every peculiarly constituted apprehensional complex — there corresponds an appearing thing. The question then is: what makes up the *inner characteristic* of a thing-apprehension, that is, of an appearance of a thing that is embedded in the total appearance and yet self-enclosed? And beyond that: how is the unity of **total space** constituted for perception — the space encompassing all bodies without being itself a body?

**The Ego and the temporal environment.** Husserl adds two further constitutive problems. First, there is the "wonderful separate position" of the Ego, constituted phenomenologically as the correlate and referential center of the thing and the whole surrounding world. This echoes what §24 already flagged about the Ego-Body as the zero-point of spatial orientation, but now formulated explicitly as a constitutive *problem* rather than a mere observation.

Second, there is a problem *parallel* to the spatial environment: the **temporal environment** and the constitution of the one time in which the temporality of every thing resides. Just as each thing has its own body within a total space, each thing has its own duration within a total time — and yet there is only *one* time. Into this time the Ego itself is integrated, not only as an Ego-Body but also according to its psychic lived experiences. Husserl notes a structural disanalogy with space: the unity of time is not like the coincidence of visual and tactile fillings in the same spatial extension. In the spatial case, different filling-strata *coincide* — they occupy the same space. In the temporal case, different things with their separate durations are *simultaneous* in numerically one time without coinciding in the spatial sense. They are separate, non-coincident things that nevertheless endure in the one identical span of time. The constitution of this simultaneity — how a single, shared time is given across a plurality of non-coincident things — is flagged as a deep and distinct problem.

The editorial footnote confirms that this closing passage on temporal environment and simultaneity was also published in Husserliana X (the time-consciousness lectures), underscoring the intimate connection between the spatial-constitutive analyses of *Thing and Space* and the temporal-constitutive analyses Husserl was pursuing in the same period.

**The place of §25 in the overall argument.** The section functions as both a summation and a transition. It crystallizes the results of Chapter 4 (§§19–24) into the two-field schema — visual field and tactile field as the proper pre-empirical substrates of thing-constitution — and gathers the outstanding constitutive problems into a compact list: the individuation of thing-apprehensions within the total field, the constitution of total space, the constitutive role of the Ego, and the parallel problem of temporal unity. These problems collectively define the agenda for the remainder of the lectures. Chapter 5 (§§26ff) will take up the kinetic synthesis of perception — how things are given through changing courses of perception rather than static snapshots — and the later sections on kinaesthetic motivation will tackle the constitution of objective space through the systematic correlation of field-transformations with bodily movement.

# 

# SECTION III: ANALYSIS OF THE KINETIC SYNTHESIS OF PERCEPTION

## Chapter 5: The Givenness of the Thing at Rest in Continuous Courses of Perception

### **§26. The various possibilities of changes in perception**

Section 26 opens Chapter 5 and marks the decisive transition from the static analysis of perception to the **kinetic** analysis. Everything from §14 through §25 worked with the idealizing fiction of an *unchanged* perception of an *unchanged* thing. Husserl now lifts this fiction and confronts the fact that perception is essentially a temporal process in which appearances change — and that only through such changing appearances can the thing be given in its fullness.

**The general character of change.** Husserl begins by stating the principle that will govern the new domain of analysis: an objective change can be constituted *only* in a change of appearance. It can be given only in a perception that itself changes in the pre-phenomenal sense. Unchanged perception filled its temporal extension with constantly the same content; changing perception fills its temporal extension with content that is *in general constantly new*. The continuity/discontinuity structure from §21 applies here in the temporal dimension: there is the thoroughgoing continuity of the temporal expanse itself, and then there is the continuity or discontinuity of the *filling* of that expanse. Change can consist in an instantaneous leap (a quality suddenly jumping to another) or in a constant, continuous transition (as in perceiving the movement of a thing or a gradual shift in its qualities), or in continuous change punctuated by isolated leaps.

**The idealizing character of unchanged perception.** Husserl then acknowledges explicitly that unchanged perception was always something of an idealizing fiction. In actual perceptual life, there are never truly lacking changes in bodily position, posture, or at least in the fluctuating gaze and accommodation of the eyes. The descriptions given in the earlier sections must therefore be taken "with an appropriately greater amount of prudence." Absolute sameness should be replaced by *general* sameness — sameness that includes imperceptible differences, where the gaps do not appear in a noticeable way.

Nevertheless, the earlier analyses were not mistaken, because the phenomenological characterization of unchanged perception — as the case in which extracted pieces or phases enter into a synthesis of *coincidence* that allows no differences to appear — remains valid as a pure type. It contrasts with changing perception, where extracted pieces found syntheses of *difference*. Husserl adds a subtler observation: sameness and difference perpetually pass over into one another. In difference there takes place a *coincidence* that increases in the direction of sameness and changes in the opposite direction; sameness itself emerges as the **limit-point** of increasing coincidence, analogous to how a null-point of intensity serves as the limit of a series of increasing intensities. At the first phenomenological level, however, the distinction is simply between coincidence without visible distinction (sameness) and the making prominent of similar yet distant distinctions (difference).

**The principle of universal change.** Husserl formulates a general principle: if we take "perception" in the broad sense of total perception — including the thing perceived in the special sense along with the Ego and the surrounding world — then in *every* change of perception, a change in the appearing world of things comes to appearance. Only an unchanged perception represents non-change: not merely a thing at rest, but its entire appearing surrounding world as resting and changeless. This universality claim means that any phenomenological change in perception is correlated with some change in the appearing world, even if the change is merely in the relative position of the Ego to the object.

**The taxonomy of changes.** The main systematic contribution of §26 is a careful **classification** of the kinds of change that can affect perception of an individual thing. Husserl distinguishes three main classes, setting aside mere changes in attention (though he notes that these, too, are phenomenological changes):

**Class I: Merely kinetic changes.** The thing remains unchanged in all its material properties (both space-filling and appended) and in its geometrical body. What changes is only the relative position of Ego and Object. This subdivides into: (a) the thing stays still and the Ego moves — I walk around the object, approach it, recede from it; (b) the Ego stays still and the thing moves; (c) both move. In all three sub-cases, the *intrinsic* determinations of the thing are unchanged; only the kinetic relation between perceiver and perceived varies.

**Class II: Changes in constitutive determinations.** The Object changes not merely kinetically but in its constitutive determinations. This further subdivides into changes in the thing's *geometrical* determinations (deformation, change of size while maintaining form) versus changes in its *material* determinations (the space-filling or spatially localized qualities). Husserl carefully distinguishes this from kinetic change: mere rotation and displacement count as kinetic, not constitutive.

**Class III: All of the above at once.** The thing can change kinetically and constitutively simultaneously.

Husserl adds that further changes, belonging to the *Ego* rather than the Object, supervene and intersect all these: accommodation, convergence, muscular tension in feeling and clasping objects. Changes in perceptual appearance are connected to these as well. These Ego-side changes will become the central theme of the kinaesthetic analyses in Section IV.

**Things in time versus processes.** Husserl closes with an important ontological remark. Things exist *in* time — they arise, endure, pass away, move, change qualitatively — but they do not in the proper sense *fill* time. Temporality does not belong among their constitutive determinations. Temporality applies rather to *processes* and *changes* as temporally extended objects, insofar as we thematize not the changing thing but the thing's *changing*. Husserl sets this aside — the lectures will not pursue the phenomenology of processes as such — but the distinction is conceptually important. It marks a difference between the thing as a spatial-material concretum that endures through time and the process as a temporal concretum that fills time with its own content. The analyses that follow will concern themselves with how an *unchanged* (or merely kinetically changing) thing is given through *changing* courses of perception — that is, with Class I changes, which are the simplest and most fundamental case.

**The significance for what follows.** The taxonomy of §26 is not idle classification. It determines the *order of investigation* for the rest of the lectures. Husserl will begin (§27ff) with the simplest case: an unchanged thing given through changing perceptions produced by the movement of the Ego (Class Ia). This is the case that isolates the pure contribution of the perceiver's own movement to the manifold of appearances — and it is therefore the case that leads directly into the analysis of kinaesthetic systems. The other cases (thing-movement, constitutive change, combined changes) are set aside as more complex variants that can be treated once the fundamental case is understood. The entire architectonic of the lectures from this point forward — the kinetic synthesis of §§27–31, the question of adequate perception in §§32–39, and the kinaesthetic constitution of Sections IV and V — rests on this initial taxonomy and the decision to begin with merely kinetic Ego-changes applied to an unchanged thing.

### **§27. Manifold appearances of the unchanged thing. The process of more precise determination**

Section 27 begins the concrete phenomenological description of what §26 classified as case Ia: the simplest and most fundamental type of changing perception, in which an *unchanged* object presents itself through a continuously unitary but *changing* manifold of appearances. This section and §28 together form the core account of what it means for a thing to be given through a multiplicity of appearances, and they introduce the key concepts that will govern the rest of the kinetic analysis.

**The setup: exploring an unchanged thing.** Husserl asks us to consider the case most akin to unchanged perception: instead of holding our eyes, head, and body perfectly still, we now run our gaze over the object, walk around it, consider it from various sides — all while the object itself remains unchanged. The perception changes in manifold ways, but the object appears as ever the same and as unchanged in itself. Husserl notes a phenomenologically important equivalence: it makes no essential difference to the *content* of the object's appearance whether we change our relative position or the object moves while we stay still. In one case the object appears in motion, in the other unmoved, but the immanent content of the appearance — abstracting from the apprehension of change of place — remains the same. This equivalence will become important in the later kinaesthetic analyses, where the distinction between Ego-movement and thing-movement must be constitutively grounded.

**Perception "by steps."** To make the analysis tractable, Husserl models the perceptual process as proceeding *by steps*: spans of change interspersed with intervals of rest. I move my eye from one fixation point to another, then hold still; I move my head and pause; and so on. The whole perception is a continuous unity that can be viewed as a chain of unchanged perceptions linked by transitional phenomena — and these transitional phenomena are themselves perceptions, since the object does not cease to appear while they play out. The same object appears throughout, but it appears in an ever different way. New sides come to appearance; previously seen sides appear in a new givenness; hidden pieces may come to proper appearance for the first time.

**Identity of the object, difference of the appearances.** The central phenomenological observation: when we examine the intervals of rest (the static "steps"), the *object* that stands there in each is the same — the intervals found **syntheses of total identification**. But the *appearances* themselves do not enter into an identifying synthesis with one another. They stand there as having "another" content. This is the fundamental structural fact of thing-perception that the whole analysis is built around: **objective identity through appearance-multiplicity**. The same thing is given as one and the same precisely *through* a manifold of appearances that are not themselves identical.

**Attention and partial identification.** Husserl distinguishes the changes in appearance that arise from the shifting of attention — making prominent this or that feature of the object within an essentially unchanged total appearance — from the changes that concern the coming to appearance of genuinely new sides or the appearing of previously seen sides in a new way. The attentional modifications are real and accompany the perceptual process at every stage, but they do not constitute the kind of change Husserl is primarily interested in. In attentional modification, the total appearance lies at the foundation; fixation and separate perception do not disrupt it. The prominent separate apprehension remains in continuity with the underlying total apprehension and is brought along into the synthetic unity of identity-consciousness.

Husserl acknowledges that parallel to these attentional modifications, modifications of the *objective* apprehension can also take place — the representation enriches itself as new dispositions are aroused and new apprehensional moments are annexed. But he sets these enrichments aside as not in principle necessary, along with the contribution of attention itself. The more important phenomenon lies elsewhere.

**The transition to §28.** Having isolated what he is *not* primarily interested in (attentional modifications), Husserl has cleared the ground for the analysis that begins in §28: the changes in appearance that consist in genuinely new sides coming to appearance, or previously seen sides appearing in a new way — changes in presentational contents and the correlative changes in apprehension. §27 thus serves as a careful phenomenological preparation: it sets up the experiential situation (exploring an unchanged thing by steps), identifies the fundamental structural fact (identity of object, multiplicity of appearances), separates the attentional from the properly perceptual modifications, and hands off to §28 the task of analyzing the latter in detail.

The section also implicitly establishes the framework that the later kinaesthetic analyses will make explicit. The "steps" of perception — the alternation of movement and rest, the exploration of the object from various positions — already presuppose a moving perceiver whose bodily changes are correlated with changes in appearance. §27 describes this correlation from the side of the appearances; the kinaesthetic analyses of Section IV will describe it from the side of the bodily movements and their sensational correlates, showing that the manifold of appearances is not a random succession but a systematically ordered manifold motivated by the kinaesthetic systems. The "chain of unchanged perceptions linked by transitional phenomena" will turn out to be a kinaesthetically governed continuum in which every appearance-change is functionally correlated with a determinate bodily movement.

### **§28. Change in the presentational contents and change in the apprehension**

Section 28 takes up the task that §27 prepared: having set aside the merely attentional modifications, Husserl now analyzes what it means for genuinely *new sides* to come to appearance, or for previously seen sides to appear *in a new way*, as we explore an unchanged thing through a changing manifold of perception. The section introduces two crucial concepts — **identity of objective relation** and **identity of sense** — that jointly articulate the structure of the identifying synthesis across different appearances.

**Parallel changes in content and apprehension.** Husserl begins with the basic observation: with every new position of the object relative to my eye, and with every turning of the eye, the presentational contents change — whether or not they still pertain to the same side of the object and the same objective determination. But the change is not confined to the sensational material. A **change in the apprehension** runs parallel to the change in the presentational contents. What was "clearly" apprehended in one appearance is apprehended "obscurely" in the next; what was given intuitively and properly now passes to non-intuitive, improper givenness, and vice versa.

**The example of the die.** Husserl illustrates with a die. One appearance offers the red surface in proper appearance; in the next, that surface no longer comes to proper appearance. What has changed is not merely that the relevant presentational contents are now absent — the apprehension itself has changed. In the second appearance, there may be a component of improper apprehension that still relates to this same red surface, but this is precisely *improper* apprehension: it is functionally conditioned by the lack of presentational contents but does not simply consist in their absence. It is a structurally different mode of intentional directedness. Moreover, the improper appearance does not necessarily have the character of determinateness; it can represent indeterminately the very same thing that stood there determinately in proper appearance.

This is an important refinement of the proper/improper distinction from §16. There, Husserl established the distinction as a static structural feature of any single perception. Now he shows how it operates *dynamically*: as we move through a manifold of perceptions, what was proper becomes improper and what was improper becomes proper. The same objective determination migrates between the two modes of givenness as different sides come to and pass from proper appearance.

**Synthesis of identification despite difference of content.** In all these cases — whether what was properly given now becomes improper, or vice versa — a **synthesis of identification** joins perception to perception. The appearances "coincide" despite their different immanent content. This is the essential structure: the identifying synthesis does not require that the appearances be alike in their internal composition. It requires only that they be *of the same object*, and this "being of the same object" is grounded in the essence of the appearances themselves, in their capacity to make identifications possible.

**The fiction of complete familiarity.** To sharpen the analysis, Husserl introduces a simplifying fiction: suppose the object is "completely known" — suppose there is no component of indeterminateness anywhere in the sphere of apprehension. Under this fiction, every appearance would contain fully determinate apprehensional components for every objective determination, whether properly or improperly given. We would then find an identifying coincidence that relates not only to whole appearances but to all their possible partial appearances and to all the articulated members of the apprehension. There would be a **mutual and univocal correspondence** between the articulated components of the different appearances, and the corresponding partial apprehensions would "coincide" — founding the consciousness "objectively the same": the same flank, the same surface form, the same coloration, the same edges.

**Identity of objective relation versus identity of sense.** Within this coincidence, Husserl draws a further distinction. When two appearances relate to the same object and intend the *same* objective determination in the *same* way — with the same degree of determinateness, the same specific content — they have **identity of sense** (*Identität des Sinnes*). The one appearance intends a red square surface; the other intends the same red square surface, even if it appears in a different adumbration, or even if it is given properly in one case and only confusedly (but with the same determinateness) in the other. Here the identification is total: not just the same objective reference but the same manner of determination.

But identity of sense can also fail while identity of objective *relation* (*Richtung*) is preserved. If one appearance "leaves open" whether the surface is red or some other color, while another determines it as red; or if one leaves the corporeal form indeterminate while the other intends it determinately — then we have the same objective direction (both appearances are about the same surface, the same form-moment) but a **distinction in sense**. One intends determinately what the other intends indeterminately, or they intend indeterminately but with different ranges of determinability.

Husserl's formulation is precise: we ascribe identity of sense to two appearances insofar as they intend the same object as determined, or determinable, *in the same way*. If the whole appearances are not identical in sense, we may still speak of identity of sense with regard to certain components, or of identity of sense of the whole appearances *with regard to* such components. The distinction is thus both holistic and componentwise — it can be applied to entire appearances or to their individual articulated moments.

**Significance for the kinetic analysis.** This distinction between identity of objective relation and identity of sense is not a mere terminological refinement. It is what makes possible the analysis of **more precise determination** (*nähere Bestimmung*) that §29 will develop. When we explore a thing through a manifold of perceptions, the successive appearances do not simply repeat each other — they *progressively determine* the object. What was given indeterminately in one appearance is determined more precisely in the next. This process presupposes that identity of objective relation can hold *without* identity of sense — that two appearances can be about the same objective moment and yet intend it with different degrees of determinateness. The distinction thus provides the conceptual apparatus for describing how perception is a process of progressive fulfillment and determination, rather than a static correspondence between appearance and object.

The section also quietly introduces the concept of **adumbration** (*Abschattung*) in its dynamic, kinetic sense — not merely as the static fact that a surface is given through a sensational content that differs from it, but as the fact that the *same* objective determination is given through *different* adumbrations as the perceptual situation changes. The red square surface of the die appears in one adumbration from this angle, in another from that angle. The adumbrations differ; the intended objective determination is the same. This dynamic concept of adumbration as a manifold correlated with changing perceptual standpoints is what the kinaesthetic analyses of Section IV will ultimately ground in the functional dependence of appearance-manifolds on bodily movement.

### **§29. More precise determination and re-determination**

Section 29 builds directly on §28's distinction between identity of objective relation and identity of sense, and develops the two fundamental synthetic processes that govern the kinetic manifold of perception: **more precise determination** (*nähere Bestimmung*) and **re-determination** or **conflict** (*Widerstreit*). Together these processes describe how the thing progressively constitutes itself — and potentially reconstitutes itself — through the course of perceptual exploration.

**Harmony of sense.** Husserl begins with the case where identity of sense is lacking — that is, where we abandon the fiction of an object completely known from all sides — but where the appearances are nonetheless in **harmony** (*Einstimmigkeit*). Two appearances have a harmonious sense when, besides the components of identical sense, the remaining components on both sides are related to one another as the more precise determination to the less. One appearance directs itself determinately to a given objective moment; the other directs itself to the same moment but indeterminately. Or both are indeterminate, but one is more narrowly circumscribed than the other. Husserl gives examples drawn from genus-species relations: quadrangle to triangle, triangle to figure, crimson red to red, "red" to "color."

But — and Husserl is emphatic on this point — we must not think here of a mediating *conceptual* consciousness, as the choice of these conceptual terms might suggest. The relation is a pure relation among appearances themselves, which enter into mutual harmony without the least consciousness of conflict. In the transition from indetermination to determination, this harmony takes on the peculiar character of a **consciousness of more precise determination**. The indeterminate apprehension leaves "open" the specific content of the objective moment — open for further determination. Its apprehensional component has no univocal relation to the full content of the moment but instead contains a manifold *determinability*.

**Determinability and its essential constraints.** The objective moment is, however, determinate "in itself" — that is, in the perception in which it is given properly and fully, it contains a fully determining component of apprehension. The harmony between this fully determining component and the earlier indeterminate component is what allows us to say the latter had already represented the *same* moment, but in an indeterminate way. The proper appearance first exhibits what was previously represented indeterminately as it "properly is": what figure the object has "upon closer inspection," what color it has, etc.

Husserl then draws a connection between this phenomenological relation of determinability and the logical relation of subordination. To every indeterminateness there pertains a generality — a range of possible determination that can be delimited by a "concept." This range indicates the general type of what can function as a specific determinateness. And to this logical genus-species structure there *corresponds* a phenomenological relation of more precise determination and determinability within the sphere of mere perception. Certain essential laws govern this: in the essence of an indeterminate apprehension of a figure, it is grounded that the apprehension can be more precisely determined only in the form of a determinate appearance of a *figure* — as triangle, quadrangle, etc. — never through fusion with an apprehensional moment of an entirely different genus, such as color. The possibilities of harmony and more precise determination are not arbitrary but are founded in essential lawfulnesses that exclude chance.

Husserl thus distinguishes three relations: **identity of sense** (both appearances intend the same determination with the same specificity), **harmony of sense** (one appearance more precisely determines what the other leaves open, without conflict), and **subordination/superordination of sense** (the more precise determination stands to the less precise as species to genus). Harmony is the most general of these; identity of sense and subordination are special cases within it.

**Conflict and re-determination.** Harmony is opposed to **non-harmony** — and this opens the analysis to a genuinely new phenomenon. The same unchanged object shows itself from various sides, step by step. At every step, the "same" object stands there; identity of objective direction is maintained throughout. But harmony of sense does not necessarily hold everywhere. Every perception of a thing involves a "construal beyond" (*Hinausgreifen*) what comes to proper appearance — the improper apprehension assigns to the object, in provoked intentions, much that is not properly given. And this construal may very well be a **misconstrual** (*Vergreifen*).

In the first step, the front side presents itself in proper appearance, and the back side is co-grasped improperly, with certain indeterminate or determinate intentions. In subsequent steps, other sides come to proper appearance — and it becomes manifest that the object is "otherwise" than it was first "envisioned." Instead of *corresponding* to the earlier apprehension, the new appearance *contradicts* it. The words "otherwise" and "contradict" refer to a **synthesis of conflict** — founded in the essence of the appearances at issue, a consciousness of non-harmony that is just as much an essential possibility as the consciousness of harmony.

But the thing does not simply dissolve in conflict. In all the steps, the *same* object stands there. The synthesis of conflict is therefore coupled with ongoing identification: the object is re-determined, not annihilated. Husserl describes what happens concretely: the back side of a sphere, co-apprehended as spherical, turns out upon inspection to have a dent. The apprehension "corrects" itself — the earlier intention is "crossed out" (*durchgestrichen*) and replaced. Yet even in the correction, general harmony is preserved at a higher level. The sphere still stands there as a *body* with a unitary form; the deformation fits into its total structure as a partial modification.

This leads Husserl to formulate a fundamental principle: **absolute being-otherwise in any respect is just as countersensical as absolute indeterminateness**. Under all circumstances, the new perception must *in general agree* with the old — must harmonize with it in general. Total disharmony at every level would destroy the identity of the object entirely. On the basis of a general, overarching harmony, various specific occurrences are then possible: total harmony, subordination and superordination of sense, determinate being-otherwise, further differentiation of what is given with respect to the possibilities included in it. And the differentiation need not be ultimate — the new perception can still leave open further determinations that await still further perceptual steps.

**The essential character of these possibilities.** Husserl closes by emphasizing that all these occurrences — harmony, more precise determination, conflict, re-determination — are founded as general possibilities in the *essence* of perception. If we see any thing at all from one side, it is in general evident that we could, while maintaining the proper appearance, vary or leave open the more precise determination of the other sides *in many ways*, within the general framework that the idea of a thing prescribes. Every proper appearance could be developed in many — though essentially limited — directions, whether toward greater determinateness or greater indeterminateness, into a full perception of a thing. And every one-sided perception could, in terms of ideal possibility, undergo contradiction instead of corroboration. The front side is not essentially decisive for the back; the back side is always thinkable "otherwise" than it was co-apprehended in the front. These are not accidental features of particular examples but essential structural possibilities belonging to the very idea of thing-perception.

**The place of §29 in the broader argument.** This section completes the conceptual apparatus needed for understanding the kinetic synthesis. §27 established the basic fact of objective identity through appearance-multiplicity. §28 distinguished identity of objective relation from identity of sense. §29 now adds the dynamic processes of harmonious determination and conflictual re-determination. Together, these three sections describe how the thing constitutes itself *progressively* through a manifold of appearances: not as a fixed object passively received, but as an intentional unity that is built up, specified, corrected, and re-specified through the ongoing course of perceptual exploration.

The concepts introduced here — especially the interplay of harmony and conflict, determination and re-determination — are foundational for the account of **adequate perception** that §§32–39 will pursue. The question there will be: what would it mean for the process of more precise determination to reach completion — for every indeterminate intention to be fulfilled, every possible conflict resolved? And the answer, as we know, will be that such completion is impossible in principle for spatial things, since every proper appearance necessarily leaves open an infinity of further determinations. The essential *interminability* of thing-perception (§39) is already implicit in the structure §29 lays bare: the fact that every one-sided perception could always be otherwise on its unseen sides, and that the construal beyond what is properly given is always potentially a misconstrual.

### **§30. Continuous synthesis of manifold perceptions**

Section 30 is one of the richest and most consequential sections in the lectures. Where §§27–29 analyzed the kinetic manifold of perception by abstracting discrete "steps" (intervals of rest linked by transitions), §30 now turns to the **continuity** itself — the continuous flow of appearance that mediates between and encompasses those steps. This yields Husserl's most developed account in these lectures of the **adumbration-continuum** as the constitutive medium of thing-givenness, and it introduces the crucial concepts of **anticipatory intention**, **continuous fulfillment**, and the **systematic totality** of adumbration-series.

**From discrete steps to continuous flow.** The analyses of §§27–29 treated perception as a chain of resting perceptions joined by transitions, extracting phases and comparing them through syntheses of identification, harmony, and conflict. But these syntheses were merely *possibilities*; their actualization does not belong to the unity of the continuous perception itself. Husserl now passes from the discrete to the continuous: the appearance changes continuously between the resting phases. This change is continuous "at least in general" — isolated discontinuities may arise within the continuous unity but are, as it were, "revoked" in that unity. Husserl sets these aside and concentrates on spans of actual continuity.

**The continuum of adumbrations.** The appearance gradually transforms itself into an ever new one. This implies a progression in differences that are still noticeable — "small" distinctions that are almost alike and yet already distinguished. As we pass continuously from phase to phase, the proper appearance changes continuously. To every objective moment that comes to proper appearance there belongs a **continuity of constitutive moments of appearance** — a series of phases proceeding by the smallest differences.

Husserl illustrates with the square surface of a cube. The adumbration of the square is not a single, fixed presentation. There is a *continuity* of adumbrations that pass over into one another perpetually — a sequence of least differences within one and the same proper genus. Likewise for the coloration of the square: not one color-adumbration but a continuity of color-adumbrations, again with generic unity and gradation in least differences. This is not contingent but **necessary**: it is precisely a manifold of adumbrations of this type that must continuously elapse so that the consciousness "the same unchanged square of the cube" can arise in appearance.

**Order, not collection.** Husserl insists that the order of adumbrations is an order *in continuity* — a genuine, structured order, not the arbitrarily interchangeable agglomeration of a mere collection. The adumbrations form a *fused unity*. The square surface thus has, in a certain sense, its own "sides" — and it can be given and possessed only in the form of these "sides," that is, through adumbrations. The object is **unity in multiplicity**, **identity in continuity**: it is what presents itself in the continuity of appearance as a continuity of adumbrations, and only in this presentation does it come to givenness and demonstrate itself for what it is.

**Perpetual harmony and unity of sense.** The presentational contents change perpetually, but they bear apprehensions in which the standing-there-itself of the square surface is constituted continually — in every phase, every part of the change, every change-differential — as a standing-there in the form of a perspectival adumbration. The apprehensions in their perpetual flowing constantly enter into a relation of harmony that presupposes and includes identity of objective direction. Within the sphere of the actual presentation of the square, this is a relation of **identity of sense**, not merely harmony — a perpetual identifying coincidence, a coincidence of sense. Yet Husserl adds a subtlety: actual identification (in the sense of a discrete comparing act) does not take place. What occurs is rather a certain **unity of the homogeneous stream** that, by its very sense, harbors the *possibility* of multiple emphases and identifications. This possibility, as founded in essence, justifies speaking of a continuous unity of sense dominating the presentations of a properly appearing determinateness. With this, the consciousness of the same, enduring, and unchanged square surface is constituted.

**Anticipatory intentions and the teleology of perception.** Husserl then introduces what may be the most significant concept in the section: the **anticipatory intention** (*vorgreifende Intention*). When a series of appearances runs its course in continuous unity, the first determination of the change — the "differential of movement" — already defines the *direction* of the course. Thereby a system of intentions is given that are continuously setting out and continuously getting fulfilled. These are anticipatory intentions: every phase refers to the following one. The series of appearances is dominated by a certain **teleology**.

Husserl is careful: this forward-directedness does not mean we focus on the appearances themselves — we are directed to the *object* as perception flows on. But every adumbration is an adumbration *of* the square, each bringing it to appearance in a different way, each bringing to appearance something that previously did not appear precisely that way. And each one **points forward**: in the stream of adumbrations, we feel ourselves drawn on from adumbration to adumbration. Each points forward objectively in a continuity, and in this forward referral, the adumbration is an **intimation** (*Andeutung*) of what is now coming. The intimation, the allusion, the intention, gets fulfilled. The one-sided view opens out to what is "omni-sided."

This passage is remarkable for the way it integrates the static structures of §§16–18 (proper/improper, full/empty intentions) with the dynamic processes of §§28–29 (harmony, determination, fulfillment) into a single flowing process. The **perpetual fusion of intention and fulfillment** within the continuous stream of adumbrations is what constitutes the progressive givenness of the object. We experience what a determination *is* not in the single view with its single adumbration — although it does stand there as self-given — but only by traversing the adumbrations, through which the determination is brought to a complete, "omni-sided" givenness.

**Degrees of completeness and the systematic totality.** Yet Husserl immediately adds prudence: completeness has various **degrees and levels**. A greater stretch of a continuous series signifies richer givenness than a smaller stretch or a single unchanged appearance. But even following the square of the cube from the moment it becomes visible to the moment it disappears again yields only one *closed series* — and this single continuity does not constitute absolutely complete givenness. The cube can move in many other ways: rotate around varied axes, approach and recede, undergo simultaneous displacement and rotation. These present ever different changes in the adumbrations of the square surface. All these continuous series stand toward one another in a **lawfully essential relation**. They continually collaborate, and only in the **all-encompassing unity** of these series does the square come to actual, "omni-sided," complete givenness.

The concluding formulation is one of the most important in the lectures: **the square is what it is only as the identical pole in the systematic unity of these adumbrations or possibilities of adumbration**. The object is not something that lurks behind the appearances; it *is* the identity constituted in and through the systematic, lawfully ordered totality of its possible adumbrations. This formulation anticipates the language of *Ideas I* (the thing as "X" or identical pole of noematic determinations) and points forward to the kinaesthetic analyses of Sections IV and V, where the "systematic unity" of adumbrations will be grounded in the functional dependence of appearance-manifolds on the ordered system of kinaesthetic possibilities. The "lawfully essential relation" among the various continuous series of adumbrations is precisely what the kinaesthetic systems will make intelligible: the fact that, for any given kinaesthetic course (eye-movement, head-turn, locomotion), a determinate series of appearances is motivated, and that the totality of kinaesthetic possibilities defines the totality of possible adumbrations in which the thing constitutes itself.

A footnote deserves mention: Husserl reflects that if we take seriously the perpetual play of intention and fulfillment as constitutive of givenness, we might doubt whether an absolutely unchanged perception can already count as a consciousness of something *given* — since such a perception is in fact a fiction, and the slightest oscillation of the eye already brings intention and fulfillment into play. "But the lightning flash in the middle of a stormy night?" he adds — a striking counter-example that suggests even an instantaneous, non-kinetic perception might claim the status of givenness, though the question remains unresolved.

### **§31. Possibility of reversing the series of appearances in the case of spatial objects, in contrast to temporal objects**

Section 30 is one of the richest and most consequential sections in the lectures. Where §§27–29 analyzed the kinetic manifold of perception by abstracting discrete "steps" (intervals of rest linked by transitions), §30 now turns to the **continuity** itself — the continuous flow of appearance that mediates between and encompasses those steps. This yields Husserl's most developed account in these lectures of the **adumbration-continuum** as the constitutive medium of thing-givenness, and it introduces the crucial concepts of **anticipatory intention**, **continuous fulfillment**, and the **systematic totality** of adumbration-series.

**From discrete steps to continuous flow.** The analyses of §§27–29 treated perception as a chain of resting perceptions joined by transitions, extracting phases and comparing them through syntheses of identification, harmony, and conflict. But these syntheses were merely *possibilities*; their actualization does not belong to the unity of the continuous perception itself. Husserl now passes from the discrete to the continuous: the appearance changes continuously between the resting phases. This change is continuous "at least in general" — isolated discontinuities may arise within the continuous unity but are, as it were, "revoked" in that unity. Husserl sets these aside and concentrates on spans of actual continuity.

**The continuum of adumbrations.** The appearance gradually transforms itself into an ever new one. This implies a progression in differences that are still noticeable — "small" distinctions that are almost alike and yet already distinguished. As we pass continuously from phase to phase, the proper appearance changes continuously. To every objective moment that comes to proper appearance there belongs a **continuity of constitutive moments of appearance** — a series of phases proceeding by the smallest differences.

Husserl illustrates with the square surface of a cube. The adumbration of the square is not a single, fixed presentation. There is a *continuity* of adumbrations that pass over into one another perpetually — a sequence of least differences within one and the same proper genus. Likewise for the coloration of the square: not one color-adumbration but a continuity of color-adumbrations, again with generic unity and gradation in least differences. This is not contingent but **necessary**: it is precisely a manifold of adumbrations of this type that must continuously elapse so that the consciousness "the same unchanged square of the cube" can arise in appearance.

**Order, not collection.** Husserl insists that the order of adumbrations is an order *in continuity* — a genuine, structured order, not the arbitrarily interchangeable agglomeration of a mere collection. The adumbrations form a *fused unity*. The square surface thus has, in a certain sense, its own "sides" — and it can be given and possessed only in the form of these "sides," that is, through adumbrations. The object is **unity in multiplicity**, **identity in continuity**: it is what presents itself in the continuity of appearance as a continuity of adumbrations, and only in this presentation does it come to givenness and demonstrate itself for what it is.

**Perpetual harmony and unity of sense.** The presentational contents change perpetually, but they bear apprehensions in which the standing-there-itself of the square surface is constituted continually — in every phase, every part of the change, every change-differential — as a standing-there in the form of a perspectival adumbration. The apprehensions in their perpetual flowing constantly enter into a relation of harmony that presupposes and includes identity of objective direction. Within the sphere of the actual presentation of the square, this is a relation of **identity of sense**, not merely harmony — a perpetual identifying coincidence, a coincidence of sense. Yet Husserl adds a subtlety: actual identification (in the sense of a discrete comparing act) does not take place. What occurs is rather a certain **unity of the homogeneous stream** that, by its very sense, harbors the *possibility* of multiple emphases and identifications. This possibility, as founded in essence, justifies speaking of a continuous unity of sense dominating the presentations of a properly appearing determinateness. With this, the consciousness of the same, enduring, and unchanged square surface is constituted.

**Anticipatory intentions and the teleology of perception.** Husserl then introduces what may be the most significant concept in the section: the **anticipatory intention** (*vorgreifende Intention*). When a series of appearances runs its course in continuous unity, the first determination of the change — the "differential of movement" — already defines the *direction* of the course. Thereby a system of intentions is given that are continuously setting out and continuously getting fulfilled. These are anticipatory intentions: every phase refers to the following one. The series of appearances is dominated by a certain **teleology**.

Husserl is careful: this forward-directedness does not mean we focus on the appearances themselves — we are directed to the *object* as perception flows on. But every adumbration is an adumbration *of* the square, each bringing it to appearance in a different way, each bringing to appearance something that previously did not appear precisely that way. And each one **points forward**: in the stream of adumbrations, we feel ourselves drawn on from adumbration to adumbration. Each points forward objectively in a continuity, and in this forward referral, the adumbration is an **intimation** (*Andeutung*) of what is now coming. The intimation, the allusion, the intention, gets fulfilled. The one-sided view opens out to what is "omni-sided."

This passage is remarkable for the way it integrates the static structures of §§16–18 (proper/improper, full/empty intentions) with the dynamic processes of §§28–29 (harmony, determination, fulfillment) into a single flowing process. The **perpetual fusion of intention and fulfillment** within the continuous stream of adumbrations is what constitutes the progressive givenness of the object. We experience what a determination *is* not in the single view with its single adumbration — although it does stand there as self-given — but only by traversing the adumbrations, through which the determination is brought to a complete, "omni-sided" givenness.

**Degrees of completeness and the systematic totality.** Yet Husserl immediately adds prudence: completeness has various **degrees and levels**. A greater stretch of a continuous series signifies richer givenness than a smaller stretch or a single unchanged appearance. But even following the square of the cube from the moment it becomes visible to the moment it disappears again yields only one *closed series* — and this single continuity does not constitute absolutely complete givenness. The cube can move in many other ways: rotate around varied axes, approach and recede, undergo simultaneous displacement and rotation.
