These present ever different changes in the adumbrations of the square surface. All these continuous series stand toward one another in a **lawfully essential relation**. They continually collaborate, and only in the **all-encompassing unity** of these series does the square come to actual, "omni-sided," complete givenness.

The concluding formulation is one of the most important in the lectures: **the square is what it is only as the identical pole in the systematic unity of these adumbrations or possibilities of adumbration**. The object is not something that lurks behind the appearances; it *is* the identity constituted in and through the systematic, lawfully ordered totality of its possible adumbrations. This formulation anticipates the language of *Ideas I* (the thing as "X" or identical pole of noematic determinations) and points forward to the kinaesthetic analyses of Sections IV and V, where the "systematic unity" of adumbrations will be grounded in the functional dependence of appearance-manifolds on the ordered system of kinaesthetic possibilities. The "lawfully essential relation" among the various continuous series of adumbrations is precisely what the kinaesthetic systems will make intelligible: the fact that, for any given kinaesthetic course (eye-movement, head-turn, locomotion), a determinate series of appearances is motivated, and that the totality of kinaesthetic possibilities defines the totality of possible adumbrations in which the thing constitutes itself.

A footnote deserves mention: Husserl reflects that if we take seriously the perpetual play of intention and fulfillment as constitutive of givenness, we might doubt whether an absolutely unchanged perception can already count as a consciousness of something *given* — since such a perception is in fact a fiction, and the slightest oscillation of the eye already brings intention and fulfillment into play. "But the lightning flash in the middle of a stormy night?" he adds — a striking counter-example that suggests even an instantaneous, non-kinetic perception might claim the status of givenness, though the question remains unresolved.

## 

## Chapter 6: The Possibility and Sense of an Adequate Perception of Spatial Things

### **§32. Intention and fulfillment in the perceptual process. Increase and decrease of the fullness of givenness**

Section 32 opens Chapter 6 ("The Possibility and Sense of an Adequate Perception of Spatial Things") and introduces an entirely new dimension of the kinetic synthesis: the phenomenon of **degrees of givenness** — the fact that within the continuous manifold of appearances, the object does not merely present itself in ever new adumbrations of *equal* standing, but presents itself **better** and **worse**, more completely and less completely, with increasing and decreasing **fullness**. This section is where Husserl's analysis of thing-perception first takes on the character of a **teleological** account, in which perception is directed toward an ideal of optimal givenness.

**Two strata of intention and fulfillment.** Husserl begins by distinguishing two interwoven but distinct plays of intention and fulfillment within the continuous synthesis. The first was already described in §30: in any continuous series of appearances, the differential of movement defines a direction, and a system of anticipatory intentions is continuously set out and fulfilled as each phase refers forward to the next. This is the general intentional structure of any continuous synthesis whatsoever — the "being drawn forward" from phase to phase in the line of continuous appurtenance. It pertains to any direction of change, regardless of whether the givenness is getting better or worse.

The second play of intentions and fulfillments is narrower and structurally different. It concerns not the mere forward-directedness of the series but the **increase and decrease of the completeness of the presentation**. This is what §32 sets out to analyze.

**The phenomenon of increasing and decreasing givenness.** Husserl illustrates with the rotation of a cube. As the cube rotates, the square surface first comes to proper appearance as a slight indication within a rather unclear, incomplete presentation. As the rotation proceeds, the presentation becomes clearer and more complete, until a **high point** or **peak** is reached — the point at which the square "best" presents itself in this direction of change. Further changes would decrease the completeness, progressing back through slight indication to complete disappearance. This holds both for the geometrical form and for the visual filling (coloration). We say the coloration "manifests itself more and more as it is in truth"; we see it ever better and finally see it best.

**The intentional structure of incompleteness.** Behind these everyday descriptions, Husserl finds special forms of unity founded in the essence of appearances. The incomplete presentation is already a consciousness of givenness — the thingly moment appears in the mode of perception. But this consciousness does not count as complete; it refers beyond itself. It is an **intimation** of what is properly intended, bearing intentions that point in the direction of more complete presentations, toward the "object itself" as it would come to givenness more fully. These intentions refer to the goal through the mediation of presentations that are by their very essence *mediating* — intervening phases in whose elapsing a coherent intention holds sway, perpetually getting fulfilled, and in the best case reaching the goal: a givenness where nothing more is missing with respect to fullness.

Fulfillment proceeding in the opposite direction has its correlate in an **"emptying"** — a continuous receding from the goal, which is still what is "intended" in every phase. The series of increasing fulfillment elapse into **limit-points or limit-regions** where increase passes over into decrease. The decreasing series have their own limits of decrease in the **null-point of appearance** — where the appearing of the moment in question first begins (or finally ceases).

**The distinction from the general forward-intentionality.** Husserl is emphatic that these are quite different intentions and fulfillments from those described in §30. The earlier ones pertained to any continuous synthesis as such — being drawn forward from phase to phase in whatever direction the movement happened to go. The present ones "take possession of the 'sense' of the appearances" in a different way: they penetrate the significance and the continuity of significance with an intention toward the complete "self-sameness" that is manifest or should be manifest in the applicable direction and respect. This penetrating intention finds perpetual fulfillment in the perpetual increase of the consciousness of givenness, but only in the limit-point as the maximal point is the goal of the increase actually attained. At that point, the intention no longer refers to fulfillment; it is itself consciousness of the attained goal.

**The multiplicity of dimensions of optimality.** Husserl then shows that the structure of increasing and decreasing givenness is not one-dimensional but **multi-dimensional** — and that the optima for different aspects of the object may diverge. The example of approaching a palace facade makes this vivid. Seen from a distance, the facade first comes into visibility; as I approach, it comes to ever better presentation until a "best" overall adumbration is reached. But this best overall view is not necessarily the best for all the *parts*: I see the surface quality of a particular section of wall better when I approach still closer, though then the overall look of the facade gets lost. For each part, further series of changes are needed, running from the most favorable overall adumbration to optimal partial adumbrations — and these are grasped in a continuous consciousness of unity as pertaining to the same wall, gathered within the overall adumbration. The partial apprehensions bear further intentions fulfilled in the mode of increased completeness.

This ramifies further when the concern is not merely a surface in its own determinateness but a spatial relation to the whole, or a fully spatial feature like the form of a jutting balcony. Merely approaching will not fulfill all the intentions; new paths of change are necessary — moving around the corporeal thing to attain, within the sphere of relatively best presentations, a clear consciousness of the givenness of corporeal form and coloration.

**The visual field and the system of adumbrations.** Husserl then turns to the changes pertaining to the movement of **focusing the eyes**, which brings to clearest vision, point by point, all parts of the side turned toward the perceiver. To this side there corresponds not a single adumbration but a **two-dimensional continuous system of adumbrations**. In every adumbration, a small, undelimited part of the visual field appears with relative completeness and clarity; this part passes over without sharp boundaries into ever further spheres of increasing unclarity. Husserl's example of reading a printed page is phenomenologically precise: the fixated word appears relatively clear, within it one letter appears most clearly at normal viewing distance, while neighboring letters and more distant ones diminish in clarity; what is farthest away is a vague *je ne sais quoi*, continuously diminishing toward the margin of the field of view. Running the eye along a line produces a continuous series of field-changes: unclarity is ever again transformed into clarity, new words enter the narrow circle of clear light, while illuminated words fall back into half-shadows and full shadows. And everywhere there is the play of intentions and fulfillments — the unclear referring to coming clarity, the clarity demonstrating what was properly intended by the unclear.

**The tactile parallel.** Finally, Husserl extends the analysis to the tactile thing. The table perceived tactually by laying my hand on it while writing is perceived better when I take my fingertips and feel the surface by running them over it. Each object has its various possibilities of presentation and its best presentation — whether overall (clasping a sphere with the palm) or partial (feeling it point by point with fingertips for the best and most distinct apprehension). But the continuity of visual space and its accompanying series of changes plays a large mediating role in grasping the identity of the various tactile presentations.

**The significance for the question of adequacy.** §32 thus establishes the multi-dimensional, multi-leveled structure of the increase and decrease of perceptual givenness — the complex topology of optima and sub-optima, of overall views and partial views, of visual and tactile best presentations, all interwoven through intentions and fulfillments across different dimensions of perceptual change. This structure is what §33 will take up in asking whether the process of increasing givenness could ever reach a final and *absolute* completion — an adequate perception of the spatial thing. The answer, as the rest of Chapter 6 will show, is negative: the multi-dimensionality of the optimality structure, combined with the essential one-sidedness of every presentation and the essential infinity of possible adumbrations, means that the ideal of adequate perception of a spatial thing is in principle unattainable. But the conceptual apparatus needed to formulate that result precisely — the notions of degrees of fullness, limit-points of increase, divergent optima across dimensions of change — is what §32 provides.

### **§33. The role of improper appearance in the process of more precise determination. The ideal of adequate perception**

Section 33 is the longest and argumentatively most ambitious section in Chapter 6, and arguably one of the most important in the entire lectures. It extends the analysis of increasing/decreasing givenness from §32 to the *totality* of the perceptual process, introduces the **ideal of adequate perception**, subjects that ideal to a sustained phenomenological critique, and arrives at the provisional — but not yet final — conclusion that this ideal is deeply problematic for spatial things.

**Extending the analysis to improper appearance.** Husserl begins by turning to the moments of improper appearance that §32 had set aside. Within the continuous stream of synthesis, improper appearances also undergo harmony of sense. A complex of improperly given moments can remain continuously improper throughout the stream while undergoing change in the form of **enrichment** — more precise determination through the fitting in of new apprehensional moments within the total framework. But enrichment necessarily also means **re-determination**: what was apprehended as homogeneous coloration turns out to contain particularities; the object now stands there not merely with more determinations but as *other* than it previously appeared, even though unchanged in itself.

There is also the conversion from improper to proper and back. Empty intentions are converted into full ones as presentational contents lend fullness to empty apprehensional components; conversely, proper moments necessarily turn improper as the perceptual situation changes. The presentational fullness contracts, becomes poorer, harbors confusions, and eventually disappears entirely, leaving only an empty apprehension of diminishing determinateness.

**The braid of increase and decrease.** Husserl then offers his most vivid characterization of the overall structure of the continuous synthesis. The synthesis as a whole is at any time "a **braid** of partial syntheses of increase and decrease" — individual strands that have no autonomy from one another. In one respect the givenness gains in completeness, in another it simultaneously loses. Enrichment on one side goes hand in hand with impoverishment on the other. The synthesis has the form of fulfillment in one direction and the form of emptying in the other.

The appearances themselves bear a "moment of increase, of relative saturation, of relative fullness or emptiness" — specifically with respect to how they bring the object to givenness. Husserl carefully distinguishes the *unitary form* of the continuous elapsing of appearances (the structure of increase/decrease itself) from the "intention toward" that holds sway in it — the goal-directed intending toward the object in its complete givenness. These are distinct, though the latter seems necessarily co-given with the former.

**The question of adequacy.** This analysis of saturation leads directly to the question that governs the rest of the chapter: is a perceptual manifold thinkable that could lead to a limit-point of **absolute saturation in all respects**? Every perception of things is inadequate: the resting perception is one-sided; the variable one brings the object to many-sided and ever richer givenness but never attains absolute givenness. What comes to givenness does so in a **dispersed** way — this moment in one appearance, that moment in another. Ascending one level of completeness with respect to one determination entails loss of the saturation given in previous appearances for other determinations.

Husserl illustrates concretely: approaching a distant object yields at first nothing but pure gain (with respect to that object, though the total perception is impoverished as fewer environing things fall within the narrowing field of view). But merely approaching cannot bestow fully saturated givenness. Even if the front side came to full saturation, the other sides would still need perception; passing to them would lose what was gained for the front. The full corporeal surface with its materializing determinations is already **unattainable** by an intention toward absolute givenness.

**The naïve ideal and its phenomenological reformulation.** Husserl traces this ideal to the naïve interpretation of perceptual givenness that dominates ordinary life: at a distance, in fog, from one side, we do not see the thing "as it is in truth"; only under optimal conditions — approaching, walking around, looking inside, ensuring good lighting — do we grasp its "true qualities." Abstracting from conditions and presupposing them as normal, one finds differences in saturation and maximal points within particular series of change. It seems that selecting the most favorable series could yield the absolutely best presentation — adequate givenness of the determination in question. And if this could be accomplished for all determinations simultaneously, we would have an appearance absolutely saturated in every respect, showing the object as it is in itself.

**The critique.** But on closer inspection, Husserl becomes skeptical. An ideal adequate appearance would contain nothing of improper givenness — no unpresented back side, no indeterminate moment. It would be a thoroughly proper, fully determining appearance. But then: would there still be a difference between appearance, what appears, and the transcendence determined thereby? The appearance would be no mere adumbration; it would contain no unfulfilled moments pointing beyond themselves.

Husserl entertains the thought experiment of an infinitely complete intellect that possesses a three-dimensional "field of view" in which things simply *are* — the field is Objective space, its partitioned structures are things. But he immediately raises a grave objection: **a field of view is not a space, and an excerpt from such a field is not a thing** — neither for us nor for God. The reason lies in §34's problem of movement: the thing is something individually identical that moves, rests, and changes location. The pre-empirical content of the visual field does not *move* in the genuine sense; what shifts in the field is a quasi-movement of contents, but quasi-movement is not movement, and similarity of successive field-contents is not identity. Individual identity of something moving arises only when the quasi-moving content is not the thing itself but a *presentation* of the thing, and the thing is what is identical in the continuous change of presentations. The field is not space but the **presentational field for everything spatial**.

This argument is directed against what Husserl calls the "field-theory of givenness" — the view that adequate givenness would consist in the thing's being really (*reell*) immanent in perception. The entire analysis shows that thing-perception is *constitutively* perspectival and adumbrational, not because of some limitation of our cognitive faculties but because the very sense of spatial thinghood — as something individually identical that can move, rest, and be encountered from infinitely many perspectives — requires presentation through inadequate, one-sided, adumbrational appearances.

**The difficulty of the maximal points.** Husserl also makes a more specific argument against treating maximal points of givenness as points of adequacy. If a maximal point gave adequate givenness of some moment of the thing, this moment could not be a *piece* (since a piece could be rendered autonomous as a thing, and no thing has adequate givenness in a single appearance). It would have to be a non-autonomous moment. But can a non-autonomous moment, inseparable from the thing, come to adequate givenness while the thing from which it is inseparable does not? This is a phenomenological contradiction.

**Phantasy and the evidence of essential possibility.** In a subtle passage near the end, Husserl clarifies what the "evidence of the possibility of fulfillment" actually amounts to. The appearance refers, by virtue of its sense, to possibilities of fulfillment — a continuous-unitary nexus of appearance in which the sense would be accomplished in every respect. The evidence of this possibility is the evident possibility of *phantasy appearances* adapted to the perceptual appearance, which as phantasy modifications of perceptual appearances make up the "representation that such and such would come to more complete givenness." We can integrate any appearance into a continuous manifold of phantasy appearances exhibiting perpetual fulfillment. Therein we intuit the essential possibility of fulfillment, of increase in the consciousness of givenness. But this essential possibility is an ideal one — it shows that the direction of increase is infinitely extendable, not that it terminates in an actually achievable limit.

**Significance.** §33 is the section where the central thesis of the lectures begins to crystallize: the perception of spatial things is **essentially and necessarily inadequate**, not as a contingent limitation but as a consequence of the constitutive structure of spatial thinghood itself. The argument will be completed in §§34–35 through the analysis of the finitude of the field and the infinity of space, and summarized in §39. But §33 already establishes the principal result and provides the phenomenological reasoning for it: the braid-structure of simultaneous increase and decrease, the inseparability of moments from the inadequately given whole, and the impossibility of collapsing the distinction between presentation and presented without destroying the very sense of thing-perception. Husserl's admission that he himself was "formerly a victim of the illusion" that adequate perception of thing-moments was possible — a reference to his 1904–05 lectures — marks this as a genuine philosophical discovery of the 1907 lectures, one with lasting consequences for the architectonics of phenomenology.

### **§34. Movement and location in the field — Objective movement and location**

Section 34 is a tightly argued section that carries forward the critique of adequate perception begun in §33 by confronting one specific and devastating problem: the constitution of **movement**, **rest**, and **location**. This problem, Husserl argues, is what definitively shows the impossibility of collapsing the distinction between the sensory field and objective space, and thereby rules out the ideal of adequate perception for spatial things.

**The identity of the thing in kinetic change.** Husserl opens with the principle that the thing is in itself completely unchanged in the series of its merely kinetic changes — it is something individually identical, both as a whole and in all its constitutive determinations. This is evident from the very sense of thinghood as demonstrated in outer perception. So far, the "field-interpretation of space" (the view that space simply *is* the sensory field, and things are pieces of it) seems to accommodate this. But Husserl argues that this appearance of accommodation rests on a temptation to conflate two fundamentally different things: the change of presentational contents *in* the visual field (their quasi-movement) and genuine Objective movement.

**Quasi-movement is not movement.** The core argument: when a thing appears to move, there is a parallel change in the pre-empirical contents of the visual field — the colored, figured section shifts its quasi-location within the field. We are naturally inclined to regard this as *itself* a movement and to attribute spatial significance to it. But the content does not genuinely *move*. Its individuality is the individuality of *this* filled piece of the field — and the piece of the field *is this one and no other*. When the content changes in the sense of a quasi-displacement, what we have are ever new parts made prominent in the visual field. They may be *similar* to previous parts — similar in pre-empirical form and color — but similarity is not identity. Even under the fiction that the entire field consists of points of the clearest vision (abolishing all differences between clear and unclear), the most complete similarity of pre-empirical figure in its quasi-movement never yields the **individual identity** of something moving. Genuine movement — the identity of *the same thing* persisting through change of location — arises only when the quasi-moving content is not itself the thing but is a *presentation* of the thing. The thing is what is identical in the continuous change of presentations. And the field is not itself space but the **presentational field for everything spatial**.

This is one of Husserl's most incisive phenomenological arguments. It shows that the very concept of *movement* — the continuous change of location of an individually identical thing — presupposes the distinction between the presenting content and the presented object. If the content *were* the thing, there would be no movement, only a kaleidoscopic succession of similar but non-identical field-sections. Movement requires transcendence; it requires that the thing be constituted *through* but not *as* its presentational appearances.

**The seeming resilience of the adequacy ideal.** Husserl then considers whether the ideal of adequate perception might survive this argument. One could say: the quasi-movement of the pre-empirical content is animated by a presentational function that constitutes, in this quasi-movement, something individually identical presenting itself in every phase. This presentation might be adequate insofar as it requires and permits no fulfillment. But Husserl immediately presses further: does such a presentation actually suffice to constitute movement and rest, change and unchange? Does the identical something *move* when the content undergoes quasi-movement? Or does it *rest*? What does the *appearance* of movement — as opposed to the appearance of rest — actually look like?

The questions are devastating. Objective rest is sometimes presented through *unchanged* appearance, but more often through *changes* in appearance (the presentational contents "stroll continuously across the field of vision" as I shift my gaze around a stationary object). And Objective movement is perfectly compatible with quasi-*rest* of the presentational contents (as when I track a moving object with my eyes, keeping it roughly centered in my visual field). The correspondence between quasi-movement/quasi-rest in the field and Objective movement/rest is not direct or univocal. It pertains to the *essence* of the relevant presentations that they are "qualified for" rest and movement respectively, but this qualification is not determined by the field-behavior of the contents alone.

**The problem of location and the infinity of space.** Connected to the problem of movement is the problem of *location*. Does Objective location present itself through quasi-location in the field? Here Husserl brings in the Kantian observation that space is represented as an infinite given magnitude. But the field is *finite*. No finite field can provide presentational means for the infinity of possible locations and changes of location that belong to the essence of space. Husserl leaves God aside and declines to "assail the evidence of the infinite," but insists that phenomenologically, everything infinite must be reducible to something like "limitlessness in the progression of intuition" — to ideal possibilities of continuous manifolds of perception, not to an actually given infinity in the field.

**The insufficiency of field-presentations.** Husserl draws the conclusion: with the mere field and its possible means of presentation — pre-empirical quality, figure, location — nothing is yet accomplished for the full constitution of spatial things. Space requires the possibility of omni-sided infinite movement and rest in a determinate location. Even adding an identifying presentation that posits individual identity in the quasi-movement of field-contents does not account for the constitution of the thing. The field alone cannot determine whether the thing moves or rests, where it is located, or how its location changes.

**The definitive argument against adequacy from the field-perspective.** Husserl then unfolds the consequences for the ideal of adequate perception. If the thing were supposed to be given adequately, the geometrical body would come to actual — not adumbrated — presentation, fully filled with materializing determinations according to all its sides, parts, points. Then the entire thing would *coincide* with its presentation — with the extension of pre-empirical sensuous data, with a fulfilled piece of the field. But then we would have "pieces of the field but not things." What remains to convert the field-piece into a phenomenon of a thing — a presentation of something individually identical? Only the presentation itself. But that "will not do."

Furthermore: how would pieces from *different* fields (visual and tactile) come to be presentations of the *same* thing? The visual and tactile primal things would need to have individually one spatial body — but each is constituted in its own field with its own extension (as §22 showed). And even if this inter-field unity could be achieved, the problem of movement would remain **insurmountable**.

**Significance.** §34 provides the specific argument that §33's general skepticism about adequate perception could not yet fully substantiate. The failure of the adequacy ideal is not merely that enrichment in one respect is accompanied by impoverishment in another (§33's "braid" argument), nor that the thing has infinitely many sides that cannot all be presented at once. It is that the very *sense* of spatial thinghood — individual identity persisting through movement, rest at a determinate location within infinite space — requires a constitutive structure that essentially outstrips what any field of sensation, however idealized, could deliver. The field is finite; space is infinite. Quasi-movement is not movement; quasi-location is not location. The thing cannot be a piece of the field precisely because it must be something that *transcends* any and all of its field-presentations. This is why the perception of spatial things is necessarily inadequate — not as a deficiency but as a constitutive condition of their being spatial things at all. §35 will formalize this result, and the remainder of the lectures will work out the positive constitutive structures (kinaesthetic systems, the transition from the oculomotor field to objective space) that make this constitutively inadequate, perspectival givenness possible.

### **§35. The field as finite means of presentation. The perception of things as necessarily inadequate**

Section 35 is the section that crystallizes the negative result toward which §§33–34 have been building. It provides the definitive a priori argument for the **necessary inadequacy** of thing-perception, grounded in the fundamental disproportion between the **finitude of the presentational field** and the **infinity of space**.

**The argument from the infinity of space.** Husserl opens with a lapidary statement of the problem. Space is an infinite manifold of possible locations and thereby offers a field of infinitely many possibilities of movement. Each thing is *a priori* moveable as a fulfilled spatial body, and so in infinitum. Therefore, the possibility must be guaranteed a priori that movement can come to presentation. But where are the presentational means for locations and changes of location *in infinitum*?

The answer: the field can offer only **finitely many** means of presentation. And this finitude generates inadequacy in **two respects**.

**The first inadequacy: intensive infinity.** In a piece of the field, a continuous body is supposed to come to presentation. But the piece of the field cannot offer an intrinsic infinity of distinguishable points. Therefore the presentation of the geometrical body, even supposing that it resides wholly within the perceptual field, must be **inadequate**. The body has a continuous spatial structure that is in principle infinitely determinable — every part has parts, every surface has sub-surfaces, every point-region can be expanded and examined more closely. To accommodate this, there must be, for the purposes of presentation, an analogue of "approaching and receding" — a process in which the piece of the field continually changes in its quasi-magnitude while counting as a presentation of the same corporeal moment. The parts and the parts of parts can undergo adumbrational expansion in infinitum, and through their identifying enrollment into the schema of the whole, every total presentation and every partial presentation stand there ever again as incomplete and capable of further completeness.

The consequence: the continuity of the corporeal thing presupposes "inadequate" perception — perception through adumbrations that are ever capable of enrichment and more precise determination. The thing's spatial continuity, as an *intensive* infinity (the infinity of determinability *within* any given region), outstrips what any finite presentational means can deliver at once.

**The second inadequacy: extensive infinity.** The finite extension of the scope of the field generates a second, complementary inadequacy. By moving, the object can step out of the field and step back in; it can fall partially within the field and partially outside it. This applies even in the process of continuous approach: claiming larger pieces of the field for the presentation of a single corporeal piece eventually exhausts the available field-space — "we no longer have a piece of the field large enough for a full presentation." However we conceive of this "approach," the intensive and extensive infinity of space, of which every thing partakes in its own way, **cannot come to presentation in an adequate fashion**.

**The economy of finite means.** Husserl draws the general conclusion: every possible presentation must **economize with limited presentational means**. It can do so only if the *same* means of presentation, finitely manifold, come into action again and again, and the infinity comes to presentation through the **serial form of the order of the operations**. The thing's infinite spatial determinability is constituted not through an infinite presentation but through the infinite *iterability* of finite presentational acts — the capacity to approach further, to turn to a new side, to magnify a region, to continue the exploration without end. And precisely because this iteration is never complete, an inadequation is necessarily involved: the individual appearance can *refer to* an infinity of possible operations and presentations, but as soon as it does so, it is precisely inadequate in terms of the guiding ideal.

**The a priori character of the argument.** Husserl characterizes this consideration as "in a certain sense, an a priori one." He analyzed the ideal of adequation and examined the possibilities of phenomenological constitution as possibilities belonging to the *nature of a spatial thing* — the necessary possibilities that must be guaranteed if things are to be constituted phenomenologically. The result is not an empirical observation about human cognitive limitations but an essential, eidetic finding about what spatial thinghood requires.

**The transition to "our" intuition of space.** Having established the a priori argument, Husserl then announces a shift to a "somewhat a posteriori" approach — though he immediately qualifies that the eidetic analysis will not be relinquished. He will now consider "our so-called human intuition of space" to see whether it can actually refer to the ideal of adequate perception as an ideal possibility intrinsic to it. He reassures: in the phenomenological reduction, the human intuition of space ceases to be "human" — it becomes the idea of this specific type of spatial intuition, which may well be a species of a higher genus to which things in a more general sense could correspond. Everything hitherto has been indifferent to whether space is three-dimensional, four-dimensional, flat, or curved; presupposed are only the congruence and continuity that belong indissociably to the sense of movement.

This leads to the investigation of the **maximum points** — the points of greatest givenness within particular series of change — which §33 already introduced. Even within our specific type of spatial intuition, it seems at first that the essence of thing-presentation includes the possibility of adequation. Husserl confesses again that he himself was "formerly a victim" of this illusion, referring to his 1904–05 lectures. The analysis of maximum points that follows (culminating in §36's treatment of optimal givenness and the direction of interest) will show that these maxima represent the best *relative* givenness within a given series of change, not *absolute* adequacy. They are relative optima within the braid of increase and decrease, not final resting points of complete fulfillment.

**The concluding clarification of maximum regions.** In the final passage before §36, Husserl provides a positive characterization of what the maximum points actually give. Within the sphere of proper appearance, all presentations are adumbrations; but these adumbrations are graduated in the sense of increase. At the limit-sphere, the givenness is clear and complete, and the "most proper self-presentation" stands there as ultimately fulfilling, referring to no further fulfillment *within that series*. This is the "consciousness of the most proper givenness" — the goal toward which the perceptual movement is oriented. But it is always relative to a specific determination and a specific direction of change. It is not the absolute givenness of the thing as such but the optimal givenness of a specific moment from a specific direction of approach.

**Significance.** §35 is the section to which the whole argument from §32 onward has been converging. Its result — the necessary inadequacy of thing-perception — is one of the central theses of the entire lectures and one of Husserl's most enduring contributions to the phenomenology of perception. The argument has two prongs that correspond to two kinds of infinity: the intensive infinity of the thing's internal spatial determinability (there is always more to see in any given region) and the extensive infinity of space itself (the thing can always be elsewhere, and there are always further spatial relations to other things). Against both kinds of infinity, the finite presentational field can marshal only finite resources, deployed iteratively in an open-ended process that can in principle never terminate. The thing is constituted not *in* a single adequate presentation but *through* an essentially interminable, necessarily inadequate manifold of finite presentations — and this interminability is not a deficiency to be lamented but the constitutive condition of there being spatial things at all.

### **§36. Optimal givenness and direction of interest**

Section 36 continues the investigation of §§33–35 into the status of maximum points of givenness, but now introduces a fundamentally new factor: the role of **interest** (*Interesse*) in determining what counts as "complete" or "optimal" givenness. This section effects a crucial displacement: the maximum points, which seemed to mark intrinsic limits of the appearance-manifold, turn out to be relative to the direction of interest that governs the perceptual process. The result deepens the argument against adequate perception and connects the analysis of thing-constitution to questions about concept-formation and practical orientation.

**Recapitulation: the maximum points.** Husserl opens by restating the state of the argument. The impossibility of the ideal of absolutely adequate perception seemed to follow on general, a priori grounds (§§34–35). But the maximum points remain to be dealt with. To every determination there belongs, in ideal possibility, an appearance or a region of appearance in which the determination counts as "completely given" — such that every other appearance finds in this region its pre-eminent fulfillment. Within the sphere of proper appearance, adumbrations are graduated in the sense of increase; at the limit-sphere, the "most proper self-presentation" stands there as ultimately fulfilling, referring to no further fulfillment. This is the consciousness of the most proper givenness — the goal toward which the perceptual movement is directed. Even individual perception already has an orientation toward such a goal, and the essence of its intention implies the possibility of a quasi-fulfilling phantasy appearance as the terminus representing maximum givenness.

Husserl illustrates: the surface of a cube seen in a mere "projection" intends itself as it would appear after the appropriate rotation into a position parallel to the frontal plane under good lighting. The surface appearing in this way is evidently the one intended — it shows fully and completely what the surface properly is, corresponding to the sense of the original appearance.

**Maximum regions, not single points.** Husserl makes two important qualifications. First, for the entire thing (not just individual determinations), we must speak of a correlated **group or sphere** of maximum points connected in the continuous series of appearances — not a single privileged appearance. Seeing the matchbox in full light from all sides, with each continuously coordinated appearance having the character of maximum givenness, is what satisfies us.

Second, this circle of maximum givennesses is **not fixed**. It can be freely varied within certain limits. The differences among appearances at the maximum are quite noticeable but count as **irrelevant** — they are without significance for the increase or decrease of completeness. Whether the sun is higher or lower, whether the viewing distance varies within not inconsiderable limits — these yield noticeable differences in appearance that nonetheless do not alter the character of maximum givenness. The meaning-intention pervades the privileged domain, and within it, every variation yields equally good appearances.

**The relativity to interest.** This is where the decisive new insight emerges. If the interest **changes**, the circle of previously satisfying appearances is transformed into an unsatisfying circle, and differences in appearance that were formerly irrelevant may now become very relevant. What previously counted as complete givenness no longer brings to sufficient givenness what now interests me. Husserl illustrates with escalating examples: the general architectural form of a house calls for certain best appearances; if the building material becomes the object of interest — whether the pillars are sandstone or cement — we must approach closer, and new series of perceptions culminate in new limits of increase. The new limits are once again domains of irrelevance *for this interest*, but different from the previous ones. The natural interest in a flower is different from the botanist's interest; accordingly the best appearances and the full givenness in which the interest is satisfied are essentially different in each case. Yet the flower is nevertheless the same flower, constituted in one and the same nexus of appearance.

**The structure of fulfillment relative to interest.** Husserl distinguishes two components of what we call "fulfillment" in this context:

First, the **consciousness of the attained goal**: this has its foundation in the appearances that terminate the process of fulfillment, insofar as these appearances are "representatives" — the appearing object, in this mode of presentation, is what was intended and is now self-given just as it was intended.

Second, the **satisfaction pertaining to the interest as such**: a satisfaction built on that interest, with a possible negative correlate in the consciousness of "lacking nothing."

The distinction is important because it shows that the consciousness of complete givenness is always doubly constituted: by the appearance-structure itself (which provides the presentational basis) and by the interest that defines what counts as complete for present purposes. There is no interest-free, absolute completeness; every completeness is completeness *relative to* an interest that selects a particular circle of appearances as satisfying.

**Philosophical significance.** The introduction of interest is not a psychologistic concession. Husserl is not saying that completeness is merely subjective or that it depends on arbitrary preferences. The interests in question are themselves eidetically structured: the botanist's interest and the ordinary person's interest define different but equally legitimate eidetic types of givenness, each with its own laws of fulfillment and its own circle of optimal appearances. What the analysis shows is that there is no interest-neutral stratum of "absolute" optimal givenness beneath these different interest-relative optima. The maximum points are constitutively bound to the direction of interest; they do not mark intrinsic boundaries of the appearance-manifold that would survive the removal of all interest.

This result reinforces the argument of §§33–35 against adequate perception, but from a new angle. It is not only that the finitude of the field cannot accommodate the infinity of space (§35's argument), or that enrichment in one respect entails impoverishment in another (§33's argument). It is also that what counts as "enough" — as complete givenness — is always relative to an interest that could always change direction, revealing new dimensions of determinability that the previous circle of optimal appearances left entirely untouched. The "limitlessness" of thing-perception is thus not only a matter of spatial infinity but of the open-endedness of possible interests, each of which defines its own trajectory of fulfillment within the inexhaustible manifold of appearances.

This section also prepares the ground for §37's brief treatment of concept-formation: the empirical concept (the meaning of a word like "flower") is essentially constituted through an intention whose fulfillment terminates in a circle of optimal appearances determined by the interest governing the concept-formation. Different interests yield different concepts of the same thing. And §39's summary will draw together all these threads — the infinity of spatial determinability, the braid of increase and decrease, the relativity to interest, and the open-endedness of re-determination — into the concluding thesis that thing-perception is a process interminable in principle.

### **§37. Direction of interest and formation of concepts**

Section 37 — "Direction of interest and formation of concepts" — takes the insight of §36 — that optimal givenness is relative to the direction of interest — and draws out two consequences: one concerning the relationship between interest, intention, and **concept-formation**, and the other, more far-reaching, concerning the intrinsic limitlessness of the appearance-manifold considered independently of any interest.

**Interest, intention, and the empirical concept.** Husserl begins by connecting the distinctions of intention and interest to the formation of **empirical concepts**. The concept of "flower" — the meaning of the word — intends something: it refers to something that comes to givenness in appearances that fulfill the intention. These appearances are intuitions of what is intended. What is *ultimately* intended, however, manifests itself in the intention that penetrates the manifold of appearances in which the intended object is constituted — or rather, in its fulfillment in the consciousness of completeness. We have the intention in the word-meaning, the intention in the appearances, and in the appearances an ultimately fulfilling intention that, through its appearing content, converts the sense of the intention into a clear, ultimately fulfilling sense in the concomitant process of fulfillment.

The ultimately fulfilling intention does not relate to a single phase of appearance but to a pre-eminent circle of appearances, which is itself variable within a sphere of irrelevance (as §36 established). The **orientation** of the concept is determined by the interest that dominates the concept-formation — that dominates the constitution of the consciousness of generality. Different interests yield different concepts of the same thing: the ordinary concept of a flower and the botanical concept involve different circles of optimal appearances and different criteria of complete givenness, though they intend the same object.

**The displacement from interest to essence.** Husserl then makes the move that gives §37 its deeper significance. He considers the view that seems to follow from the foregoing: that the maximum spheres do not pertain to the essence of the appearance *as such* but only to the interest founded therein and to its appurtenant intention. If we follow this through, no delimitation whatsoever lies in the appearances as such; their essence prescribes possibilities of unitary progression. Given any appearance, infinitely many possible series of appearance are open — series of appearances of one and the same thing. These infinitely many possible appearances are not unlimited in every respect: the unity of a possible perceptual synthesis prescribes a rule and law. But within this lawful framework, all the occurrences that pertain a priori to every appearance as a phase of synthesis remain operative: enrichment, re-determination, the taking on of new moments of indeterminateness, ever new possibilities of more precise determination.

This leads to a decisive formulation: with reference to this, it is already **evident** that we cannot speak of an adequate perception of a thing in the sense of an appearance which, as absolute givenness, would leave nothing more open — no possibilities of re-determination, enrichment, or more precise determination. The possibility is always open that the same thing might have new determinations not presented in the current appearance, or not presented in such a way that they could not, in new appearances, reveal a hitherto unsuspected richness of inner differences.

**Limitlessness even for the primal thing.** Husserl anticipates an objection: perhaps one could avoid the limitlessness of possible determinations by falling back on the "primal thing" (*Urding*) with its primal, materializing determinations — the core of spatial thinghood stripped of all appended properties. But even here, limitlessness rules. The crucial distinction is between the relations of fulfillment pertaining to the *intention* (a changing intention) holding sway in the appearances, and the intertwining of increases and decreases **founded in the essence of appearances themselves**. No possibility of absolute saturation is founded in the essence of appearances. Every saturation leaves open, ideally speaking, the possibility of further saturation.

**The limitlessness of approach.** Husserl then develops this through a specific and phenomenologically precise example: the process of **approach**. If we take extension as an indeterminate quality, a continuous increase of presentations is included phenomenologically under the title "approach." But approach has, lying in the very sense of perception, **no limit that would itself still be perception**. The sense of our perception of things includes the spatial position of the Object with reference to the Ego-center as the relational center of all spatial orientations. The approach is the approach of the Object toward this point of orientation, and in principle this can go on in indefinitum.

Husserl considers the objection: does not approach have a natural limit? Could it not go on until the distance is nil? And does not touch give us the thing at zero distance? His response is carefully differentiated. The comparison with the sense of touch does not hold, because tactile approach is not constituted in the same way as visual approach — the approaching finger does not itself appear tactually. The analogue would be grasping an object with outstretched hand and then bending the arm inward.

For the visual case, Husserl makes a crucial phenomenological observation: the immanent change of appearance that constitutes the approach, considered purely phenomenologically, has **no limit of increase**. By its very essence it can be increased to infinity. The change of the visual image does not approach a limit the way sound-intensities approach silence or luminosities approach black. The series of visual adumbrations corresponding to approach is essentially open-ended: there is no final adumbration that could not be surpassed by a still closer approach, a still finer differentiation.

**Significance.** §37 is a section where several threads of the argument converge. The connection between interest and concept-formation shows that the phenomenological analysis of thing-perception has direct implications for the theory of knowledge and the philosophy of science — the empirical concept is grounded in the interest-relative structure of perceptual fulfillment, not in some abstract conceptual operation detached from perception. The demonstration that limitlessness belongs not only to the interest-relative intention but to the **essence of appearances themselves** — that even stripping away all variable interests leaves an intrinsically open-ended manifold — closes off the last escape route for the ideal of adequate perception. And the analysis of approach as an essentially limitless process provides the specific phenomenological content for the general thesis: the visual field's capacity for adumbrational differentiation is in principle inexhaustible, and the constitution of the spatial thing through this differentiation is therefore in principle interminable.

Husserl's closing remark is characteristically measured: the fact that there exist a priori infinitely many possibilities of determining the thing is not something he would "care to designate as an infinite task" (contra the neo-Kantian formulation). Rather, it means that the absolute determination of any thing is not a reasonable goal; the determination of the thing must be guided and delimited by reasonably practical interests. This is not skepticism but phenomenological realism about the structure of perceptual constitution: the thing is given, and given in the flesh, but given in a mode that is constitutively open, never exhausted, always susceptible to further determination. Whether matters are different for the exact laws of nature in physics — where the interest is not in individual things but in universal lawfulness — Husserl leaves as a question for further investigation, pointing toward the analyses of the *thing of physics* versus the *thing of perception* that *Ideas II* will take up.

### **§38. Clarity and distinctness in the givenness of the perceived thing**

Section 38 — "Clarity and distinctness in the givenness of the perceived thing" — addresses what might seem like a last remaining challenge to the thesis of necessary inadequacy: the phenomenon of **distinctness** (*Deutlichkeit*) in the visual and tactile fields. The worry is that the gradation from indistinct to most-distinct vision might constitute an intrinsic, fixed limit of completeness in the appearance-manifold — a limit belonging to the essence of the appearances themselves rather than to any variable interest. If so, the most distinct vision would mark a privileged stratum of givenness that could serve as a foothold for the ideal of adequacy. Husserl's task in this section is to show that even this apparent limit does not restore the possibility of adequate perception.

**Multiple senses of "unclarity."** Husserl begins by distinguishing several senses of "unclarity" that have appeared in the foregoing analyses. Within the framework of these considerations, "unclear" can mean: (1) *improper appearance* as contrasted with clear, proper appearance — the back side versus the front side; (2) appearances that are *determinatively incomplete*, leaving open a more precise determination — this unclarity appears even within the sphere of proper appearance; (3) differences in the inner *richness* of the presentation due to factors like distance, position relative to the object, or physical circumstances (poor lighting, fog, night) — near versus far, favorable versus unfavorable viewing position. All of these have been treated in previous sections.

But there is a further, distinctive kind of clarity-difference that Husserl now isolates: the differences within the **visual field** between the sphere of **most distinct vision** (the foveal center) and the gradations of **ever lessening distinctness** toward the margin of the field. These are not differences in the object's illumination or in the viewing distance; they are structural features of the visual field itself. There are analogous differences in the field of touch — spheres of most distinct touch interspersed with spheres of indistinct touch.

**The apparent challenge.** Here, Husserl acknowledges, one could doubt whether distinctness constitutes a privilege that is **delimited in a fixed way** — and whether this fixed delimitation has "transcendental significance," insofar as it might pertain to the possibility of the Objectivation of space. If the Object is shifted into the sphere of most distinct vision, a modification is carried out that seemingly leaves open no further increase — and this not only empirically but *essentially*. Indistinctness, by contrast, can be conceived as stratified in indefinitum. This seems to provide a fixed, non-interest-relative limit of givenness: the most distinct visual or tactile presentation marks an absolute maximum that cannot be surpassed.

**The crucial distinction: distinctness versus presentational significance.** Husserl's response turns on a careful separation of the gradations of distinctness from other gradations that *do* have presentational significance for the object. He notes that the gradations of clarity in the visual field "lie on quite a different line" from the gradations of proper appearance discussed previously. The darkening of illumination has *Objective, physical* significance — it changes how the thing's color appears. The expansion or contraction of the visual image has *spatial* significance — it constitutes approaching and receding. But the modifications of clarity in the visual field (from foveal center to periphery) do *not* have this kind of presentational significance. If the visual field were clear everywhere, it seems this would change nothing as regards the *presentation* — it would concern only the possibility of quasi-movement within the field. The distinctness/indistinctness gradation is a structural feature of the field as a presentational medium, not a feature of the content presented through that medium.

**Why distinctness does not restore adequacy.** Even granting that every indistinct appearance bears a reference to the corresponding distinct appearances — that there is, apart from all pre-eminent interests, an originally distinguished givenness in the sphere of distinctness — the essence of the Object still implies that it comes to givenness in *every* distinct appearance and can dispense with *no* distinct appearance if it is to show itself from all sides and completely. The constitution of full givenness requires the completely ideal, **limitless nexus of appearances**. No single distinct appearance, and no finite circle of distinct appearances, can substitute for this limitless nexus.

In other words: even if we grant that most-distinct vision provides a fixed, non-relative maximum of presentational clarity *within a given appearance*, the multiplicity of appearances needed to give the thing from all sides remains infinite. Distinctness is a local property of individual presentations; it does not address the global problem of the inexhaustibility of the appearance-manifold. Each distinct appearance is still an adumbration — still one-sided, still leaving open the back, the interior, the infinitely many further determinations. The limit of distinctness is a limit within a single phase of the perceptual process, not a limit of the process itself.

**The neo-Kantian connection.** Husserl takes the opportunity to connect his results to the neo-Kantian thesis that the ultimate determination of the Object is an "infinite task" (*unendliche Aufgabe*). His analyses now allow us to see where the **phenomenological grounds** of that position reside. Every appearance includes, a priori, possibilities of re-determination and enrichment; an infinity of possible kinetic determinations arising from the sides of the Object (mastered in geometry); and the possibility of infinitely many appended determinations arising out of the nexus of experience through empirical motivation. However rich the determinations by which the Object is apprehended, every new constellation of experience introduces new motives that necessarily influence the apprehension of the thing. There exist a priori infinitely many, and ever new, possibilities of determining the thing.

Yet Husserl distances himself from the neo-Kantian formulation. He would not "care to designate" this as an infinite *task*, precisely because it is not a reasonable *goal* to absolutely determine any thing. The determination of things must be guided and delimited by reasonably practical interests. The phenomenological result is not a regulative ideal (in the Kantian sense) that we ought asymptotically to pursue; it is a structural fact about the constitution of thinghood that should inform our understanding of what rational cognition can and should aim at.

**The extension to all determinations.** In the closing passage, Husserl extends the result to cover every kind of determination without exception. For materializing determinations, the conclusion already followed from the preceding analyses; but additionally, the "thorough relativity of the sensuous qualities" — their dependence on the circumstances — introduces a further dimension of open-endedness. Appended qualities have their source in experiential motivation — the reciprocal dependence of objects on one another within the perceptual manifold — and the power of this motivation depends a priori on the number of cases in which the same object appeared under the same circumstances. From this results a limitless host of possibilities for annexing empirical determinations to the primarily materialized thing. This covers not only acoustic and other sensory determinations but all causal determinations, all determinations of acting and being acted upon, and everything physics will eventually determine as physicalistic properties.

**Significance.** §38 closes the last potential loophole in the argument against adequate perception. The distinctness-gradation in the visual field is the most plausible candidate for an intrinsic, non-relative limit of givenness. Husserl grants it a certain phenomenological privilege — the distinct presentation does enjoy a priority over the indistinct — but shows that this local privilege is powerless against the global, structural inadequacy of thing-perception. The section thereby completes the argumentative work of Chapter 6, and §39 will summarize the entire result: thing-perception is a process interminable in principle, and the thing can come to givenness only in an infinite progression of experience that is never closed, never finished, and never adequate.

### **§39. Summary. The perception of the thing as a process interminable in principle**

Section 39 — "Summary. The perception of the thing as a process interminable in principle" — is the concluding section of Chapter 6 and of the entire first major arc of the lectures (Section II, "Analysis of the Elements of Perceptual Correlation," and Section III, "Analysis of the Kinetic Synthesis of Perception"). It gathers the results of §§32–38 into a single, definitive formulation of the central thesis: thing-perception is an essentially **interminable** process that can never, even in principle, yield adequate givenness.

**The definitive thesis.** Husserl opens by restating the conclusion of the preceding lectures: the distinguishing characters of the maximum points and maximum regions do not pertain to the proper essence of appearances as such but to the interest that dominates in them. In the appearances as such there resides no termination in limits that can count as limits of complete givenness. No individual appearance and no group of appearances from a synthesis can bring the thing to adequate givenness as it is and according to everything that pertains to it. Only the **entire synthesis** could accomplish this. And this synthesis is an infinite, never closed, never finished synthesis, because in principle there always exist possibilities of presentations that are more precise, more complete, and richer in content.

**The argument from continuity and finitude.** Husserl then restates the specific phenomenological grounds, now with maximum concision. Even simulating an absolutely unchanged, motionless thing, in virtue of the **continuity** of the geometrical corporeal thing and the **finitude** of the moments that can be distinguished in any given appearance, we are relegated to an infinite progression. The appearance can, in accord with its essence, undergo constant modification in infinitum with respect to its quasi-extension: approach, understood as a peculiar modification of the expanse of the visual appearance, can ensue in infinitum, progressively offering an increase in distinctions for the appearing Object — the possibility of grasping qualitative differences and introducing partitions into the appearing surface.

**The ineliminability of re-determination.** Moreover, no pregiven apprehension can remove the possibility that the progress of actual perception will involve new determinations and re-determinations, that expectations will be disappointed, and that inhomogeneity will be attributed to what was apprehended as homogeneous. Even if we simulate an absolutely *known* thing, that in terms of which it would be absolutely known is never and nowhere given conclusively. It would have to demonstrate itself first and manifest itself as a givenness in the progression of the continuity of perception — and this givenness is never a closed and ultimate givenness. The progression of perception *first decides* about the delimitation or alteration of the apprehension, about the validity of the belief-intention that pertains to the appearance and supplements it to form a full perception.

This is one of Husserl's most powerful formulations of the fallibilism inherent in perceptual constitution. Even the "known" thing is not given in advance of the perceptual process; its knowness must *demonstrate itself* through the process, and this self-demonstration is always provisional, always liable to revision.

**The formula: expansion and partitioning in infinitum.** The spatial thing, and already every piece, no matter how small, of the surface of the thing, can find their full givenness only in the **infinite process** of demonstrating themselves through **expansion and partitioning** under the continuous and constant positing of the unity of the unextended with the extended. The same applies to the materialization of the expanse — and in the same process. This formulation captures both dimensions of the infinity involved: the extensive infinity (ever more of the thing to explore — expansion) and the intensive infinity (ever finer distinctions within any given region — partitioning). Both run in infinitum, and both are constitutively necessary for the givenness of the thing.

**The fiction of unchangedness.** Husserl adds a final, striking deepening: even the assumption that the thing is absolutely unchanged is itself a fiction. The thing is not pregiven to us with the series of appearances coming subsequently. Rather, the thing that initially appears unchanged *first demonstrates itself as such* in the appurtenant constitution of the perceptual manifold — and this self-demonstration is nothing ultimate, nothing that could be given adequately at one stroke. It is given only relatively and with reservations. In principle it is always possible that the apprehension of unchange, no matter how much corroborated in continued confirmation, will give way to the apprehension of change as a consequence of new motives in the appearances. And naturally the reverse holds as well.

This is a remarkable passage. It means that the entire analysis of §§26–38, which proceeded under the methodological simplification of an "unchanged thing," was itself operating with an idealization whose validity cannot be secured in advance but is itself constituted — and constituted fallibly — within the perceptual process. The unchangedness of the thing is not a premise of perceptual constitution but a *result* of it, and a result that is always revisable.

**Distinctness and its limits.** Husserl briefly addresses the concern left over from §38 — the gradations of distinctness in the visual field from margin to center. Even granting that indistinct appearance bears a general reference to distinct appearance, the distinctness of the presentation never signifies absolute givenness. In the privileged sphere of givenness we stand *a fortiori* in the infinite stream of relativities founded in the essence of appearances. The moments of appearance cannot be articulated a priori into ones that signify absolute givenness and others that do not. Instead, the essence of thinghood, with respect to all its moments, includes a **stream of limitless continuity**, a limitless realm of open possibilities *a parte ante*, which *a parte post* can ever be more precisely determined, delimited, and enriched, but which always have to await infinity.

**Extension to all determinations.** This holds for determinations of every kind and class: materializing determinations (as already shown), the "sensuous qualities" with their thorough relativity depending on circumstances, and the appended qualities whose source lies in experiential motivation — the reciprocal dependence of objects on one another within the perceptual manifold. From this results a limitless host of possibilities for the annexation of empirical determinations: acoustic, sensory, causal, and everything physics will eventually determine as physicalistic properties.

**The epistemological consequence.** Husserl closes with a brief but important reflection on the consequences for cognition. Things are never given conclusively and never can be. They come to givenness only in an infinite progression of experience. Does knowledge then become an aimless undertaking? Are we to be satisfied with the "infinite task"? Who would reasonably set a task whose solution, as a matter of essence, can never be achieved?

Husserl's answer is measured and precise: if the task lies in the production of absolutely complete givenness, it is a priori unsolvable — an unreasonably posited task. What follows is that the knowledge of reality **cannot have this ideal**, insofar as we may have confidence that knowledge accomplishes something actually rational by positing rational goals. On this path lie great problems, Husserl says — but it cannot be his duty to enter into them here.

**Significance for the lectures as a whole.** §39 closes the first great movement of the lectures — the analysis of thing-perception in its static and kinetic structures — with a thesis that has become one of the cornerstones of phenomenology: the **essential inadequacy** and **interminability** of thing-perception. This thesis is not a skeptical result; it is a positive characterization of the mode of being of spatial things as constituted in perception. The thing *is* the identical pole of an infinite, never-completed manifold of appearances. Its transcendence with respect to any individual perception or any finite series of perceptions is not a deficiency to be overcome but the constitutive condition of its being a spatial thing at all.

The lectures now turn — through the recapitulatory Chapter 7 (§§40–43) — to Sections IV and V, where Husserl will investigate the *positive* constitutive structures that make this inadequate, interminable, perspectival givenness possible: the kinaesthetic systems, the correlation between field-transformations and bodily movement, and the progressive constitution of three-dimensional spatial corporeality. The negative result of §39 — that adequate perception is impossible — defines the space within which the positive constitutive analysis must operate: the task is not to explain how perception could achieve adequacy but to understand how, through the systematic, kinaesthetically motivated ordering of essentially inadequate appearances, a spatial thing can be given at all.

## 

## Chapter 7: Recapitulation. The Analyses of Perception in the Framework of the Phenomenological Reduction

### **§40. The sense of the phenomenological analyses of perception**

Section 40 — "The sense of the phenomenological analyses of perception" — opens Chapter 7, a recapitulatory chapter that serves as a hinge between the analyses of thing-perception carried out in Sections II–III (§§14–39) and the kinaesthetic analyses of Sections IV–V that are about to begin. §40 provides a comprehensive methodological self-reflection: it restates the phenomenological stance of the entire investigation, rejects the traditional epistemological framing of the problem of perception, and then offers a compact summary of all the main results achieved so far.

**The rejection of the traditional problematic.** Husserl begins by sharply demarcating the phenomenological approach to perception from three traditional alternatives that he considers "perversely posed":

First, the Kantian question of 1772: what ground supports the relation of what we call "representation" to an object existing in itself? Second, the causal-physiological account: things stimulate sense organs, producing sensations and representations in the soul, from which we must somehow "conclude back" to external causes. Third, the empiricist-inferential account: all that is given are subjective lived experiences (perceptions); since these are not themselves the things, inferences must be formulated to justify assuming things exist outside. Husserl will have nothing to do with any of these questions.

**The phenomenological reduction restated.** What is at issue is not the things-in-themselves of metaphysics, not the things of physics, not the realities of psychology (souls, persons, dispositions), and not the things of ordinary life. The analysis "prescinds" from all of these — that is, it refrains from any judgment about real existence. The world of the investigation is the world of absolute givens, of absolute indubitabilities, of "phenomena" and "essences" — everything that is unaffected by positing real existence or non-existence. No proposition of science, no opinion of ordinary life, no perception or memory or anticipation is taken as a premise or ground of knowledge in its natural-attitude sense. After the phenomenological reduction, all of these come back into consideration: the proposition as a phenomenon, perception as a phenomenon, etc. What concerns us is the **essence** of perception, judgment, evidence — not their factual existence as psychological events.

Husserl identifies the enterprise as **onto-phenomenology at its lowest level**: the phenomenological investigation of the most basic stratum of the constitution of objects in experience.

**The fundamental question.** Things appear — in perceptions, memories, images, phantasies. Under phenomenological reduction and eidetic consideration, the question is: what resides in the **essence** of experience, in its originary sense? The essence of the totality of absolute phenomenological givens is relatedness to an experienced object. Perception is perception *of* a perceived; its essence is to bring an object to appearance and to posit what appears as something believed. How are we to understand this intentionality?

Husserl formulates the key principle: what resides essentially in experience can be infringed by no theoretical assumption and violated by no putative self-evidence of empirical psychology or metaphysical judgment, for every such violation is counter-sense. The sense residing in experience must supply the **ultimate norm** for measuring the legitimate sense of all interpretation of real Being. The conditions of the "possibility of experience" signify nothing other than all that resides immanently in the essence of experience. Everything established about the essence of experience is eo ipso a condition of the possibility of experience. To expect of experience something contradicting its essence is absurd.

This is an extraordinarily dense passage in which the methodological foundations of the lectures — and indeed of phenomenology as a whole — are compressed into a few sentences. The claim is that the eidetic analysis of perception has legislative authority over every other discipline's claims about what perception is and what its objects can be. Physics, psychology, and metaphysics cannot tell phenomenology what perception "really" is; rather, phenomenology establishes the essential structures that any legitimate account of perception must respect.

**The summary of results.** Husserl then provides a synoptic recapitulation of all the main distinctions and findings:

The investigation began with the evidence that a perception of a thing intends a spatial Object whose sense includes determinate determinations, and that what is so intended is nothing really (*reell*) immanent in perception. The question was: in which moments is this intentionality constituted? How does "self-givenness" show itself through an analysis of the essence of perception?

Step by step, the following was established: In perception, the perceived object stands there as believed and in the flesh. In **uni-fold** perception (a single static perception, as opposed to a continuous manifold), the object is given from only "one side"; in **mani-fold** perception, from many sides. In every uni-fold perception, a distinction holds between **presentational contents** and the **moment of apprehension**, on which are founded, in a changing way, the intentions and act-characters of a higher stratum (including belief).

The higher characters (belief, doubt, abstention, etc.) were set aside, and **appearance** was given prominence as something constant within the changing higher characters — present whether attention is directed to the object or elsewhere, whether the object counts as actually existing, as hallucinatory, as doubtful, or as something whose existence is prescinded from.

Appearance was designated as the **unification of presentational contents with apprehension** — not a summation or two things bound together, but a most intimate unity characterized as **animation** (*Beseelung*). The presentational contents do not exist for themselves with the apprehension then fitted into them; rather, the apprehension gives them animating sense, and the contents stand there in the apprehension purely and simply. The making-prominent of this distinction arises from the juxtaposition of different perceptions of the same object with different presentational contents, or from the change of intention while presentational contents remain identical (as in conflict between interpenetrating and cancelling appearances).
