In connection with this, **proper and improper appearance** were distinguished: uni-fold perception lets the thing appear from only one "side" and yet intends the thing as more than its side. Apprehension reaches further than presentation: even this property of reaching further is not something affixed as a piece to the proper apprehension but something made prominent only through evident identification and differentiation within the juxtaposition of different appearances. The intention aiming at individual objects includes a relation to the non-presented sides. This signifies that the intention has an **essential character** making it apt for identifications — the evidence that the same thing, presented from this side here, is also intended in the other perception that presents not this side but that one.

**Significance.** §40 is not merely a summary; it performs an important philosophical function within the architecture of the lectures. By restating the phenomenological reduction and the eidetic method at this juncture — just before the kinaesthetic analyses begin — Husserl reminds his audience that the analyses to follow (of bodily movement, kinaesthetic sensation, the Ego-Body, the visual and tactile fields) are not exercises in empirical psychology or physiology. They are eidetic investigations into the essential structures of perception, conducted under the same phenomenological reduction that has governed everything from §14 onward. The kinaesthetic body whose constitutive role Sections IV–V will analyze is not the body of physiology but the phenomenological body — the body as it figures essentially in the constitution of spatial thing-perception. The recapitulation ensures that this methodological continuity is not lost as the investigation moves from the relatively abstract analysis of appearance-structures to the more concrete analysis of bodily movement and sensory correlation.

### **§41. Attitude toward the thing and attitude toward the appearance (the given side) of the thing**

Section 41 — "Attitude toward the thing and attitude toward the appearance (the given side) of the thing" — is a philosophically rich and subtle section that takes up a specific objection (from a "Mr. Hofmann") to the entire framework of the lectures, and in responding to it, provides Husserl's most careful analysis in these lectures of the relationship between the **thing**, the **appearance**, and the **side** — three concepts that have been operative since §16 but whose interrelation now receives its most precise articulation. The section also develops the distinction between two fundamentally different **attitudes** or directions of attention: toward the thing and toward its appearance.

**The objection.** Hofmann objects that he cannot make the *quale* of the phenomenological datum become an objective perception, and that if he perceives a house, there is not, juxtaposed to the physical thing, still another psychic or phenomenological thing called "perception." Husserl responds that the objection rests on a misunderstanding. Naturally there is only one thing in the phenomenological sense, only one absolute givenness — and yet two evidently different **directions of judgment**: one concerned with the appearing object, the other with the appearance. We distinguish appearances numerically and according to content, and we say they are all appearances of the *same* thing. We have phenomenological evidence for this within a full epoché of thingly existence.

Husserl illustrates with the whistling sound of a steam pipe. As I approach, I say it is the same, continuously homogeneous whistling sound — that is the *object*. But if I attend to what is given phenomenologically, I find for every temporal fragment an essentially different appearance; the quale is not the same. Appearance and what appears are thus separated, and in the appearance I find the sensuous quale, the pre-empirical sensuous datum.

**Why appearance and thing seem to collapse.** Husserl diagnoses what leads to the temptation to identify appearance and thing: in the transition from considering the appearance to considering the appearing thing, the appearance does not separate itself from the thing as a second juxtaposed thing. Rather, it stands there as a "matter of the thing," as befalling the thing. The thing stands there only *in* and *with* the appearance. Conjoined to this is the **Objectivation** the appearance acquires as "a side of the thing."

**The Objectivity of the side.** Husserl develops this through the example of a green meadow on a hill covered with yellow blossoms. From every viewpoint it offers a different "appearance" — it is always the same meadow, but in a "different side." The "side" means here the appearance taken not as a subjective lived experience but as something belonging to the thing itself — an Objective determination insofar as it is a mode of the thing's self-presentation.

The meadow's coloration, for instance, cannot be a mere pre-empirical datum — it is the Objective coloration of the meadow. But which coloration? In every appearance it looks different; the pre-empirical color datum varies continuously. What is invariant — what is identical across the changing appearances — is the *Objective* coloration as the thing's own determination. But this identical Objective determination is given only *through* the varying appearances, never outside them.

**Two attitudes: thing-directed and appearance-directed.** Husserl then articulates the fundamental distinction between two attitudes. In ordinary perception, we are turned toward the *thing* — the meadow, the hill. The animated sensuous datum is lived through but is not the Object; it is not what we focus on or attend to. In the lived experience of "attending to the object," nothing else is present than the animated datum intersaturated with the intention of attention, and this intersaturation constitutes what we call attending to the object of the interpretation. This is the foundation for objective description and for the identifications in which the object manifests itself as the same.

On the other hand, if we attend to the *appearance*, a new position-taking is carried out. The appearance — the adumbration — becomes the thematic about-which. A different Object is present depending on whether we intend the meadow or the meadow-adumbration. Yet the appearing-as-such (*das Phansische*) of the "side" is, in every case, a lived experience. It pertains to the essence of the state of affairs that either position-taking is possible, and that a transition from one to the other is always available.

**The side as both subjective and Objective.** If we are in the attitude of focusing on the "side," then the side is itself nothing other than the proper appearance of the thing, which expands into the full appearance (including improper components), since proper appearance is not something autonomous. If the side is at all experienced, the thing appears as self-presentified — for the lived experience of the "presence of the thing itself" *is* nothing other than the lived experience of the "side," of the sensuous data extended in such and such a way, animated with such and such an interpretation, interwoven with the surplus of improper intentions.

When we turn from the side to the thing and back, the side *belongs to* the thing, and the thing *has* the side; the thing presents itself *in* the side. This is the relation of presentation to presented, and the interpenetration of the two lived experiences makes the relation appear especially intimate. While focusing on the side, I can express what it presents, how it allows the thing to appear, what the thing is according to the sense of the side.

The thing, however, has its environing things, which include the empirical Ego with its incarnation. The co-appearing relation between thing and Ego includes the Ego's spatial relation to the thing, and it pertains to this relation that the thing comes to presentation precisely in *this* side. The side therefore possesses a relation not only to the thing but also to the empirical Ego and to the relation between the two. The side is thus something **subjective** — "my perceptual appearance," belonging to me insofar as I occupy this or that position relative to the thing. And the side is also something **Objective** — it belongs to the thing, and in it the thing comes to givenness as "itself there."

**Not two givens but two attitudes.** Husserl's final clarification addresses the apparent paradox directly. If I turn toward the thing and then to the appearance, I do not find two givens — the thing and the appearance — as separate entities. Phenomenologically, in reflection, I find only the appearance and a different position-taking of attention. I have merely a somewhat changed fabric in which the appearance constitutes the predominant main core. But *objectively*, I have two givens, since the object "meadow" is different from the object "meadow-adumbration." We are inclined, following natural judgment, to say "here is the thing," and inclined also, attending to the appearance, to say "the appearance is there, but only in it is the thing given." The thing is not otherwise to be found; to have the thing and to have the sides of the thing in view are one and the same. We do not have two givennesses.

What we do have are various givens in this sense: always the appearance of the thing, but sometimes the appearance is **Objectivated** — itself made into the thematic about-which — and sometimes it is **Objectivating and foundational** for attention directed toward the thing. The thing not only appears but is the thematic about-which. There exists a relation among thing, appearance, and Ego — the full relational complex expounded in the analysis.

**Significance.** §41 provides the most philosophically explicit account in these lectures of what later phenomenology will develop as the **noetic-noematic correlation** and the distinction between **straightforward** and **reflective** attitudes. The analysis shows that the appearance (the side, the adumbration) has a peculiar double status: it is a subjective lived experience (belonging to the perceiver in their specific spatial relation to the thing) and simultaneously an Objective determination (belonging to the thing as a mode of its self-presentation). This double status is not a contradiction but the essential structure of intentionality itself: the thing is given *only through* appearances that are *both* subjective (mine, from here, under these conditions) and Objective (of the thing, showing how it is). The kinaesthetic analyses that follow will make this double status concrete by showing how the subjective side — the bodily kinaesthetic situation of the perceiver — is systematically correlated with the Objective side — the appearance-manifold in which the thing constitutes itself.

### **§42. Changed and unchanged perceptions**

Section 42 — "Changed and unchanged perceptions" — is a brief, densely compressed recapitulation of the entire kinetic analysis of §§26–39. Husserl explicitly notes that he does not want to continue the recapitulation in full detail but only recall as much as can be fruitfully linked up to the analyses about to begin. The section distills the results of Chapters 5 and 6 into a compact summary.

**Unchanged and changed perceptions.** Husserl restates the fundamental distinction. Unchanged perceptions — those unchanged throughout their phenomenological duration — present objects as unchanged, but they always present them from one and the same side; they are one-sided and **uni-fold**. Changed perceptions can present either changed or unchanged objects; they are in general many-sided and in any event **manifold**, insofar as the appearance is a continuum of appearance, possibly interspersed with spans of unchanged appearance. To each phase of this continuum there corresponds, through mere temporal extension, a possible unchanged appearance. The continua of appearances are therefore continuously manifold.

**The taxonomy of changes recalled.** Within the manifold perceptions, Husserl recalls the distinction among the various cases of change in relation to the appearing object. The **purely kinetic** changes stood out: the appearing Object is in itself unchanged in all its constitutive determinations — its spatial corporeality remains unchanged in all inner geometrical determinations and equally in its qualities, above all in the primarily materializing qualities. What changes is external: the thing appears as moving or resting while the perceiving Ego or its body moves — the eye, the head, or the whole Body — and the relations between thing and Body change. To these changing relations there correspond ever different modes of appearance.

**The kinetic synthesis and its general features.** The continuously manifold perceptions — these "remarkably pre-empirical temporal courses" — were studied according to their most general peculiarities: the **identity of objective direction** founded in the essence of the phases of appearance yoked together in continuity; the distinction between **harmony of sense** and **identity of sense**; the occurrences of **indeterminateness** with appurtenant more precise determinability, **enrichment**, and **re-determination**; and the talk of **fulfillment** in its various senses.

**Maximum spheres and the false ideal.** The summary then turns to the discovery that certain appearances in the continuous synthesis are pre-eminent — maximum spheres in which a privileged consciousness of givenness exists, claiming to produce more complete givenness than the non-privileged phases. This led to the general considerations of Chapter 6: whether the sense of this completeness includes the ideal of immanent, absolute givenness, or whether such givenness is excluded by the essence of perceptual givenness. Husserl states the conclusion with lapidary finality: "full givenness, as we see, involves the whole synthesis in its infinity. Thus the ideal was a false ideal."

**Significance.** §42 serves a purely architectonic function. It compresses the entire trajectory of §§26–39 into a single page, ensuring that the essential results are fresh in the reader's (or listener's) mind as the investigation is about to take a decisive new turn. The compression itself is instructive: it reveals what Husserl regards as the truly essential thread running through the kinetic analysis — the progression from unchanged/changed perception, through the kinetic case as the fundamental case, through the structures of identity, harmony, determination, and fulfillment, to the question of adequacy and its negative resolution.

The final sentence — "the ideal was a false ideal" — is deliberately stark. It marks the closure of one entire problematic (can perception achieve adequate givenness of things?) and the opening of the next (given that it cannot, how does perception nevertheless constitute things through the systematic ordering of inadequate appearances?). §43 will address one remaining unfinished piece of business — the role of belief-positing — before the lectures move on to the kinaesthetic analyses of Section IV.

### **§43. The role of belief-positing in the kinetic synthesis of perception**

Section 43 — "The role of belief-positing in the kinetic synthesis of perception" — is the final section of Chapter 7 (the recapitulatory chapter) and of Section III as a whole. It addresses the one major structural moment of perception that the entire preceding analysis deliberately set aside: **belief-positing** (*Glaubenssetzung*) and its modifications. The section serves a double function: it acknowledges the significance of belief for the constitution of things, and it explains why the analyses nonetheless proceeded legitimately without thematizing it, while pointing forward to where the investigation must now go.

**The two outstanding tasks.** Husserl begins by noting that the general considerations of §§32–39 did not close the analysis of the kinetic synthesis. Two things remain. First, the moment of belief-positing and its coordinated modifications have not been taken into account. Second, much more penetrating individual analyses of the appearances entering into the synthesis are needed — analyses that will clarify the constitution of the unchanged object in the manifold of changing appearances, and the phenomenological differences between the motion of the Object and the motion of the perceiving subject. The second task is what Sections IV and V will take up. §43 addresses the first.

**Belief as a unifying moment.** Husserl states that belief and its coordinated moments are not insignificant annexes but **essential, unifying moments** of perception. In perception in the strict sense, what appears stands there as a being — it counts as actual. Yet the essential core of the phenomenon, the appearance, can be preserved even when belief is lacking. Belief may pass into doubt, disbelief, presumption, prescinding from belief, mere inclination toward belief, and the like. The question of which of these are actually primitive and which are complex and founded is left open, though Husserl signals its importance.

**The role of belief in the continuous synthesis.** Husserl then connects belief to the kinetic synthesis specifically. The synthesis of perception is not merely a synthesis of appearances; it is also a synthesis of *belief*. Each phase bears its belief-character, and the unity of the perceptual process includes the unity of the belief-positing that runs through it. Within a harmonious course of perception, belief is continuously confirmed — each new appearance corroborates what was anticipated, and the belief that the thing is there, unchanged, identical, gains reinforcement. But when conflict arises — when the new appearance contradicts the earlier apprehension — the belief-positing is disturbed. Doubt may supervene, or the belief may be "crossed out" and replaced by a new one (re-determination).

**Experience versus mere continuity of appearances.** This leads to a distinction that Husserl regards as fundamental: **unity of experience** (*Einheit der Erfahrung*) is not merely a continuity of appearances. It *presupposes* that continuity but is something more. The thing is "not a mere unity of 'representation' but a unity of 'judgment.'" Appearances can be addressed individually or in part as appearances of things — and this happens only with reference to a phantasy-synthesis borne by phantasy-belief — but a thing will never be *constituted* without maintaining a thorough unity of possible experience. To bring a thing even in phantasy to constitution requires a unity of experience constituted as possible in phantasy.

This is a crucial clarification. Throughout the lectures, Husserl has been analyzing the appearance-manifold — the continuum of presentations, adumbrations, proper and improper appearances, the kinetic synthesis. But this entire analysis concerns only one stratum: the stratum of appearance as such, abstracted from the doxic characters (belief, doubt, etc.) that convert appearance into full perception. §43 acknowledges that the appearance-stratum is *necessary but not sufficient* for thing-constitution. The thing as an object of experience — as something posited as actual, as existing — requires the unifying function of belief running through the appearance-manifold and binding it into a coherent experiential unity.

**The limits of the present analysis.** Husserl is frank about the scope of what he is indicating. These considerations point toward "a line of fundamental investigations" that do not lie in the direction the lectures are now following. What is needed is merely to stress the unity of judgment that produces experience, so that the analyses of appearance will be understood in the correct sense and within appropriate limits. The lectures have been, and will continue to be, analyses of the *appearance-stratum* — the stratum that lies at the foundation of full perception and that can be studied in its own essential structures independently of the doxic characters built upon it. But this foundational stratum is not the whole of perception, and §43 ensures that this is explicitly acknowledged before the investigation moves on.

**Significance for the architecture of the lectures.** §43 closes out the recapitulatory Chapter 7 and with it the entire first major movement of the lectures (Sections II–III). The lectures now stand at the threshold of their second major movement: the kinaesthetic analyses of Sections IV and V. Everything from §14 through §43 has analyzed the appearance-manifold from, as it were, the *inside* — its internal structures of proper and improper givenness, its temporal and spatial extension, its kinetic synthesis, its degrees of saturation, its essential inadequacy. What has been left almost entirely implicit is the *motivation* of the appearance-manifold: what governs the order and succession of appearances, what makes the manifold be a systematically ordered totality rather than a random flux. The answer, as §44 will begin to develop, lies in the **kinaesthetic systems** — the bodily movements of the perceiving Ego that are functionally correlated with determinate series of appearance-changes. §43's acknowledgment of the belief-stratum effectively brackets one dimension of perception (the doxic) that will not be thematized further, while §44 opens the dimension (the kinaesthetic) that will be the exclusive focus of the remaining lectures.

The distinction between the unity of appearance and the unity of experience/judgment also anticipates a major architectonic feature of *Ideas I*, where the noematic stratum (roughly corresponding to the appearance-manifold analyzed here) is carefully distinguished from the noetic stratum (including the doxic theses) and from the fully constituted intentional object. §43 shows this distinction already at work in the 1907 lectures, even if the terminology is not yet stabilized.

# 

# SECTION IV: THE SIGNIFICANCE OF THE KINAESTHETIC SYSTEMS FOR THE CONSTITUTION OF THE PERCEIVED OBJECT

## Chapter 8: The Phenomenological Concept of Kinaesthesis

### **§44. Preliminary indication of the further themes of the investigation**

Section 44 — "Preliminary indication of the further themes of the investigation" — opens Chapter 8 ("The Phenomenological Concept of Kinaesthesis") and with it Section IV of the lectures, which marks the decisive turn toward the positive constitutive analysis of spatial thinghood. Where the preceding sections analyzed the *what* — the structures of the appearance-manifold and its essential inadequacy — §44 poses the *how*: how is three-dimensional spatiality actually constituted in the manifold of appearances? The section frames the guiding questions, identifies the fundamental difficulty, and sounds a note of philosophical modesty about the depth of the problems ahead.

**The task stated.** The task is to carry on the analyses of appearance, specifically the case of mere kinetic change — and to penetrate as deeply as possible into what Husserl calls the phenomenological "creation" (*Schöpfung*) of three-dimensional spatiality, the constitution of the identical corporeal thing in the manifold of its appearances. These appearances are **appearances of movement**: all spatiality is constituted — comes to givenness — in movement, both the movement of the Object and the movement of the Ego, along with the change in orientation given thereby.

Husserl acknowledges that spatiality already appears in individual, uni-fold perception — the thing is given in its corporeal space and its location relative to other things. Through a number of uni-fold perceptions, a body may appear from various sides in a unifying synthesis of identification. But in this way it does not attain **full and self-demonstrable givenness**.

**The insufficiency of discrete perceptions.** Husserl makes a crucial point that directly continues the argument of §43 about the unity of experience. In isolated, discretely given uni-fold perceptions, there does not reside enough to allow the actual identity of the appearing object to demonstrate itself. If I have a perception of the front of a familiar thing, and then a discrete second perception of the back side, I may synthetically unite them through identity of sense. But is this identity actually *given*? It is possible that the second perception relates to a *second object* entirely similar to the first — I see the first from the front and the second from the back. We would then have **similarity of sense** but not identity of sense, **similarity of objective direction** but not identity as a direction toward one and the same object.

This is a deceptively simple observation with profound consequences. It means that the identity of the thing across different perspectives cannot be established through any comparison of discrete perceptions, no matter how similar their content. Identity is demonstrated only in **continuous transition** — only if a continuous passage from the one perception to the other is guaranteed in the unity of experience. The unity of the object demonstrates itself only in the unity of the synthesis continually joining the manifold perceptions. This continuous synthesis must lie at the foundation for the logical synthesis of identification to produce the evident givenness of identity. The perceptions must be integrated as phases into the continuous synthesis, and we see this precisely only if we carry out the synthesis.

**The general principle and its application.** This holds generally and means, in the present case, that an identical and unchanged spatial body demonstrates itself as such only in a **kinetic series of perceptions** that continually brings to appearance the various sides of the thing. The body must rotate or be displaced, or I must move — my eyes, my Body — to see it from all around, approaching and receding. Or both I and the thing must move. That is the situation expressed from the standpoint of the appearing thing.

**The constitutive questions.** Husserl then poses the central questions for the remainder of the lectures: How do the appurtenant phenomenological nexuses look? What constitutes three-dimensional spatiality in the manifold of appearances? What constitutes the corporeal thing in its identity? What constitutes its manifoldly possible movement and its position in relation to the Ego-center?

Since spatiality is **doubly constituted** — once with visual and once with tactile determinations (as §22 established) — the questions can be separated: how is visual space constituted, and how is tactile space constituted, insofar as they are constituted independently of one another? And what constitutes the **identity of space**, which is materialized visually on the one hand and tactually on the other, yet in this double materialization is one identical space?

**Philosophical modesty.** Husserl concludes with a striking confession of modesty: "It is easier to pose these questions than to answer them. It would be the height of presumption on my part to claim that the following remarks are going to provide the answers." The analyses are "first beginnings" that can only show the problems are graspable and actually solvable, though they belong "among the most difficult ones in the domain of human cognition." This is not false humility; the constitutive analysis of three-dimensional spatiality from the correlation of kinaesthetic movements with appearance-manifolds is genuinely one of the hardest problems in phenomenology, and Husserl's 1907 treatment, for all its brilliance, is indeed a first approach that his later work (particularly *Ideas II* and the analyses of the 1920s–30s) will substantially develop.

**Significance.** §44 establishes the fundamental insight that governs everything to come: **identity requires continuity**. The identity of the spatial thing cannot be given through any collection of discrete perspectives, however numerous; it requires the continuous kinetic synthesis in which the thing demonstrates itself as the same through uninterrupted transitions from side to side, from near to far, from this orientation to that. This means that the constitution of the thing is inseparable from *movement* — the movement of the Ego-Body or of the thing or both — and that the analysis of spatial constitution must therefore be an analysis of the kinaesthetic conditions of continuous perceptual synthesis. The specific problems §44 identifies — the constitution of visual space, of tactile space, and of their identity — will be taken up systematically in the remaining chapters: the visual/oculomotor constitution in Chapters 9–10, the amplification beyond the oculomotor field in Chapters 11–12, and the transition to objective three-dimensional space in Chapters 13–14.

A footnote deserves mention: Husserl distinguishes *thinking* about a thing (I know in thought that I move with the earth through the space of the universe) from *perceiving* it (I do not "see" this movement). The distinction between what can be *thought* and what can be *given in perception* is precisely the distinction the remaining lectures will analyze — the conditions under which spatial facts can come to perceptual, and not merely intellectual, givenness.

### **§45. Rest and movement of the unchanged thing, in relation to the rest and movement of the perceiving Ego**

Section 45 — "Rest and movement of the unchanged thing, in relation to the rest and movement of the perceiving Ego" — begins the concrete phenomenological work of Section IV by laying out a systematic inventory of the basic cases that the kinaesthetic analysis must address. It cross-classifies the kinetic states of the thing (rest/movement) with the kinetic states of the perceiving Ego (immobility/self-movement), producing a structured overview of all the fundamental combinations and their phenomenological correlates.

**The restriction.** Husserl restates the specific restriction governing the analysis: we are concerned with the thing that is the same and unchanged in the manifold of kinetic appearances. The identity of the thing is preserved across various series of changes. Objectively, the change is merely kinetic — the entire constitutive fund of the thing (its geometrical body and the matter filling it) remains unchanged. The thing "merely" moves. The thing as identical through stereometric changes (changes of form) and material changes will be treated later; the division is a necessary one residing in the nature of the subject matter.

**The systematic cross-classification.** The unchanged thing can be at rest or in motion. Husserl introduces a two-level classification based on the perceiving Ego's kinetic state:

**Case I: The Ego persists in complete immobility.** I keep my eye, head, and entire Body still (insofar as this is significant for kinetic appearance). Within this, two sub-cases:

*Sub-case I.1: The Objective field is at rest* (in terms of appearance). Then the total phenomenon must remain unchanged — the presentational contents, the entire fund of sensations in service to the apprehension of the field of Objects, remain constant. As soon as the visual field undergoes noticeable change, movement or some other change necessarily appears. This is the limit-case of unchanged perception from §§14–25.

*Sub-case I.2: Movement appears while the Ego remains still.* The presentational visual contents in the visual field change in a manner that "remains to be investigated." Various possibilities arise within the field of Objects: individual Objects can appear at rest while others are in motion, or all can be moving. An Object appearing stationary amid moving Objects must have its presentational contents remain unchanged as long as it stays in the Objective field; if it is hidden (partially or completely), it steps out of the field. Moving Objects, conversely, require a change in presentational contents for their movement to appear.

Husserl adds an important qualification: Objective motion can take place while the content of appearances remains completely unchanged — for instance, if I move through Objective space along with my entire perceived environment. But this is *Objective* movement, not *appearance* of movement. The analysis concerns the appearance of movement, which requires change in the visual field.

**Case II: The Ego moves.** Instead of keeping the Body still, I move it. In every case, this yields phenomenological changes already by the fact that **sensations of movement** flow on in determinate series. But various systems of series of *visual* changes are joined to these movement-sensations.

Here Husserl introduces a key structural observation: the Ego-movements relevant for the appearances of things divide into **several independent systems of movement**. The eyes can move, the head can move, the trunk can move, etc. Each constitutes an independent kinetic system, and each is correlated with its own series of visual changes.

**Self-movement versus being-moved.** Within Case II, Husserl draws a fundamental distinction between **self-movement** (*Selbstbewegung*) and **being-moved** (*Bewegtwerden*). Being-moved is an Objective fact: if I am unaware of it, and above all if it does not at all *appear* to me, then it changes nothing in the world of appearing Objects. What matters is self-movement, and self-movement manifests itself in **kinaesthetic sensations**. This is the first explicit introduction of the concept that will dominate the remaining lectures.

**Eye-movement as the exemplary case.** Husserl selects eye-movements as the case to focus on, since they form a system in themselves — the rest of the body may be completely still while the eyes move. In the case of eye-movement, a thing can appear stationary while the phenomena change continuously and manifoldly. A certain **pre-eminent continuity of change** in the presentational visual contents belongs to the very self-sameness of the unchanged thing. The series of changes is again a determinate one, but is quite different when the Object appears to be in motion rather than at rest. The question then arises: how are rest and movement constituted in this "confusing profusion of appearances"?

Husserl poses the culminating question: rest in a materially and stereometrically unchanged thing signifies complete changelessness. How do manifoldness and continuously changing appearances succeed in constituting the consciousness of an *identical* body?

**Significance.** §45 serves as the transition from the programmatic framing of §44 to the specific analyses of kinaesthetic correlation that begin in §46. Its main contributions are threefold:

First, the systematic inventory of cases provides the framework within which the kinaesthetic analyses will operate. The simplest and most tractable case — eye-movement with the rest of the body still, applied to an unchanged thing — is isolated as the starting point. This is the case that will be developed in detail in Chapters 9–10 (the oculomotor field).

Second, the distinction between self-movement and being-moved identifies what is phenomenologically relevant. The kinaesthetic analysis does not concern objective bodily motion as such but the *experienced* self-movement that manifests itself in kinaesthetic sensations. Objective motion that does not appear (as when the earth carries me through space) has no phenomenological consequence. Only self-movement, with its kinaesthetic correlate, enters into the constitution of the appearing world.

Third, the identification of **independent systems of movement** (eyes, head, trunk, etc.) introduces what will become the concept of the total kinaesthetic system as a multi-dimensional structure — a system of systems, each with its own series of kinaesthetic sensations correlated with its own series of visual (or tactile) changes. The progressive analysis of these systems and their interrelation is the task of the remaining lectures: §§48–57 will analyze the oculomotor system, §§58–62 will extend beyond it, and §§63–69 will work toward the full constitution of three-dimensional spatial corporeality.

The concluding question — how changing appearances constitute the consciousness of an identical, changeless body — is the question that the entire kinaesthetic analysis exists to answer. The answer will be: through the **functional dependence** of determinate series of appearance-changes on determinate kinaesthetic sequences, a dependence that is not arbitrary but lawfully ordered, such that the identity of the thing is constituted as the invariant across the systematically motivated transformations of the appearance-manifold.

### **§46. Presentational and kinaesthetic sensations**

Section 46 — "Presentational and kinaesthetic sensations" — is one of the most important sections in the entire lectures. It introduces the concept of **kinaesthetic sensation** as a fundamentally new and distinct class of sensation, and it establishes the decisive thesis that the presentational contents of the visual (and tactile) fields are **insufficient by themselves** to constitute spatial thinghood. This section is where the analysis of thing-perception crosses a threshold: it moves from what the sensory fields can provide on their own to what they require *in addition* — namely, the kinaesthetic contribution — in order to constitute things in space.

**The apparent inconsistency.** Husserl begins by acknowledging an apparent inconsistency in the investigation. The announced topic was the visual thing and the visual constitution of space, and yet from the very outset the analysis has introduced the movements of the Body and the sensations of movement — sensations that do not belong to the category of visual contents. But Husserl immediately states the thesis that justifies this: **visual contents are not sufficient by themselves** to serve as apprehensional contents for visual spatiality and for a thing in general. The same holds for tactile contents and tactile space.

**The field-forming character of presentational sensations.** There is, Husserl concedes, a certain justification for speaking of "visual space" and "tactile space." This refers to classes of sensations with the distinctive peculiarity of **coalescing into fields**. Visual sensations (and similarly tactile ones) allow their concretion to be distinguished into two kinds of moments: the material/qualitative moments (quality, brilliance, saturation) and the extensional moments. These necessarily go together — the red-moment, with its brilliance and saturation, is what it is only as the fullness of a certain extension; the red-moment *expands*. Only sensations exhibiting this field-forming character are capable of bringing a thing to presentation and of presenting Objective matter as extended in Objective space. If a materially filled space is to appear, it must present itself in a field of sensations, in what Husserl calls a "predication of sensation" — an "image," as it were.

Classes of sensation that have *no* fields — to whose concretion no "extension" originally belongs — are therefore incapable of **projective presentation**. They can serve only for a secondary filling of space, for secondary (appended) determinations of a thing that is already constituted from another quarter. The thing may now emit a sound and now not, may now be warm and now without thermal determination — these are contingent, non-constitutive features presupposing the thing's prior spatial constitution.

**The insufficiency of projective presentation.** But projective, materializing presentation does not exhaust presentation in the broadest sense — if "presentation" is understood to include all contents of sensation, all that enters as physical datum into the unity of appearance and undergoes animating apprehension. Here Husserl is thinking of the **sensations of movement**. They play an essential role in the apprehension of every external thing, but they are *not themselves apprehended in such a way as to make representable either a proper or improper matter*. They do not belong to the "projection" of the thing. Nothing qualitative corresponds to them in the thing; they do not adumbrate bodies or present them by way of projection. **And yet without their cooperation there is no body there, no thing.**

This is the central thesis of §46, stated with maximum sharpness. The extensional moment of visual sensation (and of tactile sensation) *adumbrates* spatiality but does not *suffice* to make possible the constitution of spatiality, just as the qualitative moment does not suffice for the constitution of the Objective, space-filling quality. Still new sensations are needed — and these are the sensations of movement, which in the animating apprehension have a position and function quite different from the presentational contents. **They make possible a presentation without being presentational themselves.**

**The terminological introduction of "kinaesthetic sensation."** Husserl immediately introduces a terminological correction. "Sensation of movement" (*Bewegungsempfindung*) is unusable, since it wrongly suggests that we *sense* the movement of the thing or that the thing's movement is *presented* in these sensations. The phrase is usually related to what is self-moving and carries a psychological meaning. To exclude this, Husserl adopts the term **kinaesthetic sensation** (*kinästhetische Empfindung*), which as a foreign word is less misleading.

Kinaesthetic sensations, in the case of eye-movements, head-movements, hand-movements, etc., are **continuous sequences of sensations** that terminate at will and every phase of which can extend, with unchanged content, into a duration. Unchanged kinaesthetic sensations are thus "pure and simple" kinaesthetic sensations, versus kinaesthetic changes or sequences.

The concept is to be determined **phenomenologically**, not psychologically or psychophysically. Whether kinaesthetic sensations form a fundamentally new basic category of sensation, or whether they belong with tactile sensations under a higher genus, is a question Husserl leaves to others. What matters is the functional fact: whether functioning in visual or tactile appearances, kinaesthetic sensations have their own determinate type, distinct from all visual and tactile sensations. They are specifically bound together with presentational sensations and interwoven with them, but they **cannot blend with them in the sense of exchanging functions**. A kinaesthetic sensation cannot take on a presentational role, and a presentational sensation cannot take on a kinaesthetic role.

**Significance.** §46 introduces what is arguably the most original concept in the 1907 lectures: the kinaesthetic sensation as a non-presentational but constitutively indispensable moment of thing-perception. The thesis has three dimensions:

First, **negatively**: visual and tactile fields, with their projective, adumbrational presentations, are necessary but not sufficient for thing-constitution. The sensory field alone — no matter how rich, no matter how extensively articulated — cannot constitute a spatial thing, because (as §§33–35 showed) the field is finite while space is infinite, and (as §34 showed) quasi-movement in the field is not genuine movement.

Second, **positively**: what the field lacks is supplied by kinaesthetic sensation. The kinaesthetic contribution is what transforms the field from a mere two-dimensional expanse of sensuous data into a *presentational medium for spatial things*. Kinaesthetic sensations do not add qualitative content to the thing; they govern the *order* and *systematic transformation* of the field-contents, and it is through this governance that spatiality is constituted.

Third, **functionally**: the irreducibility of the kinaesthetic function to the presentational function means that thing-constitution involves two essentially distinct but inseparable classes of sensation, cooperating in a unity of apprehension but playing categorically different roles. The presentational sensations provide the *what* — the qualitative and extensional content through which the thing adumbrates itself. The kinaesthetic sensations provide the *how* — the ordered system of changes through which the adumbrations are traversed and the thing demonstrates its identity across perspectives.

This functional duality — presentational and kinaesthetic — will be the structural principle governing all the remaining analyses. §§48–57 will spell out the specific correlations between kinaesthetic sequences and visual field-transformations, showing how the oculomotor system constitutes the visual field as a systematic manifold of appearances. §§58–69 will extend this to the broader kinaesthetic systems and the constitution of three-dimensional space. The concept introduced here in §46 is the fulcrum on which the entire second half of the lectures turns.

### **§47. Insertion of the kinaesthetic sensations into the Body**

Section 47 — "Insertion of the kinaesthetic sensations into the Body" — closes Chapter 8 by examining a problem that arises immediately from §46's characterization of kinaesthetic sensations as non-presentational: if these sensations do not present any matter of the external thing, where do they belong phenomenologically? The answer — that they are "inserted" into the Ego-Body through a distinctive kind of apprehension — leads Husserl to a compact but extraordinarily rich analysis of the **double constitution** of the Body (Leib), the distinction between **Objectivating** and **subjectivating** apprehension, and the unique phenomenological status of the Body among physical things. This section anticipates in compressed form much of the analysis that will occupy large portions of Ideas II.

**The problem: kinaesthetic sensations and the Body.** Husserl opens by recalling that kinaesthetic sensations do not present any matter — but this applies to the appearing *external* thing. In a certain way, it does not apply to the **Ego-Body**, into which these sensations are inserted as appearances. This insertion, however, occurs in a way that essentially distinguishes the Body from all external things.

**The double character of the Body.** On the one hand, the Body is a thing — a physical thing like any other, insofar as it has its space and is filled with a proper matter and an appended matter. It is a thing among things, with its changing location, stationary or moving like other things. On the other hand, this thing is precisely a *Body* — the bearer of the Ego. The Ego has sensations, and these sensations are "localized" in the Body, partly as *thought there* and partly as *immediately there by appearance*.

Husserl introduces a key asymmetry among sensory modalities: in seeing, I do not localize the visual appearances *as appearances* in my body. But I do localize tactile sensations and the others interwoven with them, including the sensations of movement. The touching hand "appears" as *having* touch sensations. If I turn to the touched Object, smoothness and roughness appear as belonging to it. But if I attend to the touching hand itself, then it possesses the sensation of smoothness and roughness, and possesses them on or in the appearing fingertips. Likewise, the sensations of location and movement are attributed immediately to the hand and arm as encased in them.

**The reversibility of apprehension: Objectivating and subjectivating.** The most remarkable phenomenological feature Husserl identifies is the **reversibility** of the apprehension applied to these sensations. Consider touching my right hand with my left. Along with the touch-sensations and kinaesthetic sensations, there is constituted, reciprocally, the appearance of the left and right hands, the one moving over the other. At the same time, however — with a **reversal of the apprehension** — the self-moving appears in another sense, one that applies only to the Body. The *same* groups of sensations that have an Objectivating function (constituting the hand as a physical thing with tactile qualities) are apprehended, through a reversal of attention and apprehension, as **subjectivating**: as something that members of the Body, those that appear in the Objectivating function, "have" as localized within themselves.

This is the phenomenon of **double apprehension** (*Doppelauffassung*): the same sensational material undergoes two essentially different apprehensions. In the Objectivating apprehension, sensations constitute the Body as a physical thing among things and simultaneously constitute external things with their qualities. In the subjectivating apprehension, these same sensations are grasped as something the Body *has* — as subjective occurrences inserted and localized in it. The Body thereby appears not merely as a thing but as a *sensing* thing, the bearer of sensations.

**The intertwining of thing-constitution and Body-constitution.** Husserl notes that "all sorts of phenomenological analyses" are needed here, but restricts his present concern to the remarkable *correlation* between the constitution of the physical thing and the constitution of the Ego-Body. The Body is constituted like any physical thing but is **pre-eminent** in its appearance, standing above all other physical things through a class of appended determinations that pertain to it exclusively and in proper appearance: the "subjective" determinations. Kinaesthetic sensations belong here, but so do sensations of touch (which also have a primary materializing function for external things) and feelings like pain and pleasure.

If we remain with the sensations that have an Objectivating function for things, we find that they take on the double apprehension: first, the one allowing the physical thing and the Body-as-physical-thing to appear; then, the one allowing the Body to appear as sensing, as the bearer of these and those sensations. From the Objectivating sensations, appended determinations of a special type arise — subjective occurrences inserted in the Body, localized in it. Going beyond the domain of proper appearance, there arises the **introjection** of all sensations and appearances into the Ego and the Ego-Body, and likewise the possibility of attributing to other physical things "psychic occurrences" and of apprehending them as "animate Bodies." But this, Husserl notes, already transcends the current line of investigation.

**The specific concern: the physical-Objectivating function of kinaesthetic sensations.** Husserl refocuses the analysis. Kinaesthetic sensations are not essential for the *appearance* of physical things in the sense that they do not present any matter, not even an appended matter. They allow only a subjectivating apprehension — one that transforms them into appended determinations of the Body, presupposing the Body as already constituted from another quarter and presupposing that the kinaesthetic sensations have already functioned in another manner. This "other manner" is precisely the **physical-Objectivating function** — the function through which kinaesthetic sensations contribute to the constitution of the external thing, not by presenting its matter but by governing the systematic order of its appearances.

Disregarding the subjectivating objectification, the concern of the remaining lectures will be to understand this physical-Objectivating function and thereby the constitution of the thing in general: the unchanged thing, identically the same, now here, now there, now stationary, now changing its place.

**Significance.** §47 is philosophically dense beyond its modest length. Three aspects deserve emphasis:

First, the analysis of the Body as the site of the **double apprehension** introduces a theme that will become central to *Ideas II*'s account of the constitution of the *Leib* as distinct from the *Körper*. The Body is unique among things precisely because the same sensational material that constitutes it as a physical object (Körper) simultaneously constitutes it as a lived, sensing body (Leib) through the reversal of apprehension. This double constitution is not something added to the Body from outside; it belongs to the essential structure of bodily experience.

Second, the distinction between the Objectivating and subjectivating apprehension of kinaesthetic sensations clarifies how the same sensations can serve two entirely different constitutive functions. In their physical-Objectivating function (which the remaining lectures will analyze), kinaesthetic sensations contribute to the constitution of external things by governing the order of appearances. In their subjectivating function, they are grasped as belonging to the Body, as localized in its members, as indices of its self-movement. The two functions are distinguishable but inseparable in concrete experience — the exploration of an external thing is always at the same time an experience of my own bodily movement.

Third, the concept of **introjection** that Husserl briefly introduces — the attribution of subjective occurrences to the Ego-Body and, by analogy, to other bodies as animate — points toward the constitution of intersubjectivity and empathy, themes Husserl will develop elsewhere (notably in *Ideas II* and the *Cartesian Meditations*). §47 thus marks a node where the analysis of thing-constitution connects to the broader phenomenological problematic of the constitution of the psychophysical subject and of other subjects, even though Husserl does not pursue these connections here.

The section ends by sharpening the task ahead: the remaining lectures will abstract from the subjectivating dimension and focus exclusively on the physical-Objectivating function of kinaesthetic sensations — how they make possible the constitution of an identical, spatial, moveable thing. This abstraction is what allows the investigation to proceed, in Chapters 9–14, with the specific correlational analyses of kinaesthetic sequences and appearance-manifolds that constitute the positive constructive achievement of the lectures.

## 

## Chapter 9: The Correlation Between the Visual Field and the Kinaesthetic Sequences

### **§48. The presentational means of the visual field**

Section 48 — "The presentational means of the visual field" — opens Chapter 9 ("The Correlation between the Visual Field and the Kinaesthetic Sequences") and provides the first detailed phenomenological description of the visual field *as a presentational medium* — that is, not as an objective spatial surface but as the pre-empirical expanse within which visual things come to presentation. The section establishes the essential structural features of the field, carefully distinguishes the field from Objective space, and then addresses the critical question of what kind of identity can be constituted within the field alone — showing that this intra-field identity is constitutively insufficient for thinghood.

**The internal structure of the visual field.** Husserl begins with a description of what the visual field offers as presentational means. Within it, pre-empirical "matters" are extended, and we must distinguish phenomenologically between **matter** (quality in the broad sense — hue, brilliance, saturation) and **extension** (pre-empirical place, size, shape). These two moments are independently variable within certain limits: the same quality can undergo modifications of extension, and the same extension can undergo modifications of quality. Two visual concreta identical in both extension and matter can differ only in their **locations** — the one is here, the other there.

The field can be partitioned through qualitative discontinuities (where one color leaps into another), generating an ordered nexus of mutually limited parts. This partitioning characterizes the field as a **fixed system of locations**: every distinguishable concrete element has its corresponding location, its "here," and this "here" founds relations of distance. Elements can be conveyed continually to other locations (displacement, rotation), and through such analysis Husserl concludes that the visual field is a **two-dimensional manifold** — in itself congruent, continuous, utterly coherent, finite, and bounded (it has a margin beyond which there is nothing). Two-dimensionality means that the limits of the field's pieces are lines, and the limits of lines are points.

Husserl raises but does not resolve the question of whether the field is infinitely fragmentable or whether the *minima visibilia* constitute ultimate, atomic elements. The answer would require attention to the immanent similarity within the field that founds the transference of essential relations from the macroscopic to the microscopic realm.

**The field is not space.** Husserl emphatically warns that all the terms used — line, point, location, shape, size — are **not to be understood in the spatial sense**. The visual field is not a surface in Objective space. Points and lines in the field are not points and lines in Objective space and have no spatial relation to them. This is the consistent application of the principle from §§19–20 that the pre-empirical extension of appearance is to be rigorously distinguished from the Objective extension of the thing.

**Possible changes as presentational means.** Beyond the static features of matter, place, shape, and size, the field's **possible changes** also serve as presentational means. A concretum can change quasi-materially (in quality, brilliance, saturation) while maintaining its extensional limits, or it can change extensionally (quasi-displacement, quasi-rotation, quasi-contraction, quasi-expansion, quasi-distortion) while maintaining or also altering its quality. Delimitations can become vague through slackening of qualitative discontinuities. Changes of accommodation and the striking transformations that occur when visual formations are transferred to the margin of the field must also be counted among the presentational resources.

**Pre-empirical identity and substance — but not thinghood.** The most philosophically important passage in §48 addresses the question of whether the identity constitutable within the field suffices for thinghood. Husserl develops the point through a general essential law: in every continuity elapsing in the pre-empirical temporal stream, there "resides" an identity. The ideal possibility exists of intuiting the identity of an object in the stream of its phases. A sound enduring or changing is a unity in its streaming manifold — an adequate givenness in the consciousness of unity, though a transcendence given on the pure ground of immanence. Husserl had spoken in previous lectures of **pre-empirical substance and pre-empirical accidents**: the pitch of the tone is what is identical in all the pitch-moments present in the phases of the sound; the sound changes its timbre while pitch remains the same, or vice versa.

This pre-empirical substantialization applies to visual data as well. In the visual field, we can speak of an "image" changing its coloration, brilliance, or place. We have an evidential foundation for speaking of movement as mere change of place within the field.

**But this is not the identity of the thing.** Here Husserl delivers the decisive limitation. No matter how important this intra-field "identity in continuity" is as a piece of the Objectivation of the thing, it is **not the identity of the thing**. The identity of the sound is an identity in the actual phenomenological stream and does not reach beyond it; the identity of an image in pre-empirical change of location is identical only in this actual change. The thing, however, is in and with the stream not only of its *actual* changes but also of its **possible** changes — and these are infinite though firmly delimited. The empirical thing is what appears as identical in the stream of pre-empirical occurrences, but it is not something that "resides" in them, not something that could be adequately drawn out from them.

Husserl illustrates with a striking example: if an Objective thing appears stationary while the eyes move, the "image" executes a quasi-movement in the visual field. We can attend to this, notice the image changing its place. But this does not amount to perceiving a moving thing that could then, by adding further apprehensions, be identified as a stationary thing. Only a *single* thing appears, because the image, in changing its field-location, can be posited as an identity but **not as a thing**. It is no more a thing than a sound posited as identical in its intensity-changes is already a thing. The moment of location in the field, and accordingly change of location and identity in the stream of this "movement," does not in principle suffice for thinghood.

**Significance.** §48 establishes precisely what the visual field *can* and *cannot* accomplish on its own. It can provide: a two-dimensional, continuous, bounded manifold of pre-empirical locations; qualitative and extensional data filling those locations; quasi-movements and quasi-transformations of those data; and intra-field identities constituted in the temporal stream. What it *cannot* provide is the identity of the spatial thing — an identity that encompasses not only actual but all possible changes, that persists through movement and rest in infinite space, and that is constituted not "in" the field but "through" the field as a presentational medium.

This negative result is the counterpart, at a more concrete level of analysis, of §34's argument that quasi-movement in the field is not genuine movement and that the field is not space. It sets up §49's introduction of the **functional connection** between visual data and kinaesthetic sequences as the constitutive resource that transcends the field's intrinsic limitations. The thing is not an identity residing in the field; it is the identity constituted through the lawful correlation of field-contents with kinaesthetic possibilities — a correlation that §49 will begin to describe and that the remaining lectures will analyze in detail.

### **§49. Functional connection between visual data and kinaesthetic sequences. Monocularity and binocularity**

Section 49 — "Functional connection between visual data and kinaesthetic sequences. Monocularity and binocularity" — is one of the longest and most technically detailed sections in the lectures. It introduces the second group of presentational means — the kinaesthetic sensations — and establishes the precise nature of their connection to the visual field-contents. It also provides an extensive analysis of the specific presentational resources of monocular and binocular vision, including the phenomenology of depth-values and double images. The section culminates in the thesis that the kinaesthetic-visual connection is **functional** rather than **essential** — a distinction with profound constitutive consequences.

**The kinaesthetic sensations as a new class of presentational means.** Husserl opens by stating that the class of presentational means now to be discussed lies on a "completely new path." The kinaesthetic sensations stand *outside* the visual field and have no more of an intrinsic, founded connection with visual contents than visual contents have with tactile contents. Color and extension belong together — they are *founded* in one another. The color fills the extension, diffuses in it, generates a colored extension. Every change in extension affects color in a certain way, and vice versa. Hue, brilliance, and saturation belong together, as do size, shape, and location within extension. Everywhere there are appurtenances founded in essence that produce inseparable unities.

With kinaesthetic sensations, it is otherwise. They lack an **essential relation** to visual sensations. They are connected to them **functionally** but not essentially. The bond in the case of functional unity is a bond of what is *separable*; it is not the inner unity of what is mutually founded. This is the decisive claim of the section: the kinaesthetic-visual relation is a relation of **functional dependence** between essentially heterogeneous groups of sensations, not a relation of essential founding within a single genus.

**The structure of the kinaesthetic manifold.** Kinaesthetic sensations form continuous multi-dimensional systems, but they form continuous unities only as *sequences* — as linear manifolds filling spans of pre-empirical time. Only a continuous linear manifold (a temporally extended sequence) can function as a unity of kinaesthetic sensation. This contrasts with the visual field, which is a simultaneously extended two-dimensional manifold. The kinaesthetic manifold is multi-dimensional in the sense that it comprises multiple independent systems (eye-movements, head-movements, etc.), but each system unfolds its changes sequentially in time.

**Monocular presentational means.** Husserl then turns to a careful inventory of the specific presentational resources available in monocular and binocular vision, since the analysis of kinaesthetic correlation requires knowing exactly what the visual side of the correlation contains.

In the monocular case, the visual field offers: pre-empirical matter (quality, brilliance, saturation) and pre-empirical extension (place, shape, size), their possible changes (quasi-displacement, quasi-rotation, quasi-expansion, quasi-distortion, qualitative changes), and the structural features of the two-dimensional field itself.

**Binocular presentational means: double images, fusion, and depth.** The binocular case adds significantly more. Husserl describes in detail: the splitting of a single image into two images (double images) and their fusion back into one; the phenomenon of **competition** (one image becoming "transparent" and giving way to another); and most importantly, the phenomenon of **depth-values** or **relief** (*Tiefenwerte*).

Every unified binocular image, apart from its quality of fullness, displays a quality of **standing in relief**, of being differentiated in depth. This constitutes a manifold of graduated sensational moments moving within fixed limits, with one limit having the character of a null-point. These depth-moments are appended to the images, extend over the expanse of the image alongside the coloration, and follow the differences in location pertaining to the extension. But they do not form a two-dimensional manifold coinciding with extension; they form a **one-dimensional system** that fills the extensional manifold with either constant or changing depth-values. This depth-moment is founded in the moment of extension, and through it the coloration acquires not merely extension but extension *with depth*, possibly with changing relief.

Husserl insists on the distinction between pre-empirical depth and the depth of things: the sensation of depth is not the depth of things, and pre-empirical relief is not actual relief. The pre-empirical relief "covers" the appearing thing and shows only the appearing side of the binocularly grasped thing. But it remains to determine in what sense this depth-moment *presents* three-dimensional spatiality — specifically, the in-front/behind relative to "me," as distinguished from the right-left and above-below presented through extensional differences.

**The depth-moment changes with eye-movement.** The relief changes with every movement of the eyes, yet it signifies the same relief of things — a system of three-dimensional distinctions. Husserl notes that the pre-empirical distinctions of depth belong to a **completely new genus** over against the distinctions of extension. Just as temporal distance cannot be like or unlike distance in breadth, so distance in breadth cannot, pre-empirically, be like or unlike distance in depth. It would be a fundamental error to think that a line rotated "into the depths" rotates out of the visual field — nothing can rotate out of the visual field. The line displaced into depth continuously changes its extensional moment and its depth-gradations.

**Convergence sensations.** Finally, Husserl mentions the kinaesthetic sensations of convergence/divergence pertaining to binocular vision — coordinations of the sensations of the two eyes that are functionally connected to the changing divergence of double images, their distances in the double field, their fusion, and their changing depth-values.

**The transition to the analysis of creation.** Husserl closes by noting that the inventory of presentational means — the material of sensation bearing the function of apprehension, the matter "with which consciousness carries out the creation of nature" — is now complete. The task turns to focusing on the **character of this creation**: how the thing is actually constituted through the functional correlation of these heterogeneous sensational systems.

**Significance.** §49's central contribution is the thesis of the **functional** (as opposed to essential) character of the kinaesthetic-visual connection. This has several major implications:

First, it means the constitution of the spatial thing is not grounded in any single, self-sufficient sensory system. No intrinsic feature of visual sensations dictates which kinaesthetic sensations they must be paired with; the pairing is established through the regularity of actual experience. This is why Husserl will later invoke association (in a phenomenological, not psychological sense) as the principle governing the kinaesthetic-visual correlation.

Second, the functional character explains why the same visual field-contents can be compatible with different Objective states of affairs (rest or movement of the thing, as §50 will develop): the visual contents alone are indeterminate between these possibilities, and it is the kinaesthetic factor that disambiguates.

Third, the detailed analysis of binocular depth introduces a presentational resource — the one-dimensional depth-manifold — that adds something genuinely new to the constitutive apparatus beyond what monocular vision provides. But even this depth-moment is not yet three-dimensional spatiality; it is a pre-empirical sensational moment that *presents* depth through its own genus-specific gradations, and the constitution of genuine three-dimensional Objective space will require the full kinaesthetic-visual correlation that the later sections develop.

The section thus completes the inventory of the constitutive "raw materials" — the visual field with its matter and extension, the binocular depth-values, and the kinaesthetic sequences — and sets the stage for §§50–57's analysis of how these materials are actually employed in the "creation" of the spatial thing.

### **§50. The sequence of images in the case of eye-movement and in the case of the movement of the Object**

Section 50 — "The sequence of images in the case of eye-movement and in the case of the movement of the Object" — is a short but argumentatively decisive section. It provides the single most important phenomenological demonstration in the kinaesthetic analysis: the proof that **visual field-contents alone are constitutively insufficient** for distinguishing rest from movement, and that kinaesthetic sensations are therefore **indispensable** for the constitution of spatiality.

**The thought-experiment.** Husserl sets up a precise comparison between two phenomenologically distinct situations that are, from the standpoint of the visual field alone, *indistinguishable*:

*Case 1: Eye-movement with stationary Objects.* The field of Objects is stationary; only the eye moves. The visual image wanders over the visual field and undergoes a determinate series of modifications, including qualitative ones (changes as the image moves from the center toward the margin of the field, etc.).

*Case 2: Stationary eye with moving Objects.* The eye is completely still, but the Objects move. The exact same series of modifications in the visual field can elapse — and yet now *movement* appears, whereas in Case 1 what appeared was a *stationary* field of Objects viewed from a changing perspective.

Husserl generalizes: the same holds if we replace eye-movements with movements of the whole Body. In carrying out certain bodily movements, we may have the appearance of a continuously stationary field of Objects, and yet a certain series of visual images is elapsing as the images pass over into one another. If the Body is motionless and the thing appears stationary, we have a single unchanging image. But if the Object begins to move in such a way that the exact same series of visual images elapses as when the Body moved, now *movement* appears where formerly something stationary appeared.

Husserl pushes the argument further with a third scenario: the Body moves and the field of Objects simultaneously moves in such a way that it "imitates" the Body's motion — the field constantly appears in the exact same visual image. With one unchanged visual field, rest appears in one case and movement in another.

**The conclusion.** The conclusion is stated with full force: "From this we see that in fact the merely visual sequences are not sufficient for the apprehension and that they do not contain the means to bring rest and movement to an appearance where they would be discriminated." The constitution of Objective location and Objective spatiality is **essentially mediated** by the movement of the Body or, in phenomenological terms, by the kinaesthetic sensations — whether these be constant or changing kinaesthetic sequences.

**The depth and simplicity of the argument.** The argument's power lies in its simplicity. It is a phenomenological *Gedankenexperiment* that isolates the constitutive role of kinaesthetic sensation by showing that the same visual data are compatible with objectively incompatible states of affairs (rest vs. movement). Since the visual field-contents are identical in both cases, whatever constitutively distinguishes them must lie *outside* the visual field — and the only candidate is the kinaesthetic factor. When the eyes (or Body) are moving, there is a determinate kinaesthetic sequence accompanying the visual change; when the eyes are still and the Object moves, there is kinaesthetic constancy accompanying the same visual change. It is this difference in the kinaesthetic component — change versus constancy — that constitutively distinguishes the appearance of rest from the appearance of movement.

This is, in effect, the constitutive equivalent of what later perceptual psychology will call the problem of distinguishing **retinal flow** produced by eye-movement from retinal flow produced by object-movement. Husserl's analysis is notable for treating this not as an empirical-psychological problem (how does the brain disambiguate?) but as a transcendental-phenomenological one (what constitutive resources must be available in principle for the distinction to be possible at all?).

**The connection to earlier results.** The argument of §50 provides the concrete phenomenological grounding for what §46 asserted in general terms: that kinaesthetic sensations "make possible a presentation without being presentational themselves." §46 stated the thesis; §50 demonstrates it through a specific case. The visual field provides the *content* of appearance — the qualitative and extensional data through which the thing adumbrates itself. But it cannot provide the *framework* within which that content acquires spatial significance — the distinction between rest and movement, between the thing being here and the thing being there. This framework is constituted through the kinaesthetic sensations, which function not by adding new qualitative content to the appearance but by determining the *circumstances* under which visual content counts as presenting a stationary or a moving thing.

**Significance for the remainder of the lectures.** §50 establishes the indispensability result that motivates the entire subsequent analysis. Having shown that kinaesthetic sensations are constitutively necessary, the investigation can now turn to the question of *how* they function — what the specific correlation between kinaesthetic sequences and visual field-changes looks like, and how this correlation constitutes the identity of the spatial thing. This is the work of §§51–57: §51 will analyze the specific structure of the kinaesthetic-visual correlation in the case of mere oculomotor changes; §§52–53 will generalize to the total field of Objects; and §§54–57 will address the constitution of unity and the total kinaesthetic system. The simple but irrefutable demonstration of §50 — that the same visual series can present either rest or movement, depending on the kinaesthetic accompaniment — is the foundation on which all of this rests.

### **§51. Sequence of appearances in the case of mere oculomotor changes**

Section 51 — "Sequence of appearances in the case of mere oculomotor changes" — is the technical heart of the kinaesthetic analysis. Having established in §50 that kinaesthetic sensations are constitutively indispensable, §51 now describes in concrete detail how the functional correlation between kinaesthetic sequences (K-sequences) and visual image-sequences (f-sequences) actually works in the simplest case: mere oculomotor changes applied to a stationary, unchanged thing. The section introduces the formal apparatus of K/f-correlation, analyzes the nature of the associative bond between them, and identifies the fundamentally different intentional roles played by the two factors.

**The basic correlation.** Husserl asks us to consider the manifold of appearances belonging to mere rest — a thing standing there materially and stereometrically unchanged, stationary. We isolate the stratum of appearances belonging to the kinaesthesia of the eye-movements, leaving the rest of the body still and assuming fixed accommodation. The correlation is then:

If the kinaesthetic ocular sensation K₁ is constant (the eye is stationary) during a span of time, then the visual image i₁ is also constant during that time. If K₁ changes in a continuous sequence into K₂, then i₁ also changes into i₂. If K₂ reverts to K₁, then i₂ reverts to i₁ in the same time span. Every change in K **univocally conditions** a change in i, such that the same span of time filled with one change is filled with the other. Every pause in the K-change means a pause in the i-change. The dependence is **reciprocal**: the same K-sensation is accompanied by the same image, and the same image by the same K-sensation.

**The non-fixed character of the K-i connection.** But the connection between a particular K and a particular i is **not fixed once and for all**. The determinate K₀ does not in general refer to the determinate i₀ as such. If I turn my head and now find a different i accompanying the same K₀, I am not disappointed. The connection holds only **under given circumstances** — with respect to a particular stationary field of Objects. Every turning of the head introduces an entirely new field of images paired with the same K-manifold.

This is the crucial structural insight: the K-i correlation is not a rigid, one-to-one pairing between individual kinaesthetic values and individual images. It is a **systematic** correlation that holds within a given perceptual situation but varies from situation to situation.

**The nature of the associative bond.** Husserl asks what makes the connection and what distinguishes it.
