He analyzes the phenomenological concept of **association** in a careful passage that strips the concept of its psychological-dispositional connotations. Association, as he uses it here, is a phenomenological fact — a certain appurtenance and reference of the one datum to the other, such that the positing of belief in one motivates belief in the other. The bond of functional unity is a bond of what is *separable* — unlike the inner unity of what is mutually founded (e.g., color and extension). Being given together repeatedly in one consciousness creates a sort of unity whose force increases with repetition; this is an **essential law**, not a psychological generalization. The disappointing of associative intentions weakens their force and posits a counter-force.

But Husserl then observes that in the K-i case, at first no simple association appears between a particular K and a particular i that could account for their determinate connection. The connection must lie elsewhere.

**The example of the square.** Husserl develops the correlation through a concrete example. Consider a stationary square:

a — b

|       |

d — c

We move our gaze along the perimeter, fixating a, b, c, d, a in succession. To every ocular position there corresponds a determinate pre-empirical figure: f\_a passes over into f\_b, f\_c, f\_d. Simultaneously, the K-sensations transition continuously: K\_a into K\_b, K\_c, K\_d.

**The asymmetry of intentional function.** Here Husserl identifies the most fundamental structural feature of the K-f correlation. In the continuous transition, the f's (the visual images) **refer to their continuous neighbors**: intentions penetrate the series f\_a to f\_d and are continually fulfilled as the series elapses. There is a thorough **consciousness of unity** founded in these intentions. This is the anticipatory intentionality described in §30 — each adumbration points forward to the next, and the object constitutes itself as the identical pole of this intentional series.

The K's, by contrast, **do not refer to each other**. They elapse, but they are not bearers of intentions that penetrate them. They are not traversed by a consciousness of unity. Reversing the succession of K's gives us a reversed stream of f-intentions (f\_d, f\_c, f\_b, f\_a, directed toward each other and fulfilled through each other), while the K's again elapse in reverse **without this distinguishing mark**.

This asymmetry is the key to the entire kinaesthetic constitution. The two factors function in radically different ways:

The K's are the **"circumstances"** (*Umstände*). The f's are the **"appearances"** (*Erscheinungen*). A determinate change in the circumstances results in a determinate change in the appearances — specifically a change that gives the f-intentions the character of a stream, fulfills them, and instills a thorough consciousness of unity. The consciousness of unity runs *only through the f's*, not partially through the K's and partially through the f's. The K's and f's are not a mere conglomeration, but their unity is such that if K₀ passes over into K₁, then f₀ passes over *in expectation* to f₁, referring to and being fulfilled in each new phase.

**The generalization to arbitrary fixation.** The state of affairs is not limited to the case of fixating the square's perimeter. If the fixation point moves to the environment, traversing any paths whatever, the same structure holds: the f's assume new modes of change, the image travels along different quasi-lines of the visual field, but the image always remains in the consciousness of unity. Every image is penetrated by the unitary intention and fulfillment. But **only under its circumstances** does the image have this intentionality. Once an f\_a is given together with K\_a, then to every determinate change ΔK\_a there belongs, in temporal coincidence, a determinate course of fulfillment Δf\_a, and to the mere duration of K\_a there belongs the same duration of f\_a.

**Significance.** §51 is the section where the formal structure of kinaesthetic constitution is laid bare with maximum precision. Three results stand out:

First, the **functional** character of the K-f correlation: each K univocally determines an f (and vice versa) within a given perceptual situation, but the correlation is not intrinsic or essential — it varies with the Objective situation.

Second, the **asymmetry** of intentional function: the f-series is traversed by the consciousness of unity (the object-constituting intentionality), while the K-series provides the "circumstances" under which this intentionality operates. The K's do not themselves constitute anything; they *condition* the constitutive process that runs through the f's.

Third, the **temporal coincidence** of the two series: the K-sequence and the f-sequence fill the same span of pre-empirical time, and their phases correspond point for point. This temporal coincidence, combined with the functional asymmetry, is what makes it possible for the thing to constitute itself as the identical pole of a kinaesthetically motivated manifold of appearances.

This structure — circumstances conditioning appearances, with the consciousness of unity running through the appearances alone — is the constitutive schema that §§52–57 will generalize from the oculomotor case to the total kinaesthetic system, and that Sections V and VI will employ in the transition from the oculomotor field to objective three-dimensional space.

### **§52. Transfer of the just-discussed state of affairs to the total field of Objects**

Section 52 — "Transfer of the just-discussed state of affairs to the total field of Objects" — generalizes the K-f correlation established in §51 for a single Object (the square) to the **entire field of Objects**, and in doing so deepens the analysis of the associative bond between kinaesthetic and visual factors. The section is compact but introduces several essential structural insights about the nature of the kinaesthetic-visual correlation at the level of the total perceptual situation.

**The generalization: one K for the whole field.** Husserl's first move is to point out that what was described for the square applies equally to every Object in the field. But now a crucial structural fact emerges: the determinate kinaesthetic sensation K that belongs to a determinate location of the image of the square in the visual field also pertains to every image and image-point of *all* Objects of the field — the same identical K for all of them, for the **entire field of images**. Under the same kinaesthetic circumstances, we have the same field of images. Every kinaesthetic change alters the *whole* field of images at once.

This is a significant structural result. The K-factor is not correlated with individual images piecemeal; it is correlated with the total visual field as a unity. The phenomenon of a stationary field of Objects in the manifold of variabilities and invariabilities of the field of images places the continuity of K-sensations and entire fields of images into **univocal correspondence** — but with the essentially different characteristic that the K's function as circumstances (not bearers of the consciousness of unity), while the consciousness of unity exclusively penetrates the fields of images and the individual images that separate themselves within it.

**The variability of the K-image pairing across situations.** The same holds for every stationary field of Objects, but every *distinct* field presents itself in a *different* manifold of images. There is already a difference if the same Objects in the same Objective nexus are presented with different images — even if the difference is only that the coordination of images to K-values has shifted (as when I move my head very slightly without noticeable perspectival changes). The same field of images that was previously bound with K₀ may now be linked to a different K.

This confirms and extends §51's point that the K-i connection is not fixed once and for all: the pairing varies not only with the Objective situation (which things are before me) but even with slight changes in bodily orientation that shift the entire coordination without altering the perspectival content.

**The problem of association reformulated.** This variability generates a precise problem for the associative account. If association is supposed to connect the K and the visual factor, it cannot be association between a K-sensation and just any individual image, since the entire field of images is co-involved. Furthermore, it is not association with a *determinate* field of images, since every other field could just as well enter in. The same K-manifold is bound together with *all* possible manifolds of image-fields that present the unity of a stationary (and ever different) field of Objects.

What then do all these different image-fields have in common? The identical manifold of *places* of the pre-empirical field. But this manifold offers no basis for a connection, since it is precisely always there, always given together with whatever K's happen to be operative.

**The formal type of the sequence.** Husserl concludes that the connection can reside only in the **formal unity of the sequences**. Each sequence of images in which a stationary Object is presented in a field forms a **type** — whether shorter or longer, whether proceeding in one direction or the reverse. Every such sequence has its counter-sequence (reversal), and in this reversal it forms the same type. All these sequences can be bound together into comprehensive sequences forming a **universal type**.

On the other hand, every such type, actualized in the temporal stream, is given together with a determinate K-sequence in temporal coincidence. With the omni-sided transformation and retransformation of the gaze, we have again and again the parallel sequences of two serial systems: the system of images and the kinaesthetic streams. The temporal series on both sides are identical, and in their filling they correspond reciprocally and univocally. The **associative connection** joins the corresponding phases through co-existence and the pairs, in their continuous sequence, through succession.

**The role of repetition across different situations.** As ever new stationary systems of Objects offer themselves — perhaps through turning the body, or through the system's own movement and eventual return to stationarity — other systems of images of quite different type are lived through and given together with the same K-systems. But even though the manifolds of images are different and ever different across these situations, each one has in itself the **same general type**. The parallel connection of these manifolds with the ever-unchanged K-manifold possesses the same general type. This type founds the **general association** in virtue of which, if even just a differential — the beginning of a sequence that could be ordered within such a type — is given, then immediately there results apperceptively the ordering within the type: a stationary Object or field of Objects presents itself. The thing *appears*, and thereby the function of the K's is distinguished from the function of the images.

Husserl summarizes: the K-sensations are what is given or what streams on with the **same determination and form everywhere**. The images are what is **new everywhere**. Or rather, the type of covering of the always identically given visual field of places is new everywhere, and all that the images have in common is **the form of their sequence in parallel unity with the K-sequence**.

**The nature of the "association psychology."** Husserl closes by noting that this "small excursus into the associative interpretation" constitutes an "association psychology" that is **free of all transcendent suppositions**. It is a purely phenomenological account of the connections among phenomena, not a psychological theory of dispositional mechanisms in the soul.

**Significance.** §52 accomplishes three things:

First, it establishes that the kinaesthetic-visual correlation operates at the level of the **total field**, not individual images. The K-factor is a global circumstance governing the entire visual situation at once. This means the constitution of the thing is inseparable from the constitution of the total perceptual field — individual things are constituted *within* a field-wide kinaesthetic-visual correlation, not independently of it.

Second, it identifies what is **invariant** across all the different image-fields that can be paired with the same K-manifold: not any particular content, but the **formal type of the image-sequence**. The regularity that founds the association is not a regularity of individual K-i pairings but a regularity of the *form* of the parallel sequences. Every stationary field of Objects, however different its particular images, generates image-sequences of the same formal type when traversed by the same K-sequences. This formal typicality is what is associatively sedimented through experience and what enables the apperceptive constitution of spatial objectivity.

Third, it shows that the distinction between K-function and f-function is not merely a local feature of the oculomotor case but a **universal structural principle**: the K's are everywhere the same (in form and determination), the f's are everywhere new (in content), and what connects them is the formal parallelism of their sequences. This is the principle that will carry through the amplification of the oculomotor analysis to the full kinaesthetic system in §§54–57 and beyond.

### **§53. The visual field as a system of places and its possible transformations**

Section 53 — "The visual field as a system of places and its possible transformations" — closes Chapter 9 by providing a precise phenomenological description of the visual field's internal structure as a **system of places** and analyzing how this system undergoes **transformations** under kinaesthetic changes. The section identifies the respective roles of the extensional and qualitative moments in the constitution of the field's unity, and concludes by identifying the **stratification** of the manifolds of appearance that constitute a thing.

**Quality and place: their mutual dependence.** Husserl begins by analyzing how coloration and extension — the two fundamental moments of visual sensation — relate to one another in the constitution of images within the field. The unity of a coloration is *founded* in the ordered form of the extension as a system of places covered by the coloration. The concept of coloration presupposes the system of places and in a certain sense implies it. On the other hand, the *prominence* of a coloration as a unity for itself — and thereby the prominence of the entire image — is founded in the specific *discontinuity* of the coloration at its margins relative to the environment. Without qualitative discontinuity, no image is delimited and no image can be attended to for itself. This discontinuity is a condition of the possibility of the determinate image as a visual givenness.

The two moments are thus reciprocally dependent in a precise way: the places give the qualities their order and unity (two instances of the same quality are distinguished only by their different places); the qualities give the places their prominence and determinateness (the places owe their individual prominence to the qualitative discontinuity that delimits them).

**The transformation of the system of places.** As the image travels under K-circumstances, the whole images maintain a continuous coordination, but this coordination is granted in a *privileged way* to their systems of places. The qualities of the images have unity only through the systems of places and owe their coordination to these systems — though the coordination extends to all qualitative elements through the medium of the subtending places. Conversely, the systems of places owe their coordination to the character of the qualitative covering (the discontinuous gradient at the edges).

This leads to a key structural insight: the **transformation of the visual field of places in itself**, produced by any kinaesthetic change, is independent of the particular qualitative covering. The transformation that maps one system of places to another (as the gaze shifts from one fixation to another) would remain the same even if the coloration were entirely different, provided only the discontinuous gradient — the prominence — is preserved. If the coloration changed otherwise than in the presentation of the same stationary, unchanged thing, the transformation of the system of places would remain unchanged. The same applies to movement with respect to qualitative change.

This independence of the place-transformation from the particular qualitative filling is constitutively crucial. It means the kinaesthetic-visual correlation operates primarily at the level of **place-transformations** — formal mappings of the field's system of places onto itself — rather than at the level of particular qualitative contents. The K-sequence determines a determinate transformation of places; the qualities ride, as it were, on top of this transformation. This is what makes it possible for the same K-manifold to be paired with indefinitely many different image-fields (as §52 established): what all these different fields share is not any particular content but the same formal structure of place-transformations.

**The penetrating unity.** In every appearance or manifold of images in which the presentation of an object unfolds systematically in some direction, there is a unity that penetrates the pre-empirical extension and coloration. The manifold of pre-empirical extensions signifies a transformation of the visual manifold of places in itself. The colorations give the extensions fullness and prominence and come to unity through the disclosure of the images on the basis of those transformations.

**The stratification of the constitution of the thing.** Husserl closes with a remark about the architectonic structure of the constitutive analysis. The ideally closed manifolds of possible appearances of a thing contain different, characteristically closed **partial manifolds** that make up the "strata" of the constitution of the thing. The stratum that has been occupying the analysis — the stationary manifold (the manifold of appearances of an unchanged, stationary thing under mere oculomotor changes) — is one such closed stratum. Others remain to be investigated: the manifold of appearances under head-movement, under locomotion, under change of the thing itself, etc. Each stratum has its own internal structure and its own kinaesthetic-visual correlation, and the full constitution of the thing involves the integration of all these strata into a comprehensive system.

**Significance.** §53 accomplishes three things that are essential for the transition to Chapter 10:

First, it identifies the **system of places** and its transformations as the primary structural level at which the kinaesthetic-visual correlation operates. This is the level at which the formal, interest-independent structure of spatial constitution resides — the level at which the K-sequences determine the order of the appearance-manifold regardless of what particular qualities fill the field.

Second, the analysis of the independence of place-transformations from qualitative filling explains the **universality** of the kinaesthetic constitution: the same kinaesthetic system can constitute the spatial framework for any qualitative content whatsoever, which is why the same bodily movements generate spatially coherent experience regardless of what particular things happen to be before us.

Third, the concept of **strata** in the constitution of the thing provides the architectural framework for the remainder of the lectures. The oculomotor stratum (§§48–53) is the first and simplest; it must now be integrated with further strata (head-movements, locomotion, etc.) in the comprehensive constitution of three-dimensional spatiality. Chapter 10 (§§54–57) will synthesize the results of the oculomotor analysis into a general account of the thing as unity in the kinaesthetically motivated manifold, and Sections V–VI will extend the analysis beyond the oculomotor field toward the full constitution of spatial corporeality.

## 

## Chapter 10: The Thing as Unity in the Kinaesthetically Motivated Manifold of Appearances

### **§54. The consciousness of unity in the sequences of images and in the kinaesthetic sequences**

Section 54 — "The consciousness of unity in the sequences of images and in the kinaesthetic sequences" — opens Chapter 10 ("The Thing as Unity in the Kinaesthetically Motivated Manifold of Appearances") and provides the most systematic and philosophically precise account in the entire lectures of how the **identity of the thing** is constituted through the kinaesthetic-visual correlation. The section synthesizes everything established in §§48–53 into a unified description of the constitutive structure, introducing the concepts of the "intention toward" (*Hinzielung*), "quasi-intentions," and the "halo" of motivated possibilities that together define what it means for a thing to be given.

**The apprehensional character of the stationary thing.** Husserl begins with the apprehensional situation: if a complex of K and i is apprehended, in its typical elapsing, as *stationary*, then an apprehensional character is attached to it that is distributed differently to K and i. This character refers to the possible sequences of i in the total system under the possible kinaesthetic circumstances and thus bears the ideal possibilities of fulfillment in the elapsing of precisely such systems. In every such nexus of fulfillment, the images are subtended by a **consciousness of unity** — the consciousness of the self-same, which is and remains the same wherever the appurtenant appearances are fulfilled under the relevant circumstances in the sense of the general type.

This consciousness of unity constitutes the one identical stationary thing as presented identically through the images. If intentions ran only through this one system — if this systematic type were not integrated into more encompassing types — the thing would be completely constituted in this manifold, and it would have no relation to movement and change. But then it would clearly not be a *thing*. Nevertheless, an essential part of the constitution of the thing and of space is already clarified through this stratum. The main character of the constitution is already visible.

**The formal description.** Husserl then provides his most complete formal description of the constitutive structure:

A continuity of images in the visual field, extending in pre-empirical time, streams on in **temporal coincidence** and in fusion with a continuity of kinaesthetic circumstances. This continuity of images is a **linear manifold** extracted out of a **multi-dimensional manifold** of possible images. The latter includes infinitely many other linear manifolds, and each one, according to its determinate type, is encompassed by the determinate general type of the total manifold. This total manifold is of the same strength (same dimensionality) as the continuous manifold of possible K's.

Every actually elapsing double manifold of images and K's is united through the unity of the continuity of apprehension, which functionally unites the K and i belonging to every temporal phase into an apprehensional unity — an **appearance** — and unifies the appearances into a temporally flowing total appearance.

**The i-component and the K-component.** The appearance in each phase has two essentially different components: the **i-component** and the **K-component**. The former supplies the "intention toward" (*Hinzielung*); the latter supplies the **motivation** of this intention. The stream of K's determines, by way of motivation, the type and form of the "intention toward" in its elapsing. Every phase of the i-component is an "intention toward" in such a way that it penetrates the next phase — penetrates its image — by referring to it and through it: the i-component fulfills itself, penetrates the next phase, is again fulfilled, and so on. Every i is both fulfillment and fulfilling, through its apprehensional function.

This is Husserl's most precise formulation of the asymmetry identified in §51: the K-sequence *motivates* but does not itself carry the consciousness of unity; the i-sequence carries the consciousness of unity but only under the motivational governance of the K-sequence.

**The halo of quasi-intentions.** The actually elapsing series of fulfillment is only one among a manifold of possibilities. This is not an Objective fact but a phenomenological one: every phase of apprehension, in its very essence, contains a relation to *all* the possibilities. The currently actual i-phase contains, besides the "intention toward" (with its linear direction) that fulfills *a parte ante* and is at once fulfilled *a parte post*, a **halo of quasi-intentions**. Under the given K-circumstances, these quasi-intentions are not actual "intentions toward" — they are not fulfilled or fulfillments. They are only *possible* intentions: if the K's followed a different direction of change, these intentions would be motivated and actually at hand. But under the actual K-direction, they remain quasi-intentions — apprehensional moments fused with the actual i-intentions and **coloring their character**.

This is one of the most important phenomenological concepts in the lectures. The halo of quasi-intentions is what gives each appearance its character as the appearance *of a thing* — an object that could present itself from other sides, under other circumstances, in other ways. Without the halo, the appearance would be a mere succession of field-contents; with the halo, it is an appearance of something that *transcends* this particular succession and refers to infinitely many other possible successions. The quasi-intentions are the phenomenological correlate of the thing's transcendence — its being more than any actually given appearance.

**Duration and change.** If the K-stream is a stream of mere duration (the K stays constant), then K motivates the "intention toward" of the image in such a way that the image endures changelessly — i and ever again i. If the K-stream is a stream of change (K₀ → K₁), then each differential of change motivates a concomitant differential of change in the "intention toward" — the image refers in a definite line to a changing image.

**The thing as identity in all possible lines.** Here Husserl reaches the culminating formulation of the section: the thing is **not merely what is identical in this one line** — whether a line of unchange or a line of determinate change. The thing is what is identical in this line **and at the same time in all possible lines**, all possible continuities of images under all possible K-circumstances. While it stands there as a thing, it has, in terms of the apprehension, a relation to all these possibilities. It has an "intention toward" in an actual way as the intention penetrating the presently actual line. But the apprehension possesses in its essence the characteristic that makes it fit to enter into other series of fulfillments and to found the consciousness that "if K were such and such, then the 'appearance' would be so and so."

This formulation echoes and deepens §30's characterization of the thing as the "identical pole in the systematic unity of adumbrations or possibilities of adumbration." But now the characterization is kinaesthetically grounded: the "systematic unity" is the multi-dimensional manifold of K-possibilities, and the "identical pole" is what is constituted in the unity-consciousness that runs through the i-sequences under the K-motivation.

**The unity-consciousness constitutes the thing.** The unity-consciousness that develops in the continuity of appearance, along with the essentially concomitant continuity of K-motivation, **posits the unity of the thing** — it constitutes the thing. It belongs to the *sense* of this unity to be unity in a manifold of appearances, in a continuity of appearances of a determinate, ideal, infinite type. The thing is given in every actually streaming piece of this continuity and in every appearance of such a piece. In each one, the consciousness of the identity of fulfillment is alive.

But it pertains to the essence of this givenness to **leave open infinitely many possibilities** of new givenness in a determinate way as motivated possibilities. The consciousness of proper givenness, penetrating the actual continuity of images, is consciousness of givenness in an image that is fulfilled and that points ahead to new fulfillment. A line of actual givenness (most proper perception) is **bathed in a halo of quasi-intentions**. The "intention toward" is a breadth of intentions penetrating all distinguishable parts and moments of the image — and this precisely characterizes **presentation through adumbrations**, the proper presentation. To every part of the image there corresponds a part or a ray out of the ideal, completely unitary **beam of rays** (*Strahlenbündel*) of the total intention.

**Significance.** §54 is the theoretical summit of the kinaesthetic analysis. It provides the definitive answer to the question that has driven the lectures since §44: how is the identity of the spatial thing constituted in the manifold of changing appearances? The answer: through the unity-consciousness that runs through the i-sequences under K-motivation, each actual appearance carrying a halo of quasi-intentions that refer to all possible appearances under all possible K-circumstances, so that the thing stands there as the identical pole of this infinite, kinaesthetically ordered system of possibilities. The thing is not *in* any one appearance; it is the identity constituted *through* all possible appearances, actually given in each one but exhausted by none. The halo of quasi-intentions — the "if K were such, then i would be so" — is the phenomenological structure in which the thing's transcendence, its being more than any finite stretch of experience, is concretely constituted.

### **§55. System of kinaesthetic sequences and proper appearance**

Section 55 — "System of kinaesthetic sequences and proper appearance" — is the most technically developed section in Chapter 10\. It takes the general constitutive schema of §54 and works out in detail how the **proper appearance** — the side of the thing that is actually given — is constituted within the kinaesthetic-visual correlation, and specifically how the phenomena of **explicit and implicit presentation**, the gradations of **richness** in givenness, and the **privilege of clear vision** are to be understood in terms of the K-i structure. The section is long and intricate, but its central achievement can be stated concisely: it shows how the inhomogeneity of the visual field (the difference between clear central vision and unclear peripheral vision) generates a stratification *within* proper appearance that mirrors, at a finer level, the proper/improper distinction established in §16.

**Recapitulation of the constitutive schema.** Husserl begins by restating the result of §54: the mere stationary thing is constituted in a type-determined continuum of appearances in union with a continuum of motivating K-circumstances. The thing is given as enduring, unchanged, and stationary in every actually streaming piece of any such double series. Every appearance is a living consciousness of fulfillment. The total continuity of appearance is included in every actualized piece as **potentiality**: the actualized appearance contains a perception in the proper sense — a continuity of images connected by animation to a continuously penetrating "intention toward" that is always self-intended and self-fulfilled.

The "intention toward" is an **undelimited intention**: by its very essence, it does not terminate in a determinate image. It penetrates the phase and retains this character of penetration however the actual continuity of appearance may be enlarged. This unlimitedness belongs to the essence of the state of affairs.

**The halo of quasi-intentions around the K-sequence.** Just as the actual sequence of K's is one out of a profusion of unitarily intimate possibilities, so the K is at any time surrounded by a halo of apprehensional tints — quasi-intentions corresponding to these possibilities. The constant intention living in the appearance is encircled by a **dependent halo of quasi-intentions** that first give the appearance its determinate character as *the appearance of a thing*. The phenomenological structure is: "if the K's were to elapse in such and such a way, a correlative sequence of appearances of this or that kind would be motivated." These possibilities are predelineated in the essence of every actual appearance and can be related to the appearance with evidence conforming to essential law.

Without the quasi-intentions — without the reference to what *would* appear under other K-circumstances — the thing would not stand there as something unitary that could present itself from other sides. What is unitarily presented in the actual stream would have no other sides and would refer to nothing beyond itself.

**The intentional rays and the parts of the image.** The intentional rays that penetrate the actually given images connect, in a consciousness of unity, the corresponding points of the images that continually pass over into one another, thereby constituting the *same objective moment* as always coming to proper appearance. What was previously called "proper appearance" is now redescribed as the **beam of rays** of the pre-eminent intentions penetrating the images.

Within the restriction to monocular oculomotor changes, the Object comes to appearance always from the *same side* (assuming it does not partially disappear from the Objective field due to excessive eye-movement). All the living intentions motivated by K-change penetrate the relevant image, passing through to the new image.

**The inhomogeneity of the field and its consequences.** Here the analysis reaches its distinctive contribution. Husserl notes that in a homogeneous field — one in which the image could "move" while maintaining indistinguishable sameness with itself — every image in the manifold would have the same fullness and completeness of presentation, and every point of the appearing side would have an equally adequate presentational counterpart. But the actual phenomenological field is **inhomogeneous**: when an image moves toward the periphery, it never remains completely the same. The presentation of the stationary Object under mere eye-movement is necessarily a presentation through a continuity of *similar* images graduated from sameness to considerable dissimilarity.

If these present the same thing, the same side, and if every image distinguishes presentational moments within itself — and if these moments are of different richness — then a region of **richest differentiation** is distinguishable: the region of the **clearest vision** (*deutlichstes Sehen*). In this region, the image has maximum inner articulation. As the image moves away from this region, the differentiation decreases; the presentation becomes poorer, more indeterminate.

**Indeterminate and determinate intentional rays.** Husserl analyzes how the intentional rays behave as the image moves from richer to poorer presentation and back. With the elapsing of the K-circumstances, the change in the image is determined in advance, but only according to the **general type**. The indeterminateness of the change is more-precise-determinability within a certain sphere. Every new image of greater richness that presents itself offers a more precise determination. The intentional ray in the mode of indeterminateness is not itself a beam of separately distinguishable rays — though it gets explicated and determined while unfolding into a multitude of rays in the richer presentation. It has no inner separation but has its own characterization whose essence includes the *possibility* of such dispersal in the form of manifold explication.

The transition from richer to poorer presentation is different: the inner explication is not (or not completely) lost. The separation of intentional rays achieved in the richer presentation can and must survive to some extent. The thing is, from then on, "meant as it is presented in this richer presentation." But in the poorer image, a greater manifold of intentional rays penetrates a poorer manifold of distinctions. The distinguished detail "signifies more than it presents."

**Explicit and implicit presentation.** This leads to the central distinction of the section: within the sphere of proper appearances, which are all actual presentations, a distinction forms between a sphere of **explicit** and a sphere of **implicit** presentation. The completely explicit presentation — the most complete presentation within this closed manifold — presents the side of the Object so that all its intended moments are also expressly presented. In the implicit presentation, the same objective moments are intended and the same intentional rays penetrate the image, but they do not each find a corresponding distinct presentational content; the rays "stream together as one beam" and have their presentational content unitarily in content that is in itself undifferentiable.

The **priority of the completely explicit presentation** — the privilege of clear vision — is thus grounded in the kinaesthetic-visual structure: it is the presentation in which the intentional explication achieves its maximum within the closed system of the oculomotor manifold.

**Relative adequation.** Husserl connects this to the earlier analysis of optimal givenness (§§36–38). The privilege of explicit givenness becomes the privilege of interest, and the interest is fulfilled in the explicit givenness. This signifies **relatively adequate givenness** — adequation in relation to the typical manifold of motivating circumstances. The adequation resides in appearances where the level of explication is highest, where every changed objective moment finds its proper presentation. But this alters nothing regarding the fundamental fact that the presentational content of the phase is not the object; the object is what is unitary in the kinaesthetically motivated manifold of appearances.

**A possible pure oculomotor objectivity.** Husserl closes with a speculative observation: the manifold of images under mere eye-movement could *in principle* constitute an objective unity for itself — if the appearances bore no quasi-intentions referring beyond this manifold. A kind of objectivity would be constituted, one that could come to adequate givenness within the oculomotor system. It would not be a *thing* (since things require reference to movement, other sides, etc.), but it would be "a kind of Objectivity having essential traits in common with the Objectivity of the thing." This remark is significant because it identifies the oculomotor stratum as a constitutively self-contained level — a level that can be studied in its own right before being integrated into the more comprehensive constitution of the full spatial thing. It also anticipates the concept of the **phantom** (the spatial body considered purely in its visual or tactile materializing determinations, prior to the attribution of causal properties) that will play an important role in *Ideas II*.

**Significance.** §55 provides the phenomenological content for the abstract schema of §54. Where §54 described the general structure of K-motivated i-sequences and their consciousness of unity, §55 shows *how this structure plays out concretely* in the case of oculomotor perception — with the inhomogeneous visual field generating gradations of richness, the intentional rays maintaining their explication through transitions between richer and poorer presentations, and the explicit/implicit distinction organizing the sphere of proper appearance into a hierarchy of givenness. The section thus completes the analysis of the oculomotor stratum as a self-contained constitutive level and sets the stage for §56's consideration of the temporal structure of kinaesthetic sequences and §57's treatment of the total oculomotor system.

### **§56. The temporal structure of the kinaesthetic sequences**

Section 56 — "The temporal structure of the kinaesthetic sequences" — addresses a dimension of the kinaesthetic constitution that the preceding sections deliberately abstracted from: the **temporality** of the K-i process itself. The section asks what role the temporal form of the kinaesthetic stream — its speed, its reversibility, its varying density — plays in the constitution of the appearing Object.

**The temporal form of the K-i stream.** Husserl begins by noting that the entire previous analysis discussed only the *content* of the image (its location, extension, quasi-quality) without attending to the moment of temporality. But givenness is carried out as a **process in phenomenological temporality**: the total phenomenon, with its K-component and its i-component, is temporally extended. In the transition from K₀ to K₁, the images motivated thereby have their progression and stand in temporal coincidence with the K's. Like every fulfilled temporal stream, this one has a temporal form — and it can be a *changing* temporal form.

The K-stream and the i-stream can elapse **faster or slower** and can do so in varied ways, with equal or unequal velocity, according to how the temporal filling extends over the span of time. Furthermore, the streaming of the K's — and thereby the sequence of images — can be **reversed**, and again with changing temporal form.

**The irrelevance thesis.** Husserl's central claim in this section is that the temporal form of the K-i stream is, in a certain way, **irrelevant** for the appearing Object. Whether I explore the thing quickly or slowly, whether I pause longer at one phase or another, whether I reverse and retrace my steps — the same stationary thing continually stands there with unchanged content, extending the fullness of its content in a temporal form that is one and the same: a density homogeneous throughout. The varying speed and direction of my exploration do not alter what appears.

This irrelevance thesis captures a deep feature of the kinaesthetic constitution: the *content* of the constituted thing depends on which K-values are traversed and which i-values correspond to them, but not on the *temporal manner* of the traversal. The same manifold of K-i pairings, traversed in any temporal order and at any speed, constitutes the same thing. This is closely connected to the reversibility thesis of §31: the spatial thing is constituted in an appearance-manifold that can be traversed in any direction and at any rate, unlike temporal objects (melodies) whose constitution depends essentially on the temporal form of their presentation.

**And yet: temporality matters.** Husserl immediately qualifies: the temporality of the stream does have something to say about the Objectivation. Something temporal *does* appear — temporality pertains essentially to the appearing object. In the present case, the temporality has the form of the **duration of the unchanged, stationary thing**. The thing endures; its duration is constituted in the temporal stream of perception.

Where does the Objectivation of time find its "presentational foothold" in the phenomenon? In the phenomenological temporality of the appearance itself. Just as the image presents Objective location through its locality and presents Objective figure through its quasi-figure, so the temporal extension of the appearance must present the temporal duration of the thing.

Husserl then develops this in detail. The temporal extension of the K-i stream is the presentational resource for the temporal form of the Object. But the **content** of the temporal filling — what endures — is constituted through the K-i correlation, not through the temporal form. The thing's unchangedness is constituted through the fact that the same K-value always yields the same i-value (and the reversal of K always reverses i back to the original i). The thing's *duration* is constituted through the temporal extension of this process. The *rate* at which the process unfolds, however, is constitutively irrelevant to the thing's own temporal form — the thing endures with the same homogeneous density regardless of whether I explore it quickly or slowly.

**The editorial note.** A significant editorial note records that this passage (through the end of §56) was published within the time-consciousness lectures (Husserliana X). This confirms the deep connection between the spatial-constitutive analysis of *Thing and Space* and the temporal-constitutive analysis of the time-consciousness lectures. The constitution of the thing's duration through the temporal form of the K-i stream is precisely a problem at the intersection of these two fundamental constitutive domains.

**Significance.** §56 is philosophically important for three reasons:

First, the irrelevance thesis identifies what is **specifically spatial** about spatial constitution: the independence of the constituted content from the temporal manner of its perceptual traversal. This is the constitutive counterpart of the reversibility thesis (§31): spatial things, unlike temporal objects, can be explored in any order and at any speed because their identity is constituted in the *systematic correlation* of K-values with i-values, not in any particular temporal sequence. This independence from temporal form is what makes space *space* rather than time.

Second, the qualification — that temporality nonetheless enters the constitution through the duration of the thing — shows that the spatial and temporal dimensions of constitution are not simply independent. The thing is a *spatial-temporal* unity: it has both a body in space and a duration in time. The spatial constitution (through the K-i correlation) and the temporal constitution (through the temporal form of the K-i stream) are distinct but inseparable moments of the single constitutive process.

Third, the connection to the time-consciousness lectures (confirmed by the editorial note) shows that §56 is the point where Husserl's two great constitutive projects of 1907 — the analysis of spatial thing-constitution and the analysis of temporal consciousness — explicitly intersect. The constitution of the thing's duration in the temporal form of the perceptual stream is a problem that belongs simultaneously to both investigations.

### **§57. The total kinaesthetic system of the visual sphere. The oculomotor field**

Section 57 — "The total kinaesthetic system of the visual sphere. The oculomotor field" — closes Chapter 10 and with it Section IV of the lectures. It broadens the analysis from the single kinaesthetic system of eye-movement (K) to the **total kinaesthetic system** comprising all the independent but interrelated kinaesthetic variables (K, K', K'', ...) — eye-movements, head-movements, trunk-movements, locomotion — and establishes the concept of the **oculomotor field** as merely one stratum within a multi-dimensional constitutive structure. The section is transitional: it synthesizes the results of §§48–56 and charts the path forward into Section V.

**The total system of kinaesthetic circumstances.** Throughout §§48–56, the analysis was restricted to a single kinaesthetic variable: the oculomotor K, with all other bodily circumstances (head-position K', trunk-position K'', etc.) held fixed. Husserl now lifts this restriction. In reality, the kinaesthetic circumstances relevant for visual perception form a **multi-dimensional system** of independent variables. The eye-movements constitute one system; the head-movements another; the trunk-movements another; locomotion another still. Each system can vary independently, and each variation produces its own characteristic modification of the visual field.

The description given for the single oculomotor K carries over in its general structure to the total system. The coordination of the visual field — now filled this way, now that way — operates in virtue of the unity of apprehension in which "circumstances" and "appearances" are juxtaposed: the circumstances motivating, the appearances motivated. A certain field of images, filled in such and such a way, is given together with every kinaesthetic complex; a determinate change of the field of images is given with every determinate change of the kinaesthetic complex, always presupposing we are dealing with a stationary, unchanged Object. Every return of K, K', etc., into the old constellation produces the same field of images; the reversal of kinaesthetic sequences produces a reversal in the sequences of images, always in temporal coincidence.

**The associative character of the K-i connection restated.** Husserl reiterates that the connection is **associative**: the functional bond between the total kinaesthetic complex and the visual field is not a bond of essential founding but of empirical regularity. In the earlier, purely oculomotor sphere, one could imagine that all the quasi-intentions — which relate to possible appearances under broadened kinaesthetic circumstances — were absent. If K', K'', ... were absolutely constant or not present at all, the appearances would lack everything that bestows significance on them in relation to the further kinaesthetic circumstances. The entire intentional relation would be unfolded and exhausted in the manifold of intentions motivated by the K's of the eye alone.

**The complication introduced by the full system.** But the change in the images is not merely dependent on the individual K-variables taken singly; it depends on the **manifold system** (K, K', K'', ...) taken as a whole. The variation of the K''s (the non-oculomotor kinaesthetic variables), even with K held constant, determines new occurrences and manifolds of images of a **new type**. The intentional system is therefore from the very outset a very complicated one. The totality of variable circumstances forms a **closed system of possible circumstances**, and to this system there pertains a closed system of possible changes in images, which, according to its type, is determinative of the possibility of the self-unfolding givenness of a stationary field of Objects.

This is a general though now very complex type, and it includes **partial types**. We know, as it were, how a field of images looks when we keep all other circumstances constant and move only the eye. If we keep changing our bodily posture, we can then allow the system of mere eye-movements to come into play again and again. In this way the system of images that was established for K alone ever again undergoes a **transformation**. Every change of corporeal posture modifies the K's, introduces into the system of images a modification that was not predelineated in the oculomotor system itself and that was not motivated by K alone.

**New dimensions for the constitution of the thing.** Husserl draws the consequence: we thereby acquire "new dimensions for the constitution of the thing." The matter can also be expressed as follows: what was analyzed in §§48–56 was the identical, purely **oculomotor "side"** of the constituted Object — exclusively what is identical, what is constituted in the total manifold of images that, under the presupposition of a fixed corporeal posture K'₀, pertain to the total manifold of variations of the kinaesthetic ocular circumstances.

Now the K''s may run through their possible series of changes. The identical oculomotor "side" then undergoes a system of **transformations**, and it becomes the "presentational" foundation for a system of intentions relating to the totality of now possible transformations. These new intentions endow the "side" everywhere with a new and richer intentional character. What is **identical in all these "sides"** — on the basis of the manifold of appearances determined by the new intentions — makes up the **Objectivity of the higher levels**.

**The concept of the oculomotor field.** The concept that emerges from this analysis is that of the **oculomotor field**: the stratum of visual constitution corresponding to the oculomotor K-system alone, with all other kinaesthetic variables held fixed. This is a genuinely self-contained constitutive level — it has its own internal structure, its own manifold of images, its own type of K-i correlation (as §§48–56 showed). But it is only *one stratum* in the multi-dimensional constitution of the thing. The full spatial thing is constituted not in the oculomotor field alone but in the system of transformations that the oculomotor field undergoes as the further kinaesthetic variables (K', K'', ...) are varied.

The relationship between the oculomotor stratum and the higher strata is one of **foundation and transformation**: the oculomotor field provides the presentational basis, and the further kinaesthetic systems transform this basis in ways that generate the higher-dimensional Objectivity of the spatial thing. Each transformation adds new constitutive "dimensions" — not dimensions of the visual field itself (which remains two-dimensional) but dimensions of the appearance-manifold in which the thing is constituted.

**Significance and transition.** §57 is the architectural pivot of the lectures. It closes the analysis of the oculomotor stratum (§§44–56), which constituted the most detailed and technically accomplished portion of the investigation, and opens toward the analysis of the total kinaesthetic system and the constitution of three-dimensional spatiality that Section V will undertake. The key conceptual achievement is the idea of constitutive **stratification**: the thing is not constituted in a single kinaesthetic-visual correlation but in a hierarchy of strata, each corresponding to a different kinaesthetic system, each building on the previous one through a system of transformations.

The analogy with geometry — though Husserl warns against pushing it too far — is illuminating: just as the geometry of plane figures is not the geometry of the spatial body (even though every body can be resolved into plane sections), so the constitution of the oculomotor field is not the constitution of the spatial thing (even though the oculomotor stratum is a constitutive component of every spatial thing). The transition from the oculomotor field to objective space requires studying the laws according to which the oculomotor "sides" are continuously modified as the further kinaesthetic variables come into play — and this is precisely the task that Section V, with its analyses of field-amplification (§§58–62), typicality of appearance-modifications (§§63–67), and the constitution of three-dimensional corporeality (§§68–69), will take up.

The formulation that closes the section — "what is identical in all these 'sides,' on the basis of the manifold of appearances determined by those new intentions, makes up the Objectivity of the higher levels" — states in compressed form the constructive principle that governs everything to follow: the spatial thing is constituted as the identity across the system of transformations of the oculomotor field generated by the total kinaesthetic system.

## 

# SECTION V: THE TRANSITION FROM THE OCULOMOTOR FIELD TO OBJECTIVE SPACE

## Chapter 11: Amplifications of the Oculomotor Field

### **§58. The limitations of the previous analyses. Overview of the further types of modifications in appearances**

This is a major transitional section that opens Section V of the lectures ("The Transition from the Oculomotor Field to Objective Space"). Husserl steps back to survey what has been accomplished so far, names its fundamental limitation, and then lays out a comprehensive inventory of the new phenomenological occurrences that must be analyzed to complete the constitutive account. It functions as a kind of second roadmap (complementing §63's later, more refined stratification) and is one of the architectonically most important sections in the entire text.

**The limitation: only one stratum so far.** Husserl begins with a frank assessment: out of the "multi-dimensional stratification of the constitution of a thing," the preceding analyses have treated only one stratum — the constitution of identity within the oculomotor field through eye-movement alone. He draws a precise geometric analogy: this is like studying only plane figures in geometry. A body can indeed be resolved into an infinite manifold of plane sections and considered as a continuum of them, but the geometry of plane figures is not yet the geometry of the spatial body. What is missing is precisely the *laws according to which the planes and the formations lying on them are continually modified* — the modification by which formations enter into a continuous system generating spatial figures.

Husserl immediately guards against a misunderstanding of his own analogy: the system of images constituted by mere eye-movement does *not* constitute an actual plane or plane figure of objective space. Where no space is yet constituted, there is no plane; where there is no complete thing, there is no plane projection of a thing. The oculomotor field is not a geometric plane — it is a pre-spatial manifold that will only *become* interpretable as a projection surface once the full constitution of space is achieved (a point §69 will later develop explicitly).

**The inventory of new occurrences.** Husserl then catalogues the modifications that come into play once we include the fuller kinaesthetic system (the K' circumstances — head movements, bodily locomotion, etc.) alongside the K-system of eye-movement. He lists:

*Amplification of the field of objects.* Images can disappear beyond the boundary of the visual field while new images step in — objects that were not previously presented enter the field. This can happen even with mere eye-movement, but it becomes central in the fuller K-system. Husserl distinguishes two fundamental possibilities: **cyclical amplification**, where objects step out and new ones step in but ultimately the original field of objects re-appears (as when one turns around in a room — he gives a beautiful schematic notation showing how the object-series a, b, c, d cycles through b, c, d, e and eventually returns to a, b, c, d); and **infinite amplification**, where the same fields never recur and the new follows upon the new *in infinitum* (as when one walks away indefinitely without completing a revolution).

*Semblant rotation.* A continuously visible side of a stationary object appears to rotate within the series of appearances — not because the object rotates but because *we* turn. Husserl retains the word "rotation" because the phenomenon, apart from its kinaesthetic circumstances, is the same as actual object-rotation with respect to the "quasi-spatial moments of the image." Closely connected to this is the *emergence of new sides* of one and the same object: for example, viewing one square face of a cube, then turning the head so that two new faces appear while the first undergoes relative rotation. The sides are ordered determinately, and there exists the possibility of cyclical recurrence of the initial appearance. The **closure of the corporeal form** thus manifests itself in this cyclical nexus of appearances — and the completely closed body is what is identical across all such cyclical series.

*Approaching and receding.* Series of expansions and contractions of the appearing side, intrinsically interwoven with rotation.

*Covering (concealment).* One thing conceals another; the appearing side of one thing conceals sides of other things, and this is interchangeable.

**The general motivational type.** Husserl then makes crucial general observations about how these new modifications relate to the kinaesthetic systems. He insists on the universal motivational type that characterizes the appearance of rest and unchange: a determinate modification of images must belong to every KK'-modification (the combined complex of eye-movement and bodily movement circumstances), and a reversal of the modification must belong to every reversal in the kinaesthetic sequence. This is the "temporal constancy" condition. Within this type, no phenomena of approaching, receding, rotating, or concealment can arise from the K-system alone (constant K', variable K). If such phenomena *did* arise from eye-movement alone under constant bodily circumstances, rest and unchange could no longer appear — movement would have to appear instead.

**The diagonal manifold and the commutative principle.** The section closes with an important structural claim about how the different kinaesthetic systems combine. When eye-movement and body-movement vary simultaneously, there arises not two separate manifolds of images but *one* — what Husserl evocatively calls a "diagonal manifold." The modifications combine "like forces." Whether one first carries out an eye-movement while keeping the trunk motionless and then bows the body, or performs both simultaneously, is all one "apart from the temporal relations" — the end result is the same. A unitary intentional beam always traverses and unifies the images.

This is effectively a *commutativity principle* for kinaesthetic modifications: the order in which different kinaesthetic variables are varied doesn't affect the resulting appearance (setting aside temporal order). This is a deep structural requirement for the constitution of a unified spatial objectivity — it ensures that the same thing can be reached from the same position regardless of which kinaesthetic path one takes to get there.

However, Husserl notes that two types of occurrence will require *new* considerations beyond what can be handled by this general framework: the intentionality of expansion and contraction (which will constitute the infinite field and depth), and the exposition of new sides in rotation (which will constitute the closed corporeal surface).

**Why this section matters.** §58 is one of those sections where Husserl shows his architectonic mastery. It simultaneously performs three functions: it honestly delimits what the preceding analyses have achieved (a constitution of identity within the oculomotor field only); it provides a comprehensive map of the territory ahead (amplification, cyclical and infinite; rotation; receding; concealment); and it establishes the general structural principles (the motivational type, the diagonal manifold, the commutativity of kinaesthetic variations) that will govern all the subsequent analyses. The cyclical/infinite amplification distinction and the connection between cyclical nexus and corporeal closure are particularly important, because they show how the *topological* character of the thing (its being a closed surface) is constituted through the *temporal* structure of appearance-series — a point that links the *Dingvorlesung* to Husserl's broader insights about the interconnection of spatial and temporal constitution.

### **§59. Amplification of the field of Objects. Positing of the Object beyond the sphere of actual presentation**

This section addresses one of the most fundamental puzzles of perceptual constitution: how do we perceive objectivities that exceed what can be given in any single perceptual act — and indeed, how does consciousness *posit* objects and entire environments beyond the sphere of what is actually presented?

**The phenomenon: the margins of the visual field.** Husserl begins from a simple observation. Already in the case of mere eye-movement, the field of objects is not completely constant. Objects near the margin of the visual field disappear with appropriate eye-movement, and new objects enter from the margins. So there is always a "core of persistently appearing Objects" surrounded by a "marginal zone of Objects that now appear and now do not." Objects that have slipped out of the visual field are no longer perceived in the strict sense — and yet we say we perceive the room full of people, the forest, the meadow, the cornfield. What is "properly seen" at any given moment is never all that is there for our "seeing." Just as the back side of an object is co-apprehended and co-posited without being properly seen, so too is the unseen environment of the object. The presented field of objects is always a field of objects in a "world" — in a nearer and farther environment, however indeterminate, and ultimately in infinite space.

This is a profound phenomenological observation. Perception never gives us an island of objects floating in a void; it always gives us objects *in* a world, embedded in horizons of further possible experience. The question is: how do we understand this?

**Positing beyond actual presentation.** Husserl's analysis proceeds through the structure of kinaesthetic transition. While kinaesthetic circumstances remain constant, only a restricted manifold of objects presents itself. But what is properly perceived "does not count, so to speak, for itself but instead counts as an extract from a broader environment of Objects." The apprehension, and possibly the intention, *reaches beyond* proper perception. When the kinaesthetic circumstances change, each new phase ushers in a newly fulfilled visual field with a new — though partially overlapping — field of objects. In the successive elapsing of proper appearances, a *sequential perception* of a comprehensive objectivity is carried out: an objectivity that could never be perceived in a single stationary perception.

The crucial phenomenological point is that in this succession, we do not merely have various fields of objects one after another. Rather, "in the succession of perceptions one after the other, in their continuous transition, we have *one* field of Objects." The continuous succession of appearances founds the unity of one appearance — a unity that extends throughout pre-empirical time. And even this kinetic field has its further environment: "the world does not come to an end where the current kinetic perception ends."

**Complete and incomplete images.** Husserl then introduces an important distinction. There are perceptions whose objects present themselves through *complete images* — the object falls entirely within the visual field in each phase, and its side is given in a momentary or enduring appearance. And there are perceptions whose objects present themselves only through *incomplete images* — only a part of the side of the thing can be presented in the field at any one time, so that a nexus of appearances is already required to perceive even a single side. Husserl's examples are vivid: a yew-alley can have its determinate side given in a unitary sequence of perceptions as one walks along it; but the starry heavens, or the unity of a room (where the gaze must sweep right-left, above-below, etc.) require multiple interlocking sequences of appearances joined by a unitary consciousness into the unity of a total appearance of the side.

**A new inadequation.** The section closes by identifying a *new layer of inadequation* in thing-perception — distinct from the familiar inadequation whereby a thing can only appear from one "side" at a time (requiring a sequence of appearances to present all sides). Even a *single side* may transcend the visual field and require multiple sequences of appearances for its givenness. The thing — or a multiplicity of objects — may simply be too large, too extended, to fall within the visual field as a presentational field. So there is a double inadequation: the thing presents itself only from one side (requiring rotation for all sides), and even one side may present itself only partially (requiring lateral amplification).

**Why this section matters.** §59 addresses what might be called the *extensional transcendence* of the perceived world — the fact that the world always extends beyond what is actually given, and that this "beyond" is not merely inferred but is phenomenologically constituted in the very structure of perceptual apprehension. Several points are of special importance:

First, Husserl is articulating what will later be thematized as the *horizon* structure of perception — a concept central to *Ideas I*, the *Cartesian Meditations*, and the *Crisis*. The presented field is always given as an "extract" from a broader, indeterminate environment. This is not a psychological add-on but belongs to the essential structure of perceptual apprehension itself.

Second, the distinction between complete and incomplete images introduces a genuine constitutive complication. The §63 stratification (which comes later in the lectures) presupposes that these two cases have been distinguished: stratum 3 (the amplification of the oculomotor field) addresses precisely the case of objects and object-complexes that exceed the visual field. §59 is the section where this problem is first posed and its phenomenological structure laid out.

Third, the analysis underscores how the unity of the perceived world depends on the *temporal synthesis* of successive appearances. The world is not given all at once; it is constituted through the continuous joining of partial appearances into ever more comprehensive unities. This temporal dimension of spatial constitution — the fact that space is given *through time*, through the kinaesthetically motivated succession of appearances — is one of the most distinctive features of the *Dingvorlesung* and connects it intimately to Husserl's contemporaneous investigations of time-consciousness.

Finally, the section beautifully illustrates the recursive structure of Husserl's constitutive analyses: every solution generates a new problem. Having constituted the oculomotor image as a unity in manifold, we now face the question of how oculomotor fields are joined into a comprehensive field; and even that comprehensive field has its further horizon, extending ultimately into "infinite space." The constitutive project is open-ended in principle — a feature Husserl embraces rather than laments, since it reflects the essential openness and inexhaustibility of the perceived world itself.

### **§60. Objectivation on the basis of incomplete presentational means**

This section works out in meticulous detail how the constitution of spatial objectivity is accomplished when the presentational material is *extensively incomplete* — that is, when objects and object-complexes exceed what can be given in the visual field at any one time. It is the positive solution to the problem posed in §59, and it contains some of Husserl's most careful analysis of the micro-structure of perceptual constitution.

**Two kinds of incompleteness.** Husserl begins by distinguishing two forms of incompleteness. *Inner incompleteness* concerns the impoverishment of the image of an object that does fall entirely within the visual field — the fact that it is presented more or less distinctly, with more or fewer internal differentiations. *Outer incompleteness* concerns the case where the apprehension goes beyond the framework of the image that is actual at any given time — where the object or object-complex extends beyond the visual field. It is the latter that §60 investigates. Husserl immediately notes that outer incompleteness is in fact universal: any appearing thing can, with a change in kinaesthetic circumstances, be displaced beyond the margin and partially or wholly deprived of image-representation. When a thing steps entirely out of the visual field, "it does not therefore disappear, nor did it previously half-disappear; it is only unseen." We still designate the more comprehensive objective context to which the thing belongs as perceived, provided we can change gaze and body position and thereby have series of appearances in which the objects appear in their determinate order.

**The whole field modifies as a unit.** The first crucial clarification is that kinaesthetic modification touches not merely an individual image but *the whole field*. When K traverses a course K₀–K₁, it is not only the individual image *i* that changes as i₀–i₁; the entire field of images is altered as a single complex, rigorously motivated by the kinaesthetic variation. This is essential, because it means that what is constituted is not just individual objects but the *spatial context* of objects — their relative locations, distances, and orientations.

**The beam of intentional rays.** Husserl then develops what is perhaps the section's most important phenomenological concept: the image of a "beam of intentional rays" (*Strahl* and *Strahlenbündel*) penetrating the series of images. Every image presents, through each of its distinguishable pieces, a piece of the object, and the order of the objective pieces corresponds to the order of the image-pieces. But the analysis goes deeper. Every piece of an image refers to further possibilities of partitioning; it is apprehended from the outset in the sense of those possibilities, as they would manifest themselves in a richer image further along in the modification-series. The identification effected by the intentional beam penetrates the partition: as the course proceeds and inner enrichment takes place, the order is retained and merely enriched from within.

Husserl describes this with a beautiful figure: what was initially a single intentional *ray* — corresponding to an ultimate, undifferentiated part of a poorer image — acquires the character of a *beam* of rays when that part is brought into unitary relation with the corresponding part of a richer, more internally differentiated image. Each new ray within the beam posits a spatial unity, and the order of the rays is fixed, oriented according to the richer image's ordered internal structure. The originally posited unity (a single spatial part) becomes a spatially manifold whole dividing itself into inner spatial distinctions, ordered in a fixed way.

This is Husserl's phenomenological account of how perceptual detail emerges: not by adding atomic sense-data, but by the progressive internal differentiation of an already-constituted unity. The whole is always prior to the parts — parts emerge through "explication" of what was already "implicated" in the initial apprehension.

**The constitution of spatial relations between objects.** Husserl extends the analysis from individual things to their spatial context. When two things are given completely in the visual field, their images have a determinate relative location. In the kinaesthetic sequence, both undergo a unitary modification — the same modification they would undergo if they were both pieces of a single total image. This total modification includes both the modification each thing undergoes individually (constituting it as a thing) and the modification of the *distance and relative location* of the images. Through this latter modification, the spatial relation between the two things is constituted. Different positions in the visual field, under the conditions of the stationary manifold, yield different objective distances and spatial relations. Thus the fixed order of locations in the visual field grounds, through the kinaesthetic motivational structure, a fixed order in the field of objects.

**Entering and exiting the field.** The section culminates in the analysis of how objects enter and exit the objective field. Since the objects have a fixed spatial order, constituted through the unitary modification of their images, the entering and exiting can proceed only in a determinate way. Every image of an object travels over the field, continuously changing its location. Objects enter because their images emerge from the margin; they exit because their images depart beyond the margin from the other side. The entering and exiting must proceed according to the fixed order — otherwise the continuous elapsing of changes constitutive of a stationary thing would be interrupted.

Husserl provides the schematic:

(p, q, r, s)   
(q, r, s, t)   
(r, s, t, u)

— where the perceiver's eye or body moves from left to right, new objects become visible from the right, and they disappear from the visual field to the left. The schema captures the essential lawfulness: the order in which objects enter and exit is rigorously determined by their objective spatial order, and this order is itself constituted through the motivated series of field-modifications.

**Why this section matters.** §60 accomplishes several things of deep significance for the lectures as a whole.

First, it provides the constitutive account of what it means to perceive a spatial world that *exceeds* the perceptual field — not through inference or imagination supplementing perception, but through the intrinsic intentional structure of perception itself. The apprehension "reaches beyond" the image not by adding something external but through the motivational implications already present in the current appearance. This is the constitutive ground of what will later be called the *outer horizon* of perception.

Second, the analysis of the beam of intentional rays and its progressive differentiation is a remarkably sophisticated account of perceptual articulation. It anticipates in concrete detail what Husserl will later formalize (in *Experience and Judgment* and elsewhere) as the structure of explication (*Explikation*): the process by which the implicit internal richness of a perceptual object is progressively drawn out through continued perceptual engagement. The key insight is that the move from implicit to explicit is not the move from nothing to something, but the internal differentiation of an already-constituted unity.

Third, the analysis of spatial relations between objects shows that space is not constituted object by object and then assembled; rather, the spatial relations between things are constituted *simultaneously* with the things themselves, through the unitary modification of the total field. This is a holistic conception of spatial constitution — the field, not the isolated thing, is the constitutive unit.

Finally, the entering-and-exiting schema provides the foundation for §63's third constitutive stratum: the amplification of the oculomotor field through body-movement. The orderly entering and exiting of objects at the field margins, rigorously governed by the spatial order already constituted, is what makes possible the progressive constitution of a spatial world extending beyond any actual perceptual field — and ultimately extending into the "infinite space" that §59 invoked at the horizon of every perception.

### **§61. Preliminary indication of the constitution of space. Space as the ordered context of the thing**

This is one of the most philosophically ambitious sections of the lectures. Husserl provides a preliminary but sweeping account of how *space itself* — as an infinite, fixed, ordered system of locations — is constituted through the motivated streaming of image-modifications. The section bridges the analysis of field-amplification (§§59–60) and the detailed constitutive analyses to come, and it contains some of Husserl's most penetrating formulations about the nature of spatial objectivity.
