**The infinite world from finite phases.** Husserl opens by recapitulating the result of the previous sections: each perceptual phase presents only a restricted field of objects filling the visual field, yet in the exchange of images the unity of perception penetrates the continuity of phases. Successive objective complexes coalesce into ever more comprehensive manifolds. Crucially, "in the progression of the perceptual continuity, which always has the character of a unitary perception, there is constituted the appearance of infinite space and of the infinite world." This is stated almost in passing, but it is an extraordinary claim: the infinite world is not inferred or postulated — it is *constituted* in the very structure of the perceptual continuum.

**The general law of spatial constitution.** Husserl then offers what he calls an anticipatory "general consideration." It has several interlocking components:

First, the fixed order of images in the visual field plays an essential role in constituting objective order. The strictly ordered complexes of images undergo, concomitantly with the kinaesthetic series, unitary and typically determined sequences and reverse sequences of modifications. These modifications are unitary both for individual images and for the "figural constellations" of images — their ordered context.

Second, what runs through a unitary series of changes, always motivated by the elapsing of the K's, such that the same K-phases always yield the same image-phases, and the inverse K-sequences yield the inverse image-sequences, is *posited as one and the same unchanged thing*. This is the most precise formulation yet of what Husserl means by the "general type of the stationary manifold": strict, reversible, deterministic correlation between kinaesthetic circumstances and presentational content.

Husserl then states the converse: wherever an actual stream of appearances has the character of a unitary appearance of a stationary objectivity, it pertains to the *essence* of this appearance that the image-sequences be motivated by kinaesthetic circumstances in exactly this way — including the evidence that if the same K-sequence were to recur, the same image-sequence would follow. This motivated unity of modifications belongs essentially to the constitution of something identical.

Third, and most importantly: this unity of modification concerns not just individual images but the *ordered contexts* — the "constellations of places" founded by the images and their distinguishable pieces. "In the consciousness of unity that penetrates these modifications of the ordered contexts, the order of space is constituted."

**Space as the fixed identity of ordered relations.** Husserl draws out the consequences with remarkable clarity. Since the field, through its inner order, prescribes a fixed order to all images, and since the positing of unity follows the continuity in the transformations of individual images and their reciprocal orientations, "there arises the consciousness of a strictly ordered manifold of things and, ultimately, the consciousness of the world."

The fixed identity of the thing *is* the fixed identity of its space as filled in such and such a way. In this identity, every piece no matter how small, every limit of a piece, every point in its relative location, is posited as self-identical and intentionally held fast as identical — and the same applies to every orientation of one thing toward another visible together with it.

**Mediate spatial relations.** Husserl then addresses a subtle problem: how are spatial relations constituted between things that *cannot* be intuited together — things that never co-appear in a single field? His answer is that their relation is "determined only mediately." It is a *fixed* relation, because "fixed connections of order run from thing to thing through mediating things." The absolute identity and steadfastness of the mediating orders guarantee the absolute identity of the relations among things that are distant and never co-presented. Every thing can enter into the unity of perception with every other thing — this belongs to the very essence of thinghood, "without which it would have no sense" — but this unity of perception is not the unity of presentation in one temporal point or one field of images. It is a *unitary perceptual sequence* that brings one thing to appearance and then, in a sequence whose order is fixed, brings to appearance a series of mediating things terminating in the second thing.

This is a deeply important result. The unity of space — the fact that every spatial location stands in determinate distance and orientation to every other — is constituted not by some transcendent act of synthesis but through the *transitivity of mediating perceptual sequences*. The spatial relation between my house and a distant mountain is constituted through the fixed order of everything in between, secured by the lawfulness of the perceptual continuum.

**The persistence of the constituted thing.** Husserl also addresses the status of things that have exited the field entirely. "Every individual or complex thing, constituted once in an actual field, is held fast; it is what it is in the continuity of the determinate transformations under the appurtenant kinaesthetic circumstances." It remains held fast even when there is no longer any group of images presenting it. The constitution of a thing *includes* the firmly ordered nexus of possible images under possible kinaesthetic circumstances — and the appurtenant consciousness of unity, whose essence implies the evidence of the *possibility* of receiving determinate images through determinate modifications of circumstances. In other words, the constituted thing is essentially a *system of possibilities* — not just what is actually given, but the lawfully determinable manifold of what *would* be given under specifiable conditions.

**The residual question.** The section closes by posing the question that §62 will take up: how does the spatial order extend beyond the visual field? How can perception, whose phases always offer only restricted fields, become consciousness of an ever more comprehensive — and ultimately infinite — world? Husserl frames the answer by noting that in the progression from phase to phase, we never have completely new fields of objects. The modification must be a *constant modification* of the fixed order already offered; if it posits objects as identical and unchanged, it must likewise posit their order. In the simple case without concealment, new objects can enter the field only through the margins — and these must be genuinely new objects, not ones that merely become more distinct through inner enrichment of the presentation.

**Why this section matters.** §61 is philosophically central to the lectures in several respects.

First, it provides what is arguably the clearest statement in the entire text of the *constitutive principle of space*: space is constituted as the ordered context of things, through the consciousness of unity that penetrates the motivated modifications of image-constellations. Space is not a container given prior to things; it is the system of fixed relations *among* things, constituted simultaneously with them.

Second, the account of mediate spatial relations solves a classical puzzle about spatial unity. How can a single, connected space embrace things that are never co-perceived? Through the transitivity of perceptual order — the chain of mediating things linking any two spatial locations through determinate, lawfully ordered perceptual sequences. This is a constitutive analogue to the mathematical concept of a metric space defined by chain-connected distances.

Third, the characterization of the constituted thing as a system of possibilities — held fast even in the absence of actual images — anticipates the horizon-intentionality and potentiality-analysis that will become central to *Ideas I* and the later works. The thing is not the sum of its actual appearances but the identity *in* a lawfully determinable manifold of possible appearances.

Fourth, the section shows with particular clarity how the constitution of the *infinite* world proceeds from finite perceptual resources. At no point does the perceiver grasp infinity; what is constituted is the *open-endedness* of the perceptual continuum — the essential impossibility of the consecutive series of objective complexes having an end. Infinity is not a datum but a constitutive structure: the essential non-closure of the field-amplification process.

### **§62. The new entrance of images into the field. Elucidation through the example of the perception of a yew-alley**

This is one of the most concretely developed sections of the lectures. Husserl takes the abstract constitutive apparatus built up in §§59–61 and works through it in painstaking detail with a single, richly analyzed example — the perception of a yew-alley (a row of yew trees extending beyond the visual field). The section thereby demonstrates the full constitutive mechanism of field-amplification in action, and closes with a crucial excursus connecting spatial constitution to temporal objectivation.

**The example.** At a definite moment under kinaesthetic circumstances K, only the trees a, b, c, d, e present themselves in the field of images. As we gradually turn to the right, motivated by the corresponding kinaesthetic elapsing, the field shifts: b, c, d, e, f present themselves (f newly entering); then c, d, e, f, g; and so on. Husserl traces this modification with exact notation:

a, b, c, d, e → b, c, d, e, f → c, d, e, f, g

**The two dimensions of intentional connection.** Husserl identifies two distinct but interlocking intentional structures at work in this process.

First, there are the **intentional beams** that penetrate the series of images *within* each successive field, connecting images that pass over into similar images through a continuous modification of similarity, rigorously motivated by the kinaesthetic values. These beams constitute the identity of individual objects — tree b in one field is the same tree b in the next, undergoing its lawful modification. These beams operate as long as the modification transpires, ceasing when images "pass beyond the margin of the field."

Second, there is a connection among the **successive fields themselves**, insofar as they participate in the unity of the motivated course of change by which the yew-alley presents itself as sequentially given. To each determinate kinaesthetic sequence there pertains a determinate and constant course of change in the fulfillment of the same identical field of locations — certain distributions of images succeeding certain others. The stepping forth of a new tree-image from the margin is motivated, as are its determinate movement within the field and its eventual disappearance. If the motion is reversed, the entering, movement, and disappearance reverse correspondingly. Every total series of images under its kinaesthetic circumstances acquires the character of a determinate associative nexus — it points, no matter how the K's elapse, to determinately appurtenant changes, and bears within itself the implication of a manifold of other possibilities.

**The apprehensional surplus and transcendence.** Husserl insists that the mere series of images together with a directedness toward a determinate stream of change would *not yet* constitute an apprehension of the yew-alley as an objectivity transcending the images. Something more is needed: the **apprehensional character** that animates the images in each phase and founds the unity of an uninterrupted consciousness of fulfillment. Every appearance has its "apprehensional surplus" — it means more than what is strictly presented in the image. It is this surplus that justifies speaking of a visible "piece" of the yew-alley, rather than a self-standing image-complex. The complete appearance, to which this surplus belongs, finds its identifying fulfillment from phase to phase.

**Emptying and fulfilling.** The most phenomenologically subtle passage concerns what happens to tree *a* when it exits the field. Husserl's analysis is precise: the intentional ray that traversed *a* "falls into emptiness," but its intention is not lost. It remains merged in the new total appearance as an appearance of the whole objectivity, even though the alley is now visible only with regard to b through f. There takes place, with respect to *a*, a motivated "emptying" — the proper appearance of tree *a* passes over into an *empty intention*. Conversely, the empty intention from the first series (directed toward what was not yet visible) is *fulfilled* with the new entrance of f.

This dialectic of emptying and fulfilling is the engine of field-amplification. As the perceptual stream progresses, previously presented objects leave behind their "contextual intention" — they are held fast in their having-been-posited and contribute to the constituted total objectivity even after their images have vanished. Meanwhile, new objects fulfill intentions that were previously empty. The result is that "the one Objectivity that is perceived again and again, though always perceived only partially" is constituted — an objectivity with its own fixed order of parts and images, coming to visibility sequentially, piece by piece and series by series, and capable of coming to visibility in the reverse order if the kinaesthetic sequences are reversed.

**The being of the non-perceived.** Husserl draws a fundamental ontological consequence: "The Being of the non-perceived parts of the Object refers to possible, and indeed motivated, ordered sequences of self-demonstrating perceptions which connect those non-perceived parts with the actually perceived ones." This is a statement of what might be called Husserl's *constitutive verificationism* (though he would not use that term): the being of what is not perceived consists in the motivated possibility of perceiving it through determinate, firmly ordered perceptual sequences leading from what is actually perceived. The unseen tree *a* exists because there is an essentially motivated, reversible kinaesthetic path leading back to its givenness.

**Excursus: Temporal objectivation.** The section closes with a marked excursus (which Husserl himself later flagged as belonging to the time-consciousness investigations — it was also published in Husserliana X). The key insight is that the unity of the sequential and transcendent perceptual apprehension is *at once* the unity of the objectivational positing of time. In sequential perception, the phases of earlier appearance survive in fresh memory, but not as appearances of what *was*. Rather, "what was perceived earlier is not now merely presentified as something perceived earlier but is *taken over into the now*; it is posited now as still existing now." What is posited as now is not only what is currently properly perceived but also what previously existed as given and what will come to be perceived in the further phases. All of this — the currently seen, the previously seen, and the anticipatable — "endures and fills the same time."

This is a profound observation about how the spatial and temporal dimensions of constitution interlock. The being of the yew-alley as a *spatially extended, temporally enduring* objectivity is constituted in one and the same act: the temporal synthesis that unifies successive appearances is simultaneously the spatial synthesis that extends the perceived object beyond the current field. The same tree *a* that has exited the visual field is posited not as past but as *still now* — still there, still enduring, still part of the same spatial world as the currently visible trees.

Husserl closes by returning to the fundamental structural point: there is no summation of different fields of images. There is always only one field, with its fixed system of places, which neither expands nor contracts. There is also no summation of perceptions into one mega-perception in which the whole object would fall into a single enduring appearance. The unity of the world-transcending objectivity is constituted entirely *within* the perpetual passage of phases through the one visual field — through the interplay of emptying and fulfilling, holding-fast and anticipating, that animates every moment of the perceptual stream.

**Why this section matters.** §62 is the concrete heart of Husserl's account of field-amplification. Several aspects are of special importance.

The emptying/fulfilling analysis is Husserl's most detailed treatment of what it means for an object to persist beyond actual givenness. It provides the phenomenological ground for the claim in §61 that constituted things are "held fast" even without images — the mechanism is the transformation of proper appearance into empty intention, which retains its intentional force within the total apprehensional unity.

The excursus on temporal objectivation reveals that spatial constitution and time-constitution are not separate processes but two aspects of a single constitutive achievement. This interconnection will remain central throughout the rest of the lectures and connects *Thing and Space* directly to the time-consciousness investigations Husserl was pursuing in the same period.

The yew-alley example also showcases Husserl's philosophical style at its best: an abstract constitutive principle (here, the amplification of the oculomotor field through motivated entry and exit of images) is worked through with such concreteness that the reader can almost *see* the trees sliding through the visual field, the empty intentions trailing behind, and the new fulfillments arriving from the margin. It is phenomenological description in the fullest sense — making visible the invisible structures of perceptual life.

## Chapter 12: The Typicality of the Modifications of Appearances in the Oculomotor Field

### **§63. Single appearances and sequences of appearances. The stratification of the problem of constitution**

This section serves as a pivotal methodological overview — Husserl steps back to articulate the layered structure of his entire constitutive analysis and to frame the problems that the subsequent sections (§64 onward) will address.

**Single appearances vs. sequences of appearances.** Husserl begins with a fundamental distinction. For any stationary object, there are two kinds of appearance: *single appearances* (a momentary perceptual snapshot, as it were) and *sequences of appearances* (the continuous flow of changing presentations through which the object progressively gives itself). Every phase of a sequence is, or corresponds to, a single appearance — but no single appearance is ever sufficient for the *complete givenness* of a thing. Complete givenness requires, by essence, an infinite, never-closed, manifoldly infinite sequence. This is a universal feature of thing-perception.

**The problem of concealment and the visual field's narrowness.** An essential difference then emerges. Some co-existent parts of a thing can present themselves together in a single appearance — their images occupy disjunct portions of the visual field and don't cover one another. But other parts *do* conceal each other (back sides, hidden surfaces), and even parts that *in principle* don't conceal each other may still fail to present themselves simultaneously because of the *narrowness of the visual field*. The field simply can't contain everything at once. So a sequence of appearances is required, and the object gives itself "piece by piece."

This raises the central question: how can a *sequential* perception — whose phases each present only *some* members or sides — accomplish what it seems only a single, all-at-once perception could accomplish? How does perceptual consciousness constitute a unified, identical object from a temporal series of partial views?

**The stratification of constitution.** Husserl's key move here is to articulate this problem in four distinct strata, each building on the last:

1. **The most general stratum.** The thing is essentially constituted in a continuity of single perceptions on the basis of a manifold of presentational images. How does perception *transcend* the changing images and grasp, through their stream, one and the same unchanged object? This is the foundational problem of perceptual constitution as such.

2. **The oculomotor stratum (eye-movement alone).** If we abstractly isolate the case of pure eye-movement, we can treat the thing as reduced to its *oculomotor image* — the identity that persists across all the image-modifications correlated with different eye-positions. How is this identity constituted within the perpetual modification of images under their correlative kinaesthetic circumstances (K)? Husserl notes he has already treated this extensively in prior sections.

3. **The amplified field (body-movement without concealment or rotation).** Now we add a further series of bodily movements (K') beyond eye-movements, but we stipulate that no concealment or rotation occurs. Different oculomotor images become available as disjunct presentations, and the problem becomes: how is a *comprehensive* object or complex of objects — perceivable in any single position only partially — constituted through a *continuity of oculomotor fields*? This is the "amplification of the oculomotor field," which Husserl has been treating in the sections just preceding §63.

4. **The full three-dimensional thing (rotation, concealment, depth).** Finally, we bring in expansion, rotation, and concealment. The question becomes: how does a series of rotational appearances constitute a *closed corporeality* — a thing with a surface closed on all sides? This is no longer an "external amplification" of the field (adding more objects side by side) but an "inner intensification" — the oculomotor field becomes a mere *projection* of a multi-dimensional spatial thing.

**Why this section matters.** §63 is essentially Husserl's roadmap. It makes explicit the *method of stratification* — the idea that the constitution of spatial thinghood can be analyzed by successively enriching the kinaesthetic system and tracking the new types of appearance-modification each new stratum introduces. The final stratum (4) is what §64 and the following sections will take up: receding, rotating, and concealment as phenomenological occurrences in the image-field, each with its own kinaesthetic correlates. The progression from stratum to stratum also beautifully illustrates Husserl's general constitutive method: begin with the most abstract and impoverished layer, clarify its structure, then reintroduce complexity step by step, showing how each new layer transforms the sense of the constituted objectivity.

### **§64: Receding, distance, and depth as occurrences in the oculomotor field**

This section is part of Husserl's phenomenological analysis of spatial constitution — specifically, how the experience of *depth* (the near-far axis) is constituted in visual perception. It's a crucial section because depth presents a unique phenomenological problem distinct from the lateral extension of the visual field.

**The core problem: remoteness vs. distance.** Husserl begins by noting that "receding" — the experience of an object being nearer or farther — is *objectively* a relation between the object and a relational point located in or behind the perceiver's eye. But this relational point is itself *never perceived*; it falls outside the domain of possible presentation. So remoteness *as a measurable distance from an ego-point* is never genuinely given in perception. It's constituted, not directly intuited. Husserl thus draws a provisional distinction between **remoteness** (the phenomenologically accessible near/far quality) and **distance** (the objectified metric relation to an ego-point that gets constituted later, from elsewhere).

**Monocular case.** In monocular vision, remoteness presents itself through a characteristic modification of the visual image: an object *contracts* (shrinks) as it recedes and *expands* as it approaches, while preserving its figure or shape. This is a lawful, kinaesthetically motivated modification — it unfolds in correlation with certain bodily movements (the "K" or kinaesthetic circumstances), distinct from the kinaesthetic systems governing eye movement and lateral displacement. In this stream of contracting/expanding images, the *same object* appears throughout; it is apprehended as unchanged in itself, differing only in its remoteness.

**Binocular case and "relief."** Husserl then introduces a phenomenologically *different* modification that arises in binocular vision: **depth-relief** (*Relief*). When both eyes are active, the fused "competition-less double field" carries a distinctive qualitative character — a "relief" that is *intrinsic to the individual image*. This is not merely expansion/contraction but a fundamentally different sensory moment. Individual parts of the image carry their own "depth-differences." Husserl gives priority to this binocular presentation: the binocular field already has depth as an internal determination, whereas the monocular field only has form and size, and remoteness emerges only through the *temporal stream* of image-modifications.

**The key phenomenological point** is that depth/remoteness is constituted as an **oculomotor modification** — a modification of the comprehensive "oculomotor image" that integrates all eye-movements and their correlated image-changes. It belongs to its own kinaesthetic system, distinct from the one governing lateral position. This is why Husserl can say that remoteness is "independent of the position of the eyes": no matter where the eyes are directed, the same contraction-series unfolds if the perceiver recedes from the object.

**Why this matters for the broader project:** Husserl is systematically building up the constitution of three-dimensional spatial objectivity from the ground floor of kinaesthetically motivated appearance-systems. §64 shows that the third dimension (depth) requires its own constitutive account — it cannot be reduced to lateral extension — and that it involves a distinctive interplay between two types of visual field (monocular and binocular) and their respective kinaesthetic correlates. The section also shows Husserl carefully distinguishing between what is *genuinely given* phenomenologically and what is *Objectively constituted* (the ego-point, metric distance), a methodological move characteristic of the whole lecture course.

### **§65. Displacement and rotation**

This section builds directly on §64's analysis of receding/depth by introducing two further types of appearance-modification in the oculomotor field: **displacement** and **rotation**. Husserl's goal is to distinguish and interrelate the different kinds of image-modification — receding, displacement, rotation — and to show how each belongs to its own kinaesthetic system.

**Mere receding, revisited.** Husserl begins by isolating "mere" or "direct" receding — receding without any rotation. Its phenomenological signature is that the oculomotor *figure* (the shape of the image) is preserved completely while its *size* alone is continuously altered — contraction as the object recedes, expansion as it approaches. This modification is *uniform*: every distinct piece of the image undergoes the same modification, unitarily motivated by the same kinaesthetic circumstances. And if the entire field of objects is stationary, the whole field contracts or expands together. Since the visual field is always fully filled and always the same bounded system of locations, nearness means the image of one object *expands* to fill more of the field (crowding other objects out of the single appearance), while receding means it *contracts* (allowing more objects to be co-presented). The correlation is strict but it holds only in the sense of how many objects are presented in a single appearance — it's a phenomenological correlation, not a geometrical identity.

**Displacement and field-rotation.** Husserl then introduces a different kind of modification: the *displacement* of the image within the field, including its partial or total *rotation*. He is careful to specify that this is not "actual" rotation of an object in objective space — it is *apparent* displacement and rotation *in the field*, what he calls **field-rotation** (*Felddrehung*). This is a type of modification whose very possibility is predelineated in the essence of the visual (and hence oculomotor) field. It belongs to its own determinate kinaesthetic system, distinct from the one governing receding/depth.

This is an important structural claim: different types of visual occurrence — insofar as they have different **Objectivational significance** (i.e., they contribute differently to the constitution of the stationary object) — pertain to different kinaesthetic circumstances, and vice versa. The kinaesthetic systems and the types of image-modification are not arbitrarily paired; they are essentially correlated.

**Orientation and the coordinate system of the oculomotor field.** Husserl then develops the notion of *orientation* in the visual and oculomotor field. The visual field is a strictly ordered two-dimensional system with fixed orientations. The oculomotor image retains its *oculomotor orientation* as long as the same ocular position yields the same image. Eye-movement alone produces changes in the position of the image *within* the visual field, but whenever the eye returns to the same position, the image is restored — the oculomotor orientation is unchanged. However, when the *head* turns, the situation changes: the same oculomotor image now requires a different ocular position. This allows Husserl to introduce **coordinates** for the oculomotor field — a natural or "original" coordinate system predelineated by the normal head position, coinciding with the right-left and above-below orientations. Every other direction is a "mixture" of these two fundamental orientational values (a breadth-value and a height-value).

With this coordinate system in hand, **mere displacement** can be precisely characterized: it is a modification in which all parts of the image undergo the *same positional change* — for instance, a uniform shift in the breadth-direction — while the figure and size remain the same. This is phenomenologically distinct from both receding (which changes size while preserving figure and position) and rotation (which changes the orientational relations among parts).

**Why this matters.** §65 completes the initial taxonomy of appearance-modifications in the oculomotor field that §64 began. Husserl has now distinguished three fundamental types — receding, displacement, and rotation — each with its own phenomenological character, each correlated with its own kinaesthetic system. This taxonomy is essential because each type of modification plays a different constitutive role in building up the full sense of three-dimensional spatial thinghood. Receding constitutes depth, displacement constitutes lateral spatial position, and rotation will eventually (in later sections) constitute the *closed corporeality* of the thing — the fact that it has sides, a back, an interior orientation. The coordinate system introduced here also foreshadows the constitution of objective spatial orientation itself, though Husserl is careful to note (as §66 will immediately clarify) that these image-level distinctions cannot yet be naively identified with their objective correlates.

### **§66. Correction of a misunderstanding: expansion and remoteness not identical**

This is a short but characteristically Husserlian section — a self-correction delivered at the opening of a new lecture (July 22, 1907), in which Husserl retroactively tightens a terminological distinction that his previous presentation had blurred. Despite its brevity and corrective character, it makes a genuinely important phenomenological point.

**The mistake.** In §§64–65, Husserl had been describing the image-modification correlated with receding: as an object recedes, its image contracts; as it approaches, the image expands. He had then spoken loosely of the "modification of remoteness" and the "modification of expansion" as if they were the same thing — even defining "mere receding" as that expansion which occurs without rotation or change in orientation, and especially as *uniform* expansion (same shape preserved, only size altered). The problem is that this terminological identification smuggles an objective characterization into what should be a purely phenomenological description.

**Why the identification fails.** Husserl's corrective argument is elegant. What we *objectively* call "mere receding" — approaching or withdrawing in a straight line from an object — is **nowhere and never** a uniform expansion of the image. His example is vivid: if you walk toward a yew-alley (a row of trees receding into the distance), the image of the nearer trees expands much more than the image of the farther ones. The expansion is essentially *non-uniform*. And this holds, on closer inspection, for any stationary object whatsoever when approached along any straight line. Even for a single object, the parts closer to the perceiver expand at a different rate than the parts farther away.

Husserl concedes that in small kinaesthetic sequences the non-uniformity may be imperceptible — the expansion may *seem* uniform. But the very sense of the perceptual apprehension includes the possibility of progressive actualization: if you continue the approach long enough, the non-uniformity inevitably manifests. There are limits to the perceptibility of gradations in any phenomenological continuity, but the essential structure is non-uniform.

He then offers an *a priori* argument for why uniform expansion is impossible within a stationary manifold: if expansion were perfectly uniform, all parts of the object would always appear equally removed from the imaginary ego-point — but this ego-point is a definite point in objective space, and different parts of any spatially extended object are at different distances from it. Perfect uniformity is thus geometrically impossible.

**The upshot.** Expansion and remoteness must be kept terminologically distinct. They "go hand in hand" — every modification of remoteness involves a modification of expansion — but they are not identical. Expansion is the phenomenological datum, the actual modification of the image. Remoteness is the objective significance that this modification acquires in the constitutive process. Husserl resolves to call expansion "precisely expansion" and promises that the exact relation between the two will become clear as the analysis proceeds.

**Why this matters.** The section may seem minor — a lecturer catching and correcting a slip — but it exemplifies one of Husserl's deepest methodological commitments: the strict separation of the phenomenologically given (the image-modification as it actually presents itself) from the objective sense that gets constituted *through* it (remoteness as spatial distance from a point). To conflate the two is to commit a subtle version of the very mistake the phenomenological reduction is designed to prevent — reading objective, constituted determinations back into the constituting stratum. The yew-alley example also beautifully illustrates how phenomenological analysis is always accountable to what intuition actually delivers: the claim that receding \= uniform expansion seems plausible in the abstract, but concrete imaginative variation reveals that it cannot be right. This is Husserl's eidetic method in action, applied to his own prior claims.

### **§67. Continuation. Further modifications of appearances in the oculomotor field. Displacement and rotation as changes in orientation**

This is one of the richest and most architectonically significant sections of the lectures. Husserl resumes the analysis interrupted by §66's self-correction, but now pushes it far beyond a mere taxonomy of image-modifications toward the fundamental question: *which* of these modifications actually constitutes spatial thinghood, and *how*?

**The oculomotor field as a "quasi-space."** Husserl begins by recapitulating how the oculomotor field constitutes something that is already "as it were, something like space." Every qualitatively distinguished point in the visual field, tracked through the lawful variations of eye-movement, defines a fixed point of the oculomotor field. Pairs, configurations, distances, and orderings in the visual field all get "Objectivated" into a system of oculomotor places — an ordered system of positions that remains fixed across the modifications of the images filling it. We already have a system of places, each place a unity presenting itself in a manifold of appearances.

**But the oculomotor field is not yet space.** This is Husserl's crucial caveat. The oculomotor order would count as space only if all the modifications *beyond* the oculomotor system were "sunk in the realm of absolute oblivion." Just as the visual field's system of locations is not yet an *objective* order — perception doesn't posit images but posits objective unities *through* lawful image-modifications — so too the oculomotor field is not yet a field of *things*. Oculomotor unities, though they are genuine unities in manifolds, are still "images." They are not what outer perception posits as objects. Rather, it is the *lawful changes* of oculomotor unities — changes that are simultaneously changes in oculomotor location and quality — that acquire a higher apprehensional unity: the unity of the *thing*. Things arise as objectivities in a "new fixed system of order, i.e., in space."

This is a beautiful articulation of Husserl's stratified constitutive schema: each level is a genuine unity-in-manifold, but each is also merely the "image" or appearance-stratum for a still higher constitution.

**The catalogue of oculomotor modifications.** Husserl then enumerates the conceivable modifications predelineated in the essence of the oculomotor field:

*Displacement* — all points of the oculomotor image undergo the same positional change (e.g., a shift from right to left). When the whole field is displaced, some images exit the field while new ones enter from the other side. If we idealize the field as amplified to infinity, displacement means the fixating gaze must assume a new position to return to the same object.

*Rotation* — one point remains fixed while the image rotates around it, a complete rotation returning the original orientation.

*Combinations* of displacement and rotation.

*Concealment* — when a single image moves within the field, it can cover (partially or completely) a previously constituted image. This prompts an Objectivation that "holds fast to the image after it is no longer seen" — a perceptual retention of the concealed object as still there.

*Expansion* (positive and negative, i.e., contraction) — which Husserl is now careful to call simply "expansion" after §66's correction. This can be uniform or non-uniform, can encompass the whole field or only parts of it, and can be positive for one region while negative for another. Crucially, non-uniform expansion naturally produces concealment and its removal: when one piece contracts while its surroundings don't, something previously hidden steps forth from behind it, and conversely.

Husserl notes that in concrete experience, these modifications never occur in pure isolation — they are "mere abstractions," and actual appearance-streams involve all of them in shifting, intertwined relations. He also makes the important methodological remark that this general catalogue of *possible* modifications doesn't yet ask *which* of them actually function presentationally for the constitution of stationary objectivity.

**The decisive claim: expansion is what constitutes the thing and space.** This is the section's most consequential argument. Husserl contends that displacement, rotation, and concealment alone — no matter how elaborately combined — would only ever yield an "oculomotor Objectivity, simply somewhat amplified." The "object" constituted through these modifications alone would still be a kinaesthetic image, not a thing. It would be what remains identical through displacements and rotations, but it would have no *depth*, no third dimension, no spatial volume.

It is **expansion** — the "multiform system of expansions" — that makes possible a "new dimension which creates a thing out of the image and space out of the oculomotor field." Husserl is emphatic that this "new dimension" is not analogous to the geometer's generation of space by parallel-displacing a plane. That geometrical operation presupposes space already given and moves a surface *within* it. But the oculomotor field is not a surface embedded in pre-given space; there is no pre-given distance between two oculomotor fields analogous to the distance between two positions of a plane. Rather, it is a matter of "actually generating something as new, something not yet there" — constituting spatial depth for the first time through the lawful coordination of expansion-modifications with their kinaesthetic motivations.

The principle of this generation is the same as the one operative at every lower level: lawful changes of images arise under lawfully coordinated kinaesthetic circumstances, fuse into a typical system, and acquire apprehensional unity. What persists as identical through all displacements, rotations, concealments, *and* expansions is the **thing**. And the system of order in which this identity is posited is **space**.

**The final supplement: the Ego-point and full spatiality.** Husserl closes with a crucial addendum. Even displacement, rotation, and concealment contribute *something* to Objectivation beyond the oculomotor level — they provide a surplus. But without expansion, the "upon one another" (things lying over and concealing each other) acquires no relation to the "beside one another." What is missing is the integration of the Ego, through the Ego-point, into space — the relation of all spatial appearances to this relational center. Only when expansion is added do we have the full presentational material capable of presenting space. This ties back to §64's distinction between remoteness and distance: expansion is what eventually makes possible the constitution of distance from the Ego-point, and thus the full sense of three-dimensional spatial objectivity in which the perceiver is situated.

**Why this section matters for the whole.** §67 is arguably the most important section in this entire cluster (§§63–68), because it states explicitly the *constitutive principle* of spatial thinghood: the transition from oculomotor image to spatial thing is accomplished by expansion. It also exemplifies the multi-leveled structure of Husserlian constitution at its most rigorous — each stratum is a genuine unity-in-manifold that nonetheless functions as mere "appearance" for a higher level — and it guards against the temptation to model phenomenological constitution on geometric construction.

### **§68. The special significance of expansion for the constitution of space**

This section takes up the thesis announced at the end of §67 — that expansion is the decisive modification for constituting spatial thinghood — and subjects it to a critical probing. Husserl's question is essentially: *how can expansion, given its seemingly boundless variability, serve as the basis for constituting something as rigidly structured as space?*

**The problem: expansion as an ἄπειρον.** Husserl begins by raising a genuine worry about his own thesis. The visual field, considered as a continuous two-dimensional manifold, is comparable to a plane (with the caveat that phenomenological continuity doesn't satisfy the subtleties of mathematical continuity like the Archimedean axiom). In such a manifold, any closed figure can be continuously transformed — by expansion, distortion, deformation — into any other figure whatsoever. So expansion seems to be a "boundless manifold," an *apeiron* (ἄπειρον — Husserl reaches for the Anaximandrian term for the unbounded, indeterminate), which would seem to "defy every givenness of unity." If any image can be deformed into any other, how could expansion-modifications ever yield a determinate, identical objectivity?

**The answer: lawful selection.** Husserl's response draws on everything he has established about the constitutive role of kinaesthetic motivation. Not every arbitrary expansion has constitutive significance. What we have already seen throughout the lectures is that empirical unity — the givenness of an identical, self-same object — requires *lawfulness* in the manifold of appearances: "laws of selection governing the continuous transitions of presentations under the respective kinaesthetic circumstances." Out of the innumerable possible image-transformations that the visual field's nature permits, only certain *lawfully delimited series* of variations count as the appearance-manifold of a stationary, identical thing.

**The norm: absolutely identical objectivities.** Husserl introduces a crucial methodological point. Space is constituted *primarily* with absolutely identical objectivities — things that are stationary and qualitatively unchanged. These serve as the "norm or basic standard" for the subsequent constitution of changing objectivities. The thought is that once the constitution of space is secured through the simplest case (the unchanging thing), this spatial framework then furnishes the "field for the possible movements of things that now change qualitatively and now do not." Constitution proceeds from the maximally constrained case outward.

**The structure of lawful motivation.** Husserl then spells out what this lawfulness requires. If an absolutely unchanged objectivity is to be constituted in the stream of continuous presentational foundations, then for every possible variation in the kinaesthetic circumstances, there must be a *strictly determinate* kind of appearance. The manifold of appearances must form a "lawfully unitary type of motivation" — they must be bound together in a lawfully coherent way, constituting a "generally delimited selection out of all the existing possibilities," coordinated with an "equally powerful and generally determinate manifold of motivating circumstances."

Regarding the motivating circumstances themselves, Husserl makes an important clarification. We cannot speak of a "selection" among them, because we must take together the *total* manifold of circumstances that de facto play a motivating role. This doesn't mean every logically conceivable motivating variable (one could increase these ad libitum), but rather the manifold that has actually entered into associative interweaving with variations of presentational content — the kinaesthetic systems that are really operative for the perceiver. In the total apprehension of every objectivity, this entire manifold has "its representative" and is thereby available in thought and phantasy. We can run through the manifold in its various directions and follow up the appurtenant motives, starting from any assumed initial appearance of something stationary.

**The principle of discrimination.** The section closes with a crucial discriminative principle. The manifold of presentations of a *stationary* objectivity is and must be a *limited* one. Out of the innumerable possibilities for image-variation that the visual field permits, the stationary manifold renders prominent only certain lawfully delimited series. Any *discrepant* series of variations — one that doesn't fit the lawful type — is precisely what constitutes *change*: what appears therein is not something stationary but something changing. Every expansion, and indeed every distinguishable occurrence in the visual field, acquires objective significance — it *means* something, contributes to the constitution of either stationariness or change. But "not every arbitrary expansion can have constitutive significance for the constitution of an absolutely identical objectivity, and thereby for the constitution of an absolutely fixed spatiality."

**Why this section matters.** §68 addresses the deepest structural worry that Husserl's own account generates. §67 claimed that expansion is what creates "a thing out of the image and space out of the oculomotor field." But expansion, taken in the abstract, is protean and indeterminate — it seems to give you *too much* freedom, not the rigid structure of three-dimensional Euclidean space. Husserl's answer is that the determinacy doesn't come from expansion alone, but from the *lawful coordination* of expansions with kinaesthetic circumstances. The visual field's formal possibilities are filtered through the motivational structure of the perceiving body, and only certain lawfully coherent patterns of expansion count as presentations of an identical, stationary thing. This is a beautifully anti-formalist point: the structure of space is not read off the abstract topology of the visual field, but emerges from the interplay between that field and the embodied perceiver's system of possible movements. The section also establishes the constitutive priority of the stationary thing — space is first constituted as the form of order of unchanging objectivities, and only then becomes the arena within which change and movement are possible. This priority echoes a deep Husserlian conviction that runs through the whole lecture course: constitution proceeds from the simplest and most constrained cases, and richer objectivities are constituted by progressively loosening the constraints.

### **§69. Presentation of a three-dimensional Object in two-dimensional images**

This is a remarkable section in which Husserl temporarily steps *outside* the phenomenological attitude in order to use the geometry of constituted Euclidean space to illuminate, retrospectively, the lawful structure of the expansion-modifications he has been describing phenomenologically. The section is at once geometrical and deeply philosophical, and it culminates in a crucial anti-deductivist thesis about the constitution of three-dimensional space.

**The methodological shift.** Husserl signals immediately that what follows is "not phenomenological, but instead on the basis of an apriori consideration of constituted space." He is going to reason *from* already-constituted Euclidean space and the position of the Ego-point within it *back* to the lawfulness that must reside in the motivated streaming of expansions. This is a retroactive clarification — using the product of constitution to shed light on the constituting process — and Husserl is fully self-conscious about the shift. It is a methodological strategy he employs elsewhere: constitutive analysis can be supplemented by what we might call a "reverse engineering" from the constituted objectivity.

**The basic situation.** To every bodily location of the perceiver there pertains a determinate oculomotor field, containing a presentation of the stationary objectivity in the form of some oculomotor image. The oculomotor field is two-dimensional, and accordingly so is every image within it. Yet every image in this coordinated series is apprehended as possessing the sense of the *same three-dimensional Object*. Conversely, the three-dimensional object only ever appears in the form of a two-dimensional image. The object unfolds in a determinate manifold of two-dimensional images belonging to one and the same "plane" manifold of places. The continuous succession of these images is characterized as a continuous sequence of expansions of the "same" image under the relevant kinaesthetic transitions.

This is the fundamental puzzle of spatial constitution stated with maximal clarity: how does a three-dimensional objectivity present itself through an essentially two-dimensional medium?

**The projection model.** Husserl's answer invokes the geometry of central projection. Formally, the various expansions that the oculomotor image undergoes behave exactly like the *projections of a geometrical body onto a plane* — or, better, like colored plane images of the corporeal thing. Expansion applies at once to both figure and coloration, but Husserl focuses on the figure.

Every expansion has a relationship to the Ego-point: it presents a change in remoteness from that point. Stronger positive expansion corresponds to approach, stronger negative expansion (contraction) to receding. For non-uniform expansion — which, as §66 established, is always what actually occurs — more strongly expanded parts of the image correspond to lesser remoteness, more weakly expanded parts to greater remoteness. Through the motivational relation to kinaesthetic circumstances, every image is inserted apperceptively into a lawfully determinate context of expansion, and simultaneously the Ego is assigned its changing position in space. A strictly determinate correlation is thereby constituted: a determinate image from the expansion-manifold pertains to every determinate spatial point as the position of the Ego.

Husserl then asks whether there is a *plane* that so intersects the "beam of rays of remoteness" issuing from the Ego-point toward the visible points of the object that the projected images on this plane correspond exactly to the oculomotor images. In the continuum of such plane central projections, the lawfulness of expansion would receive an "Objective and spatial expression." The conditions for such a plane are: it must be able to receive projections of every possible perceptual object from all spatial locations of the Ego-point. These conditions are satisfied by a *moving plane always parallel to the frontal plane*, situated so that it lies in front of every possible object — presupposing that between the Ego-point and every possible object there is an open, unbridgeable distance. (This is the phenomenological correlate of the fact that we can never actually *reach* the zero-distance point to an object in perception.) A fixed plane can then be substituted, upon which the images vary as they would on the moving plane in its movement.

**The anti-deductivist thesis.** Husserl now makes what is arguably the section's most important philosophical claim. It is clear a priori that occurrences in a two-dimensional manifold like the oculomotor field *can* be represented univocally through occurrences on a plane. But — and this is the critical point — "not every arbitrary lawful variation of images that are continuously expanding, and whose orientation is constantly changing, *needs* to be apprehensible through the projection of a three-dimensional body in three-dimensional space."

In other words: there is nothing in the abstract structure of a two-dimensional manifold of continuously expanding images that *logically necessitates* the constitution of specifically three-dimensional Euclidean space. The oculomotor field's conversion into a manifold of expansion and displacement does not *deductively entail* three-dimensionality. We cannot derive the specific structure of our space a priori from the formal possibilities of image-modification.

What *can* be said a priori is more modest: if a fixed objectivity is to be constituted at all in streaming manifolds of presentation, then a "fixity of lawful order must hold sway" in the appearances and their motivational coordination. The forms of this lawfulness are further circumscribed by the requirement that the objectivity be a *thingly* one — things posited in fixed relations with possibilities of movement and change remaining open. Identity in movement presupposes a "perpetual continuum of places and specifically one that is in itself congruent."

**Why this section matters.** §69 does several things of deep significance:

First, it gives a precise geometric model for how three-dimensional objectivity is constituted through two-dimensional appearances: central projection from the Ego-point. This is not offered as a phenomenological description but as a retrospective geometric clarification — a way of understanding the lawful structure of the expansion-manifold once we already know what it constitutes.

Second, and more profoundly, it establishes that the three-dimensionality and Euclidean character of space is *not deducible* from the formal structure of the constituting manifold alone. This is a remarkable result. It means that the specific structure of our space is not a formal-logical necessity but something that emerges from the *particular* lawful coordination of appearances and kinaesthetic circumstances that happens to obtain. There is a contingency — or at least a factual specificity — at the heart of spatial constitution. Different lawful coordinations could, in principle, constitute spaces of different dimensionality or structure. Husserl leaves open that this could be "founded mathematically in every case," gesturing toward the possibility of a rigorous mathematical treatment of alternative constitutive structures — a thought that connects to the investigations of non-Euclidean spatial constitution in his supplementary texts (Appendix XII concerns the constitution of Riemannian things).

Third, the section crystallizes the relationship between the constituting and the constituted in a particularly illuminating way. The geometric projection model is drawn from constituted space and projected *back* onto the constituting process. This retroactive illumination is philosophically legitimate because constitution and constituted objectivity are correlative — understanding one always helps clarify the other — but it must not be confused with a genetic or causal explanation. Husserl is not saying that the visual system *performs* central projections; he is saying that the lawful structure of the expansion-manifold is *formally equivalent* to the structure of central projections in Euclidean space.

## Chapter 13: The Constitution of Space Through the Conversion of the Oculomotor Field into an Expansional and Turning Manifold

### **§70. The appurtenance of images to one identical Object**

Section 70 opens Chapter 13 ("The Constitution of Space through the Conversion of the Oculomotor Field into an Expansional and Turning Manifold") and addresses a problem that has become pressing as the analysis moves beyond the simple oculomotor field toward the full constitution of three-dimensional spatiality: the question of what determines that a given set of images in the oculomotor field **belongs to one and the same Object**, rather than to different Objects.

**The problem of appurtenance.** Within the merely oculomotor field, considered as a stationary field, there is no decisive principle for determining which images belong to the same thing. Qualitative discontinuity gives images separate existence — the Object is the unity of a figure that is qualitatively distinguished from its surroundings. But this purely field-internal criterion is insufficient. Two bordering images can be grasped as a single Objective unity, or the entire field can; disjunct images may belong to one Object (as when parts of a thing are spatially separated in the field), while contiguous images may belong to different Objects (as in concealment, where one thing covers another). The merely oculomotor field, insofar as it is unitary and stationary, possesses no principle that could decisively anticipate the future constitution of the thing with regard to the nexus of images appertaining to one Object.

**The solution: continuity of modification under kinaesthetic motivation.** When we pass from the stationary oculomotor field to the *continuity* of oculomotor fields — the change in orientation and expansion — the unity of appurtenance is constituted and principles of conjunction emerge. Husserl analyzes three types of modification in ascending order of complexity:

**Displacement.** What is displaced remains the same; the streaming on of motivating circumstances motivates the transition from image to image in such a way that the image undergoes no inner change. Only the coordinations of kinaesthetic circumstances change, and thereby the orientation in the field. Displacement already produces a bond of unity: the same image, merely relocated.

**Rotation.** Rotation goes further. The change is continuous, and there exists a **reciprocal and univocal correspondence** with respect to all distinguishable moments of the images: each moment has its correspondent in every other image, and each one has undergone the same modification. An identity penetrates the constant modification, and insofar as the modification unitarily penetrates a whole of distinguishable parts, the general proposition holds: in the result of the modification, every part that has arisen continuously out of one part of the original image presents the same thing. Unchanged preservation of the image in the phenomenological time-stream counts as a limit-case of modification.

**Concealment.** The most complex case: one image travels by and conceals another. According to the rule already established, the image not yet obliterated remains, in all its visible parts, a presentation of the same thing. But as concealment progresses, more and more is obliterated — possibly the entire Object. When the kinaesthetic movement is reversed, however, the Object is continuously rebuilt. This constant demolition and rebuilding under concealment is a system of modifications **strictly motivated by kinaesthetic circumstances**: a certain stage of concealment pertains to every kinaesthetic situation.

**The intentional structure of concealment.** Husserl provides a particularly penetrating analysis of what happens intentionally during concealment. The visible refers to the invisible, but the visible does not include in its presentational content the co-motivated intention pertaining to the invisible. The beam of rays of alert intentions penetrating the concealing image and the beam penetrating the concealed image are **separate beams** — there is no bond of presentational unity between them. As an Object is progressively concealed, its full intentions are converted into **empty** ones — its concrete intentions become "imaginary," forfeiting their presentational content. Yet they still retain the character of perceptual intentions, motivated as alive in the motivational nexus. The total motivational unity *demands* that this concealment take place in such and such a way, and that upon reversal of the kinaesthetic sequences the Object is built up again in a complete presentation.

The concealing Object thereby acquires its phenomenal relation to the concealed Object: an Object-point of the concealing Object corresponds to every point of the disappearing Object, since the same position of the oculomotor field that just now presented Object *a* now presents a point of Object *b*. But a content is presentational only in its determinately characterized nexus of motivated modifications. The content in question is integrated into the nexus constitutive for the concealing Object; the concealed Object demands a different content at that place. The content cannot belong to both simultaneously.

**Significance.** §70 addresses one of the most fundamental constitutive problems: the individuation of things within the perceptual field. The analyses of §§48–57 established the formal structure of the K-i correlation, but they operated primarily with a single Object or with the total field as an undifferentiated unity. §70 now asks: within this total field, what determines that *these* images belong together as presentations of *one* thing and *those* images belong together as presentations of *another*? The answer is: the **lawfulness of the motivated modifications**. Images belong to one Object insofar as they are connected by continuous modifications (displacement, rotation, expansion) that are univocally motivated by kinaesthetic circumstances and that exhibit the reciprocal-univocal correspondence of their distinguishable parts. The individuation of things is not given by any static, field-internal criterion (like qualitative discontinuity alone) but by the dynamic, kinaesthetically motivated structure of the appearance-modifications.

The analysis of concealment is particularly important because it shows how the unity of an Object is maintained even when the Object is entirely absent from the visual field. The empty intentions that survive concealment — motivated but unfulfilled, retaining their perceptual character within the motivational nexus — are the phenomenological ground for the Object's continued existence as a perceived reality even when no presentational content corresponds to it. This connects back to §18's analysis of empty and full intentions and to §16's proper/improper distinction, now given their concrete constitutive realization within the kinaesthetic-visual system.

The section also sets the stage for §§71–72, which will analyze the two fundamental types of modification — expansion (approaching/receding) and turning — that convert the oculomotor field into the three-dimensional manifold in which spatial corporeality is constituted. The principles of appurtenance established here — continuous modification under kinaesthetic motivation, reciprocal-univocal correspondence, the intentional structure of concealment — are the principles that will govern this transition.

### **§71. The constitutive function of expansion (approaching and receding)**

Section 71 analyzes the first of the two fundamental types of appearance-modification that convert the oculomotor field into a three-dimensional spatial manifold: **expansion** (*Erweiterung*). While §70 established the general principles of appurtenance — what makes images belong to one and the same Object — §71 now examines in detail how the specific modification of expansion constitutes spatial depth (the dimension of approaching and receding) and how the interplay of expansion with concealment generates the phenomenological basis for the corporeality of the thing.

**Expansion as a principle of selection and unification.** Husserl opens by noting that expansion provides a particular principle for distinguishing, out of the multiplicity of delimitations in the oculomotor field, which images pertain to the unity of a single thing. If the oculomotor field is already integrated into the manifold of possible expansions — if it contains sufficient indications pointing to a type of possible motivated change — then the *different ways* in which expansion distributes itself among the various pieces of the field gives determinateness to the apprehension of things and excludes the accidental and arbitrary from the delimitation of presentations.

When expansion applies unitarily to the whole field (e.g., everything expands together in the same lawful form), the thingly Objectivity must appear as a single, unique one. But typically, expansion distributes itself differently to different pieces of the field. The unity of a continuous expansion stands out against what does not participate in it or what expands in a different typical form. According to the constitutive principle established in §70: what pertains to the unity of a continuous expansion pertains to the unity of a presentation.

Yet Husserl immediately adds a complication: different types of expansion can still be joined into the unity of one Object. Consider two simultaneously visible, mutually bounded surfaces of a polyhedron that present themselves in different expansional modifications. The two series of modifications belong together — they pertain to the same kinaesthetic circumstances, they stream on together, and they form a determinate type of unitary modification. Even disjunct images (those without mutual boundary) can constitute one Objectivity if they are affected by one kinaesthetically related, typical modification — as in the case of concealment.

**Mere expansion without concealment.** Husserl first considers the simplest case: an image that undergoes mere expansion without any loss of presentational contents through concealment. The example is the oculomotor presentation of an ever-visible plane surface, such as a face of a cube. The presentations continuously pass over into one another with a **reciprocal and univocal correspondence** among all points, parts, and correlations. Each correspondent is a continuous modification of its predecessors and therefore presents the same thing. The oculomotor figure has changed — the whole figure, every extractable constellation of points, every mere pair of points. Each oculomotor distance is constantly changed, but in a kinaesthetically strictly motivated way — and therefore it presents the **same Objective distance**.

This is the constitutive core of expansion as a spatial modification: the systematic, motivated change in the pre-empirical distances between image-points, under univocal correspondence, presents the *unchangedness* of the Objective distances. Expansion at the pre-empirical level constitutes approaching and receding at the Objective level — the thing does not change, but its "nearness" or "remoteness" changes, and this is presented through the lawful modification of pre-empirical magnitudes.

**Internal concealment.** But mere expansion is an ideal limit-case. Husserl's more phenomenologically realistic analysis shows that expansion characteristically introduces **internal concealment**. His example is an undulating surface: as the modification progresses, a pair of points — one on a crest, one in a trough — undergoes continuous expansion, but then the distance suddenly disappears as the two points coincide in the image. The one point coincides with ever new points of the image presenting new, separate points of the Object. Pieces of the already Objectivated surface are missing from the actual presentation but are preserved in the consciousness of unity in the mode of being concealed. These are occurrences of **internal concealment** within the stream of unitary expansion — the Object conceals parts of *itself* from view.

**External self-concealment.** There is a further type of concealment that "often goes hand in hand with expansion." In this case, the Object conceals itself *from the outside*: as the modification progresses, the sphere of presentation shrinks from the periphery. Husserl illustrates with two examples. A round calotte that, in the quasi-rotation presented through expansion, offers itself ever more laterally: the peripheral parts of the image expand unceasingly, while other parts near the opposite edges shrink and finally disappear — concealed by the expanded part of the image, i.e., by the corresponding side of the Object itself. Or a cube whose pair of lateral surfaces undergoes modification such that one surface expands continuously while the other contracts until it reduces to a line coinciding with the edge of the first — thus disappearing behind the surface that remains visible. Yet the concealed surface is intentionally preserved in the mode of something concealed, specifically as pertaining to the whole Object, because the modifications of both surfaces — including the determinately qualified occurrences of nullification and temporally enduring concealment — belong to the unity of a motivated total modification.

**Significance.** §71 provides the concrete phenomenological content for one of the two modifications that §72 will characterize formally as the **linear** modification (as opposed to the cyclical modification of turning). Expansion is the modification through which the dimension of *depth* — the near/far dimension — is constituted. Three results are essential:

First, expansion constitutes the *identity* of Objective spatial magnitudes through the *change* of pre-empirical magnitudes. The same Objective distance is presented through continuously changing oculomotor distances, and this identity-in-change is what constitutes the consciousness that the thing approaches or recedes without changing in itself.

Second, the interplay of expansion with concealment — both internal and external self-concealment — shows that the three-dimensional corporeality of the thing is not constituted through a simple, transparent expansion of the two-dimensional image but through a complex process in which parts of the Object continually emerge and disappear. The thing's corporeality — its having volume, surfaces that face away, interior structure — manifests itself precisely in these patterns of concealment and unconcealment within the stream of kinaesthetically motivated expansion.

Third, the principle of **self-concealment** — the Object concealing parts of itself by its own corporeal form — is what fundamentally distinguishes a three-dimensional body from a two-dimensional surface. A plane surface can undergo mere expansion without concealment; a three-dimensional body cannot. The occurrence of self-concealment within the expansional modification is therefore the specific phenomenological index of corporeality, and its analysis in §71 provides the constitutive ground for the transition from the two-dimensional oculomotor field to three-dimensional spatial objectivity that §72 will complete through the analysis of turning.

### **§72. The constitutive function of turning. The linear modification of expansion and the cyclical modification of turning**

Turning (rotating around the Object or the Object rotating before the perceiver) produces a *cyclical manifold*: after a complete rotation, the same side returns. The cycle can be traversed in either direction (reversibility). The combination of the linear manifold of expansion and the cyclical manifold of turning transforms the two-dimensional oculomotor field into a three-dimensional spatial manifold. The two-dimensional field, amplified by the one-dimensional linear modification of expansion, yields three dimensions.

**Key distinction:** *Linear (orthoid) manifold* (expansion: one-dimensional, open) vs. *cyclical manifold* (turning: one-dimensional, closed). Their conjunction with the two-dimensional field constitutes three-dimensional space.

### **§73. Summary. The constitution of space and the levels of that constitution**

Husserl summarizes the levels of the constitution of space: (1) the pre-empirical sensory field (two-dimensional, pre-phenomenal); (2) the oculomotor field (the field amplified by eye/head kinaesthesis, still two-dimensional); (3) three-dimensional space, constituted through the conjunction of the oculomotor field with the linear manifold of expansion and the cyclical manifold of turning. Space is thus constituted step by step, each level presupposing and building upon the previous one.

**Key distinction:** The *levels of spatial constitution* form a hierarchy: pre-empirical field → oculomotor field → three-dimensional space. Each level is constituted through the introduction of new kinaesthetic systems.

---

## Chapter 14: Supplementary Considerations

### **§74. Delimitation of the individual thing out of the nexus of things in the case of a stationary Objectivity**

Things never appear in isolation but in nexuses. The *delimitation* of an individual thing from its environment is itself a constitutive accomplishment: the thing is marked off by the discontinuities in its spatial filling (boundaries where one thing ends and another begins or where the thing meets empty space). In the case of a stationary Objectivity, these boundaries are fixed and the individuation of things is unambiguous.

**Key distinction:** The *thing as individual* (delimited from its environment) vs. the *nexus of things* (the wider Objective context). Individuation is constituted through spatial discontinuities.

### **§75. Addendum. The apprehension of a concealment as the distance of a remoteness**

When a thing is concealed behind another thing, the concealment is apprehended as constitutive of spatial relations: the concealed thing is posited as "behind" the concealing one, at a determinate (if vague) distance. Concealment is thus not a mere absence but a positive constitutive phenomenon that contributes to the spatial ordering of things.

**Key distinction:** *Concealment* (a positive constitutive phenomenon, positing the concealed thing at a distance) vs. *mere absence* (non-givenness without spatial significance).

### **§76. The modes of givenness of empty space**

Empty space — space not filled by any thing — is itself given in perception, but in a distinctive mode. It is given as the "between" of things, the gap or interval that separates them. Its givenness is essentially dependent on the givenness of the things that bound it; empty space is not perceived independently but only as the spatial interval constituted between perceived bodies.

**Key distinction:** *Filled space* (given through the thing's materializing determinations) vs. *empty space* (given as interval or gap between things, dependent on them for its givenness).

### **§77. The mode of givenness of the interior of a body**

The interior of a body is not given in the same way as its surface. Normally, the interior is improperly apprehended — co-intended but never directly presented. Breaking the thing open would give the interior as a new surface, but this changes the thing itself. The interior thus has a distinctive mode of givenness: it is always emptily intended, never properly presented without altering the thing.

**Key distinction:** The *surface of a body* (properly presentable through appearance) vs. the *interior of a body* (always emptily intended, never properly given without destruction of the body as it is).

---

# 

# SECTION VI: THE CONSTITUTION OF OBJECTIVE CHANGE

## Chapter 15: Qualitative Changes of the Perceptual Object

### **§78. The nexus of spatial form and qualitative filling**

Husserl addresses the inseparable nexus of spatial form and qualitative filling in the constitution of the thing. A spatial form without any filling is an abstraction; so is a filling without any form. The thing is always a determinate spatial structure filled with determinate qualities. Any change in the thing must be a change in its form, its filling, or both.

**Key distinction:** *Spatial form* (the geometrical-structural aspect of the thing) vs. *qualitative filling* (the sensuous content that covers the form). These are inseparable aspects of the concrete thing.

### **§79. The thing as what is identical in qualitative change**

When the thing undergoes qualitative change (e.g., changing color), it remains identifiable as *the same thing* through the constancy of its spatial form and the continuity of the change. The thing is the identical substrate that persists through its varying qualities. The identity of the thing is not a separable feature but the unity constituted in the continuous synthesis that links the pre-change and post-change appearances.

**Key distinction:** The *identity of the thing* (persisting through change) vs. the *succession of qualities* (the changing filling). Identity is constituted through continuity and spatial constancy.

### **§80. The lawfulness of the modifications of appearances in the case of qualitative change**

Husserl analyzes the lawful structure of appearance-modifications when the thing changes qualitatively.
