The kinaesthetic-imagistic functional correlation must be modified: whereas for an unchanged thing the same kinaesthetic state always yields the same image, for a changing thing the same kinaesthetic state yields different images at different times. The change in the thing introduces a *temporal parameter* into the functional correlation, and the lawfulness of the appearance-modifications reflects the lawfulness of the Objective change.

**Key distinction:** *Functional correlation for an unchanged thing* (same K-state → same image, regardless of time) vs. *functional correlation for a changing thing* (same K-state → different image at different times, the difference reflecting Objective change).

## Chapter 16: The Constitution of Mere Movement

### **§81. The founding of all change in identity**

Chapter 16 turns from qualitative change (the topic of the preceding chapters) to the constitution of **mere movement** — i.e., movement abstracted from any simultaneous change in quality or form.

The section makes one central architectonic argument: **all change presupposes identity, and identity presupposes continuity, and continuity has a determinate founded–founding structure in which spatial extension is the ultimate foundation.**

Husserl's reasoning proceeds in three layers:

**1\. Change requires a norm in rest.** Husserl begins by transferring what he established for qualitative change to the case of movement. "Becoming otherwise" (*Anderswerden*) always refers back to a "not otherwise," i.e., to a remaining-identical. Change thus has its *norm* in the stationary state (*Ruhe*). Each phase of change must be intelligible as a phase that *could* be extended into an enduring, unchanged state — otherwise it wouldn't count as a phase of *this* thing's history at all. The deviations from the stationary manifold of appearances can't be arbitrary; they're constrained by the requirement that unity be preserved across the temporal flow.

**2\. Continuity is required, but not all moments contribute equally to unity.** Husserl then investigates *what* must remain continuous for identity to hold. He uses the example of sound: if sonic intensity changes discontinuously, *something* must still be retained continuously in the concretion — otherwise we'd have two disconnected matters in succession rather than one persisting thing. But not all "moments" (abstract dependent parts) are on the same footing. Intensity alone can't found the unity of a sound — two temporally adjacent discrete sounds don't become one just because an intensity-continuum bridges them. The reason is structural: intensity is always *intensity of* a quality, but quality is not *quality of* intensity. There's an asymmetric dependence here. Quality must be maintained continuously (in the mode of change or non-change) to constitute temporal unity in the first place; only then can the sound be said to become louder or softer.

**3\. Extension as the ὑποκείμενον — the ultimate founding stratum.** Husserl then transposes this analysis to the visual domain, and this is the decisive move. In vision, quality (color) is *not* a sufficient ground of unity either. The ultimate foundation is **extension** (*Ausdehnung*). The same extension can be filled with color continuously or discontinuously, but a thoroughgoing continuity in coloration alone would not unify two separated images. This transfers from the phenomenological to the Objective sphere: the unity of the spatial form (*Raumgestalt*) founds the unity of coloration and of all qualitative change. The spatial body is the **ὑποκείμενον** (Husserl uses the Greek term directly) — the substrate for all qualities in the specific sense, the "bearer" that is "covered" by quality everywhere, at every point.

The governing law Husserl articulates is: the filling of a continuous whole by particularities from another continuum can be discontinuous only at *individual places*, not everywhere. A body can have discontinuities of coloration (in constancy or in succession), but only against a medium of qualitative continuity.

**The concluding move** circles back to movement: since the identity of the thing presupposes the constant duration of the body as substrate, and the unity of the body requires continuity, then if one form of bodily change is movement, movement must *necessarily be continuous*. Discontinuous movement would shatter the very substrate on which thingly identity depends.

---

Architectonically, §81 is doing the foundational work for the entire chapter on movement-constitution. It establishes that spatial extension has a privileged constitutive role — it's not just one more sensory stratum alongside color, but the **founding** stratum that makes qualitative change and movement alike intelligible as changes *of an identical thing*. This connects directly to the broader trajectory of the *Dingvorlesung*, where Husserl is showing that spatiality isn't a mere empirical feature of things but is constitutively prior — the condition of possibility for thingly unity as such. You can also see here the seeds of the *Ideas II* analysis of material nature, where the res extensa as bearer of qualitative determinations gets further elaborated.

### **§82. Movement as change of location, and the appurtenant kinaesthetic motivations**

This section is the constructive core of Chapter 16, where Husserl gives the constitutive analysis of how movement is phenomenologically constituted. The fundamental thesis is that **movement is constituted entirely through a systematic shift in kinaesthetic coordination**, with no change whatsoever in the content of the appearances themselves.

The argument unfolds in three major steps:

**1\. Location-difference is kinaesthetic difference.** Husserl begins with the thought experiment of two co-existing, qualitatively identical things that differ only in location. What constitutes their difference? It can't reside in their respective manifolds of appearance, since by hypothesis those are completely similar. The difference resides solely in their **kinaesthetic coordination** — the different bodily postures and eye-movements I must undertake to bring each to givenness. To reach the appearance of the second thing, I must traverse a kinaesthetic path that is distinct from the one leading to the first. This kinaesthetic index is what individuates them spatially. What unifies them into a single co-existing world is precisely the fact that a kinaesthetically motivated path leads from the one to the other. Husserl adds that if the same kinaesthetic path led from a third thing *c* to both *a* and *a'*, the two would collapse into identity — so distinct kinaesthetic coordination is not just sufficient but *necessary* for spatial differentiation.

**2\. Movement as continuous re-coordination.** Here Husserl makes the decisive transfer: a thing that moves is always "the same" in every respect *except* location — which means the phases of movement behave exactly like a series of qualitatively identical but differently located things. Movement therefore consists in **nothing else changing except the kinaesthetic coordination**. He gives a concrete example: I sit still (constant K-complex), and a thing moves. At the start, image *a* pertains to my current posture K. As the thing moves, a new image *β* now appears coordinated to K — but *β* is the very image that, had the thing remained stationary, would have required me to adopt a different posture K'. The movement of the thing has reassigned *β* from K' to K. This reassignment propagates throughout the entire manifold: every phase of the movement brings with it a complete, systematically closed stationary manifold, but one whose kinaesthetic coordination has been continuously shifted. Movement is thus constituted not as a change in *what* appears, but as a lawful, continuous transformation of the *kinaesthetic conditions under which* things appear.

**3\. The limit-case and the opposition of rest and movement.** Husserl draws out a striking consequence. Since movement is entirely a matter of kinaesthetic re-coordination, the very same series of appearances can constitute either a stationary thing or a moving thing depending on what kinaesthetic sequence accompanies it. In particular, there's a limit-case: I can track a moving thing by following it with my body, staging a determinate kinaesthetic sequence that keeps the same image constantly present. The image doesn't change — yet the thing is moving. Conversely, if the thing is stationary and I remain still (kinaesthetic constancy), the same unchanging image constitutes rest. The judgment "this thing is stationary" versus "this thing is moving" thus rests on the relation between the image-series and the kinaesthetic series: the same enduring image plus kinaesthetic constancy \= rest; the same enduring image plus kinaesthetic change \= movement.

Husserl closes by noting that for a changing continuity of images (rather than the simple limit-case of a constant image), describing the situation is more complex, but the principle holds: the stationary character of a given image-series is a specific kinaesthetic modification, and movement stops as soon as the coordination between appearances and kinaesthetic circumstances takes on the character of stationarity.

**What's architectonically at stake:** §82 is arguably one of the most important sections in the entire *Dingvorlesung* for understanding Husserl's theory of spatial constitution, because it demonstrates that **movement is not constituted as a change in sensory content but as a lawful re-coordination of the kinaesthetic system**. This makes kinaesthesis — the felt bodily movement of the perceiving subject — constitutively prior to any objective determination of motion. The thing's movement is, so to speak, "read off" from the mismatch between what kinaesthetic posture *would have been* required for a given appearance under stationarity and what kinaesthetic posture *actually* obtains. This is a deeply anti-empiricist result: movement isn't given as a sensory datum but is constituted through a systematic lawfulness governing the correlation between appearance-manifolds and kinaesthetic systems.

You can see here how this feeds directly into the problem §83 will take up — the constitution of the Body's *own* movement (*Selbstbewegung*), where the kinaesthetic system is no longer a stable frame of reference but is itself what's moving — and also into the broader question (which Husserl flags as still unresolved at the end of §82) of how intuitions of movement can be "reinterpreted" as rest and vice versa, the phenomenological ground of the relativity of motion.

### **§83. The self-movement and the being-moved of the Body. Limits of the kinaesthetic constitution of the corporeal Body**

This is one of the richest and most layered sections in the entire *Dingvorlesung*. In §82, Husserl constituted movement through kinaesthetic re-coordination, but that analysis relied on the kinaesthetic system functioning as a stable, zero-point frame of reference — the subject sits still and the thing moves. §83 now asks: **what happens when the frame of reference itself moves?** This forces Husserl to confront the constitution of the *Leib* (the lived, corporeal Body) and the fundamental anomalies that distinguish it from every other thing.

The section proceeds through several interconnected problem-strata:

**1\. The reversibility of movement and rest.** Husserl opens with the observation that an intuition of movement can be interpreted as rest and vice versa — what he calls the source of all "sense-illusions" of location and movement. This is possible because, as §82 established, the manifold of appearances for a moving thing and a stationary thing can be *identical*; the difference lies solely in kinaesthetic coordination. But this means the constitutive system is in principle ambiguous, and the question of what counts as "really" moving versus "really" stationary is more complex than it first appeared.

**2\. The Body as anomalous thing.** Husserl then makes the pivotal admission: the entire preceding analysis has treated the Ego as a "spirit endowed with eyes, a Body-less spirit" (*ein leibloser Geist*) — a subject that has kinaesthetic sensations and appearance-manifolds but no Body that is itself a thing among things. Now the Body must be thematized. The Body *is* a thing, constituted like other things (I can look at my hand on the table, move my eyes, etc.), but it exhibits what Husserl calls **fundamental anomalies** (*grundlegende Anomalien*):

* I can never walk around my own Body, approach or recede from it at will. Every other thing in the world can "escape from me" — my Body cannot.  
* The Body always loses itself in the margin of the visual field and can never be pursued beyond that margin.  
* Even if I imagine my Body extended to infinity (e.g., feet receding infinitely), it would never vanish into a null-point the way remote external things do, because it retains its continuous connection to the Ego-center.  
* The manifold of images pertaining to the Body has a **distinctive kinaesthetic motivation**: the same kinaesthetic changes that bring the entire external world "into flux" (walking, running) leave the Body's images largely unchanged or only partly altered. It is precisely this asymmetry — the world flows while the Body remains relatively constant — that constitutes the Body as *moved*.

The Body moves without "receding from itself": the Ego-point is always co-moved. This is a constitutive peculiarity with no analogue among external things.

**3\. The double constitution of "self-moving" versus "being-moved."** The train-car example is the centerpiece. Husserl distinguishes two ways the Body can maintain its location relative to a moving external object:

**(a) Self-movement (*Selbstbewegung*):** I run alongside the train. A kinaesthetic sequence takes place to which there pertains an elapsing of the manifold of images of the outer world. The continuum of images of my Body also pertains to this kinaesthetic sequence. My Body, appearing on the whole in the same mode, "moves with me," along with all objects that remain constant in appearance under kinaesthetic change.

**(b) Being-moved (*Bewegtwerden*):** I sit in the train. There is kinaesthetic constancy — I have the kinaesthetic state of rest. Yet the images of the surroundings are *changing*, while those of the train car and my Body are *stationary*. According to the normal constitutive rule (§82), kinaesthetic constancy plus changing images should constitute the surroundings as moving. And indeed that is the *first*, foundational apprehension: the landscape appears to move, and the car appears stationary. But this "natural" reading must be overturned: in fact, I am the one being moved, and the surroundings are stationary. Husserl asks: **how is this reversal of apprehension constituted?**

**4\. Vicarious kinaesthetic motivation.** Husserl's answer is that the shaking of the car, the noise of the rolling wheels, the tactile sensations of jostling — these take over the motivational function that kinaesthetic sensations normally play. They are not directly kinaesthetic, but they are apprehended as **vicariously standing for** (*vikarierend für*) the normal kinaesthetic circumstances. The key constitutive insight: the end result — the series of appearances I have — is exactly the same whether I run to the destination myself or the car carries me there. The return to the same circumstances yields the same appearances; the "general character of a stationary manifold" is preserved. So the car-sensations can functionally replace kinaesthetic self-movement in the constitutive system. Husserl notes cases where even this vicarious motivation is absent (a hot-air balloon, where no visible or sensible series of motivating circumstances exist), flagging this as a limit-case that requires "indirect knowledge."

**5\. The constitutive principle of space.** Husserl draws from all this a general constitutive principle: **it pertains to the constitution of space that under the same circumstances, in the case of rest, the same things appear.** The "same circumstances" always include one's own body in a certain appearance, plus determinate groups of movement-sensations, pressure, tactile sensations, and even auditory sensations. This is the invariance-principle that underlies spatial objectivity.

**6\. Limits of kinaesthetic constitution — the role of intersubjectivity.** The final paragraphs mark a constitutive boundary. There are perceptual possibilities available for every other thing that are *absent* for my own Body: I cannot see myself moving the way I see other bodies move (with me kinaesthetically stationary while my body appears to recede). I cannot see myself being carried along in the train the way I see another passenger being carried. These are constitutive lacunae — possibilities of givenness that are structurally blocked by the Body's anomalous status. Husserl's response is that this gap is filled by **empathy and mutual communication**: I can relate others' assertions about their appearances to my own, and through this intersubjective mediation, constitute my Body's movement in ways that pure solitary perception cannot achieve.

**Architectonic significance:** §83 is where the *Dingvorlesung* hits its deepest stratum and also its constitutive limit. The entire preceding analysis constituted thingly space and movement from a quasi-disembodied standpoint — the "Body-less spirit" — and §83 simultaneously (a) integrates the Body into the constitutive account, showing how its anomalies are not defects but structurally necessary features of spatial constitution, and (b) marks where that account breaks down and requires supplementation by intersubjective constitution. The themes opened here — the Body as zero-point (*Nullpunkt*), vicarious motivation, the asymmetry between *Selbstbewegung* and *Bewegtwerden*, and the turn to intersubjectivity — are precisely the threads that Husserl takes up and develops in *Ideas II* (especially §§36–42 on the constitution of the Body as bearer of localized sensations) and in the *Cartesian Meditations* (Fifth Meditation). You can also see how this section connects to Claesges's concerns about the stratification of spatial constitution, since the Body here emerges as a constitutive stratum that is neither simply "subject" nor simply "object" but occupies a unique intermediate position — the hinge between kinaesthetic subjectivity and objective spatial reality.

# FINAL CONSIDERATION

### **§84. Real existence and real possibility**

This is the final lecture of the entire *Dingvorlesung* (delivered August 3, 1907), and it shifts registers dramatically. Having completed the constitutive analyses of spatial thinghood, movement, and the Body, Husserl now steps back to ask the fundamental epistemological-ontological question that the whole investigation has been building toward: **What does it mean for a thing to exist? And could it be that nothing exists at all?**

The section moves through several distinct layers:

**1\. The essence of thinghood as constituted unity in harmonious appearance.** Husserl opens with a compact summary of the entire lecture course's result: a thing is an identical intentional unity constituted in an actual or possible manifold of appearances, demonstrating its Being in a regulated, motivated nexus of appearances. Crucially, this nexus is one of **harmonious, mutually fulfilling** appearances, subtended by a continuous belief-consciousness that posits Being. Individual appearances can lack the character of actual positing, but the motivational structure of the total nexus determines basic positings — and these in turn make possible conflict, the revocation of positings ("nullifying consciousness"), and the wavering between belief-tendencies.

**2\. Being-otherwise, change, and not-being.** Husserl then distinguishes three modalities of disappointment of anticipation:

* **Re-determination** (*Umbestimmung*): The thing is, but is not as it was apprehended. The correction integrates into the thing's unity — the manifold of fulfilling perceptions remains "on the whole" the same, with only a local adjustment (e.g., a surface turns out to be a different color than anticipated). The thing persists through the correction.  
* **Change** (*Veränderung*): The thing is other from moment to moment but, in changing, is ever the same. The constitutive series are harmonious; fulfillment is disposed precisely toward the perpetual otherness, integrated into a lawful type.  
* **Not-being** (*Nichtsein*): Appearances arise that cannot be fitted into the lawfulness of posited Being. They refer to nexuses of appearance that do not flow harmoniously into the actually elapsing series. Husserl's example: a painting on a wall. The landscape in the picture appears perceptually, yet the wall is apprehended as continuous and unbroken. The fulfilling certainty of the room, the wall, and the physical picture-thing constantly *disappoints* the perceptual pretension of the depicted landscape, degrading it to fiction. Not-being thus takes its measure from Being — it is the conflict against pre-given Being that manifests a pretended Being as fiction.

**3\. The radical possibility: could nothing at all be real?** This is the philosophical climax of the section. Husserl asks: if every not-being presupposes a background of Being against which it is constituted as conflicting, does this mean that some Being *must* exist? Could it not be that *all* perception is ultimately illusory — that harmonious fulfillment might simply cease, that the series of appearances might run into one another so chaotically that no posited unity could be maintained? Could the entire stream of appearance dissolve into what Husserl calls a **"mere tumult of meaningless sensations"** (*ein bloßes Gewühl von Empfindungen*), a **"phenomenological maelstrom"** (*phänomenologischer Wirbel*)?

Husserl concedes that such a maelstrom is conceivable. It would not be absolute nothingness — it would be something — but it would be nothing that could constitute a world of things. There would be no I, no Thou, no physical world, no reality in the pregnant sense. He explicitly states: **"I do not see that \[a world\] would have to"** exist — there is no demonstrable absolute necessity that there must be a world.

This is a remarkable and philosophically daring admission. Husserl is saying that the existence of a world is not an a priori necessity but a factual state of affairs — an **irrational fact** (*irrationales Faktum*). The rationality that resides in the actual and possible nexuses of appearance, making possible the steadfast unity of things and the world, is itself a brute facticity.

**4\. The force of experience and rational positing.** Yet Husserl immediately tempers this. Every perception is a **rational positing** — not absolute, but like a force that can be overwhelmed by counter-forces. Experience is the force that guarantees the world's existence, and it is a force that **constantly draws new force from itself**: every perception integrates its force, every perception in the nexus is augmented by every other, corresponding to all the interwoven series of fulfillments. The force that grounds Being grows over the course of experience, especially through the advancing rationalization of experiential science, which secures for every exception its reintegration under a rule. The possibilities that work toward the not-being of a lawfully constituted world become **empty possibilities** — not meaningless, but irrational and baseless, overwhelmed by the ever-growing rational potency of coherent experience.

Husserl is careful to add: "I do not mean this as a proclamation of eternal truth; I merely exhort you to reflect on your own and to take the utmost care in these matters."

**5\. Real possibility versus mere possibility.** The final movement of the lecture develops a constitutive theory of modality. A non-given thing's actuality is "reduced to possibilities" — but not empty possible thoughts. These are **motivated possibilities**, grounded in the actual nexus of appearances. Husserl formalizes this: if appearance A arises under kinaesthetic circumstances K, then a functional relation is determined such that the K-sequence K→K' motivates the appearance-sequence A→A'. Any appearance is in principle compatible with any circumstances (mere phantasy-possibility), but once a definite unity AK is actualized, a **selection** occurs in the domain of possibilities: only those further connections that are functionally motivated by the actual nexus count as **real possibilities** (*reale Möglichkeiten*). These are delimited, dependent possibilities — possibilities under the assumption of an actuality, which pass over into motivated actualities when supplementary circumstances are actualized. Real possibilities can in turn ground further real possibilities in chains of hypothetical dependence.

The existence of a real thing is thus placed in relation to a **system of perceptual possibilities** — not an infinite set of free phantasy-possibilities, but a structured, motivated, functionally determined system grounded in actual experience.

**Architectonic significance:** §84 is doing several things at once. First, it provides the epistemological capstone to the entire *Dingvorlesung*: the constitutive analyses of perception, kinaesthesis, spatial form, qualitative filling, and movement all culminate in this account of what it means for the constituted thing to *be*. Second, the passage on the "phenomenological maelstrom" is one of Husserl's most philosophically exposed moments — an explicit confrontation with radical contingency that anticipates (and to some degree already answers) the problematic of the "annihilation of the world" passage in *Ideas I* §49. The key difference is that in 1907 Husserl is more cautious: he doesn't yet claim that consciousness would survive the world's annihilation as an absolute residuum, but instead frames the world's existence as an "irrational fact" whose rationality is constituted a posteriori through the growing force of coherent experience.

Third, the theory of real possibility at the end connects directly to Husserl's later work on horizonal intentionality and the potentiality structure of perception (developed extensively in *Experience and Judgment* and the *Crisis*). The idea that every actual perception motivates a determinate system of real possibilities — and that the thing's Being just *is* its place in this system — is one of Husserl's most distinctive contributions to the phenomenology of modality and anticipates much of the later discussion of the "horizon" as constitutive of objectivity.
